In
[
AB24
]
the Yang-Baxter equation was shown to imply a distributional match between the colored S6V model and a colored
q
-Boson model on the negative quadrant
\mathbb{Z}_{<0}\times\mathbb{Z}
(see the left side of Figure
3
), identifying the law of the former’s height function with the marginal law of the latter’s rightmost column; this colored
q
-Boson model is also sometimes called a “colored Hall-Littlewood process”
[
BW22
, Section 1.6]
; the
n=1
case of this object (with just one color) is called an uncolored Hall-Littlewood process. The paper
[
AB24
]
developed these types of matching results for considerably more general fused vertex models. Here, we only require the colored S6V model special case of that framework, whose Yang-Baxter equation origin is relatively quick to explain conceptually. Appendix
A
provides a self-contained proof of the result we use from
[
AB24
]
.

The colored
q
-Boson model on the negative quadrant gives rise in a fairly direct way to a colored line ensemble, which we call the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble (see Section
3.2
). Specifically, we set the location of the
i
th
curve of the color
j
line ensemble at site
y
to be the number of paths in the colored
q
-Boson model of color at least
j
that pass strictly above the point
(-i,y)
; see Figure
3
. The fact that the colored
q
-Boson model is prescribed by local Boltzmann weights implies a local Gibbs property for the full color-indexed collection of line ensembles. However, each constituent line ensemble in this colored family (except for the “first” one corresponding to all the colors in the system) will usually not marginally satisfy a Gibbs property on its own.

This correspondence identifies the rightmost column of the colored
q
-Boson model with the first (topmost) curves of the
n
line ensembles in the colored family. Therefore, the distributional match described above implies that the height function of the colored S6V model embeds as the first curves in the colored line ensemble (see Proposition
3.12
). Extracting the large scale limit for the colored S6V model thus reduces to doing so for the above colored Gibbsian line ensemble around its (top) edge. This task simplifies considerably when
q=0
, so we will first discuss that case (where our results are still new). We then explain what must be done to extend our results to all
q\in[0,1)
.

1.3.2.
Simplifications and asymptotics when
q=0

The weights of the colored (and uncolored)
q
-Boson model simplify when
q=0
to be either zero or one. This has several useful consequences.

First, in the case of only one color, the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble (i.e., uncolored Hall-Littlewood process), whose topmost curve encodes the uncolored S6V height function, becomes at
q=0
an ensemble of independent Bernoulli random walks conditioned on not intersecting. This is a Schur process, which is known to be a determinantal point process by work of Okounkov-Reshetikhin
[
OR03
]
. The finite-dimensional distributions around the top edge of the ensemble can therefore be computed exactly and shown to converge as
t\to\infty
to those of the Airy line ensemble. The convergence can then be upgraded to functional convergence of the curves by use of the non-intersecting Gibbs property and existing techniques in the theory of Gibbsian line ensembles, facilitated by the key property of monotone coupling that holds in this
q=0
case: Gibbs measures with ordered boundary conditions can likewise be stochastically ordered.

Now we pass to multiple colors. The colored
q=0
S6V height function is again related to the top curves of a colored line ensemble, consisting of a collection of several line ensembles indexed by color. The line ensemble corresponding to the lowest color in this family, which we assume to be
1
(as we may, by a color merging projection), has the marginal law of an uncolored line ensemble, in this case the above Schur process (Proposition
3.17
). The other line ensembles in the colored family are nontrivially coupled to this
1
-colored one via the colored
q=0
Boson weights. Let us explain how to extract the joint asymptotics for the
1
-colored and
2
-colored line ensembles; the case for more colors then follows inductively.

We begin by examining the
1
-colored line ensemble and conclude (as described above) that it marginally converges around its top edge to the parabolic Airy line ensemble. Next, conditioned on the
1
-colored line ensemble, we study the
2
-colored one. As all
q=0
Boson weights are either zero or one, this second line ensemble is seen to be determined completely by the first one. We then prove (Theorem
3.20
), through a careful inspection of these
q=0
Boson weights, that the
2
-colored line ensemble can be realized as an LPP problem (via a suitable “Pitman transform”) across the
1
-colored one. Therefore, the first and second line ensembles jointly converge in the scaling limit to a parabolic Airy line ensemble and an LPP problem across it, giving the Airy sheet.

Turning this back into the
q=0
colored S6V model, this implies the convergence of its height function under KPZ scaling to the Airy sheet. In the limit to ASEP, this shows that the height function for the colored TASEP under packed initial condition also converges to the Airy sheet.

When
q>0
, there are two obstructions in implementing the approach sketched above. The first is in extracting the edge limit of the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble; the second is in extracting the joint limit of the coupled line ensembles.

1.3.3.
Edge limit for uncolored line ensembles (
q>0
)

For
q>0
the basic input in Section
1.3.2
, the convergence of the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble to the parabolic Airy one, is no longer known; the reason is that the former (and likewise, S6V and ASEP) is not determinantal.

Hence, we adopt a more probabilistic route, by showing that any subsequential edge limit of the Hall-Littlewood line ensemble is Brownian Gibbsian; this means that it satisfies the Gibbs property for non-intersecting Brownian bridges, called the Brownian Gibbs property. This, with the recent strong characterization for Brownian Gibbsian line ensembles established in
[
AH23a
]
, would then uniquely identify the limit as the parabolic Airy line ensemble.
For ASEP, the use of
[
AH23a
]
can be bypassed by instead using results in
[
QS22
,
DM21
]
(though one must still verify the Brownian Gibbs property); see Remark
6.7
.

Assuming tightness under KPZ scaling of the Hall-Littlewood line ensemble around its top edge, the Brownian Gibbs property for subsequential limits follows from the Gibbs property of prelimits, along with Kolmós-Major-Tusnády type couplings between random walks and Brownian bridges. The main effort therefore rests in proving this tightness; the inputs we will need to do this are its Gibbs property and known
[
BCG16
]
one-point asymptotics for its top curve.

Previous works establishing full tightness for Gibbsian line ensembles all make substantial use of a monotonicity property (FKG inequality), stating that if one increases the boundary data then all of its paths stochastically increase. This monotonicity property is false for the Hall-Littlewood line ensemble, a fact originally observed in
[
CD18
]
, where this Gibbs property was first studied. That work showed tightness of the top curve of the Hall-Littlewood line ensemble by way of a much weaker form of monotonicity wherein, upon changing boundary conditions, certain one-point probabilities change monotonically up to a large constant. In order to extend tightness to an arbitrary number of curves, one could pursue such a weak form of monotonicity involving more curves. We were unable to produce a suitable generalization.

To explain the method we instead use, we first informally describe the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property (see Section
3.3
for a more precise definition). It states that the law of the top
k
curves on a spatial interval
(a,b)
conditioned on the entire external state of the ensemble only depends on the boundary data, i.e., the height of the top
k
curves at
\{a,b\}
and that of the
(k+1)
st
curve on
[a,b]
. The conditioned law is given explicitly by a reweighting of the law for
k
Bernoulli random walk bridges with the same boundary data, conditioned to neither intersect each other nor the
(k+1)
st
curve. The weight is proportional to a product of factors (one for each position) equal to either
1
or
1-q^{d}
, where
d
is the distance between two consecutively labeled curves at that position. The latter factor only occurs when the height difference between two consecutive curves decreases by
1
.

Now, to establish tightness of our line ensemble, we show that it suffices to
a priori
demonstrate a reasonable degree of separation between its curves. Indeed, since
q
is fixed, the factors
1-q^{d}
approach
1
as the distances
d
between the curves grows; although many such factors may exist in the product (up to the size of the spatial interval, of order
t^{2/3}
), if we can show a separation of
d\gg\log t
with high probability, then the reweighting factor becomes very close to
1
. We therefore incur little error by replacing these factors by
1
, after which the tightness analysis follows from arguments similar to those used in previously studied models.

It therefore suffices to show this
a priori
separation between curves. This is the primary technical component of the proof for tightness and is done by induction. Using the Gibbs property and the one-curve weak monotonicity (a minor refinement of that in
[
CD18
]
), we first prove that the
k
th
curve in the line ensemble is uniformly bounded below, assuming that the first
k-1
curves are uniformly separated (which holds trivially at
k=1
); then we show that the first
k
curves are likely separated at one spatial coordinate, using the control on how far down the
k
th
curve can be; and finally we conclude that the first
k
curves are uniformly separated across the full spatial interval. See Section
9
for further details.

1.3.4.
Approximate LPP representation for other colors (
q>0
)

As with the uncolored Gibbs property, the colored Gibbs property becomes more involved when
q>0
; its weights are not all equal to zero or one. So, the
2
-colored line ensemble in the family is now not deterministic upon conditioning on the
1
-colored one (which has the marginal law of an uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble; see Proposition
3.17
). In particular, the representation of the former as an LPP problem across the latter is no longer exactly true when
q>0
.

We instead prove that this LPP representation remains approximately true with high probability for any
q\in[0,1)
; see Theorem
3.20
. Our proof of this fact is combinatorial. While the colored
q
-Boson weights are not all equal to zero or one conditional on the
1
-colored line ensemble, they involve certain powers of
q
(namely, factors of
q^{A_{[k,\ell]}}
). We show that these powers become quite large, thereby exponentially penalizing the
2
-colored line ensemble, if the latter substantially differs from being an LPP problem across the
1
-colored one. Since
q<1
, this can be used to prove the above approximate LPP representation; see Section
4
for more details.

Using this approximate LPP representation, and the convergence of the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble to the parabolic Airy one, our Airy sheet limit results follow just as in the
q=0
case.

Notation

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a<b
,
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket:=\{a,\ldots,b\}
,
\llbracket a,\infty\Rparen:=\{a,a+1,\ldots\}
, and
\scalebox{0.97}{$\Lparen$}{-}\infty,a\rrbracket:=\{a,a-1,\ldots\}
. Symbols written in bold face such as
\bm{A}
or
\bm{v}
will represent vectors or tuples. For a finite set
A
,
\#A
will denote its cardinality. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,
a\wedge b
is shorthand for
\min(a,b)
.

For random objects
X
and
Y
taking values in some measurable space,
X\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}Y
means that their distributions are the same; we will often omit explicitly specifying the
\sigma
-algebra on the target space, but will specify its topology (in which case we endow the space with the associated Borel
\sigma
-algebra). For random objects
X_{n}
and
X
taking values in some common topological space,
\smash{X_{n}\xrightarrow{d}X}
means that
X_{n}
converges weakly to
X
; the topological space will be specified or obvious in the context. We will sometimes abuse notation and say
\smash{X_{n}\xrightarrow{d}\mu}
for some probability measure
\mu
on the same space. The space of continuous functions from a topological space
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
will be denoted
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
.
\mathcal{N}(m,\sigma^{2})
will denote the normal distribution with mean
m
and variance
\sigma^{2}
. For a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}
are shorthand for the conditional probability and conditional expectation given
\mathcal{F}
. Events will be written in sans serif font, e.g.,
\mathsf{E}
, and
\mathsf{E}^{c}
will denote the complement of
\mathsf{E}
.

We will sometimes write
f(\bm{\cdot})
for a function of the variable
\bm{\cdot}
, and sometimes
x\mapsto f(x)
. For
\bm{f}=(f_{1},f_{2},\ldots)
a collection of continuous functions,
\bm{f}[(\bm{\cdot},\bm{\cdot})\to(\bm{\cdot},\bm{\cdot})]
will denote the last passage percolation values in the environment given by
\bm{f}
, and is defined ahead in Definition
2.2
.

Organization of paper

Section
2
precisely defines our models and states our main results. Section
3
introduces the colored
q
-Boson model and colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble; explains the latter’s relation to the colored S6V model; sets up a framework for the convergence of the uncolored line ensemble to the parabolic Airy line ensemble (Theorem
3.19
); and states the approximate last passage percolation representation of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble (Theorem
3.20
). Section
4
proves the latter result. Section
5
explains how to fit ASEP into the vertex model framework of the other arguments. Section
6
begins setting up the ingredients for the proof of Theorem
3.19
and gives its proof assuming them. Section
7
gives the proofs of our main results. Sections
8
–
12
concern the proofs of the ingredients of Theorem
3.19
: Section
8
explains the weak monotonicity property our arguments rely on; Sections
9
and
10
contain the inductive argument yielding lower tail control of lower curves in the line ensemble as well as their uniform separation; Section
11
uses these to obtain control on the partition function; and Section
12
uses the same to establish all subsequential limits of the uncolored line ensembles have the Brownian Gibbs property.

There are also five appendices. Appendix
A
gives a self-contained proof of the mapping from
[
AB24
]
, between the colored S6V model and the colored
q
-Boson model, using the Yang-Baxter equation. Appendices
B
and
C
give routine proofs of certain miscellaneous lemmas and of asymptotics of last passage percolation and maximizer values across the parabolic Airy line ensemble. Appendix
D
gives the proofs of convergence results of the colored ASEP to the directed landscape and coupled KPZ fixed point and colored S6V to the directed landscape, using an idea communicated to us by Shalin Parekh, combined with an approximate monotonicity result in the S6V case. Appendix
E
contains the proofs of our results on the decoupling of the colored ASEP height functions and the stationary two-point function.

Code for simulations

Code for simulations of the ASEP sheet (as in the third panel of Figure
2
) and ASEP landscape is available in the arXiv source for this article.

2.
Models and statements of results

Here we define our limiting objects (Section
2.1
) and the models of study in this article and state our results (Section
2.2
for ASEP and Section
2.3
for stochastic six-vertex).

2.1.
The Airy line ensemble, Airy sheet, and directed landscape

In this section we define our limiting objects. We start with the parabolic Airy line ensemble, which is needed to give the definitions of the Airy sheet and the directed landscape.

2.1.1.
Parabolic Airy line ensemble

Definition 2.1

(Parabolic Airy line ensemble)
.

The
parabolic (and stationary) Airy line ensembles

\bm{\mathcal{P}},\bm{\mathcal{A}}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are
\mathbb{N}
-indexed families of random non-intersecting continuous curves
(\mathcal{P}_{i}(\bm{\cdot}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}:=(\mathcal{P}(i,\bm{\cdot}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\mathcal{A}_{i}(\bm{\cdot}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}:=(\mathcal{A}(i,\bm{\cdot}))_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
, related by

\mathcal{P}_{i}(x):=\mathcal{A}_{i}(x)-x^{2}.

The finite-dimensional distributions of
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
are determinantal with kernel the extended Airy kernel
K_{\mathrm{Ai}}^{\mathrm{ext}}
given by

K_{\mathrm{Ai}}^{\mathrm{ext}}\bigl((x,t);(y,s)\bigr):=\begin{cases}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{Ai}(x+\lambda)\mathrm{Ai}(y+\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda&t\geq s\\
-\int^{0}_{-\infty}e^{-\lambda(t-s)}\mathrm{Ai}(x+\lambda)\mathrm{Ai}(y+\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}\lambda&t<s,\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{Ai}
is the classical Airy function. This means the following (adopting the notation
(t_{j},y_{j})\in\bm{\mathcal{A}}
for the existence of some
k
such that
\mathcal{A}_{k}(t_{j})=y_{j}
and
\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}
for the density with respect to Lebesgue measure):
for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
(t_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(t_{m},y_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(2.1)

\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\Bigl((t_{j},y_{j})\in\bm{\mathcal{A}}\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,m\Bigr)=\det\Bigl[K_{\mathrm{Ai}}^{\mathrm{ext}}((y_{i},t_{i});(y_{j},t_{j}))\Bigr]_{1\leq i,j\leq m}\prod_{j=1}^{m}\mathrm{d}y_{j}.

These finite-dimensional distributions were first discovered in
[
PS02
]
(see also
[
Joh03
]
), and the existence of such an ensemble was shown in
[
CH14
, Theorem 3.1]
(and the uniqueness follows from the prescription (
2.1
) of the finite-dimensional distributions).

The distribution of
\mathcal{A}_{1}(0)
is known as the
GUE Tracy-Widom distribution
(discovered much earlier in random matrix theory
[
TW94
]
).

An alternative, more probabilistic, description of the Airy line ensemble is that it is the unique
[
AH23a
]
family of continuous random curves satisfying what is known as the Brownian Gibbs property; see Definition
6.4
and Proposition
6.6
for more details.

Figure 4
.
A depiction of the parabolic Airy line ensemble.

2.1.2.
Last passage percolation

We next introduce the notion of a last passage percolation (LPP) problem; this will be needed in defining the Airy sheet, as well as in some of our proofs.

Definition 2.2

(Last passage percolation)
.

Given real numbers
u<v
, a sequence of continuous functions
\bm{f}=(f_{1}
,
f_{2},\ldots,f_{n})
with
f_{i}:[u,v]\to\mathbb{R}
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket
, and natural numbers
j<k\leq n
, we define the LPP value from
(u,k)
to
(v,j)
in the environment given by
\bm{f}
by (where
t_{j-1}=v
and
t_{k}=u
)

(2.2)

\displaystyle\bm{f}[(u,k)\to(v,j)]=\sup_{t_{k}<\ldots<t_{j-1}}\sum_{i=j}^{k}\bigl(f_{i}(t_{i-1})-f_{i}(t_{i})\bigr).

2.1.3.
Airy sheet, directed landscape, and KPZ fixed point

The Airy sheet is defined via a coupling with the parabolic Airy line ensemble.

Definition 2.3

(Airy sheet,
[
DOV22
, Definition 8.1]
)
.

The
Airy sheet

\mathcal{S}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is the unique (in law) random continuous function such that:

(i)

Stationarity
:
\mathcal{S}
has the same law as
\mathcal{S}(\bm{\cdot}+x,\bm{\cdot}+x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
; and

(ii)

Coupling with
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
:
\mathcal{S}
can be coupled with a parabolic Airy line ensemble
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\mathcal{S}(0;\bm{\cdot})=\mathcal{P}_{1}(\bm{\cdot})
and for all
(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, almost surely,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(x;y)-\mathcal{S}(x;z)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\left(\bm{\mathcal{P}}[(-\sqrt{k/2x},k)\to(y,1)]-\bm{\mathcal{P}}[(-\sqrt{k/2x},k)\to(z,1)]\right).

The existence and uniqueness of the Airy sheet is established in
[
DOV22
, Proposition 8.2 and Theorem 8.3]
.

The Airy sheet can be thought of as a marginal of a four-parameter space-time object (the parameters being a pair of space-time coordinates) when the two time coordinates are set to be
0
and
1
. This four-parameter object is known as the directed landscape and is in fact constructed from the Airy sheet in a manner analogous to the construction of Brownian motion from the Gaussian distribution. We turn to defining it next.
In what follows, we let
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\shortuparrow}:=\left\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:s<t\right\}
.

Definition 2.4

(Directed landscape,
[
DOV22
, Definition 10.1]
)
.

The
directed landscape
is the unique (in law) random continuous function
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\shortuparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
that satisfies the following properties.

(i)

Airy sheet marginals
: For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
the increment over time interval
[s,s+t)
is a rescaled Airy sheet:

\displaystyle(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,s+t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(x,y)\mapsto t^{1/3}\mathcal{S}(xt^{-2/3};yt^{-2/3}).

(ii)

Independent increments
: For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and disjoint time intervals
\{(s_{i},t_{i}):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\}
, the random functions

(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,s_{i};y,t_{i})

are independent as
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
varies.

(iii)

Metric composition law
: Almost surely, for any
r<s<t
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{L}(x,r;y,t)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(x,r;z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)\Bigr).

The existence and uniqueness of the directed landscape is proved in
[
DOV22
, Theorem 10.9]
.

We also need to introduce height functions from general initial conditions in the directed landscape.

Definition 2.5

(KPZ fixed point)
.

Let
\mathrm{UC}
be the space of upper semi-continuous functions

f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
satisfying
f(x)\leq m|x|+c
for some
m,c<\infty
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. For a function
\mathfrak{h}_{0}\in\mathrm{UC}
, and
t>0
, define the
KPZ fixed point

(y,t)\mapsto\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0};y,t):\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
started from
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
at time
0
by

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0};y,t):=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathfrak{h}_{0}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t)\Bigr).

The KPZ fixed point was first defined in
[
MQR21
]
as a Markov process on UC (with the topology of local UC convergence, i.e., local Hausdorff convergence of hypographs; see
[
MQR21
, Section 3.1]
) by specifying its transition kernel through explicit formulas. This was later shown to be equivalent to (
2.4
), which arises as a scaling limit of solvable last passage percolation models, in
[
NQR20
, Corollary 4.2]
. Note that a natural coupling of
\smash{\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(j)}_{0};y,t)}
for different initial conditions
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{(j)}_{0}}
is obtained by using the same directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
in the variational formula.

2.2.
Colored ASEP

The colored (also called multi-species, -type, or -class) asymmetric simple exclusion process is an interacting particle system on
\mathbb{Z}
, where each particle has an associated
color
, which is an integer (multiple particles may share the same color). There is also an asymmetry parameter
q\in[0,1)
which is fixed. At time zero, we have a particle of some color at each site of
\mathbb{Z}
, which is encoded by the configuration
\eta_{0}=(\eta_{0}(i))_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\mathbb{Z}}
, where
\eta_{0}(i)
is the color of the particle at site
i
. The state at any later time
t
will be denoted by
\eta_{t}
.

\bm{\checkmark}
\bm{\checkmark}
\bm{\checkmark}
\bm{\checkmark}

Figure 5
.
A depiction of the graphical construction of colored ASEP. In this illustration, initially particles are in decreasing color from left to right. The arrows indicate the times where a left clock
\xi^{\mathrm{L}}_{x}
or right clock
\xi^{\mathrm{R}}_{x}
rang. If an arrow goes from site
x
to site
y
at time
t
and the color at time
t^{-}
of the particle at
x
is greater than that of the particle at
y
, the particles swap positions at time
t
(indicated by a green check mark); if not, nothing happens (red cross).

2.2.1.
The dynamics

We may construct the dynamics of colored ASEP using a variant of Harris’ graphical construction
[
Har78
]
(see Figure
5
). Fix a family of independent Poisson clocks
\bm{\xi}=\{\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{L}},\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{R}}\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
, one pair for each site in
\mathbb{Z}
, with
\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{L}}
of rate
q
and
\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{R}}
of rate
1
. Given the randomness of the clocks, the dynamics are deterministic. When the clock
\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{L}}
rings, the particle at site
x
attempts a jump to the left by one site, and when
\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{R}}
rings, it attempts a jump to the right by one site. The attempt succeeds if the particle at the target site is of color lower than the particle attempting to jump, and fails otherwise. If the attempt succeeds, the two particles swap positions. The well-definedness of this description follows from
[
Har78
, Section 10]
. Note that higher colors have higher priority, in contrast to the dynamics in terms of “classes”, where typically particles of higher class have lower priority (e.g., second class particles in comparison to first class particles). Our convention is consistent with behavior of higher color arrows in the colored S6V model that we will shortly introduce.

The
uncolored (or single-species) ASEP
corresponds to the case when there are only two colors in the system; in the typical terminology, the particles of higher color are referred to as particles, while the particles of lower color are referred to as holes.

Remark 2.6
.

An important property of colored ASEP is known as
color merging
. It says that one can project colored ASEP onto one of fewer colors by declaring all particles of color lying in a given interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
to be the same color (which is itself an element of
I
, or, more generally, lies between the largest color below
I
in the system and the smallest color above
I
in the system); that this holds is readily apparent from the above description of the dynamics. Equivalently, any function
f:\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
which is weakly monotone commutes with the Markov semigroup of the process; in particular, after merging colors we may relabel the groups in such a way that the relabeling maintains the order of the groups. The case of obtaining uncolored ASEP from colored ASEP is a particular example of color merging.

2.2.2.
Packed and step initial conditions

The initial condition we will work with from now on unless otherwise specified is the
packed initial condition
, where we have a particle of color
i
at site
-i
for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. By color merging, we can easily obtain the case of
step initial condition
for finitely many colors (and thus uncolored ASEP in the case of two colors): for integers
\ell_{k}<\ldots<\ell_{1}
, we merge the colors in
\scalebox{0.97}{$\Lparen$}{-}\infty,\ell_{k}\rrbracket
to have color
k
; those in
\llbracket\ell_{i+1}+1,\ell_{i}\rrbracket
to have color
i
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
; and those in
\llbracket\ell_{1}+1,\infty\Rparen
to have color
0
.

We note that under the packed initial condition,
\eta_{t}
is a permutation on
\mathbb{Z}
(i.e.,
\eta_{t}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
is a bijection), and thus colored ASEP can be thought of as a Markov chain on such permutations in which transpositions are applied at rate
q
or
1
depending on whether the entries are sorted or not respectively. In this sense, our results can be understood as a scaling limit for this infinite random transposition model.

2.2.3.
The ASEP sheet

As mentioned, we are now working with the packed initial condition.
The first observable of ASEP that we study, called the
colored height function
is defined, for
t\geq 0
, by

(2.5)

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t):=\#\bigl\{z>y:\eta_{t}(z)\geq-x\bigr\},

or, in words, it is the number of particles of color greater than or equal to
-x
which are strictly to the right of
y
at time
t
. Equivalently, it is the number of particles that were initially at or to the left of
x
which are strictly to the right of
y
at time
t
. It can also be thought of as a collection indexed by
x\in\mathbb{Z}
of coupled copies of the uncolored ASEP’s height function or current under step initial condition from
x
(i.e., a particle at every site at or to the left of
x
), by writing it as
(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;\bm{\cdot},t))_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
. We will refer to this coupling as the
colored coupling
; it agrees with the well-known basic coupling, which we will introduce in more detail in Section
2.2.6
.

Our results concern the scaling limit of this collection of height functions, which we define next. Let
\gamma=1-q
and fix
\alpha\in(-1,1)
. Let
\mu^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\alpha)
and
\sigma^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\alpha)
be defined by

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mu(\alpha)=\mu^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\alpha):=\tfrac{1}{4}(1-\alpha)^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma(\alpha)=\sigma^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\alpha):=\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\alpha^{2})^{2/3},

and let the spatial scaling factor
\beta^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\alpha)
be given by

(2.7)

\displaystyle\beta(\alpha)=\beta^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\alpha):=\frac{2\sigma(\alpha)^{2}}{|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|(1-|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|)}=2(1-\alpha^{2})^{1/3}.

Definition 2.7

(ASEP sheet)
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, we define the
ASEP sheet

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, for
x
and
y
such that the arguments of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
below are integers, by

(2.8)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x;y):=\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\Bigl(\mu(\alpha)2\varepsilon^{-1}+\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)\beta(\alpha)(y-x)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\\
&-h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}\bigl(\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3},0;2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}+\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3},2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}\bigr)\Bigr);\end{split}

the values at all other
x
,
y
are determined by linear interpolation.

Thus the ASEP sheet captures the spatial scaling limit of the colored ASEP at time
2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}
.

2.2.4.
An explanation of the scalings

Let us say a few words on a heuristic to understand the scalings in Definition
2.7
. The
\varepsilon^{-2/3}
spatial scaling and
\varepsilon^{1/3}
factor outside come from the KPZ universality class scaling exponents (see the surveys
[
Cor12
,
QS15
]
). We evaluate the height function at time
2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}
for the following reasons. The factor of
2
is essentially just a convention; it allows us to include fewer constant factors in the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}
to ensure that it will converge to
\mathcal{S}
and matches the conventions in earlier works such as
[
MQR21
,
QS22
]
. On the other hand, the factor of
\gamma^{-1}
is important as it allows all other scaling constants to not depend on
q
; in other words, by speeding up the process by the single particle drift, all ASEPs (i.e., corresponding to different values of
q
) look the same on the fluctuation scale, a fact which was previously known for various marginals. Finally,
\alpha
controls the macroscopic velocity of the particles around which we look, and the range
(-1,1)
of
\alpha
corresponds to the full “rarefaction” fan of the step initial condition’s hydrodynamic limit shape (as given by
\mu(\alpha)
).

Next we explain the definitions of
\mu(\alpha)
and
\sigma(\alpha)
from (
2.6
) and
\beta(\alpha)
from (
2.7
), as well as their role in (
2.8
). Essentially, the latter two are chosen such that in the limit, the local diffusion rate and parabolic curvature of
y\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(0;y)
are
2
and
1
respectively, matching those of the parabolic Airy line ensemble (Definition
2.1
). More precisely, it is known
[
Ros81
,
BF87
,
Rez91
]
(see also
[
TW09
, Theorem 3]
or
[
BO17
, Theorem 11.3]
) that the first order behavior of
\smash{h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(0,0;2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1},2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})}
is
\smash{\mu(\alpha)\cdot 2\varepsilon^{-1}=\frac{1}{4}}(1-\alpha)^{2}\cdot 2\varepsilon^{-1}
(i.e.,
\mu(\alpha)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon\smash{h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(0,0;\alpha\varepsilon^{-1},\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})}
); thus, locally, one would expect the process to behave like a Bernoulli random walk (with increments of
0
or
-1
) with drift
\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)=\frac{\mathrm{d}\ }{\mathrm{d}\alpha}\frac{1}{4}\smash{(1-\alpha)^{2}}=-\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)
, which must be subtracted off to see the fluctuations. Finally, note that the local curvature of the limit profile is
\frac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)=\smash{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\ }{\mathrm{d}\alpha^{2}}\frac{1}{4}(1-\alpha)^{2}=\frac{1}{4}}
and the approximately Bernoulli random walk behavior has increment variance
|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|(1-|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|)=\frac{1}{4}(1-\alpha^{2})
. The spatial scaling factor of
\beta(\alpha)
and the external factor of
\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}
are chosen such that

(2.9)

\displaystyle\beta(\alpha)|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|(1-|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|)\sigma(\alpha)^{-2}=2\quad\text{and}\quad\tfrac{1}{4}\beta(\alpha)^{2}\mu^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}=1,

i.e., the local diffusion rate is
2
and the local curvature is
1
; for the latter relation, note that there is an extra factor of
\frac{1}{2}
compared to the curvature discussion above. This arises because we are looking at the process with an extra factor of
2
in the time coordinate; more precisely, it arises because we expect
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP};\varepsilon}(0;y)
to be approximately

\displaystyle\mu\left(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{-1}}(2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}+\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3})\right)\cdot 2\varepsilon^{-1}

\displaystyle\approx\left(\mu(\alpha)+\beta(\alpha)\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3}\cdot(2\varepsilon^{-1})^{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)\beta(\alpha)^{2}y^{2}\varepsilon^{-4/3}\cdot\frac{1}{(2\varepsilon^{-1})^{2}}\right)\cdot 2\varepsilon^{-1},

and we see the factor of
\frac{1}{2}
coming from simplifying
(2\varepsilon^{-1})/(2\varepsilon^{-1})^{2}
in the last term.

These scaling coefficients can also be derived from the general KPZ scaling theory explained in
[
Spo14
]
, which makes use of information about the stationary measures of the system (and indeed the above explanation shares many features with this theory).
Thus we expect relations of the form (
2.9
), which allows one to derive
\sigma(\alpha)
from an expression for the law of large numbers
\mu(\alpha)
, to hold in greater generality.

2.2.5.
Main result for ASEP

Our main result for colored ASEP is proven in Section
7.1
.

Theorem 2.8

(Airy sheet convergence for ASEP)
.

Fix any asymmetry
q\in[0,1)
and any velocity
\alpha\in(-1,1)
in the rarefaction fan. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}}
weakly in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
under the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

In the rest of Section
2.2
, we give various consequences of Theorem
2.8
(some of which are obtained on combining with existing results in the literature). This requires us to first introduce the basic coupling.

2.2.6.
The basic coupling

The graphical construction of ASEP provided in Section
2.2.1
provides a natural way to couple together the evolution of uncolored ASEP from all choices of initial conditions—this is also called the
basic coupling
.

Consider countably many initial conditions
\eta_{0}^{(1)},\eta_{0}^{(2)},\ldots\in\{0,1\}{Z}
, where
\eta_{0}^{(j)}(i)
represents the state at location
i
in the
j
th
initial condition, with
0
for a hole and
1
for a particle. To define the basic coupling, as in Section
2.2.1
, we fix a family of independent Poisson clocks
\bm{\xi}=\{\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{L}},\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{R}}\}_{x\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Then we evolve
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j)}}
for each
j
according to the dynamics described in Section
2.2.1
, where we use the same family of clocks
\bm{\xi}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. For example, if
\xi_{x}^{\mathrm{L}}
rings at time
t
, then the particle at location
x
in the configuration
\smash{\eta^{(j)}_{t^{-}}}
attempts a jump to the left for each
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

We observe that this coupling recovers the colored coupling in the case that the set of particle locations in
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j+1)}}
is a subset of those in
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j)}}
for each
j
, i.e.,
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j+1)}(x)=1}
implies
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j)}=1}
; in colored ASEP, this corresponds to the initial condition with the particle at site
x
having color
\smash{\min\{j\geq 0:\smash{\eta^{(j+1)}_{0}(x)=0}\}=\#\{j:\eta_{0}^{(j)}(x)=1\}}
. Of course, the basic coupling itself is more general since it allows the coupling of arbitrary initial conditions.

We also note that we may consider the same evolution, using the fixed Poisson clocks, of an initial condition started at time
s>0
, i.e., we set the configuration at time
s
to be some
\eta
and apply the dynamics from Section
2.2.1
from there. In this way the basic coupling gives a coupling of initial conditions started at different times as well.

Using the basic coupling, we can define a four parameter process extending the ASEP sheet, which we call the ASEP landscape; here, the two additional parameters arise from allowing the time at which the colored height function is evaluated, as well as the time at which the evolution from the step initial condition is begun, to vary. We first define the evolution of the uncolored ASEP height function from an arbitrary initial condition or an initial height function; this definition is more general than (
2.5
) in that it allows initial particle configurations which are not right-finite, i.e., it is possible to have infinitely many particles initially to the right of zero.

Definition 2.9

(Bernoulli path)
.

Let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a (possibly infinite) interval. We say a function
\gamma:\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
is a
Bernoulli path
if
\gamma(x+1)-\gamma(x)\in\{0,-1\}
for all
x
with
x,x+1\in\Lambda
.

Let
h_{0}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
be a Bernoulli path. We define an associated configuration
\eta_{h_{0}}
by

(2.10)

\displaystyle\eta_{h_{0}}(y):=h_{0}(y-1)-h_{0}(y);

see Figure
6
. Note that, in the case that
h_{0}(y)=0
for all large enough
y
,
h_{0}
is related to
\eta_{h_{0}}
by
h_{0}(y)=\sum_{i=y+1}^{\infty}\eta_{h_{0}}(i)
; this is exactly the case of a right-finite initial condition.

Figure 6
.
A depiction of a Bernoulli path
h_{0}
and the associated particle configuration
\eta_{h_{0}}
. The dashed portions of the path illustrate the definition of the new height function, under the corresponding displayed particle movements.

For
h_{0}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
a Bernoulli path and
s\geq 0
, define the (uncolored) height function
(y,t)\mapsto h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;y,t)
for
t>s
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}
as follows (see also Figure
6
). For a function
f
with left limits, we use the notation
f(r^{-}):=\lim_{s\shortuparrow r}f(s)
; note that this does not require
f(r)
to be defined. Start ASEP under basic coupling at time
s
with initial particle configuration
\eta_{h_{0}}
. Whenever a particle jumps from
y
to
y+1
at time
r>s
, define
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;y,r)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;y,r^{-})+1
(and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;x,r)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;x,r^{-})
for all
x\neq y
), and whenever a particle jumps from
y
to
y-1
at time
r
, define
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;y-1,r)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;y-1,r^{-})-1
(and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;x,r)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;x,r^{-})
for all
x\neq y-1
).
The utility of the definition of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},s;y,t)
is that, while the ASEP dynamics only sees the discrete derivative of
h_{0}
(through
\eta_{h_{0}}
), here we keep track of the constant shift in
h_{0}
as well.

For multiple initial conditions
(\smash{h_{0}^{(j)}})_{j=1}^{\infty}
and times
s_{j}>0
, we couple the height functions
(y,t)\mapsto h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\smash{h_{0}^{(j)}},s_{j};y,t)
using the basic coupling for the underlying ASEP dynamics.

2.2.7.
The ASEP landscape

Now we may define the unscaled version of the ASEP landscape.
For
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
with
x<y
and
0<s<t
, we overload the notation and define

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,s;y,t)

\displaystyle:=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}\bigl(h_{0,x},s;y,t\bigr),

coupled via the basic coupling, where

h_{0,x}(z):=(x-z)\mathbbm{1}_{z\leq x}.

In words, we start the ASEP under basic coupling at time
s
with a step initial condition at
x
, and then evaluate the height function at location
y
at time
t
.

It follows immediately that this definition generalizes the definition of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)
from (
2.5
) as a process in
x
,
y
, and
t
. We also note that
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},\bm{\cdot})
corresponds to starting colored ASEP with packed initial condition at time
s
, coupled (for different values of
s
) to the same environment of Poisson clocks.

We now define the ASEP landscape and the scaled general initial condition height function.

Definition 2.10

(ASEP landscape)
.

Recall
\mu(\alpha)
,
\sigma(\alpha)
, and
\beta(\alpha)
from (
2.6
) and (
2.7
). For
\varepsilon>0
, the
ASEP landscape
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x,s;y,t):=\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\Bigl(\mu(\alpha)(t-s)\cdot 2\varepsilon^{-1}+\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)\beta(\alpha)(y-x)\varepsilon^{-2/3}

\displaystyle-h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}\bigl(\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3},2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}s;2\alpha(t-s)\varepsilon^{-1}+\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3},2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t\bigr)\Bigr).

As in the case of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}
in (
2.8
), this definition is for those
x
,
y
such that the arguments of
\smash{h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}}
are integers, and the value at all other
x
,
y
are determined by linear interpolation.

Definition 2.11
.

For general initial condition
h_{0}
, we define a scaled version of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},0;y,t)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(h_{0};y,t):=\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\Bigl(\mu(\alpha)t\cdot 2\varepsilon^{-1}+\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3}

\displaystyle-h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}\bigl(h_{0},0;2\alpha t\varepsilon^{-1}+\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3},2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t\bigr)\Bigr),

for
y
such that the argument of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
is an integer, and by linear interpolation elsewhere.

The next corollary of Theorem
2.8
gives the scaling limit of the ASEP landscape, as well as of the height functions under general initial conditions coupled via the basic coupling. This will be proven in Appendix
D
, using an idea communicated to us by Shalin Parekh. For a set
\mathcal{T}
, we define
\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}:=\{(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}:s<t\}
. Below, for a topological space
\mathcal{X}
and a set
A
,
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})^{A}
is endowed the product topology (across
A
) of the topology on
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{X},\mathbb{R})
of uniform convergence on compact sets.

Corollary 2.12

(Directed landscape and KPZ fixed point convergence for ASEP)
.

Fix
q\in

[0,1)
and a countable set
\mathcal{T}\subseteq[0,\infty)
. Then, the following limits hold as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(1)

(Directed landscape). For any velocity
\alpha\in(-1,1)
, in
\smash{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})^{\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}}
,

\smash{\bigl(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)\bigr)_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}\xrightarrow{d}\bigl(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)\bigr)_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}}.

(2)

As a special case, for any
\alpha\in(-1,1)
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{k}<\ldots<x_{1}
, in
\smash{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})^{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\mathcal{T}}}
,

\smash{\bigl(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x_{i},0;\bm{\cdot},t)\bigr)_{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{T}}}\xrightarrow{d}\bigl(\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;\bm{\cdot},t)\bigr)_{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{T}}.

(3)

(Coupled KPZ fixed point). Fix
\alpha=0
. For each
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
, suppose
\smash{h_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon}}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
is a sequence of (possibly random, and independent of the ASEP dynamics) Bernoulli paths and
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}}
is a (possibly random, and independent of
\mathcal{L}
) continuous function. Assume they satisfy
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}}(x)\leq C(1+|x|^{1/2})
for some
C<\infty
almost surely for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, such that
x\mapsto\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon}(x)}:=-2\varepsilon^{1/3}\smash{(h_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon}(2x\varepsilon^{-2/3})+x\varepsilon^{-2/3})}
converges to
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
for
i=1,\ldots,k
jointly in distribution under the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets (where we regard
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot})}
as a continuous function by linear interpolation). Then, in
\smash{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})^{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\mathcal{T}}}
,

\bigl(\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(h_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon};\bm{\cdot},t)\bigr)_{{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{T}}}\xrightarrow{d}\smash{\bigl(\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}};\bm{\cdot},t)\bigr)_{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{T}},

where the lefthand side is coupled via the basic coupling and the righthand side is coupled as in (
2.4
) under the same directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
.

Item (3) is stated only for
\alpha=0
as its proof will use the marginal convergence (i.e., for each
j
separately) of
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},T}(h_{0}^{(j)};y,t)}
to
\smash{\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(j)}_{0}};y,t)
from
[
QS22
]
, which is stated and proved therein only for
\alpha=0
.

Note that the convergence to the directed landscape and KPZ fixed point stated here is for times in a fixed countable set, and not uniform convergence as a continuous function for all times in an interval. Obtaining the latter form of convergence would require proving certain tightness estimates on ASEP as a temporal process that we do not pursue here.

Remark 2.13

(Scaling limit of ASEP with finitely many colors)
.

By color merging, Corollary
2.12
(2) is equivalent to the following statement on the space-time scaling limit of colored ASEP with finitely many colors. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha\in(-1,1)
, and fix real numbers
x_{k}<\ldots<x_{1}
. Consider colored ASEP with initial condition of holes (or particles of color zero) at locations
\llbracket\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{1}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+1,\infty\Rparen
; particles of color
k
at locations
\scalebox{0.97}{$\Lparen$}{-}\infty,\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{k}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor\rrbracket
; and particles of color
i
at all locations in
\llbracket\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{i+1}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+1,\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{i}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor\rrbracket
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
.
By color merging,
(y,t)\mapsto(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x_{1},0;y,t),\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x_{k},0;y,t))
is exactly a centered and rescaled version of the height functions associated to ASEP with
k
different colors, evaluated around the location
2\alpha t\varepsilon^{-1}
and, by Corollary
2.12
(2), they converge to the directed landscape.

2.2.8.
Convergence to stationary horizon

Introduced in
[
Bus24
]
, the
stationary horizon

G=(G_{\xi})_{\xi\in\mathbb{R}}
is the unique process (under certain slope conditions)
[
BSS24
]
taking values in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
of jointly invariant initial conditions for the KPZ fixed point under the directed landscape coupling (
2.4
). A precise definition of the process can be found, for example, in
[
BSS22
, Appendix D]
. It is expected to be the universal scaling limit of multi-type invariant distributions in the KPZ universality class.

By combining Corollary
2.12
with recent results of
[
BSS23
]
, we can obtain weak convergence to the stationary horizon for the (rescaled) height function corresponding to the stationary colored ASEP. In the case of colored TASEP, this was proven (under a stronger topology) in
[
BSS22
]
, using detailed information of the structure of the multi-species invariant measure
[
FM07
]
. For colored ASEP, these stationary measures have been the topic of several recent studies
[
EPM09
,
CGW15
,
Mar20
,
CMW22
,
ANP23
]
, and their structure is considerably more involved. While a direct proof of their convergence to the stationary horizon might take some effort, here we deduce it as a quick consequence of a different convergence statement (Corollary
2.12
).

Now we give the setup for stating this stationary horizon convergence.
First, given
k\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence of densities
\bm{\rho}=(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{k})\in(0,1)^{k}
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\rho_{i}\leq 1
, there exists a unique translation-invariant stationary distribution
\mu_{\bm{\rho}}
for colored ASEP with colors
1,\ldots,k
such that the marginal distribution of the particle configuration obtained by considering only particles of colors
j
through
k
(i.e., merging colors
j
through
k
and regarding particles of lower colors as holes) is product Bernoulli with density
\sum_{i=j}^{k}\rho_{i}

[
Mar20
, Theorem 3.2]
.

In order to obtain the stationary horizon scaling limit, we need the component densities of
\bm{\rho}
to be perturbed on the diffusive scale around density
\rho=\frac{1}{2}
; this choice of density corresponds to the choice of slope
\alpha=0
in Corollary
2.12
(3), and the extension to general
\rho
would require an extension of the results of
[
MQR21
,
QS22
]
to general
\alpha
. More precisely, let
\varepsilon
be a scaling parameter and
\bm{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
with
\xi_{1}<\ldots<\xi_{k}
. Consider
\bm{\rho}=\bm{\rho}^{\varepsilon}_{\bm{\xi}}=(\rho_{1},\ldots,\rho_{k})
with
\smash{\rho_{k}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\xi_{1}\varepsilon^{1/3}}
and
\rho_{k-i}=\smash{\frac{1}{2}}(\xi_{i+1}-\xi_{i})\varepsilon^{1/3}
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
. Let
\smash{\eta\in\llbracket 0,k\rrbracket^{\smash{\mathbb{Z}}}}
(0 for holes) be a particle configuration sampled according to
\mu_{\bm{\rho}}
. Define the initial conditions
h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}
for
j=1,\ldots,k
by

\displaystyle h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}(0)=0\quad\text{and}\quad h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}(x-1)-h_{0}^{(j)}(x)=\mathbbm{1}_{\eta(x)\geq k-j+1}.

By the stationarity of
\eta
,
(h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon})
satisfies
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon},0;\bm{\cdot},t)-h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon},0;0,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot})
jointly over
j=1,\ldots,k
. Further, for each
j
,
\smash{h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}}
is marginally distributed as a two-sided Bernoulli random walk with drift
-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\xi_{j}\varepsilon^{1/3}
. Below we regard
h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}
as a continuous function on
\mathbb{R}
by linear interpolation.

Corollary 2.14

(Stationary horizon convergence for ASEP)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\xi_{1}<\ldots<\xi_{k}
, and let
\smash{h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}}
be as above. As
\varepsilon\to 0
, in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets,

(2.11)

\left(x\mapsto-2\varepsilon^{1/3}\left(h_{0}^{(j),\varepsilon}\bigl(2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\bigr)+x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\right)\right)_{j=1}^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(G_{\xi_{j}})_{j=1}^{k}.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Corollary
2.12
(3) combined with
[
BSS23
, Theorem 2.4]
. Assumption 1 of the latter is satisfied by the fact that the
i
th
component of the lefthand side of (
2.11
) converges to a Brownian motion of drift
2\xi_{i}
, for each
i
; Assumption 2 is merely that we consider the jointly invariant distribution; and Assumption 3 is satisfied due to Corollary
2.12
(3).
∎

2.2.9.
Decoupling for colored ASEP and non-linear fluctuating hydrodynamics

Over the past decade, a set of predictions for the asymptotic behavior of multi-component KPZ models has been developed under the name of non-linear fluctuating hydrodynamics—see for instance
[
Spo14a
,
FSS13
,
MS13
,
SS15
,
Spo16
]
. Among those predictions is the decoupling of height function fluctuations and stationary two-point functions between two components started in initial data with different characteristic lines.
Here we consider the colored ASEP as a multi-component KPZ model and verify a strong form of such decoupling, focusing on the two-component case for simplicity. Specifically, we run the colored ASEP for time of order
\varepsilon^{-1}
and examine its two colored height functions when their associated characteristic slopes differ by order
\beta\varepsilon^{1/3}
. We show that they become asymptotically independent, after first sending
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
and then
\beta\rightarrow\infty
. The advantage in this double scaling limit is that, after the first KPZ limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, we can relate the height functions (and stationary two-point functions) to the directed landscape; we then prove the decoupling in the
\beta\rightarrow\infty
limit by geometric considerations of the latter. Our results show that the asymptotic decoupling of the colored height functions already manifests itself if the associated characteristic slopes differ by an amount growing barely larger than
\varepsilon^{1/3}
. This suggests, though does not directly imply as stated, that this decoupling should also hold when the characteristic slopes are uniformly bounded away from each other (formally corresponding to taking
\beta\rightarrow\infty
simultaneously as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, at rate
\varepsilon^{-1/3}
); we expect this could be proved by using the proof machinery developed here but do not pursue that.

In order to state our first decoupling result (about height functions), let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. Let
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous (possibly random) and satisfy the following slope property: there exists
R<\infty
such that, almost surely, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

(2.12)

\begin{split}|\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)}(y)|&\leq|y|+R|\beta|\\
|\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}(y)-2\beta y|&\leq|y|+R|\beta|.\end{split}

The above condition will be assumed on the limit of the ASEP initial condition height functions we work with. We note that the condition does not specify the slopes precisely and only up to a window of width
2
, but this will not be important for our purposes.

The following corollary gives the asymptotic independence of the ASEP height functions as the scaling parameter
\varepsilon\to 0
followed by taking the slope parameter
\beta\to\infty
. The first part concerns when the height functions are evaluated at the same point, while in the second they are evaluated along their respective (distinct) characteristic lines emanating from the origin (note that
\beta
controls the slope of the characteristic, so that, at time
2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t
, the characteristic of the initial condition converging to
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)}}
is near
0
and that of the initial condition converging to
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}}
is near
\lfloor 2\beta\varepsilon^{-2/3}t\rfloor
). The corollary will be proven in Appendix
E
.

Corollary 2.15

(Asymptotic independence of colored ASEP height functions)
.

Fix
R>0
,
T\geq 1
, and
\mathcal{T}\subseteq[T^{-1},T]
finite. For any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
i\in\{1,2\}
, suppose
h_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
is a Bernoulli path such that, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

-2\varepsilon^{1/3}\left(h_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon}(\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor)+\lfloor x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}

in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets, where
\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{0},\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are independent (in particular, they may be deterministic), continuous, and satisfy (
2.12
) with
\beta
and
R
almost surely. For
i\in\{1,2\}
and
\varepsilon>0
, define
\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
by (recall Definition
2.11
)

\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon}(x,t)=-2\varepsilon^{1/3}\left(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}\bigl(h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0},0;\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor,2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t\bigr)+\lfloor x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor-\tfrac{1}{2}t\varepsilon^{-1}\right),

coupled by the basic coupling across
i\in\{1,2\}
. There exist constants
C,\delta>0
depending on
R
and
T
such that the following holds. (1) For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exist independent processes
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1),\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2),\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}\times[T^{-1},T]\to\mathbb{R}
and a coupling of
(\mathfrak{h}^{(1),\varepsilon},\mathfrak{h}^{(2),\varepsilon})
with
(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1),\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2),\varepsilon})
such that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{|\beta|\to\infty}\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\sup_{|x|\leq\delta|\beta|^{1/2}}\left|\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon}(x,s)-\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i),\varepsilon}(x,s)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right)=0.

(2) For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exist independent processes
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1),\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{char}},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2),\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{char}}:\mathbb{R}\times[T^{-1},T]\to\mathbb{R}
and a coupling of
(\mathfrak{h}^{(1),\varepsilon},\mathfrak{h}^{(2),\varepsilon})
with
(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1),\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{char}},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2),\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{char}})
such that,

(2.14)

\begin{split}\lim_{|\beta|\to\infty}\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\sup_{|x|\leq\delta|\beta|^{1/12}t}\left|\mathfrak{h}^{(1),\varepsilon}(x,s)-\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1),\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{char}}(x,s)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right)&=0\quad\text{and}\\
\lim_{|\beta|\to\infty}\,\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\sup_{|x+\beta s|\leq\delta|\beta|^{1/2}t}\left|\mathfrak{h}^{(2),\varepsilon}(x,s)-\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2),\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{char}}(x,s)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right)&=0,\end{split}

i.e., asymptotic independence holds as
|\beta|\to\infty
when evaluating at locations near the respective characteristic lines emanating from the common point
0
.

A version of Corollary
2.15
can also be formulated and proved for more than two colors in an entirely similar way, though we do not do so here. Also, though the statement assumes the initial conditions satisfy (
2.12
) almost surely for a fixed value of
R
, one can of course apply the result to independent random initial conditions on the event that they satisfy (
2.12
) with that
R
, and bound the probability that they violate (
2.12
) separately.
One can also weaken the assumption of independence of the limiting initial conditions
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)}}
and
\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}}
to a suitably quantified form of asymptotic independence as
\beta\to\infty
, e.g., of the form of a coupling with independent processes with quantified error as in (
2.13
); see Remark
E.4
.
For instance, such a condition would be satisfied by the multi-color limiting stationary initial condition, the stationary horizon (see Lemma
E.6
).

Corollary
2.15
is really a statement about the limiting objects, KPZ fixed points coupled via the directed landscape. Indeed, Corollary
2.15
will be proved via an analogous statement for the limiting objects (Proposition
E.1
).

Next we turn to the statement on the decorrelation of the space-time covariance, or two-point function, under stationary initial data for the colored model.
For each
\beta>0
and
\varepsilon>0
, let
\tilde{\eta}_{0}\in\{0,1,2\}^{\mathbb{Z}}
be sampled from the two-color stationary distribution
\mu_{\bm{\rho}}
of ASEP with density vector
\bm{\rho}=(\frac{1}{2}\beta\varepsilon^{1/3},\frac{1}{2})
as described in Section
2.2.8
. Thus particles of color
2
form an independent Bernoulli(
\frac{1}{2}
) sequence, while particles of color
1
and
2
combined form an independent Bernoulli(
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\varepsilon^{1/3}
) sequence. The particle configuration at time
t
after evolution under the colored ASEP dynamics is denoted
\tilde{\eta}_{t}
. For
t\geq 0
, we define
\smash{\eta_{t}^{(1)}},\smash{\eta_{t}^{(2)}}\in\{0,1\}{Z}
to be the particles of color at least 1 and particles of color 2, respectively, i.e., by

\smash{\eta_{t}^{(1)}}(x)=\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\eta}_{t}(x)\geq 1}\quad\text{and}\quad\smash{\eta_{t}^{(2)}(x)}=\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\eta}_{t}(x)=2}.

Then the two-point function (or the space-time covariance matrix)
\bm{S}^{\beta,\varepsilon}=(S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{kl})_{k,\ell\in\{1,2\}}
is defined by

\displaystyle S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{k\ell}(t,x)=\mathrm{Cov}(\eta^{(k)}_{0}(0),\eta^{(\ell)}_{t}(x)).

In the below, we will say that, for fixed
k,\ell\in\{1,2\}
and a function
A:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{\beta\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-2/3}S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{k\ell}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor)=A(x)

in the sense of integration against smooth, compactly supported test functions if for any
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
which is smooth and compactly supported it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\int_{\mathbb{R}}S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{k\ell}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=\int{R}A(x)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Equivalently (by discretizing the integral over a mesh of width
\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{2/3}
), for all smooth and compactly supported
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, with
f_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by
f_{\varepsilon}(x)=f(\varepsilon^{2/3}x)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{k\ell}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},i)f_{\varepsilon}(\tfrac{1}{2}i)=\int{R}A(x)f(x)\,\mathrm{d}x.

The following is the statement on the limit of the space-time covariance. It will be proven in Appendix
E
. In the below,
g_{\mathrm{BR}}(x)
the variance of the Baik-Rains distribution
F_{\mathrm{BR};x}
as defined, for instance, in
[
FS06
, eq. (1.20)]
(and originally in
[
BR00
, Definition 3]
under a slightly different scaling and in a different form).

Corollary 2.16

(Scaling limit of stationary colored ASEP two-point function)
.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}
be fixed. It holds that, in the sense of integration against smooth compactly supported test functions,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\bm{S}^{\beta,\varepsilon}\bigl(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor\bigr)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{32}g^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{BR}}(x).\end{bmatrix}

Further, for each
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and when
\{k,\ell\}=\{1,2\}
, if
x=x(\beta)\in\mathbb{R}
is such that
|x|\leq\beta^{2}
, then it holds that, in the sense of integration against smooth compactly supported test functions,

(2.16)

\begin{split}\lim_{\beta\to\infty}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-2/3}S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{kl}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor)&=0.\end{split}

In particular, (
2.16
) holds when
x=-\beta
, i.e,
x
is the location on the characteristic line at time
2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}
associated to an initial density of
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\varepsilon^{1/3}
.

In the above, the statement (
2.15
) that the single-species two-point function (appropriately scaled) has a limit governed by KPZ statistics when evaluated in the vicinity of the associated characteristic line (of the color 2 particles), and (under the same scaling) converges to zero away from the characteristic line, is known from prior work (following quickly from results in
[
LS23
]
).

Instead of evaluating the space-time covariance near the characteristic line associated to the color 2 particles, one could do the same near the characteristic line associated to the combined color 1 and color 2 particles (which together have density
\frac{1}{2}(1+\beta\varepsilon^{1/3})
). Indeed, if one evaluates the space-time covariance at
\lfloor 2(x-\beta)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor
for fixed
x
, then one would obtain the limit (again
\varepsilon\to 0
first and then
\beta\to\infty
) of
\varepsilon^{-2/3}S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{k,\ell}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},\lfloor 2(x-\beta)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor)
to be
\frac{1}{32}g^{\prime\prime}_{\mathrm{BR}}(x)
for
k=\ell=1
and
0
for
k=\ell=2
. That this covariance converges to
0
when
k\neq\ell
is already included in (
2.16
).

2.3.
Colored stochastic six-vertex model

The colored stochastic six-vertex model (S6V) we consider is defined on the domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
for some choice of
N\in\mathbb{N}
. The model has two parameters: a quantization (or asymmetry) parameter
q\in[0,1)
and a rapidity (or spectral) parameter
z\in(0,1)
, which are both fixed.

2.3.1.
Configurations and weights

A configuration of the model is described as follows. First, an
arrow configuration
is a tuple
(a,i;b,j)
such that
i,j,a,b\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
and
\{i,a\}=\{j,b\}
; see the left panel of Figure
7
. Under this notation,
i
,
a
,
j
, and
b
can be interpreted as the
colors
of the arrows horizontally entering, vertically entering, horizontally exiting, and vertically exiting the vertex, respectively. Here, an arrow of color
-\infty
may be viewed as the absence of an arrow along an edge. The condition
\{i,a\}=\{j,b\}
is called
colored arrow conservation
, i.e., the number of incoming arrows of each color is equal to the number of outgoing arrows of the same color.

A configuration of the model consists of an assignment of arrow configurations, one to each vertex
v
of the domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
; we will denote the arrow configuration at
v
by
(a_{v},i_{v};b_{v},j_{v})
. We require that the arrow configurations are
consistent
:
i_{(x,y)}=j_{(x-1,y)}
and
a_{(x,y)}=b_{(x,y-1)}
, i.e., an arrow of color
k
is horizontally entering at
(x,y)
if and only if an arrow of color
k
is horizontally exiting at
(x-1,y)
, and similarly an arrow of color
k
is vertically entering at
(x,y)
if and only if an arrow of color
k
is vertically exiting at
(x,y-1)
. We will refer to the colors of the arrows entering and exiting a vertex
v
(horizontally and vertically) as the incoming and outgoing arrow configurations at
v
respectively, and the combined set of incoming and outgoing arrows as the arrow configuration at
v
.
One consequence of this description is that the colored arrows form up-right paths; see the right panel of Figure
7
.

The
boundary condition
of the model is specified as follows. Let
\sigma:\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
. Then the colored S6V model with boundary condition
\sigma
is defined as the model where an arrow of color
\sigma(k)
enters horizontally at
(1,k)
(i.e.,
i_{(1,k)}=\sigma(k)
) for
k\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
, and an arrow of color
-\infty
enters vertically at
(\ell,-N)
(i.e.,
a_{(\ell,-N)}=-\infty
) for
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
; see the right panel of Figure 6. Note that arrows enter only at heights in
\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
, so there are only finitely many arrows in the system.

Remark 2.17
.

The restriction that we impose of no arrows entering above height
N
is to make the upcoming definition (
2.17
) of the colored height function simpler (because of the just noted finiteness); we will soon restrict our attention to the height function’s behavior in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
, where the entrance of arrows above height
N
would have no distributional effect anyway. Allowing arrows to enter on
\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
allows us to consider arrows of negative color without recentering, which will make our height function definition simpler, and will later allow us to couple directly with colored ASEP on all of
\mathbb{Z}
.

i_{v}
j_{v}
a_{v}
b_{v}

(0,-3)
j_{(4,-2)}=-\infty
j_{(4,-1)}=-2
j_{(4,0)}=-\infty
j_{(4,1)}=-1
j_{(4,2)}=0

Figure 7
.
Left: a depiction of the labels for the arrow configuration at a vertex
v
. Right: A sample configuration of the colored stochastic six-vertex model with
N=2
and the packed boundary condition, i.e.,
\sigma(k)=k
for
k\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
. The numbers on the left boundary next to an arrow is the color of the arrow. Any edges without arrows should be interpreted as an arrow with color
-\infty
, i.e., a color lower than the colors of all the other arrows in the system.
