Figure 8
.
The weights for colored S6V written for colors
i
and
j
satisfying
i<j
.

The probability measure on configurations is defined by the following sampling procedure. We start at the vertex
(1,-N)
. Here there is a horizontally incoming arrow determined by the boundary condition
\sigma
and no vertically entering arrow. We determine the outgoing arrow configuration by choosing each of the two possible configurations (whether the horizontally incoming arrow exits vertically or horizontally) with probability equal to the vertex weight read from Figure
8
; note that this uses that the vertex weights are
stochastic
, i.e., non-negative and for a given incoming arrow configuration, the sum of the weights of the possible outgoing arrow configurations satisfying arrow conservation is 1. We then proceed iteratively in a Markovian fashion: if the outgoing arrow configuration at all vertices in the domain
\mathcal{D}_{n}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen:x+y<n\}
has been determined, the configuration for vertices on the line
x+y=n
is determined by picking the outgoing arrow configuration at each vertex independently with probability equal to that vertex’s weight (again regarding an absence of an arrow along an edge as an arrow of color
-\infty
); note that the incoming arrows configuration at each such vertex is already determined by the requirement of consistency of the overall configuration, and that the order in which the outgoing arrow configurations are sampled does not matter.

The
uncolored stochastic six vertex model
corresponds to the case of colored S6V with two colors: the arrows of lower color are typically not drawn, leaving only arrows of a single color which may then be regarded as uncolored.

Remark 2.18
.

Like the colored ASEP, the colored S6V model also satisfies color merging: we can project onto a colored S6V model with fewer colors by declaring all arrows of color lying in an interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
to be the same color, which is an element of
I
. Equivalently, the Markov semigroup of the process (viewed as a Markov chain on configurations on vertical lines
x=t
indexed by the time parameter
t
) commutes with any weakly monotone function
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}\to\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
. That this is true follows from the observation that the vertex weights in Figure
8
are determined by only the order relation of the colors of the incident arrows and not their precise value. The uncolored S6V model can again be obtained from the colored one via color merging.

2.3.2.
The S6V sheet

Recall the notation
j_{v}
from Section
2.3.1
for the color of the arrow horizontally exiting the vertex
v
. Fix a boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
. We define the
colored height function

(2.17)

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t):=\#\bigl\{k>y:j_{(t,k)}\geq x\bigr\}

for
x\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
,
y\in\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
, and
t\in\mathbb{N}
. In words,
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)
is the number of arrows of color at least
x
that exit horizontally from some vertex in
\{t\}\times\llbracket y+1,\infty\Rparen
.

Note that we did not include the boundary condition
\sigma
in the notation (
2.17
). The boundary configuration we primarily work with is the
packed boundary condition
(see right panel of Figure
7
), though some of our results will hold for general boundary conditions as well (see Sections
3.2
and
3.4
). The packed boundary condition corresponds to
\sigma(k)=k
, i.e, the arrows entering each vertex on the left boundary of the domain have color decreasing by
1
from top to bottom. We will restrict to the packed boundary condition in the rest of this article unless explicitly mentioned otherwise.

In terms of starting position under packed boundary condition,
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)
above is the number of arrows of any color entering horizontally at a vertex
(1,k)
for some
k\in\llbracket x,N\rrbracket
that exit horizontally from
\{t\}\times\llbracket y+1,\infty\Rparen
. As suggested by the notation, the horizontal coordinate should be thought of as time.
Note that
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)
is finite since we started with finitely many arrows on the left boundary. Note also that though the distribution of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
depends on
N
, we omit it from the notation (though as alluded to earlier in Remark
2.17
, the distribution of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)
jointly over
x,y\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
will not depend on
N
).

As in the colored ASEP, the collection
(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;\bm{\cdot},t))_{x\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket}
is a coupled collection of copies of the height functions under
step initial condition
from
x
for uncolored S6V, which means arrows enter at all heights in
\llbracket x,N\rrbracket
(again recalling from Remark
2.17
that the
N
cutoff is irrelevant for the domain where we will consider the colored height function). We again refer to this coupling as the
colored coupling
.

Now we define a rescaled version of the height function, which we will call the S6V sheet.
Fix
q\in[0,1)
,
z\in(0,1)
and a velocity
\alpha\in(z,z^{-1})
(which corresponds to the full rarefaction fan). Define
\mu(\alpha)
and
\sigma(\alpha)
by

(2.18)

\begin{split}\mu(\alpha)&=\mu^{\mathrm{S6V}}(\alpha):=-\frac{(\sqrt{\alpha}-\sqrt{z})^{2}}{1-z}\quad\text{and}\\
\sigma(\alpha)&=\sigma^{\mathrm{S6V}}(\alpha):=\frac{\alpha^{-1/6}z^{1/6}(1-\sqrt{z\alpha})^{2/3}(\sqrt{\alpha}-\sqrt{z})^{2/3}}{1-z};\end{split}

these are exactly the limit shape and fluctuation scale expressions as first proven in
[
BCG16
]
.
Next define the spatial scaling factor
\beta(\alpha)=\beta^{\mathrm{S6V}}(\alpha)
by

(2.19)

\beta(\alpha)=\beta^{\mathrm{S6V}}(\alpha):=\frac{2\sigma(\alpha)^{2}}{|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|(1-|\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)|)}.

Definition 2.19

(S6V sheet)
.

For
\varepsilon>0
, we define the
S6V sheet

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, in the case that
|x|,|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-1/6}
and the arguments of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
below are integers, by

(2.20)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x;y):=\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\Bigl(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}\bigl(\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3},0;\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}+\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3},\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\bigr)\\
&+\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3}-N-\mu(\alpha)\varepsilon^{-1}-\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)\beta(\alpha)(y-x)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\Bigr);\end{split}

for
|x|,|y|\in[-\varepsilon^{-1/6},\varepsilon^{-1/6}]
where the arguments of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
are not integers we define
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x;y)
by linear interpolation so that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
is continuous on
\smash{[-\varepsilon^{-1/6},\varepsilon^{-1/6}]^{2}}
, and we define it on
\smash{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\setminus(\smash{[-\varepsilon^{-1/6},\varepsilon^{-1/6}]^{2}})
in an arbitrary way such that the resulting function is again continuous.

The choice of
[-\varepsilon^{-1/6},\varepsilon^{-1/6}]
is merely to ensure that
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
is well-defined, i.e., its arguments fall within its domain (for all
\varepsilon>0
); this is true under the assumption we will later adopt that
N
is at least of order
\varepsilon^{-1}
. The value of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
outside
[-\varepsilon^{-1/6},\varepsilon^{-1/6}]^{2}
will not be relevant for our results which will only concern uniform convergence on compact sets as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Note that again we omit
N
from the notation.
The choice of scalings (i.e.,
\beta(\alpha)
and
\sigma(\alpha)
) can be understood by the same heuristics as in Section
2.2.4
. We also observe that this definition differs qualitatively slightly from that of the ASEP sheet in Definition
2.7
in that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
involves
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
without a negative sign, unlike in ASEP, and there is an extra additive term of
\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3}
here. The first is because the proof of Theorem
2.8
will be via a limit of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
(as mentioned before), based on a matching between
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)
and
-h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(-x,0;-y,t)
(omitting some terms; see (
5.3
) for more precision), and relying on a symmetry of the Airy sheet:
\mathcal{S}(x;y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(-x;-y)
as processes. The introduction of the
\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3}
term is to ensure
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot};\bm{\cdot})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot}+r;\bm{\cdot}+r)
, since
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t)
does not itself have this property; the term is absent in
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}
since
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t)
does have this property.

Our main result for the colored stochastic six-vertex model is the following, where recall we work with the packed boundary condition. It is proved in Section
7.1
.

Theorem 2.20

(Airy sheet convergence for S6V)
.

Fix any asymmetry
q\in[0,1)
, spectral parameter
z\in(0,1)
, and velocity
\alpha\in(z,z^{-1})
in the rarefaction fan. Assume
N\geq 2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}
. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}}
weakly in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

The lower bound on
N
in Theorem
2.20
is merely to ensure that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
is well-defined.

2.3.3.
The basic coupling for S6V

We next give an analog of ASEP’s basic coupling for S6V, which again corresponds to using the same randomness for the evolution of different initial conditions. This will allow us to define the S6V landscape which we show converges to the directed landscape.
We work in the model of uncolored S6V, or, equivalently, colored S6V with two colors, which we take to be
1
and
-\infty
, where as usual
-\infty
represents the absence of an arrow (as discussed in Section
2.3.1
).

Consider finitely many initial conditions
\eta_{0}^{(1)},\ldots,\eta_{0}^{(K)}\in\{-\infty,1\}^{\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen}
where
\eta_{0}^{(j)}(x)
represents the presence or absence of a horizontal arrow at location
(1,x)
, i.e.,
\smash{i_{(1,x)}=\eta_{0}^{(j)}(x)}
. As a notational convention, we will use
\eta
for uncolored initial conditions (as opposed to
\sigma
for colored initial conditions as in Section
2.3.1
).

Let
b^{\shortuparrow}=q(1-z)/(1-qz)
and
b^{\shortrightarrow}=(1-z)/(1-qz)
which, from Figure
8
, are the probabilities of the arrow of higher color going straight through a vertex vertically or horizontally, respectively. We start with a collection of independent
0
-
1
Bernoulli random variables
\{\smash{X^{\shortuparrow}_{v},X^{\shortrightarrow}_{v}\}_{v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen}}
, where
\mathbb{P}(\smash{X^{\shortuparrow}_{v}}=1)=b^{\shortuparrow}
and
\mathbb{P}(\smash{X^{\shortrightarrow}_{v}}=1)=b^{\shortrightarrow}
for each
\smash{v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen}
.

Given this randomness, the configuration is specified deterministically from the initial condition as follows. For any vertex
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
such that the incoming arrow configuration has been assigned to the vertex at
(x,y)
(either by the boundary conditions or by the assignment of outgoing arrow configurations to the vertices at
(x-1,y)
and
(x,y-1)
, along with consistency), let
I_{\shortrightarrow},I_{\shortuparrow}\in\{-\infty,1\}
respectively be
i_{(x,y)}
and
a_{(x,y)}
, the colors of the horizontally and vertically incoming arrows at
(x,y)
respectively. The colors
O_{\shortrightarrow},O_{\shortuparrow}\in\{-\infty,1\}
of the horizontally and vertically outgoing arrows from
(x,y)
(i.e.,
j_{(x,y)}
and
b_{(x,y)}
), respectively are defined by:

(1)

If
I_{\shortrightarrow}=I_{\shortuparrow}
, then
O_{\shortrightarrow}=O_{\shortuparrow}=I_{\shortrightarrow}=I_{\shortuparrow}
.

(2)

If
(I_{\shortrightarrow},I_{\shortuparrow})=(-\infty,1)
, then
(O_{\shortrightarrow},O_{\shortuparrow})=(-\infty,1)
if
X^{\shortuparrow}_{(x,y)}=1
and
(O_{\shortrightarrow},O_{\shortuparrow})=(1,-\infty)
if
X^{\shortuparrow}_{(x,y)}=0
.

(3)

If
(I_{\shortrightarrow},I_{\shortuparrow})=(1,-\infty)
, then
(O_{\shortrightarrow},O_{\shortuparrow})=(1,-\infty)
if
X^{\shortrightarrow}_{(x,y)}=1
and
(O_{\shortrightarrow},O_{\shortuparrow})=(-\infty,1)
if
X^{\shortrightarrow}_{(x,y)}=0
.

Note that for any times
s_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
, we may also specify (horizontally entering) boundary data
\eta_{0}^{(j)}
at vertices on a vertical line
(s_{j},i)
,
i\in\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
and obtain the evolution of the arrows to the right using this definition, i.e., we may couple together the evolutions starting from different initial conditions at different times (by using the same randomness
(X_{v}^{\shortuparrow},X_{v}^{\shortrightarrow})_{v}
).

We observe as in the case of the ASEP that the basic coupling specializes to the colored coupling in the case that the set of arrows in
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j+1)}}
is a subset of those in
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j)}}
for each
j
, i.e.,
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j+1)}(x)=1}
implies
\smash{\eta_{0}^{(j)}(x)=1}
for any
x\in\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
.

Next we define the S6V height function when started from a general initial height function. Let
h_{0}:\llbracket-N-1,\infty\Rparen\to\mathbb{Z}
be a Bernoulli path such that
h_{0}(x)=0
for all large enough
x
. As in ASEP (recall (
2.10
)), we define an associated arrow configuration
\eta_{h_{0}}\in\{-\infty,1\}^{\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen}
by

(2.21)

\displaystyle\eta_{h_{0}}(x):=\begin{cases}1&h_{0}(x-1)-h_{0}(x)=1\\
-\infty&h_{0}(x-1)-h_{0}(x)=0.\end{cases}

The assumption of
h_{0}
being asymptotically zero implies that
\eta_{h_{0}}
consists of only finitely many arrows (note that we did not assume the analogous condition of right-finiteness for ASEP from a general height function, but it will be convenient to impose this condition for S6V).

Now, for such a Bernoulli path
h_{0}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the S6V model with initial condition
\eta_{h_{0}}
started at time
s\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
(i.e.,
\eta_{h_{0}}
is the configuration on
\{s\}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
), and let
j_{(x,y)}
be the arrow exiting horizontally from
(x,y)
. Define the height function
(y,t)\mapsto h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h_{0},s;y,t)
for
t>s
(with
s,t\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
) and
y\in\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
by

(2.22)

h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h_{0},s;y,t):=\#\bigl\{k>y:j_{(t,k)}=1\bigr\}.

For multiple initial conditions
(h_{0}^{(k)})_{k=1}^{K}
and times
s_{k}>0
, the height functions
(y,t)\mapsto\smash{h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h_{0}^{(k)},s_{j};y,t)}
are coupled together via the basic coupling for S6V.

2.3.4.
The S6V landscape

Using this coupling we may define the S6V landscape. As in the ASEP case, we start with a precursor: for
x,y\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
with
x<y
and
0\leq s<t
in
\mathbb{N}
, we overload the notation and define

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,s;y,t):=h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h_{0,x},s;y,t)

coupled via the basic coupling, where, for
z\in\llbracket-N-1,\infty\Rparen
,

h_{0,x}(z)=(x-z)\mathbbm{1}_{z\leq x};

note that this function is zero for all large
z
. It follows that
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,s;y,t)
generalizes
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)
as a process in
x
,
y
, and
t
.

Definition 2.21

(S6V landscape)
.

Recall
\mu(\alpha)
,
\sigma(\alpha)
, and
\beta(\alpha)
from (
2.18
) and (
2.19
). We define the
S6V landscape
for
\varepsilon>0
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x,s;y,t):=\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\Bigl(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}\bigl(\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3},\lfloor s\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor;\alpha(t-s)\varepsilon^{-1}+\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3},\lfloor t\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\bigr)

\displaystyle+\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3}-N-\mu(\alpha)(t-s)\varepsilon^{-1}-\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)\beta(\alpha)(y-x)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\Bigr);

again this definition is for
|x|,|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-1/6}
such that the arguments of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
are integers, under the assumption that
N
is at least of order
\varepsilon^{-1}
. For all other
x,y
, the function is defined by linear interpolation so that it is continuous in
x
and
y
.

We now state the convergence result for
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
, which is proved in Appendix
D
by using an approximate monotonicity estimate for the S6V model under basic coupling (Proposition
D.3
) together with an idea communicated to us by Shalin Parekh. Recall that for a set
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}=\{(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}:s<t\}
.

Corollary 2.22

(Directed landscape convergence for S6V)
.

Fix
q\in[0,1)
,
z\in(0,1)
,
\alpha\in(z,z^{-1})
, and a countable set
\mathcal{T}\subseteq[0,\infty)
. Assume
N\geq 2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}
. Then, the following limits hold as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(1)

\smash{(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t))_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}\xrightarrow{d}(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t))_{(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}}
in
\smash{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})^{\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}}
under the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

(2)

As a special case, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{k}<\ldots<x_{1}
,

\smash{(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x_{i},0;\bm{\cdot},t))_{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{T}}\xrightarrow{d}(\mathcal{L}(x_{i},0;\bm{\cdot},t))_{i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{T}}}

in
\smash{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})^{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\mathcal{T}}}
under the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

Remark 2.23

(Scaling limit of S6V with finitely many colors)
.

Analogous to the ASEP case (Remark
2.13
), Corollary
2.22
(2) can be interpreted as a scaling limit result for the S6V with finitely many
k\geq 1
colors via color merging. More precisely, fix real numbers
x_{k}<\ldots<x_{1}
. Consider colored S6V with initial condition of arrows of color
k
at locations
\llbracket\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{1}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+1,N\rrbracket
; arrows of color
0
at locations
\llbracket-N,\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{k}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor\rrbracket
; and arrows of color
k-i
at all locations in
\llbracket\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{i+1}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+1,\lfloor\beta(\alpha)x_{i}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor\rrbracket
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
.
Then, by color merging,
(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x_{1};\bm{\cdot}),\ldots,\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x_{k};\bm{\cdot}))
is exactly a centered and rescaled version of the height functions associated to the colored S6V with
k
different colors and, by Corollary
2.22
(2), they converge to the directed landscape.

Remark 2.24
.

As mentioned in Section
1.2
, we do not pursue a S6V version of Corollary
2.12
(3), i.e., joint convergence of multiple initial conditions to the KPZ fixed point (though we expect such a result still holds). This is because there is not currently a S6V analog of the work of
[
QS22
]
, i.e., convergence from a single general initial condition to the KPZ fixed point.

However, the stationary measures for the colored S6V coincide with those of the colored ASEP (as is a consequence of the convergence of the former to the latter; see
[
AB24
, Proposition B.2]
); so, the stationary horizon convergence of the colored ASEP (Corollary
2.14
) implies the same for the colored S6V.

3.
Colored
q
-Boson model, line ensembles, and Gibbs properties

As we saw in Definition
2.3
, the Airy sheet is given in terms of a last passage percolation problem in the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
. As outlined in Section
1.3.2
, the starting point of our proof is a prelimiting analog of this relation, in terms of a certain colored line ensemble, called the
colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble
, that is associated to the colored S6V model and was recently introduced in
[
AB24
]
(we reproduce the argument for the relation between the colored line ensemble and the colored S6V model in Appendix
A
). This line ensemble is defined in terms of an auxiliary vertex model which we refer to as the
colored
q
-Boson model
, and we introduce it in Section
3.1
. We will then introduce the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble and its Gibbs property in Section
3.2
. Section
3.3
states the convergence of uncolored line ensembles to
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
. Section
3.4
states the precise prelimiting LPP representation of higher color height functions.

3.1.
Colored
q
-Boson model

The
colored and uncolored
q
-Boson models
we are introducing are vertex models on the domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
for
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
; note that this is different from the domain for the colored stochastic six-vertex model, and we will make the connection between the two in Proposition
3.12
. As in the stochastic six vertex model introduced in Section
2.3
, there are two parameters: a
quantization parameter

q\in[0,1)
and a
spectral parameter

z\in(0,1)
, both of which are held fixed. We will focus on the colored model for the time being. Again as in the colored S6V model, informally (see the next paragraph for a more precise description), a configuration of the model consists of colored arrows, with the color of an arrow being an integer in
\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. These arrows enter the domain at the left boundary and move through the domain forming up-right paths, satisfying colored arrow conservation at each vertex; we will specify the colors of these arrows via a boundary condition
\sigma
shortly (Section
3.1.2
). There is also an exiting boundary condition: all arrows in the system exit vertically from
(0,N+M)
.

An important difference compared to the colored S6V model is that here an arbitrary number of arrows may occupy the same vertical edge (though horizontal edges are still restricted to at most 1 arrow), as well as the fact that the weights (as will be introduced ahead in Figure
10
) are not stochastic, i.e., do not sum to
1
when summing over all possible outgoing vertex configurations for fixed incoming vertex configuration; thus the model’s probability distribution is defined via a product of vertex weights with an additional non-trivial normalization factor. Finally, note that in the colored
q
-Boson model we allow arrows to have only positive colors.

\cdots
z
z
z
z
1
1
1
\sigma(1)
\sigma(2)
\sigma(3)
M
N
(-1,N+2)

Figure 9
.
The colored
q
-Boson model. The
z\to
and
1\to
at the left indicate the spectral parameters of the

\mathtt{L}

weights associated to vertices in the corresponding rows, the expressions above the entry point of the arrows indicate the corresponding arrow’s color, and the
\bm{\cdots}
indicate that there are infinitely many columns, though, by definition of the boundary conditions, the arrows travel purely horizontally except until finitely many columns from the right.

3.1.1.
Arrow configurations

More formally, for a vertex
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
, we denote the arrow configuration at
v
by a tuple
(\bm{A}_{v},i_{v};\bm{B}_{v},j_{v})
, where
i_{v},j_{v}\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
and
\bm{A}_{v},\bm{B}_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
are vectors
(A_{v,1},\ldots,A_{v,N})
and
(B_{v,1},\ldots,B_{v,N})
, such that
A_{v,k}+\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}=k}=B_{v,k}+\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}=k}
for each
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. Here
i_{v}
and
j_{v}
represent the colors of the horizontally incoming and outgoing arrows at
v
, respectively, while
A_{v,k},B_{v,k}
represent the number of arrows of color
k
vertically entering and exiting at
v
, respectively, for
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. For this model, color 0 will represent no arrow (unlike the colored S6V model in Section
2.3.1
, where
-\infty
did). The condition
A_{v,k}+\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}=k}=B_{v,k}+\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}=k}
is that of
colored arrow conservation
. A configuration of the model is an assignment of arrow configurations to all vertices of
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
that is
consistent
, i.e., the arrows entering a vertex horizontally or vertically are respectively the same as the arrows horizontally exiting the vertex immediately to the left or vertically exiting the vertex immediately below.

3.1.2.
Boundary conditions

Let
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
be a function. The boundary condition
\sigma
of arrows is specified as follows: informally, at
(-\infty,i)
for
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
an arrow of color
\sigma(i)
enters and moves purely horizontally for all but finitely many edges, and no arrow enters at row
i
for
i\in\llbracket N+1,N+M\rrbracket
; further, all arrows which originally entered the system vertically exit at
(0,N+M)
. More formally, the arrow configuration at
v=(-K,i)
is given by
(0^{N},\sigma(i);0^{N},\sigma(i))
for
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and by
(0^{N},0;0^{N},0)
for
i\in\llbracket N+1,N+M\rrbracket
, for all large enough
K
, and no arrows vertically enter the system through the bottom boundary
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\{1\}
. Further, the configuration of arrows vertically exiting
(0,N+M)
is
(\#\sigma^{-1}(\{j\}))_{j=1}^{N}
, and no arrows horizontally or vertically exit the system elsewhere.
We emphasize that we only consider boundary conditions
\sigma
taking values at least
1
; in other words, we require that an arrow enters the
i
th
row for each
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
.

.
The
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights for
1\leq i<j\leq N
. Here for
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,
A_{[i,N]}=\smash{\sum_{j=i}^{N}A_{j}}
. The notation
\smash{\bm{A}_{\ell}^{+}}
and
\smash{\bm{A}_{\ell}^{-}}
respectively means the vector obtained by increasing or decreasing the
\ell
th
component of
\bm{A}
by
1
; similarly
\bm{A}_{k\ell}^{+-}
is the vector obtained by increasing the
k
th
component of
\bm{A}
by
1
and decreasing the
\ell
th
component by 1.

3.1.3.
The colored
q
-Boson weights and probability measure

The weight of a configuration is given by the product of the vertex weights at each vertex of the domain. The vertex weights are specified as follows. First we define the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights in Figure
10
, which will also be called the
colored
q
-Boson weights
(and are the same as the ones in
[
BW22
, eq. (1.2.2)]
or
[
AB24
, Figure 5]
, with
s=0
). The vertex weights in our model are of two types, coming from the weights in Figure
10
with two different values of
u
: those on the bottom
N
rows, i.e, on
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, are given by the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights with
u=1
, while those on the top
M
rows, i.e., on
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket N+1,N+M\rrbracket
, are given by the same with
u=z
as fixed above; see Figure
9
.

Definition 3.1
.

Fix a boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. This specifies a collection of consistent configurations that satisfy this boundary condition and which have positive weight. We may define a probability measure supported on this collection, which we call the
colored
q
-Boson measure
with boundary condition
\sigma
. The measure is defined by specifying that

(3.1)

the probability of a given configuration is proportional to its weight;

the normalization constant is finite and equals
\bigl(\frac{1-qz}{1-z}\bigr)^{NM}
.

The
uncolored
q
-Boson model
is the case where only arrows of color
1
are present, i.e.,
\sigma(k)=1
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
(and thus there is no boundary condition to be specified, i.e., there is only one choice for
\sigma
).

The normalization constant in (
3.1
) is the sum of weights of all configurations and its expression is derived in
[
AB24
, Lemma 2.9]
via the Yang-Baxter equation relating the colored
q
-Boson and colored S6V weights; equivalently, this can be seen as a Cauchy identity for certain functions associated to the vertex model. We reproduce the former argument in Appendix
A
.

3.1.4.
Color merging

The colored
q
-Boson model satisfies a form of color merging, like the colored S6V model (Remark
2.18
). Take the colored
q
-Boson measure with boundary condition
\sigma
and identify any interval
I\subseteq\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
of colors with an element of
I
, and if desired relabel all the colors in a weakly increasing way (preserving the original orderings). The resulting projection is the colored
q
-Boson measure with boundary condition
\sigma^{\prime}
obtained by performing the same color identification and relabeling in
\sigma
. A more precise statement is as follows.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
. Let
\tau:\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
be non-decreasing and such that
\tau^{-1}(\{0\})=\{0\}
. Then
(j_{v})_{v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket}
under the colored
q
-Boson measure with boundary condition
\tau\circ\sigma
has the same distribution as
(\tau(j_{v}))_{v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket}
under the colored
q
-Boson measure with boundary condition
\sigma
.

In particular, defining
\tau
by
\tau(0)=0
and
\tau(j)=1
for
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, we see that the colored
q
-Boson model with all colors ignored (i.e., only total arrow counts are observed) yields the uncolored
q
-Boson model.

That this color merging property holds is not as immediate as in the case of colored S6V. Indeed, in the latter it was a consequence of the vertex weights being determined by the relative order of the colors of arrows and not their precise values; this implicitly used that the normalization constant or partition function for the measure is
1
(or, rather, independent of the boundary condition), since the weights are stochastic, i.e, for a fixed incoming arrow configuration, summing the weights over all valid outgoing arrow configurations yields
1
. In contrast, the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights are not determined purely by the relative order and are also not stochastic.

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

The verification of color merging requires the following fact. First, for
\tau:\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
non-decreasing with
\tau^{-1}(\{0\})=\{0\}
, define
\rho_{\tau}:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
by the following: for
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,
(\rho_{\tau}(\bm{A}))_{k}=\sum_{i\in\tau^{-1}(\{k\})}A_{i}
. Then, it is a straightforward calculation that for
\bm{A}_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
,
i_{v}\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
,
\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{v}\in\rho_{\tau}(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N})
, and
\lambda\in\tau(\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket)
,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{B}_{v},j_{v}:\tau(j_{v})=\lambda,\\
\rho_{\tau}(\bm{B}_{v})=\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{v}\end{subarray}}\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}(\bm{A}_{v},i_{v};\bm{B}_{v},j_{v})=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}\bigl(\rho_{\tau}(\bm{A}_{v}),\tau(i_{v});\widetilde{\bm{B}}_{v},\lambda\bigr).

In words, if we fix the incoming arrow configurations and sum the vertex weight over all outgoing arrow configuration under the constraint that the outgoing arrow colors, on merging according to
\tau
, are fixed, we obtain the vertex weight with the colors merged according to
\tau
. That the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights have this property has been observed a number of times in the literature; see, for example,
[
AB24
, eq. (4.9)]
, or
[
BW22
, Proposition 2.4.2]
or
[
FW13
]
in the case of merging all colors with color 1.

Fix a configuration
\bm{\omega}
of the colored
q
-Boson model with boundary condition
\tau\circ\sigma
. Repeatedly applying (
3.2
) implies that the weight of
\bm{\omega}
is the same as the sum of the weights of all configurations of the colored
q
-Boson model with boundary condition
\sigma
such that one obtains
\bm{\omega}
on color merging according to
\tau
. Next, repeatedly applying the special case of (
3.2
), with
\tau(k)=1
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
\tau(0)=0
, yields that the partition functions for the models with boundary conditions
\sigma
and
\tau\circ\sigma
, respectively, are the same. These two inferences complete the proof by recalling the definition (
3.1
) of the colored
q
-Boson measure.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Unlike the colored S6V model, in the colored
q
-Boson model one cannot merge colors with color zero (no arrows). This is a consequence of the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights not being stochastic. It can be seen by the fact that the analog of (
3.2
) for merging with
0
(i.e.,
\tau^{-1}({0})
contains a non-zero integer) does not hold.

3.1.5.
Gibbs property for
q
-Boson models

Next we discuss two Gibbs properties, one each for the colored and uncolored
q
-Boson models, that are consequences of the above description, and which will be used in later arguments. By Gibbs property, we mean a spatial Markov property: informally, for a domain
\Lambda
, the conditional distribution of the configuration strictly inside
\Lambda
, given the configuration on the boundary of
\Lambda
as well as outside
\Lambda
, is explicit and depends only on the configuration on the boundary of
\Lambda
.

We start with the uncolored Gibbs property. We may write the arrow configuration at a vertex
v
as
(A_{v},i_{v};B_{v},j_{v})
where
i_{v},j_{v}\in\{0,1\}
(presence of arrow) and
A_{v},B_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
(number of arrows). Because of the form of the weight of a configuration as a product of vertex weights, that this model enjoys a Gibbs property is immediate; the dependency of the conditional distribution on the configuration on the boundary of the domain is imposed via the requirement of consistency and arrow conservation. To state the Gibbs property more precisely, fix a rectangle
\Lambda:=\llbracket-k,-\ell\rrbracket\times\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
, let
\Lambda^{c}=\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket\setminus\Lambda
, and let
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}}
(“uB” short for uncolored
q
-Boson) be the
\sigma
-algebra (where
\sigma(\bm{\cdot})
for
\bm{\cdot}
some collection of random variables means the
\sigma
-algebra generated by that collection)

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}:=\sigma\left(\left\{j_{v}:v\in\Lambda^{c}\right\}\right);

note that, by consistency, for every
v
in the left boundary of
\Lambda
, it holds that
i_{v}
is
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}
-measurable, and by consistency along with arrow conservation, for every
v
in the bottom boundary of
\Lambda
, it holds that
A_{v}
is
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}
-measurable. This implies that, for
v
the top left corner of
\Lambda
,
B_{v}
is
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}
-measurable and, iteratively, that the same holds for every
v
in the top boundary of
\Lambda
. Continuing reasoning in this way, we see that
B_{v}
is
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}
-measurable for every
v
in
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket\setminus(\llbracket-k,-\ell+1\rrbracket\times\llbracket a,b-1\rrbracket)
. See the left panel of Figure
11
.

\Gamma_{k}

Figure 11
.
Left: The setup for the Gibbs property in the uncolored
q
-Boson model. Outgoing arrow counts outside the region
\Lambda
outlined in blue are conditioned upon, and arrows have been displayed in this depiction if the arrow count is non-zero and has been conditioned on. Arrow counts entering the blue region are conditioned on due to consistency, but those exiting it are not. Right: The setup for the Gibbs property in the colored
q
-Boson model. Horizontally outgoing colored arrows counts are conditioned on strictly to the left of the blue dashed line (which extends to the ends of the domain in both directions), while only total outgoing arrow counts (depicted in black) are conditioned on to the right (including on
\Gamma_{k}
).

For the statement of the Gibbs property, we adopt the notation
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}
for the vertex weights in the model:
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{1}
for
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z}
for
v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket N+1,N+M\rrbracket
.

Lemma 3.4

(Uncolored
q
-Boson Gibbs property)
.

Let
\Lambda=\llbracket-k,-\ell\rrbracket\times\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
and
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}}
be as in (
3.3
). Under the uncolored
q
-Boson measure, the conditional distribution of
((A_{v},i_{v};B_{v},j_{v}):v\in\Lambda)
given
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{uB}}_{\Lambda^{c}}}
is supported on tuples
((\smash{A^{\prime}_{v},i^{\prime}_{v};B^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v}}):v\in\Lambda)
such that arrow conservation and consistency are satisfied inside
\Lambda
and with respect to the boundary conditions; call the (random) set of such tuples
\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{uB}}
. Further, the conditional probability that
((A_{v},i_{v};B_{v},j_{v}):v\in\Lambda)=((A^{\prime}_{v},i^{\prime}_{v};B^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v}):v\in\Lambda)
, for any fixed (i.e., deterministic) selection of the righthand side, is proportional to
\prod_{v\in\Lambda}{\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}}_{z,v}(A^{\prime}_{v},i^{\prime}_{v};B^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})\mathbbm{1}_{(A^{\prime}_{v},i^{\prime}_{v};B^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Lambda}\in\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{uB}}}
.

Proof.

This follows from the form of the uncolored
q
-Boson measure as a product of vertex weights.
∎

Next we turn to a Gibbs property for the colored
q
-Boson model. Informally, it explains how to “reveal” colored arrow counts, conditional on total arrow counts.
Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\Lambda_{k}=\scalebox{0.97}{$\Lparen$}{-}\infty,-k\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
and
\Gamma_{k}=\{-k\}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
, and let
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}}
(“cB” short for colored
q
-Boson) be the
\sigma
-algebra

(3.4)

\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}}:=\sigma\left(\bigl\{\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}\geq 1}:v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket\bigr\}\cup\bigl\{i_{v}:v\in\Lambda_{k}\bigr\}\right).

For
\bm{A}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
, let
|\bm{A}|:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}A_{i}
. Note that by colored arrow conservation and consistency, the collection of random variables
\{(|\bm{A}_{v}|;|\bm{B}_{v}|,\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}\geq 1}):v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket\}
and
\{(\bm{A}_{v};\bm{B}_{v},j_{v}):v\in\Lambda_{k+1}\}
is
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}
-measurable. Thus, in words,
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}}
contains the information of the incoming colored arrow counts at
\Gamma_{k}
, incoming and outgoing total arrow counts of every vertex in the domain, and incoming and outgoing colored arrow counts of every vertex in
\Lambda_{k+1}
.
See the right panel of Figure
11
.

The following is the Gibbs property when conditioning on
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}
; because of the heterogeneous nature of the random variables being conditioned (a mixture of colored and total arrow counts), its proof is slightly more complicated than that of Lemma
3.4
. Recall the definition of
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}
from before Lemma
3.4
.

Lemma 3.5

(Colored
q
-Boson Gibbs property)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, and let
\Gamma_{k}
and
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}}
be as in (
3.4
). Under the colored
q
-Boson measure with boundary condition
\sigma
, the conditional distribution of
((\bm{A}_{v};\bm{B}_{v},j_{v}):v\in\Gamma_{k})
given
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}}
is supported on tuples
((\smash{\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v}}):v\in\Gamma_{k})
such that (i)
\mathbbm{1}_{j^{\prime}_{v}\geq 1}=\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}\geq 1}
for all
v\in\Gamma_{k}
and (ii) colored arrow conservation and consistency are satisfied. Call the (random) set of all such tuples
\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{cB}}
. Further, the conditional probability that
((\bm{A}_{v};\bm{B}_{v},j_{v}):v\in\Gamma_{k})=((\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v}):v\in\Gamma_{k})
, for any fixed (i.e., deterministic) selection of the righthand side, is proportional to
\prod_{v\in\Gamma_{k}}\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v},i_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})\mathbbm{1}_{(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Gamma_{k}}\in\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{cB}}}
.

Proof.

Let
\Lambda_{k}^{c}:=\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket\setminus\Lambda_{k}=\llbracket-k+1,0\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
. Let
\bm{\omega}=((\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v}):v\in\Gamma_{k})
and let
\mathrm{Val}_{k}(\bm{\omega})
(for “valid”) be the collection of configurations
(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v},i^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c}}
which satisfy colored arrow conservation and consistency as well as the following:

\bullet

i^{\prime}_{(-k+1,x)}=j^{\prime}_{(-k,x)}
for
x\in\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
(consistency of incoming arrows to
\Gamma_{k-1}
with outgoing arrows from
\Gamma_{k}
as given by
\bm{\omega}
),

\bullet

\bm{B}^{\prime}_{(0,N+M)}=(\#\sigma^{-1}(\{j\}))_{j=1}^{N}
(all arrows exit vertically from the rightmost column), and

\bullet

\mathbbm{1}_{i^{\prime}_{v}\geq 1}=\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}\geq 1}
,
\mathbbm{1}_{j^{\prime}_{v}\geq 1}=\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}\geq 1}
,
|\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v}|=|\bm{A}_{v}|
, and
|\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v}|=|\bm{B}_{v}|
for all
v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c}
(respecting the total arrow counts conditioned on).

It is immediate from the definition of the colored
q
-Boson measure that the
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}
-conditional probability of the event
\{((\bm{A}_{v};\bm{B}_{v},j_{v}):v\in\Gamma_{k})=\bm{\omega}\}
is proportional to

(3.5)

\displaystyle\prod_{v\in\Gamma_{k}}\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v},i_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})\cdot\left(\sum_{(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v},i^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c}}\in\mathrm{Val}_{k}(\bm{\omega})}\prod_{w\in\Lambda_{k}^{c}}\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,w}(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{w},i^{\prime}_{w};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{w},j^{\prime}_{w})\right).

To absorb the factor in parentheses into the normalization constant, we must show that it is
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{k}}
-measurable. The lemma will therefore be implied by the following claim: that factor’s value depends only on
((|\bm{A}_{v}|,\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}\geq 1};|\bm{B}_{v}|,\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}\geq 1}):v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c})
, which has been conditioned upon (it is determined by
(\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}\geq 1}:v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c})
by consistency and arrow conservation).
This claim in turn follows by applying (
3.2
) (with
\tau(k)=1
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
\tau(0)=0
) iteratively for each factor in the second product in (
3.5
) (i.e., for each vertex in
\Lambda_{k}^{c}
); notice that the sum in (
3.5
) is over
\mathrm{Val}_{k}(\bm{\omega})
, which fixes the total arrow counts at each vertex according to
((|\bm{A}_{v}|,\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}\geq 1};|\bm{B}_{v}|,\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}\geq 1}):v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c})
. This yields that the parenthetical quantity in (
3.5
) equals the partition function for the uncolored
q
-Boson measure on configurations
(\tilde{A}_{v},\tilde{i}_{v};\tilde{B}_{v},\tilde{j}_{v})_{v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c}}
(where
A_{v},B_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
i_{v},j_{v}\in\{0,1\}
represent total arrow counts, as in the beginning of Section
3.1.5
) constrained to satisfy the uncolored analogs of the three bullet points above, namely,
\tilde{i}_{(-k+1,x)}=\mathbbm{1}_{j_{(-k,x)}\geq 1}
for
x\in\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
;
\tilde{B}_{(0,N+M)}=N
; and
\tilde{i}_{v}=\mathbbm{1}_{i_{v}\geq 1},\tilde{j}_{v}=\mathbbm{1}_{j_{v}\geq 1}
,
\tilde{A}_{v}=|\bm{A}_{v}|
, and
\tilde{B}_{v}=|\bm{B}_{v}|
for all
v\in\Lambda_{k}^{c}
(this actually includes the first point since
\mathbbm{1}_{j_{(-k,x)}\geq 1}=\mathbbm{1}_{i_{(-k+1,x)}\geq 1}
). This verifies the claim and completes the proof.
∎

3.2.
Colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble

Next we define a rewriting of a configuration sampled from the colored
q
-Boson measure in a more probabilistically appealing form, namely, the colored line ensemble. First we specify what we mean by a discrete line ensemble.

Definition 3.6

(Discrete line ensemble)
.

Fix a (possibly infinite) interval
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
. A
\Lambda
-indexed discrete line ensemble

\bm{L}=(L_{1},L_{2},\ldots)
is a random variable defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
taking values in the space of functions
\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
(here the first argument is written as a subscript, i.e.,
L_{i}(\bm{\cdot}):=\bm{L}(i,\bm{\cdot})
), endowed with the discrete topology, such that: (i)
L_{i}(y+1)-L_{i}(y)\in\{0,-1\}
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
y
such that
y,y+1\in\Lambda
, and (ii)
L_{i}(y)\geq L_{i+1}(y)
for any
y\in\Lambda
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Note that we take discrete line ensembles, and all the line ensembles we work with in this paper, to have an infinite number of curves. This differs slightly from some previous studies focused on tightness of line ensembles (e.g.,
[
CH14
,
CH16
,
CD18
]
).

We will refer to the set of vertices
\{-k\}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
in the domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
of the colored
q
-Boson model as the
k
th
column. The colored line ensemble will be given in terms of colored arrow counts in columns at a finite distance from the rightmost (zeroth) column. Recall that, in the notation from Section
3.1.1
, for
y\in\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
j_{(-k,y)}
is the color of the arrow exiting horizontally from vertex
(-k,y)
; and
j_{(-k,y)}=0
if there is no such arrow.

Definition 3.7

(Colored and uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensembles)
.

Let
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and let
\{(\bm{A}_{v},i_{v};\bm{B}_{v},j_{v}):v\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket\}
be a configuration sampled from the colored
q
-Boson measure with boundary condition
\sigma
(from Section
3.1.2
). For each
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, we define the discrete line ensemble
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(k)}}=(L^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(k)}}_{1},L^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(k)}}_{2},\ldots):\mathbb{N}\times\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
by letting
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{i}(y)}
be the number of arrows of color at least
k
which exit horizontally in the
i
th
column strictly above row
y
, i.e,

(3.6)

L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{i}(y):=\#\bigl\{y^{\prime}>y:j_{(-i,y^{\prime})}\geq k\bigr\}.

The object
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL}}=(\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)},\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)},\ldots,\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(N)})
is called the
colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble
with boundary condition
\sigma
(see Figure
3
). The line ensemble
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
is called the
uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble
.

Remark 3.8
.

The terminology comes from the fact that, if one interprets the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble as random sequences of compositions (see
[
AB24
, Definition 4.1]
), the resulting process is the same as the colored Hall-Littlewood process introduced in
[
BW22
, Section 1.6]
(though we do not need or prove this).

We call
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble as it is determined purely by total arrow counts in the colored
q
-Boson model. As in the uncolored
q
-Boson model, there is no boundary condition to be specified for the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble.
Analogous to the colored case, the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble is associated to the (uncolored) Hall-Littlewood process (see e.g.,
[
BC14
,
Dim18
]
), a collection of measures on sequences of partitions that is specified by a choice of specializations and a down-right path. The specializations (in some cases) can be thought of as our choices of spectral parameters, and the down-right path as the choice of boundary conditions of the uncolored
q
-Boson model (allowing arrows to horizontally enter only at rows with spectral parameter
1
, but which may be different than the bottom
N
rows as we have fixed here; see also
[
AB24
, Remark 3.11]
). The choice of boundary condition and spectral parameters we have fixed (and the only one considered in this article) is equivalent to the “ascending Hall-Littlewood process” with a homogeneous choice of specializations.

Note that we do not include
\sigma
in the notation for the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensembles, but we will specify
\sigma
in the relevant statements. Next we record some simple consequences of Definition
3.7
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Fix
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, and for each
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, let
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}=(L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{1},L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{2}
,
\ldots)
be defined as in (
3.6
) with boundary condition
\sigma
. It holds deterministically that, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
y\in\llbracket 0,N+M-1\rrbracket
,

(3.7)

(i)

L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{i}(y)\geq L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k+1)}_{i}(y)
,

(ii)

L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{i}(y)\geq L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{i+1}(y)
, and

(iii)

L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{i}(y+1)-L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{i}(y)=-\mathbbm{1}_{j_{(-i,y+1)}\geq k}\in\{0,-1\}
.

Proof.

The first and third points are immediate from the definition. The second relies on the fact that arrows can only move up and to the right, so that any arrow exiting the
(i+1)
st
column at a row higher than
y
must exit the
i
th
column at a row higher than
y
.
∎

We next record a statement of color merging for the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble.

Lemma 3.10

(Color merging for
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL}}
)
.

Fix
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, and let
\tau:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
be surjective and non-decreasing. Let
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},\sigma}
and
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},\tau\circ\sigma}
be the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensembles with boundary conditions
\sigma
and
\tau\circ\sigma
as in Definition
3.7
, respectively. For
j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, let
t_{j}=\min\tau^{-1}(\{j\})
. Then

\displaystyle(\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},\sigma,(t_{j})})_{j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},\tau\circ\sigma,(j)})_{j\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket}.

In particular, defining
\tau
by
\tau(j)=1
for all
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, it follows that
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},\sigma,(1)}
under any boundary condition
\sigma
has the same distribution as
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},\sigma^{\prime},(1)}
with
\sigma^{\prime}(j)=1
for all
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, i.e., the colored line ensemble associated with the uncolored
q
-Boson model.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of combining Definition
3.7
with the color merging statement Lemma
3.2
.
∎

3.2.1.
Restricting the domain

We need to connect the top curve of
\smash{\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}}
for different
j
with the height function of the colored S6V model (we focus on S6V since we will ultimately deal with ASEP via a limit to it from the former; see Section
5
). To do so, we must first modify the setup of the colored S6V model slightly. Recall from (
2.17
) that the prelimiting analog
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
of
\mathcal{S}
concerns counts of the number of arrows above a varying vertex on the vertical line
x=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
, and that arrows horizontally enter the system from the left side at coordinates
(1,i)
for
i\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
. In the packed boundary condition, the arrow entering horizontally at
(1,i)
has color
i
, and thus all arrow colors entering at or below the
x
-axis are not positive. In contrast, note that the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble we defined are associated with only positive colors. (We needed to allow arrows of negative color in the colored S6V model, as
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
needs to allow negative values in the first argument.)

We first record a consequence of color merging (of the colored S6V model) that says that, when considering the height function for colors
1
or higher, we may restrict the domain to
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
without changing the distribution of the colored height function. This will address the discrepancy pointed out in the previous paragraph.

Lemma 3.11
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\sigma:\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket\to\scalebox{0.97}{$\Lparen$}{-}\infty,N\rrbracket\cup\{-\infty\}
,
\sigma_{\geq 1}:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
be such that
\sigma(k)=\sigma_{\geq 1}(k)\geq 1
for
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
\sigma(k)\leq 0
for
k\in\llbracket-N,0\rrbracket
. Consider the colored S6V model on the domains
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
and
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket 1,\infty\Rparen
with boundary conditions
\sigma
and
\sigma_{\geq 1}
, respectively (i.e., an arrow of color
\sigma(k)
or
\sigma_{\geq 1}(k)
enters horizontally at
(1,k)
for
k\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
and
\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, respectively), and let
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
and
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{\geq 1}
be their respective colored height functions as defined in (
2.17
). Then
(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t))_{x,y,t\in\mathbb{N}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{\geq 1}(x,0;y,t))_{x,y,t\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of color merging for colored S6V (Remark
2.18
): in the colored S6V model on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
with boundary condition
\sigma
in the statement, we merge the colors
\scalebox{0.97}{$\Lparen$}{-}\infty,0\rrbracket\cup\{-\infty\}
with
-\infty
and thus obtain the colored S6V model on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket 1,\infty\Rparen
with boundary condition
\sigma_{\geq 1}
.
∎

As noted, the convergence of the S6V and ASEP sheets to
\mathcal{S}
is a statement about the colored height functions at a fixed time, so to prove Theorems
2.8
and
2.20
we can restrict our attention to the colored height function at time
M
for fixed
M
, i.e.,
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},M)
.
Note that we switched the notation for the time argument from
t
to
M
; this is so as to match the notation of the colored
q
-Boson model as well as
[
AB24
]
.

3.2.2.
Relation to colored S6V

With these preliminaries, we may state the crucial link between the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble and the height functions in colored S6V. Its proof, originally due to
[
AB24
]
, relies on the Yang-Baxter equation of colored S6V and the colored
q
-Boson model. Because our arguments fundamentally rely on this result, we give a self-contained proof in Appendix
A
.

Proposition 3.12

(
[
AB24
, Theorem 4.7]
)
.

Fix
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,
q\in[0,1)
, and
z\in(0,1)
. Let
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
be the height function associated with the colored S6V on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket 1,\infty\Rparen
as in (
2.17
) and
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}
be as in Definition
3.7
(with the associated colored
q
-Boson model on domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
), both with boundary condition
\sigma
. We have the equality in distribution of
(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(k,0;y,M))_{y\in\llbracket 1,N-1\rrbracket,k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
and
(L^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(k)}}_{1}(y))_{y\in\llbracket 1,N-1\rrbracket,k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket}
.

Next we move to describing a Gibbs property of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble.

3.2.3.
Colored Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property

From the definition of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble in terms of the colored
q
-Boson model, and the Gibbs property of the latter recorded in Lemma
3.5
, we obtain a Gibbs property for the former. We restrict ourselves to stating the case where the system contains only two colors, i.e.,
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,2\rrbracket
, as this will turn out to suffice for our purposes (by performing a color merging later, see Section
4
), though more general Gibbs properties involving more colors also hold. In this case,
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
and
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}
determine the colored arrow configurations at all vertices in the system via Definition
3.7
. Let
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cHL}}_{k}}
(“cHL” short for colored Hall-Littlewood) be the
\sigma
-algebra

(3.8)

\displaystyle\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cHL}}_{k}}=\sigma\left(\bigl\{\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}_{i}}(x),\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}_{j}}(x):i\in\mathbb{N},j\in\llbracket k+1,\infty\Rparen,x\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket\bigr\}\right);

in words, we condition on all curves of the line ensemble
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
, and the curves indexed
k+1
or larger of
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}
.
We now state the Gibbs property of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble that we will need. Recall the notation
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}
from before Lemma
3.4
.

Lemma 3.13

(Colored Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property)
.

Under the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble measure (with boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,2\rrbracket
), the conditional distribution of
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}_{k}}(\bm{\cdot})
given
\smash{\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cHL}}_{k}}
is supported on the collection of Bernoulli paths
L^{\prime}:\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
such that (i)
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}_{k}}(\bm{\cdot})-L^{\prime}(\bm{\cdot})
is a Bernoulli path and (ii)
L^{\prime}(\bm{\cdot})\geq L^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}_{k+1}(\bm{\cdot})
with
L^{\prime}(N+M)=0
and
L^{\prime}(0)=L^{\smash{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}}_{k+1}(0)
; call the (random) set of such Bernoulli paths
\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{cHL}}
.

For such a Bernoulli path
L^{\prime}
, let
(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v},i_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j_{v}^{\prime})_{v\in\Gamma_{k}}
be the associated tuple determined by
L^{\prime}(\bm{\cdot})
,
L^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(1)}}_{k}(\bm{\cdot})
,
L^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(1)}}_{k+1}(\bm{\cdot})
, and
L^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(2)}}_{k+1}(\bm{\cdot})
on the event that
L^{\mathrm{cHL},\smash{(2)}}_{k}(\bm{\cdot})=L^{\prime}(\bm{\cdot})
(we write
i_{v}
and not
i_{v}^{\prime}
as it has no dependence on
L^{\prime}(\bm{\cdot})
). The condition
L^{\prime}(\bm{\cdot})\in\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{cHL}}
is equivalent to
(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Gamma_{k}}\in\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{cB}}
(the latter as in Lemma
3.5
). Further, the conditional probability that
L^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}_{k}(\bm{\cdot})=L^{\prime}(\bm{\cdot})
, for any fixed (i.e., deterministic) selection of the righthand side, is proportional to
\prod_{v\in\Gamma_{k}}\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,v}(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v},i_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})\mathbbm{1}_{(\bm{A}^{\prime}_{v};\bm{B}^{\prime}_{v},j^{\prime}_{v})_{v\in\Gamma_{k}}\in\mathrm{Supp}^{\mathrm{cB}}}
.

Proof.

The lemma follows from Lemma
3.5
and Definition
3.7
relating the color Hall-Littlewood line ensemble and the arrow configurations of the colored
q
-Boson model; note that (i) and (ii) in the hypotheses of Lemma
3.13
correspond to (i) and (ii) in Lemma
3.5
(for (i), using that
L^{\smash{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}}_{k}(\bm{\cdot})-\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)}_{k}}(\bm{\cdot})
decreases by
1
from
x
to
x+1
exactly at those
x
such that an arrow of color
1
exits horizontally from
(-k,x)
, by Lemma
3.9
(iii)).
∎

There is also a Gibbs property for the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble, i.e.,
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
, obtained by translating Lemma
3.4
. We will discuss this in more detail in Section
3.3
next.

3.3.
Convergence of line ensembles

In this section we discuss convergence results for a class of discrete line ensembles possessing a certain Gibbs property (that of the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble).
The statement we give for the limit of line ensembles is under a general framework which we introduce now. Because of the general setup, these line ensembles are not necessarily related to the
q
-Boson vertex model; having said that, as mentioned, the example the framework will be applied to in this paper is the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
.

3.3.1.
Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property

The line ensembles we consider will possess the
Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property
, which describes the interactions that curves in the ensemble have with each other.
We start by precisely specifying the data we condition on in the Gibbs property.

Definition 3.14
.

Let
\bm{L}:\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
be a discrete line ensemble (recall Definition
3.6
). Let
\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\subset\mathbb{N}
and
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subset\Lambda
be intervals. We define
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\llbracket j,k\rrbracket,\llbracket a,b\rrbracket,\bm{L})
to be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the collection of random variables
\{L_{i}(x):(i,x)\in\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda\setminus(\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket a+1,b-1\rrbracket)\}
.

When the line ensemble under discussion is clear, we will drop
\bm{L}
from the notation. Similarly when
j=1
, we will replace
\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
by simply
k
. Thus
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket a,b\rrbracket)
would denote
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,\llbracket a,b\rrbracket,\bm{L})
.

Next, recall the definition of a Bernoulli path from Definition
2.9
. For
a,b,x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a<b
,
y\leq x\leq y+b-a
, we define a
Bernoulli random walk bridge

B:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
from
(a,x)
to
(b,y)
to be a uniformly selected random path from the set of all Bernoulli paths
\gamma:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
with
\gamma(a)=x
and
\gamma(b)=y
(assuming this set is non-empty).

Definition 3.15

(Weight factor)
.

Let
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
and fix
q\in[0,1)
. Let
\bm{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
,
f
, and
g
be a collection of Bernoulli paths (
f
and
g
should be thought of as upper and lower boundary curves respectively), all defined on
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, with
\gamma_{i}(x)\geq\gamma_{i+1}(x)
for
i\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket
,
\gamma_{1}(x)\leq f(x)
, and
\gamma_{k}(x)\geq g(x)
for all
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
. We define the weight factor
W(\bm{\gamma},f,g,\llbracket a,b\rrbracket)
by

(3.9)

\displaystyle W(\bm{\gamma},f,g,\llbracket a,b\rrbracket):=\prod_{i=0}^{k}\prod_{x=a+1}^{b}\left(1-q^{\Delta_{i}(x-1)}\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(x)=\Delta_{i}(x-1)-1}\right),

where
\Delta_{i}(x)=\gamma_{i}(x)-\gamma_{i+1}(x)
and
\gamma_{0}=f
and
\gamma_{k+1}=g
by convention. For curves which do not satisfy the ordering conditions mentioned above, we define
W(\bm{\gamma},f,g,\llbracket a,b\rrbracket)=0
; observe that (
3.9
) equals 0 if at any location the separation between consecutive curves drops from
0
to
-1
. See also Figure
12
.
When the interval under consideration is obvious from the context we will drop
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
from the notation. Moreover, if
f=\infty
, we drop it from the notation; if in addition
g=-\infty
, then we drop it from the notation as well.

Sometimes, to emphasize whether we are focusing on interaction with the upper curve
f
, between the curves of
\bm{\gamma}
, or with the lower curve
g
, we will write

\displaystyle W(\bm{\gamma},f,g)=W_{\mathrm{up}}(\gamma_{1},f)\cdot W_{\mathrm{int}}(\bm{\gamma})\cdot W_{\mathrm{low}}(\gamma_{k},g),

where, in (
3.9
),
W_{\mathrm{up}}(\gamma_{1},f)
is the factor corresponding to
i=0
,
W_{\mathrm{int}}(\bm{\gamma})
is the product corresponding to
i=1,\ldots,k-1
, and
W_{\mathrm{low}}(\gamma_{k},g)
to the factor with
i=k
.

The following is the Gibbs property that the line ensembles in the general framework we are setting up will possess; it originally appeared and was studied in
[
CD18
]
.

Definition 3.16
.

(Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property)
We say a discrete line ensemble
\bm{L}:\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
satisfies the (uncolored)
Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property
(with parameter
q\in[0,1)
) if the following holds for any
\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\subset\mathbb{N}
and
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subset\Lambda
. Conditionally on
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\llbracket j,k\rrbracket,\llbracket a,b\rrbracket,\bm{L})
, the distribution of
\bm{L}|_{\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket a,b\rrbracket}
is given by a collection of
k-j+1
Bernoulli random walk bridges
\bm{B}=(B_{j},\ldots,B_{k})
, where
B_{i}(a)=L_{i}(a)
and
B_{i}(b)=L_{i}(b)
for each
i\in\llbracket j,k\rrbracket
, reweighted by the Radon-Nikodym derivative
W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})/\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}[W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})]
, where
L_{0}\equiv\infty
by convention.

Equivalently, for any bounded function
F:\mathbb{Z}^{\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket a,b\rrbracket}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[F(L_{j}|_{\llbracket a,b\rrbracket},\ldots,L_{k}|_{\llbracket a,b\rrbracket})\right]=\frac{\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[F(B_{j},\ldots,B_{k})W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})\right]}{\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})\right]}

(here
\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}[\bm{\cdot}]
is shorthand for
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\cdot}\mid\mathcal{F}]
, and we will also use analogous notation
\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}(\bm{\cdot})
for
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\cdot}\mid\mathcal{F})
).
We will sometimes write
Z=\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}[W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})]
, but this will always be made explicit.

As above, we will usually refer to this property as the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property (dropping the “uncolored” adjective); this will not cause confusion as we will always explicitly say “colored” when referring to its colored analog.

This Gibbs property is satisfied by
\smash{\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}}
, but not in general by
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}
for
j\geq 2
.

Proposition 3.17
.

\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}|_{\mathbb{N}\times\llbracket 0,N-1\rrbracket}=(L^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}_{1}|_{\llbracket 0,N-1\rrbracket},L^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}_{2}|_{\llbracket 0,N-1\rrbracket},\ldots)
has the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property for any boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Lemma
3.4
after translating to the line ensemble notation from Definition
3.7
, using also from Lemma
3.10
that
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
is in distribution the line ensemble associated to the uncolored
q
-Boson model.
∎

A derivation of Proposition
3.17
is also contained in
[
AB24
, Proposition 5.1]
, and an alternate derivation is present in
[
CD18
, Proposition 3.9]
after matching
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
with the (uncolored) Hall-Littlewood process.

\Delta_{0}(x+1)=2
\Delta_{1}(x)=1

Figure 12
.
An example of the Hall-Littlewood weight factor with a single curve
\gamma
. The top and bottom curves (in black) are
f
and
g
, respectively, and are fixed, and the blue curve is
\gamma
.
In calculating the weight factor
W
, the separation of
\gamma
from
f
and
g
as captured by
\Delta_{i}(\bm{\cdot})
is used. In this example,
W(\gamma,f,g)=(1-q^{2})\cdot(1-q)^{2}
: the separation
\Delta_{0}(\bm{\cdot})
between
f
and
\gamma
reduces only at the penultimate step, from
2
to
1
, and the separation
\Delta_{1}(\bm{\cdot})
between
\gamma
and
g
reduces twice, each time from
1
to
0
.

3.3.2.
Heuristic behavior of the Gibbs property

For intuition, the Gibbs property should be thought of as a resampling property, as in Figure
15
ahead: it says that the following resampling procedure leaves the distribution of the line ensemble unchanged. First, with
\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\Lambda
fixed, we erase
\bm{L}
on
\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket a+1,b-1\rrbracket
while retaining the information of its values on
\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda\setminus(\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket a+1,b-1\rrbracket)
. Then we attempt to replace
L_{j},\ldots,L_{k}
on
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
with a tuple
\bm{B}
of
k-j+1
independent Bernoulli random walk bridges with endpoints of
B_{i}
equaling
L_{i}(a)
and
L_{i}(b)
at
a
and
b
, respectively: the attempt succeeds with probability
W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})
as given in Definition
3.15
, independent of everything else; note that if any individual curve of
\bm{B}
crosses
L_{j-1}
,
L_{k+1}
, or any other curve of
\bm{B}
, then
W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})=0
and the attempt certainly fails. On the event of
\bm{B}
successfully replacing
L_{j},\ldots,L_{k}
on
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, the former’s law is exactly that of
k
independent Bernoulli random walk bridges under the reweighting by the Radon-Nikodym derivative
W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})/\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}[W(\bm{B},L_{j-1},L_{k+1})]
. The basic idea underlying many of our arguments is that behavior which is unlikely for such reweighted Bernoulli random walk bridges must also be unlikely for the original line ensemble.

The above is really a perspective on Gibbs properties of line ensembles in general, and now we discuss the particular features of the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property that constitute a major challenge and necessitate the development of new techniques, compared to previous studies of line ensembles with different Gibbs properties. In our setup, we will consider sequences of line ensembles indexed by a parameter
N
(which should not be confused with the
N
parameter of the colored S6V model or the colored
q
-Boson model in Section
3.1
). Our goal in Section
3.3
is to give a result of convergence of these line ensembles, rescaled by the KPZ exponents, to the parabolic Airy line ensemble. This means that we scale the spatial argument of the line ensemble by
N^{2/3}
and the value of the lines by
N^{1/3}
(both up to constants) in order to obtain a tight, or unit-order, sequence.

At short range, the resampling property penalizes curves coming closer together, as encoded by the indicator function in (
3.9
). This is a somewhat complicated interaction as it is not always active, and, more importantly, does not satisfy a certain convenient stochastic monotonicity property. Before discussing these aspects, let us first consider how the interaction behaves on the larger scale on which our limits will be taken. This means evaluating the weight function (
3.9
) when
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
is an interval of size of order
N^{2/3}
and
\Delta_{i}(x)
is of order
N^{1/3}
: then
W
is
\smash{(1-q^{\Theta(N^{1/3})})^{\Theta(kN^{2/3})}}
, i.e., essentially 1 when
q\in[0,1)
is fixed. This assumes the separation between consecutive curves is of order
N^{1/3}
throughout the interval; if instead at some location consecutive curves cross, the weight factor becomes zero as before. Thus at large
N
the weight factor is expected to behave like the indicator function of the curves not intersecting, mimicking the Brownian Gibbs property (see Definition
6.4
) for the parabolic Airy line ensemble (the putative limit of our discrete one).

3.3.3.
Lack of stochastic monotonicity

Actually establishing this separation (that the above heuristic relies on) is non-trivial and will constitute the bulk of the proof of our convergence results. Some challenges are exactly the earlier mentioned complicated nature of the interaction at short range, from which we must argue we can escape to a larger scale, and that the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property does not possess a stochastic monotonicity property present in other Gibbs properties previously studied. Stochastic monotonicity means that if one considers the law of the curves of the line ensemble inside
\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
conditional on the boundary data, and then considers the same law with the boundary points increased and/or the upper and lower curves increased, the second law is stochastically larger than the first (i.e., there is a coupling of the two laws under which each curve in the second collection, with higher boundary data, is pointwise larger than the corresponding curve in the first collection). The Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property fails to satisfy this because
W
from (
3.9
) is a monotone function of the discrete derivative of the distance between consecutive curves and not of the distance itself. See Figure
13
for an example demonstrating the lack of stochastic monotonicity. The violations of stochastic monotonicity can be fairly severe and it is also possible to find counterexamples to versions of stochastic monotonicity with more conditions imposed, e.g., comparing only boundary conditions which share the same endpoints.

(0,0)
(0,-k)
W(B,g)=1-q^{k+1}
\mathbb{P}(B)=\frac{1-q^{k+1}}{2-q^{k+1}}
(0,0)
(0,-k)
W(B,g)=1
\mathbb{P}(B)=\frac{1}{2-q^{k+1}}

Figure 13
.
An example of the failure of stochastic monotonicity for the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property. Here
g
is the lower boundary curve drawn in black (indexed by its starting location
(0,-k)
) and
B
is the random curve drawn in blue (whose endpoints are fixed). We see that the probability of the higher possibility for
B
equals
\smash{\frac{1-q^{k+1}}{2-q^{k+1}}}
, which is increasing in
k
, while stochastic monotonicity requires it to be non-increasing.

The approach to line ensemble tightness we develop relies instead on a form of weak monotonicity for a single curve (a minor refinement of a notion introduced in
[
CD18
]
); see Section
8
. By this we mean a comparison of partition functions (up to a, possibly large, constant) of certain pairs of laws corresponding to a single curve under different boundary conditions, which translates to a comparison (up to the same constant) of one-point probabilities for a single curve. In particular, it does not hold for collections of more than one curve, and does not imply the existence of a coupling of the two laws such that all points on the pair of curves are ordered. Using this weak monotonicity, we iteratively establish uniform separation of order
N^{1/3}
between curves further and further down in the line ensemble, simultaneously establishing control on how far down such curves can be. We will discuss and develop this more later (see Section
9
); here we next introduce the general framework under which our arguments operate.

3.3.4.
Assumptions on line ensembles and their tightness

In this section we formulate some assumptions on general line ensembles under which we will prove tightness.
Before stating them, we specify the space we will work on and some conventions about the objects.

Our sequence of discrete line ensembles will be denoted by
\bm{L}^{N}=(L_{1}^{N},L_{2}^{N},\ldots)
. Here
N
is an indexing parameter which has no relation to the parameter in the colored S6V or colored
q
-Boson models.
Though discrete valued, we will regard
\bm{L}^{N}
as an element of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, by linearly interpolating
L^{N}_{i}(x)
for
x\in[j,j+1]
for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
; here
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}
is given the product topology of the discrete topology on
\mathbb{N}
and the usual Euclidean topology on
\mathbb{R}
. We endow the space
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets, i.e.,
\smash{\bm{f}^{(n)}},\bm{f}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
satisfy
\smash{\bm{f}^{(n)}}\to\bm{f}
if for any compact set
K\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
fixed,
\smash{f^{(n)}_{i}}(x)\to f_{i}(x)
uniformly over
x\in K
.

Further, we allow a sequence
g_{N}
going to
\infty
with
N\to\infty
such that the values of
L^{N}_{i}(x)
are not specified for
|x|\geq g_{N}N^{2/3}
, as these values do not affect the existence or value of the limits we consider (which are determined by uniform convergence on compact sets on the
N^{2/3}
spatial scale); in particular,
L^{N}_{i}(x)
may be defined for such values in any way such that
\bm{L}^{N}
is an element of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, without regard for satisfying the assumptions below.

Now we may turn to the precise assumptions we assume on our line ensembles
\bm{L}^{N}=(L^{N}_{1},L^{N}_{2},\ldots)
.

Assumption 1

(Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property)
.

\bm{L}^{N}
has the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property with parameter
q\in[0,1)
fixed (Definition
3.16
).

Assumption 2

(One-point tightness of top curve around parabola)
.

There exist
p\in(-1,0)
and
\lambda>0
such that, for any
\delta>0
there exists
M=M(\delta)
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\,\limsup_{N\to\infty}\,\mathbb{P}\left(|L^{N}_{1}(xN^{2/3})-pxN^{2/3}+\lambda x^{2}N^{1/3}|>MN^{1/3}\right)\leq\delta.

Equivalently, for any
\delta>0
there exists
M
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
there exists
N_{0}
such that, for
N>N_{0}
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|L^{N}_{1}(xN^{2/3})-pxN^{2/3}+\lambda x^{2}N^{1/3}|>MN^{1/3}\right)\leq\delta.

By our convention on the definition of
L^{N}_{i}(x)
for
|x|\geq g_{N}N^{2/3}
, Assumption
1
is only needed for
\bm{L}^{N}|_{\mathbb{N}\times[-g_{N}N^{2/3},g_{N}N^{2/3}]}
. We will not explicitly mention this point or the general point about the definition of
L^{N}_{i}(x)
for
|x|\geq g_{N}N^{2/3}
further.

We note that Assumption
2
is satisfied if it is known that the union over
x\in\mathbb{R}
of the set of subsequential weak limits of
\{N^{-1/3}(L^{N}_{1}(xN^{2/3})-pxN^{2/3}+\lambda x^{2}N^{1/3})\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is a tight collection (for instance, as will be the case for our models of interest, if it is a single distribution, e.g., a constant multiple of the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution).

We will need to rescale our line ensembles to state our results. For
p\in(-1,0)
,
\lambda>0
, and
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we introduce a scaling operator
T_{p,\lambda,N}:\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})\to\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
defined by the following for
\bm{f}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}
:

(3.11)

\displaystyle\bigl(T_{p,\lambda,N}(\bm{f})\bigr)_{i}(x):=\sigma^{-1}N^{-1/3}\left(f_{i}(\beta xN^{2/3})-p\beta xN^{2/3}\right)

where
\beta
and
\sigma
are such that

(3.12)

\displaystyle\sigma^{-1}\beta^{2}\lambda=1\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma^{-2}\beta p(1-p)=2.

For
\bm{L}^{N}
satisfying Assumption
2
, and with
p
and
\lambda
as there, define
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(3.13)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}:=T_{p,\lambda,N}(\bm{L}^{N}).

The equations (
3.12
) are the analogs of the conditions (
2.9
). The first condition will ensure that
\smash{\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{1}(x)}
decays like
-x^{2}
; the second will ensure that the large
N
limit of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
has the Brownian Gibbs property (Definition
6.4
) with the correct diffusion coefficient of 2.

Now we may state the tightness result we prove, which is a key result needed to establish our main results, Theorems
2.8
and
2.20
; the proof will be given in Section
6.4
.

Theorem 3.18

(Tightness of line ensembles)
.

Let
\bm{L}^{N}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a line ensemble satisfying Assumptions
1
and
2
. Then
\{\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is tight in the space
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, under the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

The following is a third assumption which will be needed to ensure that there is a unique limiting line ensemble, and that it is the parabolic Airy line ensemble
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
from Definition
2.1
.

Assumption 3

(GUE Tracy-Widom convergence)
.

There exists
x\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{1}(x)+x^{2}\xrightarrow{\smash{d}}\mathrm{GUE}\text{-}\mathrm{TW}
