(the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution, as defined at the end of Definition
2.1
) as
N\to\infty
.

The following gives the convergence to
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
of the rescaled line ensemble
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
. It will be proved in Section
6
as a quick consequence of the tightness from Theorem
3.18
, together with the recent characterization result for Brownian Gibbsian line ensembles from
[
AH23a
]
.

Theorem 3.19
.

Suppose
\bm{L}^{N}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
. Then any subsequential limit of
\{\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is
\bm{\mathcal{P}}+\mathfrak{c}
, where
\mathfrak{c}\in\mathbb{R}
is a random variable independent of
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
(but which may depend on the subsequence taken),
under the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets. If additionally Assumption
3
is satisfied, then
\mathfrak{c}=0
, i.e,
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}\xrightarrow{\smash{d}}\bm{\mathcal{P}}
.

3.4.
Last passage percolation in the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble

We now turn to stating our results on the relation between
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
and
(\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(2)},\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(3)},\ldots)
(under general boundary condition
\sigma
) as defined in (
3.6
).
In particular, we give a precise statement of the approximate representation of
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{1}}
as a last passage percolation (LPP) problem in
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
, which in fact holds exactly when
q=0
.

Recall the definition of
\smash{\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}}
from (
3.6
) and the notation for LPP problems from (
2.2
); this notation is for an LPP problem in an environment given by a family of continuous functions, and for this purpose as well as in the next result, we regard
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
as a collection of continuous functions on the real interval
[0,N+M]
by linear interpolation, as in the setup introduced in Section
3.3.4
. We prove the next result in Section
4
.

Theorem 3.20
.

Fix
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
, any boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
q\in[0,1)
. Let
(\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)},\ldots,\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(N)})
be the associated colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble as given by Definition
3.7
. There exist positive constants
c
(absolute), and
K=K(q)
such that for any
j,k\geq 1
and
m\geq K\log N
,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{y\in[0,N+M]}\left|L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{1}(y)-\sup_{z\leq y}\left(L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{k+1}(z)+\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]\right)\right|\geq km\right)\leq kq^{cm^{2}}.

When
q=0
,
L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{1}(y)=\sup_{z\leq y}(L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{k+1}(z)+\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}[(z,k)\to(y,1)])
for all
y\in[0,N+M]
,
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Recall the definition of the Airy sheet from Definition
2.3
, the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
from (
2.20
), and the relation of
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{1}}
to the S6V height function
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
from Proposition
3.12
. At this point in our discussion, it should not be hard to believe that, given the representation of
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{1}}
in Theorem
3.20
and the convergence of
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
to
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
from Theorem
3.19
, the convergence of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
to
\mathcal{S}
will follow (and similarly the convergence of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}
which, as mentioned in the beginning of Section
3
, will be proved by a limit to ASEP from S6V). Indeed, given the results we have stated in this section, the argument to establish this is at this point well-known, following presentations in
[
DOV22
]
and
[
DV21a
]
; as a result we give the proof of Theorems
2.20
and
2.8
in Section
7.1
after relegating some of the standard details to Appendix
C
.

4.
Approximate LPP representation

In this section we prove Theorem
3.20
.
Recall that we allow a general boundary condition encoded by a function
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
, where
\sigma(k)
is the color of the arrow horizontally entering the domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
of the colored
q
-Boson model at
(1,k)
.

4.1.
Pitman transform and the
q=0
case

For two functions
f,g:\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the
Pitman transform

\mathrm{PT}(f,g):\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}(f,g)(y):=f(y)+\max_{0\leq y^{\prime}\leq y}\left(g(y^{\prime})-f(y^{\prime})\right).

The Pitman transform should be thought of as an LPP problem across two curves, with the bottom one equal to
g
and the top equal to
f
(this interpretation assumes
g(0)=0
, as will be the case in our context). Indeed, we will ultimately access the LPP value across
k
lines by iterated applications of
\mathrm{PT}
.

We start by stating and proving a statement in the case of
q=0
, in order to make the source of the Pitman transform in the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble more transparent; we will subsequently turn to obtaining a similar statement in the case of general
q\in[0,1)
in Section
4.2
.

In this section we will mainly restrict to the case where
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,2\rrbracket
, i.e., arrows in the colored
q
-Boson model are only of color
1
or
2
, as this will suffice for the proof of Theorem
3.20
(see the end of this section) by color merging. We drop the superscript of “cHL” when writing the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble; for instance we will write
\smash{L^{(j)}_{i}}
for
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{i}}
. We will also refer to arrows of color 2 as
2
-arrows, and analogously
1
-arrows for arrows of color
1
.
In particular, recalling the relation of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble and the colored
q
-Boson model from Definition
3.7
,
\smash{L^{(2)}_{k}}
corresponds to counts of 2-arrows in the
k
th
column (i.e.,
\{-k\}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
) of the colored
q
-Boson model, while
\smash{L^{(1)}_{k}}
corresponds to combined arrow counts of
1
- and
2
-arrows in the same column.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
q=0
. For any boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,2\rrbracket
and any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

L^{(2)}_{k}=\mathrm{PT}\left(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1}\right).

It turns out that a lower bound actually holds deterministically for all
q\in[0,1)
. We state and prove it first before giving the proof of Lemma
4.1
that equality holds when
q=0
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
q\in[0,1)
, boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,2\rrbracket
, and
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

L^{(2)}_{k}\geq\mathrm{PT}\left(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1}\right).

Proof.

Fix
y
. Recalling the definition (
4.1
) of the Pitman transform, we must show that

\displaystyle L^{(2)}_{k}(y)\geq L^{(1)}_{k}(y)+\max_{y^{\prime}\leq y}\left(L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y^{\prime})-L^{(1)}_{k}(y^{\prime})\right).

Let us write
O_{k}(y)=L^{(1)}_{k}(y)-L^{(2)}_{k}(y)
, so that
O_{k}(y)
is the number of
1
-arrows exiting the
k
th
column in the colored
q
-Boson model at row strictly higher than
y
. Then, from the previous display, what we have to show is equivalent to

(4.2)

\displaystyle\min_{y^{\prime}\leq y}\left(O_{k}(y^{\prime})+L^{(2)}_{k}(y^{\prime})-L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y^{\prime})\right)\geq O_{k}(y).

Now, recall from (
3.7
) that
L^{(2)}_{k}(y^{\prime})\geq L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y^{\prime})
for all
y^{\prime}
and observe that
O_{k}(y)
is non-increasing in
y
by its definition as the count of
1
-arrows (analogous to the fact that
\smash{L^{(j)}_{k}(y+1)\leq L^{(j)}_{k}(y)}
noted in the third part of (
3.7
)). This yields that the lefthand side of the previous display is at least the righthand side.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

We continue with the notation
O_{k}(y)=L^{(1)}_{k}(y)-L^{(2)}_{k}(y)
from the previous proof for the count of 1-arrows exiting the
k
th
column at row strictly higher than
y
. By Lemma
4.2
, we must show (
4.2
) holds with equality, i.e., for some
y^{\prime}\leq y
,

(4.3)

O_{k}(y^{\prime})+L^{(2)}_{k}(y^{\prime})-L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y^{\prime})=O_{k}(y).

First suppose that
O_{k}(y^{\prime})=O_{k}(y)
for all
0\leq y^{\prime}\leq y
, which is the case that no
1
-arrows exit horizontally from any such vertex
(-k,y^{\prime})
. We observe that
\smash{L^{(2)}_{k}(0)=L^{(2)}_{k+1}(0)}
, since both quantities are simply the total number of
2
-arrows in the system, and we have established (
4.3
) at
y^{\prime}=0
.

Next we assume that there exists at least one vertex
(-k,y^{\prime})
with
y^{\prime}\leq y
from which a 1-arrow exits horizontally. Let
y_{0}
be the largest such
y^{\prime}
; equivalently,
y_{0}
is the largest
y^{\prime}\leq y
such that
O_{k}(y^{\prime})=O_{k}(y^{\prime}-1)-1
. Observe that then
O_{k}(y_{0})=O_{k}(y)
since
O_{k}
is non-increasing for each
k
.

We claim that then
L^{(2)}_{k}(y_{0})=L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y_{0})
, which would establish (
4.3
). Indeed, we know from the weights in the
q=0
case from Figure
10
that since a
1
-arrow exits horizontally from
(-k,y_{0})
, there is no
2
-arrow entering that vertex from the left or bottom. The arrows vertically entering
(-k,y_{0})
are precisely the arrows that have exited
(-(k+1),y^{\prime})
horizontally for some
y^{\prime}<y_{0}
and which have not exited the
k
th
column strictly below
y_{0}
. Thus all 2-arrows in the
(k+1)
st
column that have horizontally exited that column strictly below row
y_{0}
have also exited the
k
th
column strictly below row
y_{0}
, and, as we already noted, a
2
-arrow does not exit horizontally from
(-k,y_{0})
or
(-(k+1),y_{0})
. In other words,
L^{(2)}_{k}(0)-L^{(2)}_{k}(y_{0})=L^{(2)}_{k+1}(0)-L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y_{0})
, which implies our claim, since
L^{(2)}_{k}(0)=L^{(2)}_{k+1}(0)
.
∎

As we saw in Lemma
4.2
,
L^{(2)}_{k}(y)\geq\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1})(y)
deterministically. The next statement gives a bound on how much the deviation can be in the case of general
q\in[0,1)
and is the basic ingredient of the proof of Theorem
3.20
.

Proposition 4.3
.

There exist positive constants
c
and
K=K(q)
such that, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,2\rrbracket
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\in[0,1)
, and
m\geq K\log N
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}\left(L^{(2)}_{k}(y)-\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1})(y)\right)\geq m\right)\leq q^{cm^{2}}.

Before turning to the proof of this statement in Section
4.2
, we use it to establish Theorem
3.20
.

Proof of Theorem
3.20
.

Recall that we have dropped the superscript
\mathrm{cHL}
from the notation of the colored line ensembles. Next, it suffices to specialize to the case of
j=2
. Indeed, the original statement will follow from this special case by Lemma
3.10
(color merging), i.e., the fact that the joint distribution of
(\bm{L}^{(1)},\bm{L}^{(j)})
associated to boundary condition
\sigma
is the same as that of
(\bm{L}^{(1)},\bm{L}^{(2)})
associated to boundary condition
\tau\circ\sigma
, where
\tau(i)=1
if
i\in\llbracket 1,j-1\rrbracket
and
\tau(i)=2
if
i\in\llbracket j,N\rrbracket
.
In other words, we merge the colors in
\llbracket 1,j-1\rrbracket
to be
1
and those in
\llbracket j,N\rrbracket
to be
2
.

Inductively define
\mathrm{PT}^{(1)}=\mathrm{PT}
and for
k\geq 2

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}^{(k)}\left((L^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1}\right):=\mathrm{PT}^{(k-1)}\left((L^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k-1},\mathrm{PT}\left(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1}\right)\right);

It is easy to show that

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{PT}^{(k)}\left((L^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1}\right)=\max_{z\leq y}\left(L^{(2)}_{k+1}(z)+\bm{L}^{(1)}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]\right).

With this we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}\left|L^{(2)}_{1}(y)-\max_{z\leq y}\left(L^{(2)}_{k+1}(z)+\bm{L}^{(1)}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]\right)\right|\geq km\right)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}\left|L^{(2)}_{1}(y)-\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{1},L^{(2)}_{2})(y)\right|\geq m\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}\left|\mathrm{PT}^{(j)}\left((L^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{j},L^{(2)}_{j+1}\right)(y)-\mathrm{PT}^{(j+1)}\left((L^{(1)}_{i})_{i=1}^{j+1},L^{(2)}_{j+2}\right)(y)\right|\geq m\right).

In the
q=0
case, Lemma
4.1
says that the previous display is
0
for any
m>0
. For the case of
q\in(0,1)
, we observe that if
h
is a function with
\max|h|\leq\varepsilon
for some
\varepsilon>0
, then it follows from the representation (
4.5
) of
\mathrm{PT}^{(k)}
and the triangle inequality that, for any
f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}
and
g
,

(4.7)

\displaystyle|\mathrm{PT}^{(k)}((f_{i})_{i=1}^{k},g)-\mathrm{PT}^{(k)}((f)_{i=1}^{k},g+h)|\leq 2\varepsilon.

From Lemma
4.2
and Proposition
4.3
it holds that, for any
j\geq 0
and
m\geq K\log N
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}\left|L^{(2)}_{j+1}(y)-\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{j+1},L^{(2)}_{j+2})(y)\right|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}m\right)\leq q^{cm^{2}}.

Combining this with (
4.7
) and the definition (
4.4
) of
\mathrm{PT}^{(j)}
yields that the
j
th
summand in (
4.6
) is upper bounded by
q^{cm^{2}}
which, on tracing back, completes the proof.
∎

The rest of the section is devoted to proving Proposition
4.3
.

4.2.
The case of general
q

As we saw in the proof of Lemma
4.1
, the Pitman transform representation was essentially a consequence of the fact that, when
q=0
, the colored
q
-Boson vertex weights force
2
-arrows to exit horizontally before
1
-arrows. In
q>0
,
1
-arrows can exit before
2
-arrows, but will be penalized in weight by a factor of
q^{m}
, where
m
is the number of
2
-arrows vertically entering at the vertex where the
1
-arrow is exiting (see Figure
10
). At a high level, the heuristic behind the proof of Proposition
4.3
is as follows: on the event that the deviation of the height function in the
q>0
case from the Pitman transform expression (as it would be in the
q=0
case) is
m
, we can find order
m
many different locations where a 1-arrow horizontally exits before a
2
-arrow, and where there are order
m
many
2
-arrows “in queue”, i.e., vertically entering; this will lead to the
\smash{q^{cm^{2}}}
probability bound for this event.

The precise proof of Proposition
4.3
requires introducing some notation.
Let
U=N+M
(the number of rows in the colored
q
-Boson model). Fix
\bm{v}=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{U})\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}
and
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{U})\in\{0,1\}^{U}
. Here
v_{y}
represents the color of the arrow horizontally entering at the
y
th
row and
x_{y}
represents the number (of non-zero color) arrows horizontally exiting at the
y
th
row, both counted from the bottom (see Figure
14
).

For a
\bm{v}\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
and
y\in\llbracket 1,U\rrbracket
, let the height function (which we call
\mathrm{Ht}
for short) of the
j
th
color be defined by

\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},y):=\#\Bigl\{y^{\prime}>y:v_{y^{\prime}}=j\Bigr\}=\sum_{y^{\prime}=y+1}^{U}\mathbbm{1}_{v_{y^{\prime}}=j},

i.e., the number of arrows of color
j
strictly above row
y
in
\bm{v}
.

We say that
\bm{v}
and
\bm{x}
are
compatible
if they can arise as the incoming color counts and outgoing total arrow counts at a column, respectively, while satisfying arrow conservation; more precisely, if and only if
\sum_{j=1}^{2}(\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},0)-\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},y))\geq\sum_{y^{\prime}=1}^{y}x_{y^{\prime}}
for each
y
and equality holds at
y=U
.

For given
\bm{v}\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}
and
\bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{U}
which are compatible, define
\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
(short for “valid”)

\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x}):=\left\{\bm{w}\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}:\parbox{239.00298pt}{\centering$w_{y}=0\iff x_{y}=0$, $\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{w},0)=\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},0),$\\
$\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{w},y)\geq\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},y)\text{ for }j=1,2,\ y\in\llbracket 0,U-1\rrbracket$\@add@centering}\right\},

to be the set of valid assignments of outgoing colors at a column with incoming color counts given by
\bm{v}
and outgoing arrow counts given by
\bm{x}
(see Figure
14
); here, valid informally means that colored arrow conservation holds.

v_{1}
x_{1}
v_{2}
x_{2}
v_{3}
x_{3}
v_{4}
x_{4}
1
0
0
0
2
1
0
1
\bm{v}
\bm{x}
1
0
0
0
2
2
0
1
\bm{v}
\bm{w}

Figure 14
.
A depiction of
\bm{v}\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}
,
\bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{U}
(left panel), an example where they are compatible (middle panel), and an example of a
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
(right panel). Here the
1
and
0
in black on the right side of the middle panel respectively indicate the presence or absence of an arrow of any color, while the colored numbers on the left side of the middle panel and both sides of the right panel indicate the color of the arrow.

For
\bm{v}\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}
and
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
, define
\mathrm{VertCt}_{j}
(short for “vertical count”) by

(4.8)

\begin{split}\mathrm{VertCt}_{j}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y)&:=\bigl(\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},0)-\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},y)\bigr)-\bigl(\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{w},0)-\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{w},y)\bigr)\\
&=\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{w},y)-\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},y)=\sum_{y^{\prime}=1}^{y}\mathbbm{1}_{v_{y^{\prime}}=j}-\mathbbm{1}_{w_{y^{\prime}}=j}\end{split}

be the number of arrows of color
j
vertically exiting the vertex at height
y
; the second equality is since
\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{v},0)=\mathrm{Ht}_{j}(\bm{w},0)
is the total number of
j
-arrows in the system (equal since
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
). Note that
\mathrm{VertCt}_{j}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y)
is a function only of
v_{1},\ldots,v_{y}
and
w_{1},\ldots,w_{y}
.

We next define a specific assignment
\bm{w}^{*}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
for any given compatible pair
(\bm{v},\bm{x})
, which is the deterministic (and unique) assignment of colors to the outgoing arrows that would occur in the
q=0
colored
q
-Boson model. It is defined as follows: we specify
w^{*}_{y}
iteratively starting with
y=1
and continuing in increasing order by:

\bullet

w^{*}_{y}=2
if
\mathbbm{1}_{v_{y}=2}+\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)\geq 1
(i.e., if there is at least one
2
-arrow that can exit the vertex at height
y
) and
x_{y}=1
; note that the condition is well-defined since
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)
does not depend on
w^{*}_{y^{\prime}}
for any
y^{\prime}\geq y
;

\bullet

w^{*}_{y}=1
if
\mathbbm{1}_{v_{y}=2}+\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)=0
and
\mathbbm{1}_{v_{y}=1}+\mathrm{VertCt}_{1}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)\geq 1
(i.e., if there are no 2-arrows and at least one
1
-arrow that can exit the vertex at height
y
) and
x_{y}=1
;

\bullet

w^{*}_{y}=0
if
x_{y}=0
.

The fact that
(\bm{v},\bm{x})
are compatible ensures that exactly one of the above three conditions holds for each
y
.

Fix
\bm{v}\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}
and
\bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{U}
which are compatible, let
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
, and let
\bm{w}^{*}
be as defined above. We define the Pitman error
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})
of
\bm{w}
by

\displaystyle\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w}):=\max_{y\leq U}\bigl(\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y)-\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y)\bigr),

i.e., it is the maximal height difference of
2
-arrows between
\bm{w}
and
\bm{w}^{*}
over all rows. Observe that
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y)\geq\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y)
for each
y\leq U
. Indeed, as we said, it is easy to show that
\bm{w}^{*}
is the configuration that would arise in the
q=0
setting, so by Lemma
4.1
,
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},\bm{\cdot})
may be regarded as the Pitman transform of the incoming color counts
\bm{v}
and the outgoing arrow counts
\bm{x}
; then Lemma
4.2
implies that
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y)\geq\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y)
. Alternatively, since
\bm{w}^{*}
always releases
2
-arrows as soon as it is possible,
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y)
is minimal over all
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
for any
y
, which again verifies that
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y)\geq\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y)
.

We need some notation for the weight of configurations. For a compatible pair
(\bm{v},\bm{x})
and
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
, let
\bm{\mathrm{VertCt}}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y)=(\mathrm{VertCt}_{1}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y),\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y))
. Further let
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,y}=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{1}
if
y\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,y}=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z}
(recall that
z\in(0,1)
is the fixed spectral parameter) if
y\in\llbracket N+1,N+M\rrbracket
, where
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
is read from Figure
10
. Now define

(4.9)

\mathrm{Wgt}_{y}(\bm{v},\bm{w}):=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z,y}\bigl(\bm{\mathrm{VertCt}}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1),v_{y};\bm{\mathrm{VertCt}}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y),w_{y}\bigr)

to be the vertex weight at
y
, with incoming and outgoing horizontal arrows of
v_{y}
and
w_{y}
, respectively, and incoming and outgoing vertical arrows of
\bm{\mathrm{VertCt}}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)
and
\bm{\mathrm{VertCt}}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y)
, respectively. Next, define the weight of the configuration determined by
\bm{v}
and
\bm{w}
by

(4.10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}):=\prod_{y=1}^{U}\mathrm{Wgt}_{y}(\bm{v},\bm{w}).

The next lemma is a deterministic statement about the weights of configurations
w
with high values of
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})
.

Lemma 4.4
.

Fix
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
. There exist positive absolute constants
c
and
C
and
K=K(q)
, such that the following holds for any spectral parameter
z\in(0,1)
(which may depend on
N,M
). Fix a compatible pair
(\bm{v},\bm{x})\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}\times\{0,1\}^{U}
. There exists a mapping
f:\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})\to\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
such that, if
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
with
m:=\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})
and
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})>0
, then
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})/\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},f(\bm{w}))\leq Cq^{cm^{2}}
whenever
m>K\log N
. Further, for any
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
with
m:=\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})
and
m\geq K\log N
,
\#f^{-1}(\{\bm{w}\})\leq N^{2m}
.

This statement should be interpreted as giving a bound on the conditional probability of a given configuration exhibiting
\max_{y}\smash{L^{(2)}_{k}(y)-\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1})(y)}\geq m
given
\smash{L^{(1)}_{k}}
and
\smash{L^{(2)}_{k+1}}
(which will determine
\bm{v}
and
\bm{x}
). Then Proposition
4.3
will follow by averaging over the conditioned data; we give this proof now before proving Lemma
4.4
in Section
4.3
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Recall from (
3.8
) that
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cHL}}_{k}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\bm{L}^{(1)}
,
L^{(2)}_{k+1},L^{(2)}_{k+2}
,
\ldots
. We condition on
\mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cHL}}_{k}
and use the tower property of conditional expectations to write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}L^{(2)}_{k}(y)-\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1})(y)\geq m\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}L^{(2)}_{k}(y)-\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1})(y)\geq m\ \Big|\ \mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cHL}}_{k}\right)\right].

Let
\bm{v}\in\{0,1,2\}^{U}
and
\bm{x}\in\{0,1\}^{U}
be given by

\displaystyle v_{y}=\left(L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y-1)-L^{(2)}_{k+1}(y)\right)+\left(L^{(1)}_{k+1}(y-1)-L^{(1)}_{k+1}(y)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad x_{y}=L^{(1)}_{k}(y-1)-L^{(1)}_{k}(y);

in words, they are the horizontally incoming colors and the horizontally outgoing arrow counts at column
k
. They are compatible. Let
A(\bm{v},\bm{x},m^{\prime})
be the set of
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
such that
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})=m^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})>0
. Now, it is immediate from the Gibbs property of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble, Lemma
3.13
(and can also be seen directly from the Gibbs property of the colored
q
-Boson model, Lemma
3.5
), that

(4.11)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}L^{(2)}_{k}(y)-\mathrm{PT}(L^{(1)}_{k},L^{(2)}_{k+1})(y)\geq m\ \Big|\ \mathcal{F}^{\mathrm{cHL}}_{k}\right)\\
&=\frac{\sum_{m^{\prime}=m}^{\infty}\sum_{\bm{w}\in A(\bm{v},\bm{x},m^{\prime})}\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\sum_{w\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})}\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}.\end{split}

For each
\bm{w}\in A(\bm{v},\bm{x},m^{\prime})
, let
f(\bm{w})\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
be the configuration obtained from Lemma
4.4
, and recall
\#f^{-1}(\bm{w})\leq N^{2m^{\prime}}
. Since
m^{\prime}\geq m\geq K\log N
, we know that
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})/\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},f(\bm{w}))
is upper bounded by
Cq^{c(m^{\prime})^{2}}
.
So we see that, since
\#f^{-1}(\{\bm{w}\})\leq N^{2m^{\prime}}
for
\bm{w}\in A(\bm{v},\bm{x},m^{\prime})
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{w}\in A(\bm{v},\bm{x},m^{\prime})}\!\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})\leq Cq^{c(m^{\prime})^{2}}\!\sum_{\bm{w}\in A(\bm{v},\bm{x},m^{\prime})}\!\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},f(\bm{w}))\leq CN^{2m^{\prime}}q^{c(m^{\prime})^{2}}\!\sum_{\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})}\!\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}).

Substituting this into (
4.11
), and using that
q^{\frac{1}{2}c(m^{\prime})^{2}}\leq C^{-1}N^{-2m^{\prime}}
by raising
K
if necessary (and recalling
m^{\prime}\geq m\geq K\log N
), yields that the righthand side of that equation is upper bounded by
q^{c^{\prime}m^{2}}
for some absolute constant
c>0
. This completes the proof.
∎

4.3.
Construction of the alternate configuration

Proof of Lemma
4.4
.

First, to ensure that the
f
we need to produce is defined for all inputs, for any
\bm{w}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
such that
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})<K\log N
(for a
K=K(q)
to be set later) or
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})=0
, we define
f(\bm{w})=\bm{w}
. So in the remainder of the proof, we may assume that
\bm{w}
is such that
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})>K\log N
and
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})>0
.
It will be useful to keep track of a running Pitman error by row
y
, for which we define

\displaystyle\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w},y):=\max_{y^{\prime}\leq y}\left(\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y^{\prime})-\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y^{\prime})\right).

Let
m^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
. We fix
y=y(m^{\prime})
to be the smallest such that
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w},y)=m^{\prime}
; since
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w},0)=0
,
\bm{w}
is such that
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w},U)=\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})=m
, and
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w},y^{\prime})
increases by at most
1
when
y^{\prime}
increases by
1
, such a
y
exists.

We claim that
w_{y}=1
and
w_{y}^{*}=2
. We first observe that since
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w},y)>\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w},y-1)
and
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},\bm{\cdot})
,
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},\bm{\cdot})
are non-increasing and have increments in
\{-1,0\}
, it must hold that
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y)=\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y-1)
and
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y)=\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y-1)-1
. This means that
w^{*}_{y}=2
, which implies
w_{y}=1
since
w_{y}\in\{1,2\}
(both
\bm{w}
and
\bm{w}^{*}
have the same outgoing arrow counts) and
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y)=\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y-1)
.

Next we claim that
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)\geq m^{\prime}
. To see this we first write, using the definition (
4.8
) of
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)
and noting that, by definition of
y
,
\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y-1)-\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y-1)=m^{\prime}-1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y-1)-\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{v},y-1)

(4.12)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)+\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w},y-1)-\mathrm{Ht}_{2}(\bm{w}^{*},y-1)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)+m^{\prime}-1.

Now it suffices to show that
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)\geq 1
. We know that
w_{y}^{*}=2
, so
v_{y}=2
or
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)\geq 1
(or both). But
w_{y}=1
and the weight of the vertex with a horizontally incoming
2
-arrow and an outgoing
1
-arrow is zero, by Figure
10
. So we have that
v_{y}\neq 2
(since we have assumed
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})>0
), which implies that
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)\geq 1
. Returning to (
4.12
), we have shown that
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)\geq m^{\prime}
.
Thus we have now established that with
y
defined as above, it holds that

(4.13)

\displaystyle w_{y}=1,\,w^{*}_{y}=2,\,\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)\geq m^{\prime},\text{ and }\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{*},y-1)\geq 1.

With these preliminaries in place, we turn to exhibiting a particular configuration
f(\bm{w})
such that
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{w})/\mathrm{Wgt}(f(\bm{w}))\leq Cq^{cm^{2}}
.
We let
K_{0}:=2(\log q^{-1})^{-1}
and
K:=2K_{0}
. For
m\geq K\log N
, we will define a sequence of configurations
\bm{w}^{(0)},\bm{w}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{w}^{(R)}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
where
\bm{w}^{(0)}:=\bm{w}
and
R:=m-K_{0}\log N
such that
\bm{w}^{(i)}
has
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w}^{(i)})\geq m-i
and

(4.14)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{(i-1)})}{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{(i)})}\leq q^{m-i-K_{0}\log N-1}(1+q^{K_{0}\log N-1})^{N}.

We define
f(\bm{w})
to be
\bm{w}^{(R)}
. Clearly this will suffice to establish
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{w})/\mathrm{Wgt}(f(\bm{w}))\leq Cq^{cm^{2}}
as then by multiplying together the inequalities we would obtain that, for absolute constants
c
and
C
,

(4.15)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{(R)})}\leq q^{mR-\sum_{i=1}^{R}i-RK_{0}\log N-R}(1+q^{K_{0}\log N-1})^{RN}\leq Cq^{cm^{2}},

the final inequality for
m\geq K\log N
. So now we must show (
4.14
); we will establish that
\#f^{-1}(\{\tilde{\bm{w}}\})\leq N^{2m}
for any
\tilde{\bm{w}}
with
\mathrm{PE}(\tilde{\bm{w}})=m\geq K\log N
and
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})>0
after specifying the sequence of configurations.

Reindexing, to establish (
4.14
), we must show that if we have a configuration
\bm{w}
with
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})\geq m^{\prime}\geq K\log N
, then we can produce a configuration
\bm{w}^{\prime}
with
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w}^{\prime})\geq m^{\prime}-1
and

(4.16)

\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}\leq q^{m^{\prime}-K_{0}\log N-1}(1+q^{K_{0}\log N-1})^{N}.

Now, by (
4.13
), since
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w})=m
, for any
m^{\prime}\in\llbracket K\log N,m\rrbracket
there exists a first vertex (whose row height we labeled
y
), where a
1
-arrow exits horizontally and there are at least
m^{\prime}
2-arrows entering vertically, i.e.,
w_{y}=1
and
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)\geq m^{\prime}
. Since we have assumed
m^{\prime}\geq K\log N
and all
2
-arrows eventually exit, there exists a value
y^{\prime}>y
which is the smallest one greater than
y
such that
v_{y^{\prime}}\neq 2
,
w_{y^{\prime}}=2
, and
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{w},y^{\prime}-1)=\lceil\frac{1}{2}K\log N\rceil=\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil
. In particular, for all
u\in\llbracket y,y^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
,

(4.17)

\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},u)\geq\tfrac{1}{2}K\log N=K_{0}\log N.

We define
\bm{w}^{\prime}
by swapping the exiting arrows at
y
and
y^{\prime}
, i.e., by
w^{\prime}_{u}=w_{u}
for all
u\not\in\{y,y^{\prime}\}
,
w^{\prime}_{y^{\prime}}=w_{y}=1
, and
w^{\prime}_{y}=w_{y^{\prime}}=2
. Observe that since there are at most
N
possible locations for such a swap, and
f(\bm{w})
is defined by a sequence of
R=m-K_{0}\log N<m
such swaps, it follows that
\#f^{-1}(\{\tilde{\bm{w}}\})\leq\binom{N}{2}^{m}<N^{2m}
for any
\tilde{\bm{w}}\in\mathrm{Val}(\bm{v},\bm{x})
with
\mathrm{PE(}\tilde{\bm{w}})=m
. Next observe that, for
u\in\llbracket y,y^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
,

(4.18)

\begin{split}\mathrm{VertCt}_{1}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime},u)&=\mathrm{VertCt}_{1}(\bm{v},\bm{w},u)+1\ \text{ and }\\
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime},u)&=\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},u)-1.\end{split}

It is immediate that
\mathrm{PE}(\bm{w}^{\prime})\geq m^{\prime}-1
. To upper bound
\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})/\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})
, we note that the vertex configurations at
u
are identical in
\bm{w}
and
\bm{w}^{\prime}
for
u\not\in\llbracket y,y^{\prime}\rrbracket
, so the ratio is the ratio of the product of the vertex weights for vertices at
u
with
u\in\llbracket y,y^{\prime}\rrbracket
, i.e., (recall the definition of
\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}
and
\mathrm{Wgt}
from (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) respectively)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}=\prod_{u=y}^{y^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}.

To estimate this, we first note that
w_{u}=w^{\prime}_{u}
for
u\in\llbracket y+1,y^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
and recall that (
4.18
) holds for
u\in\llbracket y,y^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
.

Recall the spectral parameter
z\in(0,1)
. For a vertex height
u\in\llbracket 1,U\rrbracket
, let

(4.19)

\displaystyle\tilde{z}(u):=z\text{ if }u\in\llbracket N+1,N+M\rrbracket\text{ and }\tilde{z}(u):=1\text{ if }u\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,

and, for
j\in\{1,2\}
,

(4.20)

\displaystyle A_{j}(u):=\mathrm{VertCt}_{j}(\bm{v},\bm{w},u-1).

In the following, we will be reading vertex weights from Figure
10
. Now, since
w_{y}=1
,
\mathrm{Wgt}_{y}(\bm{v},\bm{w})=\tilde{z}(y)q^{A_{2}(y)}
or
\tilde{z}(y)(1-q^{A_{1}(y)})q^{A_{2}(y)}
, depending on whether
v_{y}=1
or
0
respectively. Since
A_{2}(y)\geq m
as shown in (
4.13
), both possibilities for the weight are upper bounded by
\tilde{z}(y)q^{m}
. Similarly since
w_{y}^{\prime}=2
and
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime},y-1)=\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y-1)=A_{2}(y)
,
\mathrm{Wgt}_{y}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})=\smash{\tilde{z}(y)(1-q^{A_{2}(y)})}\geq\tilde{z}(y)(1-q^{m})
. Thus

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}_{y}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}_{y}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}\leq\frac{\tilde{z}(y)q^{m}}{\tilde{z}(y)(1-q^{m})}=\frac{q^{m}}{1-q^{m}}.

Next we turn to the
y^{\prime}
vertex. Here,
w_{y^{\prime}}=2
and
A_{2}(y^{\prime})=\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil
(see before (
4.17
)), so
\mathrm{Wgt}_{y^{\prime}}(\bm{v},\bm{w})=\tilde{z}(y^{\prime})(1-q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil})
independently of
v_{y^{\prime}}\in\{0,1\}
, while
\mathrm{Wgt}_{y^{\prime}}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})=\tilde{z}(y^{\prime})q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}
or
\tilde{z}(y^{\prime})(1-q^{\mathrm{VertCt}_{1}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime},y^{\prime}-1)})q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}
(depending on
v_{y^{\prime}}=1
or
0
respectively, since
v_{y^{\prime}}\neq 2
), which is lower bounded by
\tilde{z}(y^{\prime})(1-q)q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}
, since, as noted in (
4.18
),
\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime},y^{\prime}-1)=\mathrm{VertCt}_{2}(\bm{v},\bm{w},y^{\prime}-1)-1=\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1
. So

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}_{y^{\prime}}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}_{y^{\prime}}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}\leq\frac{\tilde{z}(y^{\prime})(1-q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil})}{\tilde{z}(y^{\prime})(1-q)q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}}=\frac{1-q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil}}{(1-q)q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}}.

Finally we turn to looking at the weight of vertex
u
for
u\in\llbracket y+1,y^{\prime}-1\rrbracket
. Recall that
w_{u}=w^{\prime}_{u}
for this range of
u
. If
w_{u}=0
, the vertex weights are
1
for both
\bm{w}
and
\bm{w}^{\prime}
. If
w_{u}=1
, then, using (
4.18
), and with
\tilde{z}(u)
as in (
4.19
) and
A_{j}(u)
as in (
4.20
),

\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{z}(u)(1-q^{A_{1}(u)})q^{A_{2}(u)}}{\tilde{z}(u)(1-q^{A_{1}(u)+1})q^{A_{2}(u)-1}}&v_{u}=0\\[12.0pt]
\displaystyle\frac{\tilde{z}(u)q^{A_{2}(u)}}{\tilde{z}(u)q^{A_{2}(u)-1}}&v_{u}=1,\end{cases}

which is at most
1
. Finally, we consider the case
w_{u}=2
. Recall that
A_{2}(u)\geq\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil
by (
4.17
). If
v_{u}=2
,
\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w})/\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})=1
, while if
v_{u}=0
or
1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}=\frac{\tilde{z}(u)(1-q^{A_{2}(u)})}{\tilde{z}(u)(1-q^{A_{2}(u)-1})}=1+q^{A_{2}(u)-1}\cdot\frac{1-q}{1-q^{A_{2}(u)-1}}\leq 1+q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}.

Combining the above inequalities, and using that the number of locations
u
where
w_{u}\neq 0
is
N
(and that if
w_{u}=0
the ratio is 1), we obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}=\prod_{u=y}^{y^{\prime}}\frac{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w})}{\mathrm{Wgt}_{u}(\bm{v},\bm{w}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{q^{m}}{1-q^{m}}\cdot\frac{1-q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil}}{q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}}\left(1+q^{K_{0}\log N-1}\right)^{N}

\displaystyle\leq q^{m-\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil}\left(1+q^{\lceil K_{0}\log N\rceil-1}\right)^{N}.

This implies (
4.16
), completing the proof.
∎

5.
Properties of ASEP and S6V

In this section we collect some properties of the colored S6V model and ASEP that will be useful in future sections, in particular, a form of stationarity and one-point tightness.

5.1.
Colored stochastic six-vertex model

We work with packed boundary condition for the colored S6V model; recall the definition from Section
2.3
. In particular, we have arrows entering horizontally at every site along
\{1\}\times\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
, with the color of the arrow entering
(1,k)
being
k
. Also recall that the color of the arrow exiting horizontally from
(t,k)
is
j_{(t,k)}
and from (
2.17
) that

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)=\#\left\{k>y:j_{(t,k)}\geq x\right\}

for
x\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
and
y\in\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
.
It is easy to check from this description that, for any
k\in\llbracket-N,N\rrbracket
fixed,

(5.1)

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x+k,0;y+k,t)+k

as processes in
(x,y,t)
on the domain
\llbracket\max(-N-k,-N),\min(N-k,N)\rrbracket\times\llbracket\max(-N-k,-N),\infty\Rparen\times\mathbb{N}
; this uses that, when
y\geq x
,
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)-(N-y)
is the number of arrows starting at a site
(1,i)
for some
i\in\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
which horizontally exit a vertex
(t,i)
for some
i\in\llbracket y+1,\infty\Rparen
, which is a quantity whose distribution is invariant on shifting
x
and
y
by the same constant.

The following records a one-point convergence result on the limiting one-point fluctuations of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
in its rescaled form
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
as defined in (
2.20
) (which recall involves a parameter
\alpha
controlling the location in the rarefaction fan where the height function is evaluated). It was originally essentially shown in
[
BCG16
]
, but proved in the slightly improved form we require in
[
CD18
]
.

Proposition 5.1

(One-point distributional limit of S6V)
.

Let
q\in

[0,1)
,
z\in(0,1)
, and
\alpha\in(z,z^{-1})
. Fix
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x;y)+(x-y)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathrm{GUE}\text{-}\mathrm{TW}}
, the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution (see end of Definition
2.1
).

Proof.

By (
5.1
) and the expression defining
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
in Definition
2.19
, it follows that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x;y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(0;y-x)
, so it suffices to prove the case
x=0
. When
q\in(0,1)
, this case is an immediate consequence of combining
[
CD18
, Theorems 2.3 and 2.5]
and translating the notation. When
q=0
, we first note that by Proposition
3.12
,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(0;\bm{\cdot})
is a rescaled version of the top line of the uncolored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble
\smash{\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}(\bm{\cdot})}
from Section
3.2
, which consists of non-intersecting Bernoulli paths. The scaling limit of the latter is proven in
[
DFF+21
, Corollary 1.3]
as well as in
[
DNV23
, Theorem 1.5]
to be
\mathcal{P}
, from which the one-point statement we claim follows immediately.
∎

5.2.
Colored ASEP and relation to colored S6V

Next we recall the model of colored ASEP from Section
2.2
.
Because the approximate LPP representation of Theorem
3.20
is in terms of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble associated to the colored
q
-Boson model which has a direct distributional equivalence only with colored S6V, we need to relate colored ASEP to colored S6V.

Consider the colored S6V model under packed boundary condition with

(5.2)

N=\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor

for
\delta,t>0
with
q\in[0,1)
and
z=\frac{1-\delta}{1-\delta q}
(from Figure
8
, this means that the probability of the higher color arrow moving straight through a vertex vertically is
q\delta
and horizontally is
\delta
). We will evaluate the colored height function on the vertical line
\{M\}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
with
M=\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor
. Consider colored ASEP with the usual jump rates of
1
to the right and
q
to the left. It is known from
[
BCG16
,
Agg17
]
that by evaluating the height function of the S6V model near
(M,N)=(\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)
one obtains the ASEP height function in the distributional limit as
\delta\to 0
.

Unfortunately, this description poses issues to use directly, namely, in the
\delta\to 0
limit the domain becomes infinite even for fixed
t
. This means that estimates we wish to apply in the ASEP setting like Theorem
3.20
(probability bound on the deviation
m
from the approximate LPP representation), which only apply for
m\geq K(q)\log N
, would not hold.

5.2.1.
Effective coupling

We instead make use of an effective coupling proven in
[
AB24
]
of colored S6V with
N=\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor
as in (
5.2
) and colored ASEP at time
t
which allows to take
\delta\to 0
at a particular rate with
t\to\infty
. We will take
\delta=t^{-\theta}
for some
\theta>0
large enough. We define

(5.3)

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(x,0;y,t):=\lfloor t^{1+\theta}\rfloor+x+1-h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;\lfloor t^{1+\theta}\rfloor-y-1,\lfloor t^{1+\theta}\rfloor);

the first argument of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
is
-x
because
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;\bm{\cdot},t)
involves counts of particles of color greater than or equal to
-x
and not
x
(recall (
2.5
)). As we will see, the presence of negative signs in going between
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}
and
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
arises from the fact that the ASEP particle configuration can be read off (under a coupling that we will introduce shortly) of a horizontal line in the S6V model, but the S6V height function considers arrow counts on a vertical line.

We note that (
5.1
) implies that

(5.4)

h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(\bm{\cdot}+k,0;\bm{\cdot}+k,t)

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
fixed, matching the relation satisfied by
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
(it is easily seen from the latter’s definition (
2.5
) that (
5.4
) holds with
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
in place of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}
).

The properties of the coupling between
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}
and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
that we need is as follows.

Lemma 5.2

(Effective coupling of ASEP with S6V)
.

There exists
C>0
such that, for any
\theta>0
and
t\geq 1
, there is a coupling such that
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(x,0;y,t)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)
for all
|x|,|y|\leq 2t
with probability at least
1-Ct^{3-\theta/8}
.

In the remainder of the paper we will assume
\theta
is a large fixed constant such as
40
so that, by the Borel-Cantelli lemma, Lemma
5.2
implies that with probability one
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(x,0;y,t)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)
for all
|x|,|y|\leq 2t
and for all
t
large enough. To prove Lemma
5.2
, we will need the following statements for colored ASEP and colored S6V, which are essentially implications of the finite speed of propagation in the models. As their proofs are straightforward, we defer them to Appendix
B
. Define

(5.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t):=\#\{z\in\llbracket y+1,\lfloor 4t\rfloor\rrbracket:\eta_{t}(z)\geq-x\},

where recall
(\eta_{t}(k))_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
records the state of the colored ASEP at time
t
. In words,
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
is a variant of the colored ASEP height function (
2.5
) where particles beyond location
\lfloor 4t\rfloor
are not counted.

Lemma 5.3
.

There exist
C,c>0
such that, for all
t>0
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-ct)
,

(5.6)

\displaystyle\left(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)\right)_{|x|,|y|\leq 2t}=\bigl(\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)\bigr)_{|x|,|y|\leq 2t},

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\delta
,
t>0
,
N=\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor
, and
z=\frac{1-\delta}{1-\delta q}
. There exist absolute constants
C,c>0
such that, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-ct)
, it holds that, for all
|x|\leq 2t
and
z\geq\lfloor 4t\rfloor
,

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-z,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)=\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-x+1,

where note the righthand side is the total number of arrows of color at least
x
in the system.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Let
\delta>0
. Recall
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-y-1,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)
is the number of arrows of color greater than or equal to
-x
passing strictly above
(\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-y-1)
, and let
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
be as in (
5.5
). Also recall the packed boundary and initial conditions for colored S6V and colored ASEP, respectively. We note that in both colored S6V and colored ASEP, color merging (Remarks
2.6
and
2.18
) implies that the distribution of
(\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t))_{|x|,|y|\leq 2t}
and
\smash{(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t))_{|x|,|y|\leq 2t}}
are unchanged by merging all particles/arrows of color strictly greater than
\lfloor 2t\rfloor
to have color
\lfloor 2t\rfloor+1
, and merging all particles/arrows of color strictly smaller than
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
to have color
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor-1
. We work with these color merged versions of the packed initial conditions (this is required as the result we invoke from
[
AB24
]
states that only finitely many colors exist in the systems).

We work with the coupling whose existence is asserted in
[
AB24
, Proposition B.2]
. Then we know that, on an event
\mathsf{E}_{1}
with probability at least
1-C\delta^{1/8}t^{3}
(and we work on this event in the remainder of the proof), for all
|x|\leq 2t
,
|y|\leq 4t
, there is an arrow of color
x
exiting vertically from
(\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor+y,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)
(i.e., along the horizontal line at height
\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor
) if and only if there is a particle of color
x
at location
y
in colored ASEP at time
t
. However, the S6V height function we defined in (
2.17
) is in terms of arrow counts on a vertical and not horizontal line.

To handle this, we claim that, with probability at least
1-32\delta t^{2}
, the color of the arrow exiting horizontally from
(\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-y)
is the same as that of the arrow exiting vertically from
(\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor+y,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)
for all
y\in\llbracket-\lfloor 4t\rfloor,\lfloor 4t\rfloor\rrbracket
; call the event that this occurs for a given
y
by
\mathsf{E}_{2}(y)
and define
\mathsf{E}_{2}=\cap_{y\in\llbracket-\lfloor 4t\rfloor,\lfloor 4t\rfloor\rrbracket}\mathsf{E}_{2}(y)
. Let
\bm{v}_{t}=(\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)
and
\ell_{t}=\lfloor 4t\rfloor
, and define the squares
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
with corners given by
\{\bm{v}_{t},\bm{v}_{t}+(-\ell_{t},0),\bm{v}_{t}+(0,\ell_{t}),\bm{v}_{t}+(-\ell_{t},\ell_{t})\}
and
\{\bm{v}_{t},\bm{v}_{t}+(\ell_{t},0),\bm{v}_{t}+(0,-\ell_{t}),\bm{v}_{t}+(\ell_{t},-\ell_{t})\}
, respectively. To see that our claim holds, note first that for
\mathsf{E}_{2}(y)
to not hold for some
y\in\llbracket-\lfloor 4t\rfloor,\lfloor 4t\rfloor\rrbracket
, it must be the case that at least one vertex in the squares
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
(for the cases of
y\leq 0
and
y>0
respectively) allows at least one arrow to pass straight through vertically or horizontally; the probability of this happening at any given vertex is at most
\delta
. A union bound over the
32t^{2}
vertices in the two squares proves the claim that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E}_{2}^{c})\leq 32\delta t^{2}
.

Let
\mathsf{E}_{3}
and
\mathsf{E}_{4}
be the events that
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)
and
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 4t\rfloor,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)=\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-x+1
hold for all
|x|,|y|\leq 2t
. By Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
,
\mathbb{P}((\mathsf{E}_{3}\cup\mathsf{E}_{4})^{c})\leq C\exp(-ct)
for some
C,c>0
.

Set
\delta=t^{-\theta}
. Under the coupling above and on
\mathsf{E}:=\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}\cap\mathsf{E}_{3}\cap\mathsf{E}_{4}
, we will show that
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(x,0;y,t)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)
for all
|x|,|y|\leq 2t
. First, by the definition of
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
, on
\mathsf{E}_{1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{2}
and for
|x|,|y|\leq 2t
,

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)

\displaystyle=\#\left\{k\in\llbracket y+1,\lfloor 4t\rfloor\rrbracket:j_{(\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-k)}\geq-x\right\}

\displaystyle=h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 4t\rfloor-1,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)-h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-y-1,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)

\displaystyle=\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor+x+1-h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-y-1,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(x,0;y,t),

the penultimate equality on
\mathsf{E}_{3}
. The proof is completed by the fact that
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,0;y,t)
on
\mathsf{E}_{4}
and noting that it holds for all large enough
C
and all
t\geq 1
that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E}^{c})\leq C(\exp(-ct)+\delta t^{2}+\delta^{1/8}t^{3})\leq C\delta^{1/8}t^{3}
(since
\delta=t^{-\theta}
).
∎

5.2.2.
One-point tightness

The one-point tightness for
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}
that we require follows from Lemma
5.2
and the following one-point convergence result for
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
. Recall the ASEP sheet
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}
from (
2.8
) and the parameter
\alpha
controlling the macroscopic location around which the height function
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
is evaluated.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
q\in[0,1)
and
\alpha\in(-1,1)
. Fix
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x;y)+(x-y)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathrm{GUE}\text{-}\mathrm{TW}
, the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution.

Proof.

Again by stationarity of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
(as noted after (
5.4
)) it suffices to prove the case
x=0
. This follows from
[
TW09
, Theorem 3]
, and is also stated in a form closer to ours in
[
BO17
, Theorem 11.3]
.
∎

We can combine this result with the coupling in Lemma
5.2
to obtain a one-point tightness statement for
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}
. The rescaled version
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}
we work with is defined by (
2.8
) with
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}
in place of
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}
with
\theta=40
, i.e., for
\alpha\in(-1,1)
fixed and recalling
\mu(\alpha)
,
\sigma(\alpha)
, and
\beta(\alpha)
from (
2.6
) and (
2.7
),

(5.7)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}(x;y):=\sigma(\alpha)^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\Bigl(\mu(\alpha)2\varepsilon^{-1}+\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)\beta(\alpha)(y-x)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\\
&-h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}\bigl(\beta(\alpha)x\varepsilon^{-2/3},0;2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}+\beta(\alpha)y\varepsilon^{-2/3},2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}\bigr)\Bigr).\end{split}

Corollary 5.6
.

Fix
\theta=40
. Let
q\in[0,1)
,
\alpha\in(-1,1)
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}(x;y)+(x-y)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathrm{GUE}\text{-}\mathrm{TW}
, the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
5.2
and Proposition
5.5
.
∎

6.
Preliminaries for line ensemble tightness

In this section we start by introducing some line ensemble notions that will be necessary in the proof of Theorem
3.18
on tightness of line ensembles. Then in Section
6.2
we introduce the Brownian Gibbs property and give the proof of Theorem
3.19
(on the convergence of line ensembles to the parabolic Airy line ensemble), assuming Theorem
3.18
and that all subsequential limits possess the Brownian Gibbs property, which will be proven in later sections. In Section
6.3
we will relate the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble, with appropriate centerings, to colored ASEP (in the approximate form (
7.7
)) and colored S6V such that the former’s top curves are the height functions of the respective models, in such a way that the corresponding uncolored line ensembles satisfy the assumptions introduced in Section
3.3.4
. Finally in Section
6.4
we collect some ingredient statements needed to prove Theorem
3.18
and give the latter’s proof assuming them; the ingredient statements will be proved in later sections.

6.1.
The Strong Gibbs property

We will need at one or two places a stronger version of the Gibbs property which allows us to resample on certain random domains known as stopping domains, analogous to the strong Markov property and stopping times.

Definition 6.1

(Stopping domain)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\bm{L}
be a discrete line ensemble (Definition
3.6
). A pair of random variables
\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\in\mathbb{Z}
forms a stopping domain
\llbracket\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\rrbracket
with respect to
(L_{1},\ldots,L_{k})
if, for all
\ell,r\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\ell<r
,

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{l}\leq\ell,\mathfrak{r}\geq r\}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket\ell,r\rrbracket,\bm{L}).

We define the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\rrbracket,\bm{L})
to be the collection of all events
A
such that
A\cap\{\mathfrak{l}\leq\ell,\mathfrak{r}\geq r\}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket\ell,r\rrbracket,\bm{L})
(as in Definition
3.14
) for all
\ell<r
.

Proposition 6.2

(Strong Gibbs property)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\llbracket\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\rrbracket
be a stopping domain. Let
F:\{(\ell,r,f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}):\ell<r\text{ and }(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})\in\mathbb{R}^{\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket\ell,r\rrbracket}\}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded. If
\bm{L}
is a discrete line ensemble that has the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property, it holds that, almost surely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r},L_{1},\ldots,L_{k})\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\rrbracket,\bm{L})\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}[F(\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r},B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})W(\bm{B},L_{k+1})\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\rrbracket,\bm{L})]}{\mathbb{E}[W(\bm{B},L_{k+1})\mid\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\rrbracket,\bm{L})]},

where
\bm{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
is a collection of
k
independent Bernoulli random walk bridges with
B_{i}(x)=L_{i}(x)
for
x\in\{\mathfrak{l},\mathfrak{r}\}
and
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Similar statements have been proven for continuous line ensembles, e.g.,
[
CH14
, Lemma 2.5]
. The proof amounts to decomposing based on the exact value of
\mathfrak{l}
and
\mathfrak{r}
(which is straightforward here as everything is discrete) and using the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property on each of the resulting events; we do not record the details here.

6.2.
The Brownian Gibbs property

To introduce the Brownian Gibbs property, we will need notation for the
\sigma
-algebra we condition on for continuous line ensembles. We first precisely define the meaning of a continuous line ensemble.

Definition 6.3

(Continuous line ensemble)
.

Fix a (possibly infinite) interval
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. A
\Lambda
-indexed continuous line ensemble

\bm{\mathfrak{L}}=(\mathfrak{L}_{1},\mathfrak{L}_{2},\ldots)
is a random variable defined on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, taking values in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda,\mathbb{R})
, such that (again writing the first argument of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
as a subscript, i.e.,
\mathfrak{L}_{i}(\bm{\cdot}):=\bm{\mathfrak{L}}(i,\bm{\cdot})
)
\mathfrak{L}_{i}(y)\geq\mathfrak{L}_{i+1}(y)
for any
y\in\Lambda
and
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Here
\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda
is given the product topology of the discrete topology and the Euclidean topology, and
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda,\mathbb{R})
has the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

Recall again the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}
in the case of discrete line ensembles from Definition
3.14
.
In the case of a continuous line ensemble
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}:\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we will write, for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
j,k\in\mathbb{N}
with
a<b
and
j<k
,
\smash{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\llbracket j,k\rrbracket,[a,b],\bm{\mathfrak{L}})}
to be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the collection of random variables

\bigl\{\mathfrak{L}_{i}(x):(i,x)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\setminus(\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times[a,b])\bigr\}.

(Compared to Definition
3.14
, we have replaced
\llbracket a+1,b-1\rrbracket
with
[a,b]
in the second bullet point, so that
\mathfrak{L}_{i}(a)
and
\mathfrak{L}_{i}(b)
are still conditioned on, for each
i\in\llbracket j,k\rrbracket
). We will again often adopt the shorthand
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,[a,b],\bm{L})=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,[a,b],\bm{L})
.

Definition 6.4

(Brownian Gibbs property)
.

A line ensemble
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}=(\mathfrak{L}_{1},\mathfrak{L}_{2},\ldots):\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to satisfy the Brownian Gibbs property if the following holds for any
\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and
[a,b]\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\llbracket j,k\rrbracket,[a,b],\bm{\mathfrak{L}})
, the distribution of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}|_{\llbracket j,k\rrbracket\times[a,b]}
is that of
k-j+1
independent rate two Brownian bridges
B^{\mathrm{Br}}_{j},\ldots,B^{\mathrm{Br}}_{k}
, with
B^{\mathrm{Br}}_{i}(x)=\mathfrak{L}_{i}(x)
for each
x\in\{a,b\}
and
i\in\llbracket j,k\rrbracket
, conditioned on
\mathfrak{L}_{j-1}(x)>B^{\mathrm{Br}}_{j}(x)>\ldots>B^{\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x)>\mathfrak{L}_{k+1}(x)
for all
x\in[a,b]
(with
\mathfrak{L}_{0}\equiv\infty
by convention).

Figure 15
.
A depiction of a special case, with
k=1
, of the Brownian Gibbs property: in the left panel is the original line ensemble
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
, in the middle panel we condition on the data which is depicted, essentially forgetting what has been erased, and in the right panel we resample the forgotten data, which is conditionally distributed as a rate two Brownian bridge between the endpoints, conditioned to avoid the second curve
\mathfrak{L}_{2}
.

Recall now the framework for line ensemble convergence in Section
3.3.4
. In particular, Theorem
3.18
asserts that for any sequence of discrete line ensembles
\bm{L}^{N}
satisfying Assumptions
1
and
2
, the rescaled line ensembles
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
defined in (
3.13
) forms a tight sequence, and Theorem
3.19
characterizes the subsequential limits and says that, under the additional Assumption
3
, the original sequence converges to
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
.

Theorem
3.19
is an immediate consequence of knowing that all subsequential limit points of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
satisfy the Brownian Gibbs property, on combining with a recent characterization result for
\bm{\mathcal{P}}

[
AH23a
]
. These two statements are given precisely next. Proposition
6.5
is proved in Section
12
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\bm{L}^{N}
satisfy Assumptions
1
and
2
, let
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
be defined as in (
3.13
), and let
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
be any subsequential weak limit of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
as
N\to\infty
. Then
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
has the Brownian Gibbs property.

Proposition 6.6

(
[
AH23a
, Corollary 2.11 (2)]
)
.

Suppose
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
is a line ensemble with the Brownian Gibbs property such that, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a constant
C=C(\varepsilon)>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathfrak{L}_{1}(x)+x^{2}|>\varepsilon|x|+C\right)\leq\varepsilon

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then there exists a parabolic Airy line ensemble
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
and an independent random variable
\mathfrak{c}
such that
\mathfrak{L}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{P}_{i}(x)+\mathfrak{c}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

We give the proof of Theorem
3.19
given these two statements and Theorem
3.18
now.

Proof of Theorem
3.19
.

By Theorem
3.18
and Proposition
6.5
, we know that
\{\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is tight and that all subsequential limits have the Brownian Gibbs property. Let
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
be such a subsequential limit. By the characterization theorem (Proposition
6.6
), it follows that
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}
equals the parabolic Airy line ensemble up to an independent random constant shift. Then Assumption
3
guarantees that the constant shift is zero, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

In Section
7
, we will apply the strong characterization Proposition
6.6
of
[
AH23a
]
(indirectly, through Theorem
3.19
) to the line ensembles coming from S6V and ASEP.
In the case of ASEP for
\alpha=0
, we can circumvent Proposition
6.6
. Indeed, in Appendix
B.3
, we will give an alternate proof of that case using
[
QS22
, Theorem 2.2]
(along with Lemma
5.2
) to infer that
\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{1}
converges to the parabolic Airy
2
process and the characterization theorem from
[
DM21
]
(which asserts that if two Brownian Gibbsian line ensembles have the same top curve, in law, then the entire line ensembles are equal in law) to conclude that that limiting line ensemble is the parabolic Airy line ensemble.

In the next section we will define centered versions of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensembles which are associated to S6V and ASEP, and verify that they satisfy the assumptions from Section
3.3.4
.

6.3.
Verifying the assumptions for colored S6V and colored ASEP

6.3.1.
S6V

Recall that we work in the domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
and that we evaluate the colored height function on the vertical line
x=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
.
Fix
q\in[0,1)
,
z\in(0,1)
, and
\alpha\in(z,z^{-1})
and recall
\mu(\alpha)
from (
2.18
) and
\smash{L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{i}}
from (
3.6
) with parameters
q
,
z
,
N
, and
M=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
. Define
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}
for
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
by, for
y\in\llbracket-\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor,N+M-\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\rrbracket
,

(6.1)

\displaystyle L^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}_{i}(y):=L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{i}(\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor+y)+j-N-\lfloor\mu(\alpha)\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor.

Observe that, since
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(j,0;\bm{\cdot},\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{1}(\bm{\cdot})
as functions on
\llbracket 1,N-1\rrbracket
from Proposition
3.12
, it follows that, as functions on
\llbracket-\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor+1,N-1-\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\rrbracket
and jointly over
j\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
,

(6.2)

\displaystyle L^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}_{1}(\bm{\cdot})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(j,0;\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor+\bm{\cdot},\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor)+j-N-\lfloor\mu(\alpha)\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor.

Lemma 6.8
.

\bm{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},(1),\varepsilon}
satisfies Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
with
p=\mu^{\prime}(\alpha)
and
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}\mu^{\prime\prime}(\alpha)
(as defined in (
2.18
)) and with
\varepsilon^{-1}
in place of
N
.

Proof.

Since
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
marginally has the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property (Proposition
3.17
), and since the Gibbs property is not changed by deterministic constant shifts of the curves, it follows that
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},(1),\varepsilon}
has the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property.
That Assumptions
2
and
3
hold follows immediately from combining Propositions
3.12
and
5.1
with (
6.2
).
∎

6.3.2.
ASEP

Fix
q\in[0,1)
,
\alpha\in(-1,1)
and define
\gamma=1-q
. Let
\theta=40
and

K_{\varepsilon,\theta}=\lfloor(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})^{1+\theta}\rfloor-\lfloor 2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor-1.

Next we define line ensembles
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}
for
j\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})^{1+\theta}\rfloor\rrbracket
. Recall
\mu(\alpha)=\smash{\frac{1}{4}(1-\alpha)^{2}}
from (
2.6
). They are specified by setting, for
y\in\llbracket 1-K_{\varepsilon,\theta},N+M-K_{\varepsilon,\theta}\rrbracket
, and where the parameters for the righthand side are
z=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon q}
and
M=N=\smash{\lfloor(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})^{1+\theta}\rfloor}
,

(6.3)

\begin{split}L^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}_{i}(y)&:=L^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}_{i}\left(K_{\varepsilon,\theta}+y\right)-\lfloor(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})^{1+\theta}\rfloor+j-1+\lfloor\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)^{2}\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor.\end{split}

The relation between
L^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}_{1}
and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}
(recall (
5.3
) and
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(j,0;\bm{\cdot},\lfloor(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})^{1+\theta}\rfloor)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}L^{\smash{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}}_{1}(\bm{\cdot})
) is

(6.4)

\displaystyle L^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}_{1}(\bm{\cdot})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}-h^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta}(-j,0;\lfloor 2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor-\bm{\cdot},\lfloor 2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor)+\lfloor\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)^{2}\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor.

Lemma 6.9
.

\bm{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(1),\theta,\varepsilon}
satisfies Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
with
p=-\frac{1}{2}(1-\alpha)
and
\lambda=\frac{1}{8}
and with
\varepsilon^{-1}
in place of
N
.

Proof.

As in the case of
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},(1),\varepsilon}
, it is immediate from Proposition
3.17
that
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(1),\theta,\varepsilon}
has the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property. It follows from Corollary
5.6
that Assumptions
2
and
3
hold.
∎

Next, we set up some of the ingredients for the proof of Theorem
3.18
, which will also be useful in Section
7
on the tightness and limit of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}
. In particular, we are working in the setup of Theorem
3.18
, i.e., we have a sequence of discrete line ensembles
\bm{L}^{N}
indexed by
N
and defined on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{Z}
that satisfy Assumptions
1
and
2
.

6.4.
Ingredients for proof of tightness

Proving tightness of the path measures requires us to establish one-point tightness, as well as control on the modulus of continuity, for all curves. We state the estimates we need and then use them to give the proof of Theorem
3.18
.

6.4.1.
One-point tightness of all curves

Note that from Assumption
2
we have one-point tightness for
L^{N}_{1}
and, since line ensembles which satisfy the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property must by definition be ordered, we therefore have control on the upper tail of
L^{N}_{k}
for every
k
. The lower tail for the
k
th
curve for
k\geq 2
is more delicate, however, and we record a statement for it now.

Theorem 6.10
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>0
, and
\varepsilon>0
. Then there exist
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon)
and
R=R(k,T,\varepsilon)>0
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{sN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\left(L^{N}_{k}(s)-ps+\lambda s^{2}N^{-1}\right)\leq-RN^{1/3}\right)<\varepsilon.

We will prove Theorem
6.10
in Sections
9
and
10
. As mentioned in Sections
1.3.3
and
3.3.3
, the proof inductively interweaves uniform lower tail control of the
k
th
curve over an interval with control over uniform separation between the
(k-1)
st
and
k
th
curves, both on the fluctuation scale
N^{1/3}
. Obtaining control on these two quantities forms the bulk of the technical work of the proof of Theorem
3.18
.

6.4.2.
Modulus of continuity

To prove tightness of path measures, we also need control on the modulus of continuity of
L^{N}_{k}
. As in earlier works establishing tightness for line ensembles with Gibbs properties, this comes down to controlling the partition function of the Gibbs property, in order to transfer modulus of continuity estimates from the base path measure (in this case, Bernoulli random walk bridges) to the line ensemble. For an interval
[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]
, recall from Definition
3.16
that the partition function of the top
k
curves is

(6.5)

\displaystyle Z^{k,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N}(-TN^{2/3}),\bm{L}^{N}(T^{2/3}),L^{N}_{k+1}}_{N}:=\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}[W(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k},L^{N}_{k+1})],

where
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,\llbracket-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}\rrbracket,\bm{L}^{N})
and
(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
is a collection of
k
independent Bernoulli random walk bridges with
B_{i}
from
(-TN^{2/3},L^{N}_{i}(-TN^{2/3}))
to
(TN^{2/3},L^{N}_{i}(TN^{2/3}))
.

Remark 6.11
.

Note here that we have written certain quantities, such as
\pm TN^{2/3}
, without floors, though they are meant to be integers to be completely precise. Here as in the rest of the paper we will often drop the requisite floor symbols to ease the notation, since this will not affect our arguments.

The control we need on the partition function is the following. It is proved in Section
11
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
T\geq 2
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exist
\delta=\delta(k,T,\varepsilon)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Z^{k,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N}(-TN^{2/3}),\bm{L}^{N}(TN^{2/3}),L^{N}_{k+1}}_{N}>\delta\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

The control we need on the base path measure of Bernoulli random walk bridges is as follows. As its proof is quite standard, we defer it to Appendix
B
.

Lemma 6.13
.

There exist
C
and
c>0
such that, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
p\in(-1,0)
,
B
a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(0,0)
to
(n,pn)
, all
0\leq y<z\leq n
, and
M>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x\in[y,z]}|B(x)-B(y)-p(x-y)|>M|z-y|^{1/2}\right)\leq C\exp(-cM^{2}).

At several locations, we will also need to use normal or Brownian bridge approximations for Bernoulli random walk bridges. We will require the Brownian bridges to have an explicit variance coming from the increment of the Bernoulli random walk bridge being approximated. For this we make use of the following Komlós-Major-Tusnády (KMT) coupling result for Bernoulli random walk bridges, which is obtained by combining
[
CD18
, Theorem 4.5]
with the exponential Markov inequality.

Lemma 6.14

(KMT coupling,
[
CD18
, Theorem 4.5]
)
.

Let
p\in(-1,0)
and
\{z_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. For each
N
, let
B^{N}
be a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(0,0)
to
(N,z_{N})
. There exist positive constants
C,c<\infty
(depending on
p
) such that
there is a coupling between
B^{N}
and a Brownian bridge
B^{\mathrm{Br}}
with variance
p(1-p)
from
(0,0)
to
(N,z_{N})
with

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq N}|B^{\mathrm{Br}}(t)-B^{N}(t)|>R\right)\leq C\exp\Bigl(-cR+C(\log N)^{2}+N^{-1}|z_{N}-pN|^{2}\Bigr).

Note that the variance of the Brownian bridge being
p(1-p)
can be understood by the fact that the Bernoulli random walk bridge, if it has the endpoint
(N,z_{N})\approx(N,pN)
, will behave like a Bernoulli random walk with parameter
p
, whose increments indeed have variance
p(1-p)
.

Assuming Proposition
6.12
, we can give the following corollary on the modulus of continuity of
L^{N}_{k}
. Recall from Section
3.3.4
that we view
L^{N}_{k}
as a
\mathbb{R}
-valued function via linear interpolation.

Corollary 6.15
.

Let
T\geq 2
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. For any
\varepsilon>0
and
\rho>0
, there exist
\delta=\delta(k,T,\varepsilon,\rho)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon,\rho)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x|,|y|\leq TN^{2/3},|x-y|\leq\delta N^{2/3}}|L^{N}_{k}(x)-L^{N}_{k}(y)-p(x-y)|\geq\rho N^{1/3}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Remark 6.16
.

Before giving the proof of Corollary
6.15
, we point out a notational practice that will be used many times throughout the rest of the paper: we will often introduce events (e.g.,
\mathsf{A}(X)
) whose definition involves an abstract
\mathbb{R}
-valued process
X
and then in the course of the proof write different processes (e.g.,
L^{N}_{k}
) in place of
X
to mean the event whose definition is that of
\mathsf{A}(X)
with the process in question in place of
X
. In spite of a random process being part of the notation for the event, the event itself is fixed, i.e., does not depend on the instantiation of the process. This convention will be convenient because when applying the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property we will often be working with events whose only difference is that a collection of Bernoulli random walk bridges is in place of a collection of curves of the line ensemble
\bm{L}^{N}
.

Proof of Corollary
6.15
.

For a random process
X:[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]\to\mathbb{R}
, define

\mathsf{ModCon}_{N}(\delta,\rho,X):=\left\{\sup_{|x|,|y|\leq TN^{2/3},|x-y|\leq\delta N^{2/3}}|X(x)-X(y)-p(x-y)|\geq\rho N^{1/3}\right\}.

So
\mathsf{ModCon}_{N}(\delta,\rho,L^{N}_{k})
is the event whose probability we are trying to upper bound. Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k,[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}],\bm{L}^{N})
,

\displaystyle Z_{N}:=Z^{k,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N}(-TN^{2/3}),\bm{L}^{N}(TN^{2/3}),L^{N}_{k+1}}_{N},

and
\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M)
be the event defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M):=\left\{Z_{N}>\delta^{\prime}\right\}\cap\left\{|L^{N}_{1}(\pm TN^{2/3})|,|L^{N}_{k}(\pm TN^{2/3})|\leq MN^{1/3}\right\};

we pick
\delta^{\prime}
and
M
such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M))\geq 1-\varepsilon
, which is possible by Proposition
6.12
, one-point tightness of
L^{N}_{1}
from Assumption
2
, and Theorem
6.10
. Note that this event is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.

Now by using this bound on
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M))
, the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property, and the fact that
W\leq 1
(
W
being from Definition
3.15
), the probability we are bounding equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\mathsf{ModCon}_{N}(\delta,\rho,L^{N}_{k})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\mathsf{ModCon}_{N}(\delta,\rho,L^{N}_{k})\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M)}\right]+\varepsilon

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{N}^{-1}\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{ModCon}_{N}(\delta,\rho,B_{k})}W\left(\bm{B},L^{N}_{k+1}\right)\right]\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M)}\right]+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{N}^{-1}\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\mathsf{ModCon}_{N}(\delta,\rho,B_{k})\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M)}\right]+\varepsilon,

where
\bm{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k})
is a collection of
k
independent Bernoulli random walk bridges with
B_{i}
’s endpoints given by
L^{N}_{i}(-TN^{2/3})
and
L^{N}_{i}(TN^{2/3})
. Since we have a lower bound of
\delta^{\prime}
on
Z_{N}
on the event
\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M)
, we obtain that the righthand side of the previous display is upper bounded by

\displaystyle(\delta^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\mathsf{ModCon}_{N}(\delta,\rho,B_{k})\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{Fav}(\delta^{\prime},M)}\right]+\varepsilon.

By the modulus of continuity estimates for Bernoulli random walks from Lemma
6.13
, the probability inside the expectation in the previous line can be made smaller than
\delta^{\prime}\varepsilon
for an appropriately small choice of
\delta
. Relabeling
\varepsilon
completes the proof.
∎

Assuming Theorem
6.10
on the lower tail and Proposition
6.12
(therefore Corollary
6.15
), we may quickly give the proof of tightness.

Proof of Theorem
3.18
.

Recall that
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
is defined by (
3.13
).
By an easy extension of
[
Bil13
, Theorem 7.3]
(see also
[
DFF+21
, Lemma 2.4]
), to show tightness of
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{N}
it suffices to show, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

\lim_{R\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{k}(0)|\geq R\right)=0
and

(2)

For all
\varepsilon>0
and
M\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x|,|y|\leq M,|x-y|\leq\delta}|\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{k}(x)-\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{k}(y)|\geq\varepsilon\right)=0.

The first item is immediate from Theorem
6.10
and Assumption
2
, using the ordering of the curves, while (2) follows from Corollary
6.15
.
∎

7.
Airy sheet and tightness of S6V sheet

In this section we address tightness and convergence to the Airy sheet of the colored height functions of our models. As in Section
5
, we restrict to packed boundary or initial conditions.

7.1.
Convergence to the Airy sheet

We will give a single proof of Theorems
2.8
and
2.20
using a unified notation for the objects arising from both S6V and ASEP, as follows. We will use
\bm{L}^{(j),N}
for line ensembles corresponding to different colors in the two models. The S6V case will correspond to identifying
\bm{L}^{(j),N}
with
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}
, and the ASEP case to identifying the same with
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}
, as defined in (
6.1
) and (
6.3
), and in both cases under the identification of
N
with
\varepsilon^{-1}
. Next recall the scaling operator
T_{p,\lambda,N}
from (
3.11
). For a sequence (indexed by
N
) of collections of line ensembles
\{\bm{L}^{(j),N}\}_{j}
such that
\smash{\bm{L}^{(1),N}}
satisfies Assumptions
1
,
2
(with parameters
p\in(-1,0)
and
\lambda>0
), and
3
, we define the rescaled line ensemble
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(j),N}
by

\displaystyle\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(j),N}=T_{p,\lambda,N}(\bm{L}^{(j),N});

we will use this definition and assume that
\bm{L}^{(1)}
satisfies Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
throughout this section without further comment. Note that Theorem
3.19
implies
\smash{\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bm{\mathcal{P}}}
, the parabolic Airy line ensemble (Definition
2.1
).

Recall that the top lines of the line ensembles
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}
and
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}
correspond to the height functions of arrows or particles of color above a threshold determined by
j
(the threshold being
j
for S6V and
-j
for ASEP), and that varying
j
under the colored coupling of the underlying models corresponds to varying the location of the start of the step initial condition.
To encode the line ensembles corresponding to the step initial condition started, in rescaled coordinates, at
x
in a notationally unified way, we define the following. Recalling
\beta
from (
3.12
), let

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{k}(x;y):=\mathfrak{L}^{(\lfloor\beta xN^{2/3}\rfloor),N}_{k}(y).

Next we define
\mathcal{S}^{N}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
by

(7.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y):=\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{1}(x;y)

for
(x,y)\in[-N^{1/6},N^{1/6}]^{2}
and in an arbitrary way such that the resulting function is continuous for all
(x,y)\not\in[-N^{1/6},N^{1/6}]^{2}
; the latter will not affect our limit statements since we work in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.
This definition encompasses the S6V and ASEP cases by taking
(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)
to equal
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
and
(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}(-x;-y)
respectively (as defined in (
2.20
) and (
2.8
)), with
N=\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
. That this is consistent with the definitions of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
and
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}
(and the analogs for ASEP) follows from the definitions (
6.1
) for S6V and (
6.3
) for ASEP, along with Proposition
3.12
(relating the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble and the S6V height functions).

We also assume the following stationarity property for all
z
fixed, where
g_{N}\leq N
is a fixed sequence with
g_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
: as processes in
x,y
over the domain defined by
x,y,x+z,y+z\in[-g_{N},g_{N}]
,

(7.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}^{N}(x+z;y+z);

that this property holds for the S6V and ASEP cases follows for S6V from (
5.1
) (and the presence of the
+j
term in the definition (
6.1
)) along with (
6.2
), and for ASEP from (
5.4
