) and (
6.4
).

We will soon need to invoke Theorem
3.20
on the approximate representation of
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL}(j)}
in terms of a last passage percolation problem in
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)}
. To avoid a notational clash with the symbol
N
indexing the sequence of line ensembles
\bm{L}^{N}
, in this section we will use

\mathtt{N}

for the parameter implicit in
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}
from Definition
3.7
(and thus also the parameter defining the domain of the colored S6V model from Section
2.3
). We will also make the assumption that there exists
D>0
such that

(7.4)

\displaystyle\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}\leq N^{D},

which we will later verify holds in our cases of interest.

The following is a convergence statement for
\mathcal{S}^{N}
(i.e., S6V and ASEP simultaneously); it will be proved in Section
7.2
.

Theorem 7.1
.

Assume that
\bm{L}^{(1),N}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
, and is such that
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\bm{\mathcal{P}}
as
N\to\infty
, in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
, and that (
7.3
) and (
7.4
) hold.
Further assume (
3.14
) holds with
\bm{L}^{(1),N},\bm{L}^{(j),N}
in place of
\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(1)},\bm{L}^{\mathrm{cHL},(j)}
, respectively. Then
\mathcal{S}^{N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets as
N\to\infty
.

We now prove Theorems
2.8
and
2.20
.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

We take
\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)=\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}(-x;-y)
as processes in
(x,y)
with
N=\varepsilon^{-1}
, and note from (
5.2
) that

\mathtt{N}

in this case equals
\lfloor(2\gamma^{-1})^{1+\theta}\varepsilon^{-(1+\theta)}\rfloor=\lfloor(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})^{41}\rfloor
; so (
7.4
) holds. Analogously we take
\bm{L}^{(j),N}=\bm{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}
. Now,
\bm{L}^{(1),N}
satisfies Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
3
by Lemma
6.9
, so, by Theorem
3.19
,
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{\to}}\bm{\mathcal{P}}
. Next, (
7.3
) holds due to (
5.4
), and (
3.14
) holds due to Theorem
3.20
along with (
6.3
).
Thus Theorem
7.1
yields that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}(x;y)\to\mathcal{S}(-x;-y)
in distribution as continuous processes on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. By Lemma
5.2
, we then obtain that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x;y)\to\mathcal{S}(-x;-y)
in the same topology as
\varepsilon\to 0
as well. That
\smash{\mathcal{S}(-x;-y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(x;y)}
by
[
DV21a
, Proposition 1.23]
as processes completes the proof of Theorem
2.8
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.20
.

Recall that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
is defined in terms of a colored S6V model defined on
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}},\infty\Rparen
. Let
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
be the same with
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}=\varepsilon^{-2}
if the original
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}>\varepsilon^{-2}
, and let
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}=\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
otherwise; let
\tilde{\bm{L}}^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}
be the associated colored line ensemble from (
6.1
). We claim that there is a coupling of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
such that the two agree on
[-\varepsilon^{-1/6},\varepsilon^{-1/6}]^{2}
with probability
1-o(1)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Assuming this claim, it is sufficient to prove that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and this will follow from Theorem
7.1
with
N=\varepsilon^{-1}
,
\mathcal{S}^{N}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
, and
\bm{L}^{(j),N}=\tilde{\bm{L}}^{\mathrm{S6V},(j),\varepsilon}
once its assumptions are verified. Now,
\bm{L}^{(1),N}
satisfies Assumption
1
,
2
, and
3
by Lemma
6.8
, so, by Theorem
3.19
,
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{\to}}\bm{\mathcal{P}}
. The remaining assumptions of Theorem
7.1
are verified since the conditions (
7.3
), (
7.4
), and (
3.14
) are satisfied by (
5.1
), by (
6.2
), and by combining Theorem
3.20
with (
6.1
), respectively.

The claim we made on the coupling between
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
follows immediately from
[
AB24
, Lemma B.3]
, which is a finite speed of propagation estimate: roughly speaking, it says that two colored S6V models which have boundary conditions agreeing on a large interval have a coupling such that the arrow configurations agree in a large region. In our case, the boundary conditions of the two systems agree on
\llbracket-\lfloor\varepsilon^{-2}\rfloor,\lfloor\varepsilon^{-2}\rfloor\rrbracket
and we require the arrow configurations to agree on
\{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor\}\times\llbracket\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}-2\varepsilon^{-5/6}\rfloor,\lfloor\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}+2\varepsilon^{-5/6}\rfloor\rrbracket
.
[
AB24
, Lemma B.3]
yields that there is a coupling such that this holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\varepsilon^{-2})
for some
c>0
(take
U,V,T,K
there to respectively be
-\lfloor\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{-2}\rfloor
,
\lfloor\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{-2}\rfloor
,
\lfloor R\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
, and
\lfloor\delta R^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}\rfloor
for some large constant
R
and small constant
\delta>0
). This completes the proof of Theorem
2.20
.
∎

In the next section we begin setting up some statements needed for the proof of Theorem
7.1
before giving the proof at the end of the section.

7.2.
Setup for Airy sheet convergence

For a sequence of random variables
\{X_{N,k}\}_{N,k=1}^{\infty}
defined on a common probability space, we adopt the notation

(7.5)

X_{N,k}=\mathfrak{o}(r_{N,k})\quad\text{if}\quad\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(|X_{N,k}|\geq\varepsilon r_{N,k})<\infty

for every
\varepsilon>0
, i.e.,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}X_{N,k}/r_{N,k}=0
, where the inner limit should be understood as any weak limit of
\{X_{N,k}/r_{N,k}\}_{N}
and the outer limit as an almost sure one, by the Borel-Cantelli lemma. The notation also makes sense if
X_{N,k}=X_{N}
and
r_{N,k}=r_{N}
for all
k
for some sequences
X_{N}
,
r_{N}
such that
X_{N}/r_{N}\to 0
almost surely as
N\to\infty
. Also,
X_{N,k}\geq\mathfrak{o}(r_{N,k})
means that
X_{N,k}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{N,k}\leq 0}=\mathfrak{o}(r_{N,k})
, and
X_{N,k}\leq\mathfrak{o}(r_{N,k})
means
X_{N,k}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{N,k}\geq 0}=\mathfrak{o}(r_{N,k})
.

Next recall the notation (
2.2
) for last passage percolation (LPP) values.
In the notation (
7.1
) and for
\delta>0
, consider (
3.14
) of Theorem
3.20
, as assumed in Theorem
7.1
, with
k
set to
N^{1/6}
and
m=\sigma\delta N^{1/3}
with
\sigma
as in (
3.12
). A union bound over
x
implies that, for
\delta>K(q)N^{-1/3}\log\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}
(which, under (
7.4
), holds if
\delta>DK(q)N^{-1/3}\log N
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x,|y|\in(0,g_{N}]}\left|\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)-\max_{w\leq y}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{N^{1/6}}(x;w)+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(w,N^{1/6}-1)\to(y,1)]\right)\right|\geq\delta N^{1/6}\right)

(7.6)

\displaystyle\leq CN^{\frac{7}{6}}q^{c\sigma^{2}\delta^{2}N^{2/3}}.

Now set
\delta=N^{-1/4}
and note that
N^{-1/11}\gg\delta N^{1/6}=N^{-1/12}
.
For fixed
q\in[0,1)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, (
7.6
) implies that for all
x,|y|\in(0,g_{N}]
and
N\geq N_{0}
, with
\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11})
a random quantity satisfying (
7.5
) whose distribution does not depend on
x
or
y
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)

\displaystyle=\max_{w\leq y}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{N^{1/6}}(x;w)+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(w,N^{1/6}-1)\to(y,1)]\right)+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11}),

\displaystyle=\max_{w\leq z\leq y}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{N^{1/6}}(x;w)+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(w,N^{1/6}-1)\to(z,k)]+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]\right)

(7.7)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11}),

where in the second line we used the fact that the LPP problem is a variational problem to split it up at line
k
(see for example
[
DOV22
, Lemma 3.2]
).

Let
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y)
be the maximizer (of the maximum over
z
) in (
7.7
) (the largest one in the event that there are multiple). The following records control on the location of
Z^{N}_{k}
which will be necessary for the proofs of Theorem
2.8
and
2.20
. Because its proof is similar to
[
DOV22
, Lemma 7.1]
, we defer it to Appendix
C
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
7.1
, it holds that, for any
x>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle Z^{N}_{k}(x,y)=-\sqrt{\frac{k}{2x}}+\mathfrak{o}(k^{1/2}).

Moreover,
\{Z^{N}_{k}(x,y)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight for each fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x>0
, and
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

To prove Theorem
7.1
, we also need to know that
\mathcal{S}^{N}
forms a tight sequence in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
, recorded in the following proposition. It will be proved in Section
7.3
, where we will establish the necessary modulus of continuity estimates.

Proposition 7.3
.

Under the hypotheses of Theorem
7.1
,
\{\mathcal{S}^{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is tight in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
under the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.

The following monotonicity statement on differences of LPP values will be useful in the proof of Theorem
7.1
and has been proved many times in the literature; see for example
[
DOV22
, Proposition 3.8]
,
[
GH23
, Lemma 2.10]
, or
[
BBS20
, Lemma B.2]
.

Lemma 7.4

(Crossing lemma,
[
DOV22
,
GH23
,
BBS20
]
)
.

Let
\bm{f}=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k})
be a sequence of continuous functions with
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then, for any
y_{1}<y_{2}

\displaystyle z\mapsto f[(z,k)\to(y_{1},1)]-f[(z,k)\to(y_{2},1)]

is non-increasing.

Now we may give the proof of Theorem
7.1
; given all the machinery developed by now, its proof is quite similar to that of
[
DV21a
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Proof of Theorem
7.1
.

We know from Proposition
7.3
that
\mathcal{S}^{N}
is tight as a law on
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets. So we only need to show that all subsequential limits equal
\mathcal{S}
, equivalently, that they satisfy (i) and (ii) in Definition
2.3
.

That (i) holds follows from (
7.3
). We will next show that (ii) holds for any subsequential limit of
\mathcal{S}^{N}
.
Recall from (
7.7
) that, for all
x\in[0,g_{N}]
,
y_{i}\in[-g_{N},g_{N}]
simultaneously, with
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{i})

\displaystyle=\sup_{w\leq z\leq y_{i}}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{N^{1/6}}(x;w)+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(w,N^{1/6}-1)\to(z,k)]+\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}[(z,k)\to(y_{i},1)]\right)

(7.8)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11})

holds. We assume in what follows that
y_{1}<y_{2}
, without loss of generality.

From Proposition
7.2
, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x>0
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{1})
and
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2})
which are tight sequences in
N
(for each fixed
k
,
x
, and
y
) such that the above supremum in
z
is attained at
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{i})
.

Consider the tight collection of random objects

(7.9)

\displaystyle\left\{\mathcal{S}^{N}\right\}_{N=1}^{\infty},\{\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty},\left\{Z^{N}_{k}(x,y):k\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0},y\in\mathbb{Q}\right\}_{N=1}^{\infty};

for the first collection, each member is an element of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
endowed with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets; for the second collection, each member is an element of
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R})
again endowed with the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets; and for the third collection, each member is a function
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{R}
, i.e., an element of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{Q}_{>0}\times\mathbb{Q}}
. For this last space we have the usual topology generated by cylinders, which corresponds to convergence of all finite collections.

As we noted above, tightness for
\mathcal{S}^{N}
is a consequence of Proposition
7.3
. We have assumed tightness for
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}
and that it weakly converges to
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
, the parabolic Airy line ensemble. Finally, tightness of the final collection in the previous display follows from the tightness of
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y)
for fixed
k
,
x
, and
y
from Proposition
7.2
(since the topology is characterized by convergence of finite collections).

As a result, we have tightness of all the collections in (
7.9
) jointly, which means we can extract a subsequence along which all four collections converge. We assume for notational convenience that the subsequence is the whole sequence, i.e., we will continue labeling the sequence by
N
. We denote the limiting objects by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}},\bm{\mathcal{P}},\left\{Z_{k}(x,y):k\in\mathbb{N},x\in\mathbb{Q}_{>0},y\in\mathbb{Q}\right\},

where
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
is the parabolic Airy line ensemble, as noted above.
That
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(0;\bm{\cdot})=\mathcal{P}_{1}(\bm{\cdot})
follows immediately from the definition (
7.2
) of
\smash{\mathcal{S}^{N}(0;\bm{\cdot})}
, so the first part of (ii) of Definition
2.3
is verified.
Next recall from Proposition
7.2
that, almost surely,

(7.10)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{Z_{k}(x,y_{i})}{\sqrt{k}}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2x}}.

Returning to (
7.8
) and by the definition of
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2})
, we see that, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(7.11)

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{2})&=\sup_{w\leq Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2})}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{N^{1/6}}(x;w)+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(w,N^{1/6}-1)\to(Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)]\right)\\
&\qquad+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)\to(y_{2},1)]+\mathfrak{o}(1)\\
\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{1})&\geq\sup_{w\leq Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2})}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{N^{1/6}}(x;w)+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(w,N^{1/6}-1)\to(Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)]\right)\\
&\qquad+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)\to(y_{1},1)]+\mathfrak{o}(1).\end{split}

Subtracting the first from the second then yields that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}\left[(Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)\to(y_{1},1)\right]-\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}\left[(Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)\to(y_{2},1)\right]

(7.12)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathfrak{o}(1).

By the convergence of
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2})
to
Z_{k}(x,y_{2})
and the uniform convergence on compact sets of
\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}
to
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
, taking
N\to\infty
yields

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{1})-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{2})\geq\bm{\mathcal{P}}\left[(Z_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)\to(y_{1},1)\right]-\bm{\mathcal{P}}\left[(Z_{k}(x,y_{2}),k)\to(y_{2},1)\right].

Now, we know from the crossing lemma (Lemma
7.4
) and since
y_{1}<y_{2}
that the righthand side is non-increasing in the argument which equals
Z_{k}(x,y_{2})
; we will use this to replace
Z_{k}(x,y_{2})
by approximately
-\sqrt{k/(2x)}
. In more detail, fix
\varepsilon>0
. We know from (
7.10
) that for all large enough
k
(depending on
\varepsilon
),

(7.13)

\displaystyle Z_{k}(x,y_{2})\leq-\sqrt{\frac{k}{2(x+\varepsilon)}}.

Thus it follows that for all such
k
,

(7.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{1})-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{2})\geq\bm{\mathcal{P}}\left[(\sqrt{k/(2(x+\varepsilon))},k)\to(y_{1},1)\right]-\bm{\mathcal{P}}\left[(\sqrt{k/(2(x+\varepsilon))},k)\to(y_{2},1)\right].

Now taking
k\to\infty
in the righthand side and recalling the definition (
2.3
) of
\mathcal{S}
increments yields that, for every
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{1})-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{2})\geq\mathcal{S}(x+\varepsilon,y_{1})-\mathcal{S}(x+\varepsilon,y_{2}).

Taking
\varepsilon\to 0
and invoking the continuity of
\mathcal{S}
yields
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{1})-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{2})\geq\mathcal{S}(x;y_{1})-\mathcal{S}(x;y_{2})
. (Note that it is only in this step that we crucially rely on the fact that
\mathcal{S}
exists, has the coupling with
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
, and is continuous; without these facts, it would not be possible to take
\varepsilon\to 0
in (
7.14
) directly to obtain the same with
\varepsilon=0
, as one first has to take
k\to\infty
, at which point continuity is not
a priori
available.)

An analogous argument using
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{1})
in place of
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{2})
will give (
7.11
) with an equality for
\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{1})
and a lower bound for
\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{2})
(with
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y_{1})
on the righthand side for both); then we obtain a lower bound for
\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{2})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{1})
analogous to (
7.12
). Since
y_{1}<y_{2}
, a lower bound on
Z_{k}(x,y_{1})
in place of (
7.13
), again invoking Lemma
7.4
, and taking the limit
N\to\infty
and then
\varepsilon\to 0
as above yields
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{1})-\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(x;y_{2})\leq\mathcal{S}(x;y_{1})-\mathcal{S}(x;y_{2})
. This completes the proof after noting
\mathcal{S}
itself enjoys the desired coupling with
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
, which yields it for
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
as well.
∎

7.3.
Tightness of
\mathcal{S}^{N}

In this section we show tightness of
\mathcal{S}^{N}
in
\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})
, i.e., prove Proposition
7.3
; recall that
\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)
is just shorthand for the objects
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x;y)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}(-x;-y)
, as processes.
To prove tightness of the process, it is sufficient to prove one-point tightness and uniform-in-
N
modulus of continuity estimates.
The basic idea is to reduce the modulus of continuity estimates for
\mathcal{S}^{N}
into the same where one argument of
\mathcal{S}^{N}
is fixed; then, we can make use of modulus of continuity estimates for line ensembles.

The first ingredient for this is the following symmetrization lemma, which follows as a consequence of general shift-invariance results for colored vertex models
[
BGW22
,
Gal21
]
.

0
y
x
z_{-4}
z_{-3}
z_{-2}
z_{-1}
z_{0}
z_{1}
z_{2}
z_{3}
z_{4}
0
-y
-x
z_{-4}
z_{-3}
z_{-2}
z_{-1}
z_{0}
z_{1}
z_{2}
z_{3}
z_{4}

Figure 16
.
A depiction of the transformation of the domain of the colored S6V model used in the proof of Lemma
7.5
. The
z_{i}
labels at the left side indicate the spectral parameter for the colored S6V weights on the corresponding row.

Lemma 7.5
.

It holds that
\mathcal{S}^{N}(y;x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{\mathcal{S}}^{N}(-x;-y)
as processes in
(x,y)
.

Proof.

Recall that

\mathtt{N}

is the parameter controlling the dimension of the domain of the colored S6V model. To establish the lemma, it suffices to show, for fixed
K\in\llbracket-\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}},\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}\rrbracket
and
t\in\mathbb{N}
, that

(7.15)

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(y,0;K+x,t)+y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;K-y,t)-x

jointly across all
x,y
such that
K+x,K-y\in\llbracket-\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}},\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}\rrbracket
, as
\mathcal{S}^{N}(y;x)
and
\mathcal{S}^{N}(-x;-y)
are scaled and shifted versions of the lefthand and righthand sides, respectively, in both the cases of ASEP and S6V (recall (
7.2
), (
2.20
), (
5.7
)). It further suffices to establish the case of
K=0
; for combining the
(K,x,y)\mapsto(0,K+x,y)
case of (
7.15
) with (
5.1
) yields

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(y,0;K+x,t)+y\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-K-x,0;-y,t)-K-x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;K-y,t)-x.

We show the
K=0
case of (
7.15
) now. First, in the case of
x<y
, the height functions are trivially equal:
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(y,0;x,t)=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}-y+1
and
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;-y,t)=\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}+x+1
, since in both cases the lefthand sides are just the total number of arrows of the corresponding colors in the system.

So we may assume
x\geq y
. We now invoke
[
Gal21
, Theorem 1.6]
; let us explain the special case of its content that we need briefly now. Consider two colored S6V models on
\llbracket 1,t\rrbracket\times\llbracket-\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}},\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}\rrbracket
, with spectral parameters
z_{i}
at row
i
in the first and
z_{\tau(i)}
at row
i
in the second, where
\tau:\llbracket-\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}},\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}\rrbracket\to\llbracket-\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}},\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}\rrbracket
is a permutation; see Figure
16
. In more detail, the measure is defined by setting the vertex weights (as in Figure
8
) of vertices in row
i
with spectral parameter
z_{i}
(or
z_{\tau(i)}
in place of
z
, and the probability of a particular arrow configuration is the product of the vertex weights.

Then
[
Gal21
, Theorem 1.6]
yields the following distributional equality. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
x_{i},x_{i}^{\prime},y_{i},y_{i}^{\prime}\in\llbracket-\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}},\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{N}$}}\rrbracket
for
i=1,\ldots,k
with
x_{i}\geq y_{i}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}\geq y_{i}^{\prime}
, and under the additional condition that
\{z_{j}:y_{i}\leq j\leq x_{i}\}=\{z_{\tau(j)}:y_{i}^{\prime}\leq j\leq x_{i}^{\prime}\}
(as multisets) for each
i
. Then,

(7.16)

\begin{split}&\left(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{1}(y_{1},0;x_{1},t)+y_{1},\ldots,h^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{1}(y_{k},0;x_{k},t)+y_{k}\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\left(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{2}(y_{1}^{\prime},0;x_{1}^{\prime},t)+y_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,h^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{2}(y_{k}^{\prime},0;x_{k}^{\prime},t)+y_{k}^{\prime}\right),\end{split}

where the subscript corresponds to whether the statistic is in the first or second colored S6V model (that is, left or right in Figure
16
) just described. (in the notation of
[
Gal21
]
, Theorem 1.6 there does not include the
+y_{i}
or
+y_{i}^{\prime}
terms above; this is because the height function considered in
[
Gal21
]
counts the number of colored arrows below a given height and not above as in our setting. Translating from one to the other introduces the mentioned terms.)

For our situation, let
\tau(i)=-i
, i.e., we reverse the order of the formal spectral parameters, and for any choice of
x_{i}\geq y_{i}
, define
x_{i}^{\prime}=-y_{i}
and
y_{i}^{\prime}=-x_{i}
. Then
x_{i}^{\prime}\geq y_{i}^{\prime}
and clearly it holds that
\{z_{\tau(j)}:y_{i}^{\prime}\leq j\leq x_{i}^{\prime}\}=\{z_{j}:y_{i}\leq j\leq x_{i}\}
. Applying the distributional equality of (
7.16
) and specializing to the case that all spectral parameters are equal yields that

\displaystyle\left(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(y,0;x,t)+y\right)_{x,y\in\llbracket-\mathtt{N},\mathtt{N}\rrbracket,x\geq y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\left(h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(-x,0;-y,t)-x\right)_{x,y\in\llbracket-\mathtt{N},\mathtt{N}\rrbracket,x\geq y},

which yields the
K=0
case of (
7.15
) and completes the proof.
∎

A second ingredient to prove the modulus of continuity estimates is the following monotonicity statement, sometimes called a quadrangle inequality, which will allow us to reduce the supremum over two arguments present in the modulus of continuity to a supremum over a single one; it is the analog of the crossing lemma (Lemma
7.4
) for S6V and ASEP. In the context of last passage percolation models, such quadrangle inequalities have previously been used in similar ways (e.g.,
[
DV21a
]
) as well as for other purposes (e.g.,
[
BGH21
,
BBS20
,
BSS24
,
GH23
,
GHZ23
]
).

Lemma 7.6

(Quadrangle inequality)
.

For any
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
and
y_{1}\leq y_{2}
,

\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};y_{1})+\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};y_{2})\geq\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};y_{2})+\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};y_{1}).

Proof.

Recall from (
2.17
) the definition
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y,t)=\#\left\{k>y:j_{(t,k)}\geq x\right\}
. It suffices from the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{N}
in the cases of ASEP (
5.7
) and S6V (
2.20
) in terms of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
to prove that
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x_{1},0;y_{1},t)+h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x_{2},0;y_{2},t)\geq h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x_{1},0;y_{2},t)+h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x_{2},0;y_{1},t)
for any
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
,
y_{1}\leq y_{2}
,
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
To that end, observe that for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
y_{1}\leq y_{2}
,

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y_{1},t)-h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;y_{2},t)

\displaystyle=\#\bigl\{k\in\llbracket y_{1}+1,y_{2}\rrbracket:j_{(t,k)}\geq x\bigr\},

which is clearly non-increasing in
x
, as was to be shown.
∎

With these ingredients we give the proof of Proposition
7.3
.

Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

We need to prove one-point tightness of
\mathcal{S}^{N}
as well as establish uniform-in-
N
modulus of continuity estimates. The tightness of
\{\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
for fixed
x,y
follows immediately from the stationarity of
\mathcal{S}^{N}
(recall (
7.3
)) and Assumption
2
. For uniform-in-
N
modulus of continuity, we first observe that, from Lemma
7.6
, for any
x,y\in[-K,K]
and
x_{1}\leq x_{2}
and
y_{1}\leq y_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{N}(K;y_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(K;y_{2})\leq\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y_{2})\leq\mathcal{S}^{N}(-K;y_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(-K;y_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};K)-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};K)\leq\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};y)-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};y)\leq\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};-K)-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};-K).

In particular, we see that, for any
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in[-K,K]
,

\displaystyle|\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};y_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};y_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};y_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};y_{2})|+|\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};y_{2})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};y_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{z\in\{+K,-K\}}|\mathcal{S}^{N}(z;y_{1})-\mathcal{S}^{N}(z;y_{2})|+\max_{z\in\{+K,-K\}}|\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{1};z)-\mathcal{S}^{N}(x_{2};z)|.

By Lemma
7.5
the marginal distribution of the second term is the same as that of the first with
x_{i}
in place of
y_{i}
. Finally, the modulus of continuity over a single argument, i.e., of
y\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)
for
x
fixed, is the same as that of the top line of a rescaled line ensemble (see (
7.2
)) and therefore follows from Corollary
6.15
. So by Corollary
6.15
and a union bound, for any
\rho>0
and
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
such that, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
, whenever
|y_{1}-y_{2}|\leq\delta
and
|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leq\delta
, the previous display is upper bounded by
\rho
. This completes the proof.
∎

8.
Weak monotonicity statements for one path

As indicated in Section
3.3.3
, a technical challenge in proving tightness for Hall-Littlewood Gibbs line ensembles is that the Gibbs property does not enjoy a form of stochastic monotonicity that previous proofs of tightness of prelimiting line ensembles have relied heavily upon.

This was a difficulty faced in
[
CD18
]
as well, and is one of the reasons the analysis there is restricted to the top curve. To get around the lack of monotonicity, they found a weaker form of monotonicity for a single curve with a lower boundary condition that does hold, in terms of a comparison of partition functions up to a constant multiplicative factor. Our analysis will rely on a similar weak monotonicity statement, but with the observation that it holds under slightly more general conditions (Corollary
8.5
), namely in the presence of fixed upper and lower curves that the random curve must avoid, in addition to a possibly different lower boundary curve it interacts with via the weight factor
W
from Definition
3.15
.

In the next section, we introduce some monotonicity statements for basic objects such as non-intersecting Bernoulli random walk bridges. In Section
8.2
we give the weak monotonicity statements.

8.1.
Monotonicity statements for random walks and Gaussian objects

Here we collect some well-known statements of forms of monotonicity enjoyed by objects underlying the Gibbs properties, such as non-intersecting random walks and Gaussian random variables.

The following is a straightforward computation using the form of the density of the normal distribution, and we point the reader to its proof in
[
CHH23
]
. Recall the notation
\mathcal{N}(m,\sigma^{2})
for the normal distribution with mean
m
and variance
\sigma^{2}
.

Lemma 8.1

(
[
CHH23
, Lemma 5.15]
)
.

Fix
r>0
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\sigma^{2}>0
. Let
X
be distributed as
\mathcal{N}(m,\sigma^{2})
. Then the quantity

\mathbb{P}(X\geq s+r\mid X\geq s)
is a strictly decreasing function of
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

The next lemma quotes a form of stochastic monotonicity in the boundary data of non-intersecting Bernoulli random walk bridges. The result has appeared in the literature a number of times, e.g.,
[
CEP00
, Lemma 18]
or, in notation closer to ours,
[
DFF+21
, Lemma 7.3]
. Recall the definition of a Bernoulli path from Definition
2.9
and that of a Bernoulli random walk bridge from before Definition
3.15
.

Lemma 8.2

(
[
CEP00
,
DFF+21
]
)
.

Fix an interval
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
. Let
\vec{x}^{*},\vec{y}^{*}\in\mathbb{Z}^{k}
be such that
x^{*}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq x^{*}_{k}
and
y^{*}_{1}\geq\ldots\geq y^{*}_{k}
for
*\in\{\shortuparrow,\downarrow\}
. Let
f^{*},g^{*}:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
be Bernoulli paths such that
f^{*}(a)\geq x^{*}_{1}
,
f^{*}(b)\geq y^{*}_{1}
,
g^{*}(a)\leq x^{*}_{k}
,
g^{*}(b)\leq y^{*}_{k}
for
*\in\{\shortuparrow,\downarrow\}
. Suppose
f^{\shortuparrow}(x)\geq f^{\downarrow}(x)
,
g^{\shortuparrow}(x)\geq g^{\downarrow}(x)
for all
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
and
x^{\shortuparrow}_{i}\geq x^{\downarrow}_{i}
,
y^{\shortuparrow}_{i}\geq y^{\downarrow}_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Let
\bm{B}^{*}=(B^{*}_{1},\ldots,B^{*}_{k})
be a collection of independent Bernoulli random walk bridges, with
B^{*}_{i}
from
(a,x^{*}_{i})
to
(b,y^{*}_{i})
, conditioned on
g^{*}(x)\leq B^{*}_{k}(x)\leq\ldots\leq B^{*}_{1}(x)\leq f^{*}(x)
for all
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
and for
*\in\{\shortuparrow,\downarrow\}
. Then there is a coupling between
\bm{B}^{\shortuparrow}
and
\bm{B}^{\downarrow}
such that

\displaystyle B^{\shortuparrow}_{i}(x)\geq B^{\downarrow}_{i}(x)

for all
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
and
i=1,\ldots,k
.

8.2.
Weak monotonicity

We start with a deterministic lemma about the weight of certain pairs of ordered paths with the same endpoints constrained to lie above a curve
\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}
. Define

c(q):=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{i})>0.

In the following, we call a down-step an increment of
-1
in a Bernoulli path and a flat step an increment of
0
. Flipping a down-step to a flat-step (or vice versa) means keeping the left endpoint the same and changing the increment from
-1
to
0
(or from
0
to
-1
) and keeping all other increments the same. We also recall the definition of the weight factor
W
and the notational device
W_{\mathrm{low}}
from (
3.9
).

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a fixed interval and
\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
be a Bernoulli path. Let
\ell_{\downarrow},\ell_{\shortuparrow}:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
be Bernoulli paths with the same endpoints satisfying the property that there exist
t\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
and
k\leq\min(t-a,b-t)
such that flipping
k
down-steps in
\llbracket a,t\rrbracket
in
\ell_{\downarrow}
to flat-steps and
k
flat-steps in
\llbracket t,b\rrbracket
in
\ell_{\downarrow}
to down-steps yields
\ell_{\shortuparrow}
. Then

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{low}}(\ell_{\downarrow},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})\leq c(q)^{-1}\cdot W_{\mathrm{low}}(\ell_{\shortuparrow},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}).

While the hypotheses on
\ell_{\downarrow}
and
\ell_{\shortuparrow}
imply that
\ell_{\downarrow}\leq\ell_{\shortuparrow}
pointwise, the two are not equivalent. This is because we require all the flat-to-up switches and all the up-to-flat switches to occur on either side of a given location
t
, which is a stronger condition; an example of a pair of paths which are pointwise ordered but do not satisfy this stronger condition is given in Figure
17
.

We also point out the important detail that Lemma
8.3
requires the interaction through
W
to be only of a single path
\ell_{\downarrow}
or
\ell_{\shortuparrow}
, and with only a lower boundary curve; in particular, the comparison of partition functions is not claimed in the presence of an upper boundary.

1

Figure 17
.
The solid blue path is pointwise (weakly) larger than the black path, but the two paths do not satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma
8.3
; to go from black to blue, one needs to switch the black path’s down-steps at positions 1 and 3 to flat-steps, and its flat-steps at positions 2 and 4 to down-steps. The dashed blue path is the changed portion of the solid blue path obtained after flipping the first down-step (at position 2) to a flat-step and the second flat-step (at position 3) to a down-step.

Lemma
8.3
was originally proved in the course of the proof of
[
CD18
, Lemma 4.1]
; we reproduce the proof here.

Proof of Lemma
8.3
.

We assume that
\ell_{\downarrow}(i)\geq\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}(i)
for all
i\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
as otherwise the lefthand side of the inequality to be shown is zero and we are done.
Let
x_{1}<\ldots<x_{k}\leq t
and
t\leq y_{k}<\ldots<y_{1}
be respectively the coordinates of the left endpoints of the down-steps that are flipped to flat-steps and of the flat-steps that are flipped to down-steps in going from
\ell_{\downarrow}
to
\ell_{\shortuparrow}
. Define
\ell_{\downarrow}=\ell_{0},\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}=\ell_{\shortuparrow}
by letting
\ell_{j}
be obtained from
\ell_{\downarrow}
after performing the flips at
x_{1},\ldots,x_{j}
and
y_{1},\ldots,y_{j}
only.

Now,
\ell_{j}
differs from
\ell_{j-1}
only on
\llbracket x_{j}+1,y_{j}\rrbracket
, where the former is raised by 1 compared to the latter. Recall that
W_{\mathrm{low}}(\ell_{i},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})=\prod_{x=a+1}^{b}(1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{i}(x-1)-\Delta_{i}(x)=1}q^{\Delta_{i}(x-1)})
, where
\Delta_{i}(x)=\ell_{i}(x)-\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}(x)
. By examining how each factor of the product changes on going from
W_{\mathrm{low}}(\ell_{j-1},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})
to
W_{\mathrm{low}}(\ell_{j},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})
, it follows that:

\bullet

the factor corresponding to
x=x_{j}+1
changes from
1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{j-1}(x_{j})-\Delta_{j-1}(x_{j}+1)=1}q^{\Delta_{j-1}(x_{j})}
to 1, i.e., it (weakly) increases;

\bullet

the factors corresponding to
x\in\llbracket x_{j}+2,y_{j}\rrbracket
change from
1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{j-1}(x-1)-\Delta_{j-1}(x)=1}q^{\Delta_{j-1}(x-1)}
to
1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{j}(x-1)-\Delta_{j}(x)=1}q^{\Delta_{j}(x-1)}=1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{j-1}(x-1)-\Delta_{j-1}(x)=1}q^{\Delta_{j-1}(x-1)+1}
, i.e., they (weakly) increase;

\bullet

the factor corresponding to
x=y_{j}+1
changes from
1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{j-1}(y_{j})-\Delta_{j-1}(y_{j}+1)=1}q^{\Delta_{j-1}(y_{j})}
to
1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{j}(y_{j})-\Delta_{j}(y_{j}+1)=1}q^{\Delta_{j}(y_{j})}=1-\mathbbm{1}_{\Delta_{j-1}(y_{j})-\Delta_{j-1}(y_{j}+1)=0}q^{\Delta_{j-1}(y_{j})+1}
.

The last change can be a decrease depending on the indicator function, but the decrease is by at most a factor of
1-q^{m_{j}}
, where
m_{j}=1+\min_{x\in\llbracket x_{j}+1,y_{j}\rrbracket}(\ell_{j-1}(x)-\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}(x))
. Thus we have shown that

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{low}}(\ell_{j-1},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})\leq(1-q^{m_{j}})^{-1}W_{\mathrm{low}}(\ell_{j},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}).

Since
\ell_{j}
differs from
\ell_{j-1}
by increasing the path by 1 on
\llbracket x_{j}+1,y_{j}\rrbracket
, we obtain
m_{j}\geq m_{j-1}+1
. This implies the claim.
∎

Corollary 8.4
.

Let
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a fixed interval and
\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
be a Bernoulli path. Suppose
B^{\downarrow}
and
B^{\shortuparrow}
are random Bernoulli paths with respective laws
\mathbb{P}_{1}
and
\mathbb{P}_{2}
which can be coupled such that they almost surely satisfy the hypothesis of Lemma
8.3
(in particular
t
and
k
there may be random). Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\left[W_{\mathrm{low}}(B^{\downarrow},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})\right]\leq c(q)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{2}\left[W_{\mathrm{low}}(B^{\shortuparrow},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})\right].

Proof.

This follows by taking expectations in the inequality of Lemma
8.3
.
∎

The following shows that we have the comparison of partition functions in the case of paths which interact with a lower boundary via the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs weight and additionally avoid some upper and lower curves (the latter may differ from the lower boundary curve). We will use this fact heavily in our arguments as a replacement for the full monotonicity property enjoyed by line ensembles and Gibbs properties previously studied.

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a fixed interval and
\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
be a Bernoulli path. Let
f,g:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
be Bernoulli paths with
g(i)\leq f(i)
for all
i\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
. Let
t\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
,
z\in\mathbb{Z}
,
x\in\llbracket g(a),f(a)\rrbracket
, and
y\in\llbracket g(b),f(b)\rrbracket
be such that, with
B
a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(a,x)
to
(b,y)
(we assume there exists at least one Bernoulli path connecting those two points),

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(g(i)\leq B(i)\leq f(i)\text{ for all }i\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket,\ell(t)\leq z\Bigr)>0.

Let
B^{\downarrow}
and
B^{\shortuparrow}
be distributed as Bernoulli random walk bridges from
(a,x)
to
(b,y)
conditioned to satisfy
g(x)\leq B^{\downarrow}(x)\leq f(x)
and
g(x)\leq B^{\shortuparrow}(x)\leq f(x)
for all
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, with
B^{\downarrow}
additionally conditioned on
B^{\downarrow}(t)\leq z
. Let
\mathbb{E}_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{2}
be the expectation operators associated with their respective laws. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{1}\left[W_{\mathrm{low}}(B^{\downarrow},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})\right]\leq c(q)^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{2}\left[W_{\mathrm{low}}(B^{\shortuparrow},\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})\right].

Before proving Corollary
8.5
, let us give an example showing how it will be used to obtain a comparison of one-point probabilities. Let
\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}
,
f
, and
g
be as in the corollary and fix
t
. Let
B
be a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(a,x)
to
(b,y)
, conditioned to avoid
f
and
g
, and let
L
be the same but reweighted by the Radon-Nikodym derivative
W_{\mathrm{low}}(L,\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})/\mathbb{E}[W_{\mathrm{low}}(L,\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})]
. Then we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(L(t)\leq z\right)=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{B(t)\leq z}W_{\mathrm{low}}(B,\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})]}{\mathbb{E}[W_{\mathrm{low}}(B,\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})]}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}[W_{\mathrm{low}}(B,\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})\mid B(t)\leq z]}{\mathbb{E}[W_{\mathrm{low}}(B,\ell_{\mathrm{bot}})]}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(B(t)\leq z\right)

\displaystyle\leq c(q)^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(B(t)\leq z\right),

the last inequality by Corollary
8.5
. The final probability is simply a non-intersecting Bernoulli random walk bridge, for which tools like monotonicity (Lemma
8.2
) and two-point estimates for the underlying unconditioned Bernoulli random walk bridge (Lemma
6.13
) are available. We emphasize again, however, that while Corollary
8.5
allows the interaction via
W_{\mathrm{low}}
to be with a lower curve
\ell_{\mathrm{bot}}
, it required that there be no upper curve for that interaction (unlike the case with the full weight factor
W
); in applications, when we will have to work with the full factor
W
, we will need to make other context-specific arguments to remove the upper boundary interaction before performing the above argument to get a probability comparison. We also note that the comparison is only of one-point probabilities and is thus not simply the same up to a multiplicative constant as the form of stochastic monotonicity available for non-intersecting Bernoulli random walk bridges (Lemma
8.2
).

Now we turn to the proof of Corollary
8.5
. We will need the following quantitative form of monotonicity for a single Bernoulli random walk bridge conditioned on avoiding an upper and lower boundary curve. A similar statement appeared for (multiple) non-intersecting Brownian bridges in
[
AH23
, Lemma 2.4]
. Its proof consists of a construction of a Markov chain on pairs of paths (respectively with the two boundary conditions) whose dynamics preserve the quantitative ordering and whose stationary distribution has the desired marginals. Similar ideas have appeared many times, e.g.,
[
CH14
,
DFF+21
,
AH23
]
, and thus we defer the proof to Appendix
B
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be a fixed interval and
f,g:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
be Bernoulli paths with
g(i)\leq f(i)
for all
i\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
. Let
x^{\shortuparrow},x^{\downarrow},y^{\shortuparrow},y^{\downarrow}\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
g(a)\leq x^{\downarrow}\leq x^{\shortuparrow}\leq f(a)
and
g(b)\leq y^{\downarrow}\leq y^{\shortuparrow}\leq f(b)
. Let
M=\max(x^{\shortuparrow}-x^{\downarrow},y^{\shortuparrow}-y^{\downarrow})
. Let
B^{*}
be a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(a,x^{*})
to
(b,y^{*})
conditioned on
g(x)\leq B^{*}(x)\leq f(x)
for
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, for
*\in\{\shortuparrow,\downarrow\}
. There exists a coupling of
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
such that, almost surely for all
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle B^{\downarrow}(x)\leq B^{\shortuparrow}(x)\leq B^{\downarrow}(x)+M.

Proof of Corollary
8.5
.

We may assume that
z<f(t)
as otherwise
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
have the same distribution and the claim is trivial. Next we observe that
B^{\shortuparrow}(t)
stochastically dominates
B^{\downarrow}(t)
, using Lemma
8.2
.

As a consequence, it is sufficient to show that a coupling meeting the hypothesis of Lemma
8.3
exists between two Bernoulli random walk bridges
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
from
(a,x)
to
(b,y)
conditioned on avoiding both
f
and
g
with
B^{*}
additionally conditioned on
B^{*}(t)=z^{*}
for
*\in\{\downarrow,\shortuparrow\}
, where
z^{\shortuparrow}\geq z^{\downarrow}
. Indeed, with the latter statement in hand, one can obtain the desired coupling between the original
B^{\downarrow}
and
B^{\shortuparrow}
by first coupling
B^{\shortuparrow}(t)
and
B^{\downarrow}(t)
via the stochastic domination, and then making use of the previous statement which is conditioned on the value of
B^{*}(t)
.

To show the statement we have reduced to, it is sufficient to prove the case where
z^{\shortuparrow}=z^{\downarrow}+1
, as then the full statement follows by iteration. By the Gibbs property for non-intersecting Bernoulli random walk bridges, it is further sufficient to show that there exists a coupling between
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
where the former is now a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(a,x)
to
(t,z)
and the latter is one from
(a,x)
to
(t,z-1)
, both conditioned on avoiding
f
and
g
on
\llbracket a,t\rrbracket
, with the property that
B^{\shortuparrow}
is obtained from
B^{\downarrow}
by flipping one down-step of the latter to a flat-step; a similar proof will then show the analogous statement on
\llbracket t,b\rrbracket
, and one obtains the desired coupling by concatenating the couplings on either side of
t
(which yields the correct marginal distributions by the Gibbs property for non-intersecting Bernoulli random walk bridges).

Finally, we prove the last statement we have reduced to by induction on the interval length
k=t-a
. More explicitly, we establish by induction on
k
that there exists a coupling between two Bernoulli random walk bridges
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
, the first from
(a,x)
to
(t,z)
and the second from
(a,x)
to
(t,z-1)
, each conditioned on avoiding both
f
and
g
, such that
B^{\shortuparrow}
is obtained from
B^{\downarrow}
by flipping a single down-step to a flat-step. Here
z
is assumed to be such that such paths exist.

In the case that
k=1
the claim is trivial, as
B^{\shortuparrow}
must consist of a single flat-step and
B^{\downarrow}
a single down-step. Assuming the statement holds for
k
, we now show it for
k+1
. Using Lemma
8.6
(as the right endpoint of
B^{\shortuparrow}
is higher by 1 than
B^{\downarrow}
), we first couple
B^{\shortuparrow}(t-1)
and
B^{\downarrow}(t-1)
so that
B^{\downarrow}(t-1)\leq B^{\shortuparrow}(t-1)\leq B^{\downarrow}(t-1)+1
. Note that there are three possibilities for the individual values of
B^{\shortuparrow}(t-1)
, and
B^{\downarrow}(t-1)
and we give the coupling of the whole paths depending on the case:

(1)

B^{\shortuparrow}(t-1)=z+1
,
B^{\downarrow}(t-1)=z
: in this case both paths must follow down steps in the last slot, and we couple
B^{\shortuparrow}|_{\llbracket a,t-1\rrbracket}
and
B^{\downarrow}|_{\llbracket a,t-1\rrbracket}
using the induction hypothesis.

(2)

B^{\shortuparrow}(t-1)=z
,
B^{\downarrow}(t-1)=z
: in this case
B^{\shortuparrow}
must have an flat-step in the last slot and
B^{\downarrow}
must have a down-step, and we couple
B^{\shortuparrow}|_{\llbracket a,t-1\rrbracket}
and
B^{\downarrow}|_{\llbracket a,t-1\rrbracket}
to be equal.

(3)

B^{\shortuparrow}(t-1)=z
,
B^{\downarrow}(t-1)=z-1
: in this case
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
must both have a flat-step in the last slot, and we couple
B^{\shortuparrow}|_{\llbracket a,t-1\rrbracket}
and
B^{\downarrow}|_{\llbracket a,t-1\rrbracket}
using the induction hypothesis.

(Note that the case of
B^{\shortuparrow}(t-1)=z+1
,
B^{\downarrow}(t-1)=z-1
is excluded by the coupling at
t-1
.)
It is immediate that the above coupling satisfies the hypothesis of Lemma
8.3
and has the correct marginals. This completes the induction step and hence the proof.
∎

9.
Lower tail estimates

In this section we start developing the proof of Theorem
6.10
on the lower tail of the
k
th
curve of
\bm{L}^{N}
(recall the definitions and hypotheses from Section
6
). In particular, in this section, as well as Sections
10
,
11
, and
12
,
\bm{L}^{N}
will be assumed to satisfy Assumptions
1
and
2
without explicit mention.

9.1.
The pieces for the lower tail

Theorem
6.10
is proved by an induction argument. The induction hypothesis will combine the lower tail estimate for a given curve with a statement that curves remain uniformly separated; the ultimate result of uniform separation will also be useful at several other locations (in particular, in the proof of Proposition
6.5
, that weak limits have the Brownian Gibbs property) and we record it as a separate theorem.

In what follows, for a (measurable) set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, an integer
k\in\mathbb{N}
, a (possibly infinite) interval
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{N}
with
\llbracket 1,k+1\rrbracket\subseteq\Lambda
, a stochastic process
\bm{X}:\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\delta>0
, we define the event

(9.1)

\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k}(\delta,A,\bm{X})=\left\{\min_{i=1,\ldots,k}\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in A}\bigl(X_{i}(x)-X_{i+1}(x)\bigr)\geq\delta N^{1/3}\right\}.

We will sometimes drop some of the parameters from the notation, depending on the context, when it is obvious what they are. We also recall the convention for the notation for event names mentioned in Remark
6.16
. The use of the event
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k}
(and similar ones) is that, on it, the weight factor
W
(
3.9
) is very close to 1 for all large enough
N
, as stated next.

Lemma 9.1
.

Fix an interval
[a,b]
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\in[0,1)

\varepsilon>0
, and
\delta>0
. Let
\bm{X}:\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\times\llbracket aN^{2/3},bN^{2/3}\rrbracket
be a collection of Bernoulli paths. There exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(q,\varepsilon,\delta,b-a,k)
such that, if
X_{i}(x)>X_{i+1}(x)+\delta N^{1/3}
for all
x\in\llbracket aN^{2/3},bN^{2/3}\rrbracket
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket
, then, for
N>N_{0}
,

W(\bm{X},\llbracket aN^{2/3},bN^{2/3}\rrbracket)>1-\varepsilon.

Proof.

Under the conditions assumed,
W(\bm{X},\llbracket aN^{2/3},bN^{2/3}\rrbracket)>(1-q^{\delta N^{1/3}})^{k(b-a)N^{2/3}}
, from which the claim follows.
∎

Next we state the result on uniform separation. It will be proved later in this section.

Theorem 9.2

(Uniform separation)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
T>2
, and
\varepsilon>0
. There exist
\delta=\delta(k,T,\varepsilon)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k}(\delta,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N})\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

We also adopt the following convenient notational convention in the following sections:
\overline{L}^{N}_{k}
will denote
L^{N}_{k}
with the linear growth removed, i.e., with
p
as in Assumption
2

\displaystyle\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)=L^{N}_{k}(x)-px.

An important part of the induction step proving Theorems
6.10
and
9.2
is captured in the next proposition.

Proposition 9.3

(Lower tail inductive step)
.

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose for any
T\geq 2
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
M=M(k,T,\varepsilon)
and
\delta=\delta(k,T,\varepsilon)>0
such that

(9.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{sN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(s)<-MN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N})^{c}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Then for any
T\geq 2
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
R=R(k,T,\varepsilon)
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{sN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(s)<-RN^{1/3}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

We will prove this statement in Section
9.2
.
To complete the induction step we also need a bound on the separation between the
k
and
(k+1)
th
curves. This is the next proposition.

Proposition 9.4

(Uniform separation inductive step)
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{j}
be as in (
9.1
). Suppose for any
\varepsilon>0
and
T\geq 2
there exist
\eta=\eta(k,T,\varepsilon)>0
,
M=M(k,T,\varepsilon)
, and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

(9.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\eta,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N})^{c}\Bigr)<\varepsilon\quad\text{and}{\quad}\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)<-MN^{1/3}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Then for any
\varepsilon>0
and
T\geq 2
, there exist
\delta=\delta(k,T,\varepsilon)>0
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-1}(\delta,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N})\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

We will prove Proposition
9.4
in Section
10
. Next we will prove Theorems
6.10
and
9.2
together using Propositions
9.3
and
9.4
.

Proof of Theorems
6.10
and
9.2
.

We prove the following statement by induction, which implies both theorems: for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
M=M(\varepsilon,k,T)
and
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,k,T)
such that

(9.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\geq-MN^{1/3}\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-1}(\delta,[-T,T],\bm{L}^{N})\right)\geq 1-\varepsilon.

By convention,
L^{N}_{0}\equiv\infty
so the
k=0
case is trivially true. We now show the induction statement that if the displayed statement holds for
k-1
, it also holds for
k
.

Indeed, the induction hypothesis says that the hypotheses (
9.2
) of Proposition
9.3
hold, which yields that for any
\varepsilon>0
and
T\geq 2
, there exists
M
such that the first inequality in (
9.4
) holds. Now the first hypothesis in (
9.3
) of Proposition
9.4
holds by the induction hypothesis, and the second hypothesis in (
9.3
) holds by the deduction just made. Thus we obtain the second inequality in (
9.4
) from Proposition
9.4
, completing the induction step and thus the proof.
∎

9.2.
Setting up the proof of Proposition
9.3

We start by stating that the
k
th
curve cannot be uniformly low on a large interval. This is the first step in the proof of Proposition
9.3
, and will be proved in Section
9.3
.
For an interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
and
R>0
, we define the event

(9.5)

\mathsf{Low}^{I}_{k}(R):=\left\{\sup_{sN^{-2/3}\in I}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(s)\leq-RN^{1/3}\right\}.

Lemma 9.5
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and let
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta,\{T,U\},\bm{L}^{N})
be as in (
9.1
).
Suppose for any
\varepsilon>0
,
T
, and
U
there exist
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,k,T,U)>0
,
M=M(\varepsilon,k,U)
, and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,T,\varepsilon)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{sN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(s)<-MN^{1/3}\right)\leq\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta,\{T,U\},\bm{L}^{N})^{c}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Then for any
\varepsilon>0
and
t>0
, there exist
U=U(\varepsilon,T)
,
R_{\mathrm{low}}=R_{\mathrm{low}}(T,U,\varepsilon,k)
, and
N_{0}=N_{0}(k,U,\varepsilon)
such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Low}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{\mathrm{low}})\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Next we state a lower tail bound for the
k
th
curve at a single point on the event that there exist points on either side which are not too low, and conditioned on the
(k-1)
st
curve not being too low. This is the second step in the proof of Proposition
9.3
; it will be proved in Section
9.4
.

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
[a,b]
be an interval,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\delta>0
. Then there exist
N_{0}=N_{0}(\delta,b-a)
,
C=C(b-a)>0
, and
c=c(k)>0
such that, for any
t\in[a,b]
,
R>0
,
K\geq 1
, and
N>N_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\parbox{182.84958pt}{\centering$\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(tN^{2/3})<-(R+K)N^{1/3}$,\\
$\displaystyle\sup_{xN^{-2/3}\in[a,t]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\wedge\displaystyle\sup_{xN^{-2/3}\in[t,b]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\geq-RN^{1/3}$\@add@centering}\!\!\Bigg|\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[a,b]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)>-(R-\delta)N^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp\left(-cK^{2}/(b-a)\right).

Further,
C
can be taken to be increasing in
b-a
, and
N_{0}
can be taken to be increasing in
b-a
and decreasing in
\delta
.

Assuming these two lemmas, getting the uniform bound Proposition
9.3
is a fairly standard chaining argument; we give it now before returning to the proofs of Lemmas
9.5
and
9.6
in Sections
9.3
and
9.4
, respectively.

Proof of Proposition
9.3
.

We start by proving a lower tail bound for
L^{N}_{k}
at the boundaries and midpoint of
[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]
, and then we will turn to setting up a chaining argument to extend to the infimum over the whole interval.

For
U>T
and
R>0
, let
\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[-U,-T]}(\frac{1}{4}R)
and
\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[T,U]}(\frac{1}{4}R)
be as in (
9.5
).
Using Lemma
9.5
(whose hypothesis is assumed in (
9.2
)), and the same after reflecting in the line
\{x=0\}
, we obtain
U>T
and
R
such that

(9.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[-U,-T]}(\tfrac{1}{4}R)\cup\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[T,U]}(\tfrac{1}{4}R)\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{3}\varepsilon;

we may raise
R
if necessary so that
\exp(-cR^{2}/(2U))<\tfrac{1}{3}\varepsilon
, where
c
is as in Lemma
9.6
(which is possible by the monotonicity of the above probability in
R
).
Next, using (
9.2
), we may raise
R
further if necessary so that

(9.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{sN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(s)\leq-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{3}\varepsilon.

Let
\mathsf{NotLow}_{k}=(\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[-U,-T]}(\tfrac{1}{4}R)\cup\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[T,U]}(\tfrac{1}{4}R))^{c}
. Then we see that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(-TN^{2/3})<-\tfrac{1}{2}RN^{1/3}\ \Big|\ \inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)>-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(-TN^{2/3})<-\tfrac{1}{2}RN^{1/3},\mathsf{NotLow}_{k}\ \Big|\ \inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)>-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[-U,-T]}(\tfrac{1}{4}R)\cup\mathsf{Low}_{k}^{[T,U]}(\tfrac{1}{4}R)\ \Big|\ \inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)>-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-cR^{2}/(2U))+\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon,

the penultimate inequality using Lemma
9.6
, (
9.6
), and (
9.7
). A similar argument, along with a union bound and a relabeling of
\varepsilon
, yields

(9.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\min_{xN^{-2/3}\in\{-T,0,T\}}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)<-\tfrac{1}{2}RN^{1/3}\ \Big|\ \inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)>-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right)<\varepsilon.

Now we set up the chaining argument to obtain a bound on the lower tail of the infimum of
\overline{L}^{N}_{k}
. For any integer
\ell\geq 0
, let
S_{\ell}:=\{\lfloor T(-1+i2^{-\ell})N^{2/3}\rfloor:i=0,1,\ldots,2^{\ell+1}\}
(we will omit the floors in the notation for convenience in the rest of the proof) and define

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\ell}:=\left\{\inf_{x\in S_{\ell}}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)>-\alpha_{\ell}RN^{1/3}\right\},

where

\displaystyle\alpha_{\ell}:=\frac{1}{2}+\delta\sum_{j=1}^{\ell}2^{-j/4},

with
\delta=\frac{1}{4}(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-j/4})^{-1}>0
, so that
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha_{\ell}<\frac{3}{4}
for all
\ell\geq 0
. It is easy to see that

\displaystyle\left\{\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)<-RN^{1/3}\right\}\subseteq\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{\lceil\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}N\rceil}(\mathsf{E}_{\ell})^{c}.

Indeed, at
\ell=\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}N
, the spacing between consecutive points in
S_{\ell}
becomes equal to
TN^{2/3-1/2}=TN^{1/6}
. Since
L^{N}_{k}
is a Bernoulli path and since
\alpha_{\ell}\leq\frac{3}{4}
, this implies that, on the complement of the righthand side of the previous display, for
R
and
N
sufficiently large we have

\displaystyle\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\geq\inf_{x\in\cup_{\ell=0}^{2^{-1}\log_{2}N}S_{\ell}}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)-TN^{1/6}>-\tfrac{3}{4}RN^{1/3}-TN^{1/6}>-RN^{1/3}.

Further, it holds that (for convenience defining
\mathsf{E}_{-1}=\Omega
, i.e., the entire space)

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{\lceil\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}N\rceil}\mathsf{E}_{\ell}^{c}=\bigcup_{\ell=0}^{\lceil\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}N\rceil}(\mathsf{E}_{\ell-1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{\ell}^{c}).

Thus, since we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)<-RN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-T,T]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)<-RN^{1/3}\ \Big|\ \inf_{sN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(s)>-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{sN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(s)\leq-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right)

and the second term is bounded by
\frac{1}{3}\varepsilon
by (
9.7
),
it is enough to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{\frac{1}{2}\log_{2}N}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{E}_{\ell-1}\cap\mathsf{E}_{\ell}^{c}\ \Big|\ \inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)>-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3}\right).

We have bounded
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E}_{0}^{c}\mid\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[-U,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)>-\tfrac{1}{8}RN^{1/3})
by
\varepsilon
above in (
9.8
). For the
\ell\geq 1
terms, we observe that Lemma
9.6
(with
R
there equal to
\alpha_{\ell-1}R
,
K=(\alpha_{\ell}-\alpha_{\ell-1})R
, and
\delta=\alpha_{\ell-1}-\frac{1}{8}>0
uniformly over
\ell
) implies that there exists
N_{0}
independent of
\ell
such that, for
N>N_{0}
, the
\ell
th
term is upper bounded (using a union bound over the
2^{\ell+1}
points in
S_{\ell}
and noting that
\alpha_{\ell}-\alpha_{\ell-1}=\delta 2^{-\ell/4}
and the separation between points in
S_{\ell}
is
2^{-\ell}
) by

\displaystyle 2^{\ell+1}\cdot C\exp\left(-c(\delta 2^{-\ell/4}R)^{2}/2^{-\ell}\right)=2^{\ell+1}\cdot C\exp(-c\delta^{2}2^{\ell/2}R^{2}).

Summing this from
\ell=1
to
\infty
yields a bound of
C\exp(-c\delta^{2}R^{2})
, which can be made smaller than
\varepsilon
by raising
R
further if required. Relabeling
\varepsilon
completes the proof.
∎

9.3.
The
k
th
curve cannot be uniformly too low on an interval

Proof of Lemma
9.5
.

The idea of the proof is the following. Under the event
\mathsf{Low}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{\mathrm{low}})
for a large enough interval
[T,U]
, it will be likely that the top
k-1
curves fall, as the
k
th
curve that typically “supports” them is too far down. In particular, under this conditioning, the 1
st
curve will with reasonable probability be at a low position around say the midpoint of
[T,U]
. This event’s (unconditional) probability is known to be small using lower tail control from Assumption
2
. Since the event we conditioned on turned an unlikely event into a likely one, it must have a low probability. The basic proof idea originates in
[
CH14
]
, but is made more complicated here by the lack of stochastic monotonicity of the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property. Now we give the details.

For
R_{1}
a constant to be chosen next, let

\displaystyle\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(x,R_{1}):=\left\{\overline{L}^{N}_{1}(xN^{2/3})+\lambda x^{2}N^{1/3}\leq R_{1}N^{1/3}\right\},

and define, for
R_{\mathrm{low}}
to be set,

(9.9)

\mathsf{A}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{1}):=\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(T,R_{1})\cap\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(U,R_{1})\cap\mathsf{Low}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{\mathrm{low}}).

Now by Assumption
2
, there exist
R_{1}=R_{1}(\varepsilon)
and
N_{0}=N_{0}(T,U,\varepsilon)
large enough such that, for all
U
and for
N\geq N_{0}
,

(9.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(T,R_{1})^{c}\cup\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(U,R_{1})^{c}\Bigr)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon;

note that
R_{1}
can be set independently of
U
. We set
R_{1}
to be such a value for the remainder of the proof.

Next let
\bm{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1})
be a collection of
k-1
independent Bernoulli random walk bridges, the
i
th
one having endpoints

(9.11)

\displaystyle u_{i}

\displaystyle=(TN^{2/3},L^{N}_{i}(TN^{2/3}))\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{i}=(UN^{2/3},L^{N}_{i}(UN^{2/3})).

Note that these points are all upper bounded by
pTN^{2/3}-\lambda T^{2}N^{1/3}+R_{1}N^{1/3}
and
pUN^{2/3}-\lambda U^{2}N^{1/3}+R_{1}N^{1/3}
, respectively, on
\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(T,R_{1})\cap\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(U,R_{1})
.

Let
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta,\{T,U\},\bm{L}^{N})
be as in (
9.1
).
By hypothesis we know there exists
\delta=\delta(T,U,\varepsilon,k)
such that

(9.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta,\{T,U\},\bm{L}^{N})^{c}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon.

Also let
R_{k-1}=R_{k-1}(T,U,\varepsilon,k)
be such that, with

\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1}):=\left\{\min_{xN^{-2/3}\in\{T,U\}}\overline{L}^{N}_{k-1}(x)\geq-R_{k-1}N^{1/3}\right\},

it holds that

(9.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})^{c}\right)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon,

which is possible by the hypothesis of Lemma
9.5
that we are working under.

We now set
R_{\mathrm{low}}=R_{k-1}+U
in the definition (
9.9
) of
\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})
. Also let
C_{N}(T,U)
be the midpoint of the line joining the upper bounds on
u_{1}
and
v_{1}
mentioned above; more precisely, it is given by

(9.14)

\begin{split}C_{N}(T,U)&:=p\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}(T+U)N^{2/3}-\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda(T^{2}+U^{2})N^{1/3}+R_{1}N^{1/3}\\
&=p\cdot\tfrac{1}{2}(T+U)N^{2/3}-\tfrac{1}{4}\lambda(T+U)^{2}N^{1/3}-\tfrac{1}{4}\lambda(U-T)^{2}N^{1/3}+R_{1}N^{1/3}.\end{split}

Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(k-1,\llbracket TN^{2/3},UN^{2/3}\rrbracket,\bm{L}^{N})
be as in Definition
3.14
, i.e., the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
\{L^{N}_{i}(x):(i,x)\not\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket\times\llbracket TN^{2/3}+1,UN^{2/3}-1\rrbracket\}
. Note that
\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})
,
\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})
, and
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta):=\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta,\{T,U\},\bm{L}^{N})
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable. For a process
X:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, define the event
\mathsf{UpTail}(U,X)
by

(9.15)

\displaystyle\mathsf{UpTail}(U,X):=\left\{X(\tfrac{1}{2}(T+U)N^{2/3})\geq C_{N}(T,U)+\tfrac{1}{8}\lambda(U-T)^{2}N^{1/3}\right\},

and recall the notational convention from Remark
6.16
.
Then, by the previous bounds (
9.12
) and (
9.13
) on
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta)^{c})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})^{c})
, respectively, and the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,L^{N}_{1})\mid\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,L^{N}_{1}),\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta),\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})\mid\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1}))}

(9.16)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{UpTail}(U,B_{1})}W(\bm{B},L^{N}_{k})\right]\frac{\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta),\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})}}{\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[W(\bm{B},L^{N}_{k})\right]}\ \Big|\ \mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1}))},

where recall from (
9.11
) that
\bm{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{k-1})
is a family of Bernoulli random walk bridges on
[TN^{2/3},UN^{2/3}]
, with
B_{i}
starting and ending at
L^{N}_{i}(TN^{2/3})
and
L^{N}_{i}(UN^{2/3})
, respectively, for
i\in\llbracket 1,k-1\rrbracket
, and
W
is as in Definition
3.16
.

For an interval
I
, define the event

(9.17)

\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(I,\bm{B}):=\Bigl\{B_{i}(x)\geq B_{i+1}(x)\text{ for all }i\in\llbracket 1,k-2\rrbracket,x\in I\Bigr\}.

In the rest of the proof we adopt the shorthand
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})=\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}([TN^{2/3},UN^{2/3}],\bm{B})
. Since
W(\bm{B})=0
on
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})^{c}
, it follows that the ratio of
\mathcal{F}
-conditional probabilities in (
9.16
) equals

(9.18)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{UpTail}(U,B_{1})}W(\bm{B})\mid\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})\right]}{\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}\left[W(\bm{B})\mid\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})\right]}.

Controlling the denominator.
Next, we lower bound the denominator of (
9.18
) on the event
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta)\cap\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})
and conditionally on
\mathsf{A}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{1})
. We claim that there exists
\rho=\rho(\delta,T,U,k)>0
such that, on
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta)
,

(9.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\min_{i=1,\ldots,k-2}\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[T,U]}\left(B_{i}(x)-B_{i+1}(x)\right)\geq\rho N^{1/3}\ \Big|\ \mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}.

To see this, we first let
\alpha=\alpha(T,U,\delta,k)>0
be such that
\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}(\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(B))\geq\alpha
on
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta)
; the existence of
\alpha
uses that the separation of
B_{i}
from
B_{i+1}
at the endpoints of
TN^{2/3}
and
UN^{2/3}
is at least
\delta N^{1/3}
on
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta)
, and that
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})
holds if the fluctuation of each bridge is at most
\frac{1}{4}\delta N^{1/3}
on the interval of size
(U-T)N^{2/3}
, which has positive probability, e.g., by convergence of (rescaled)
\bm{B}
to independent Brownian bridges with endpoint separation at least
\delta
(see e.g.,
[
DM21
, Lemma 5.2]
). Then, by a union bound and the independence of
B_{i}
we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\min_{i=1,\ldots,k-2}\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[T,U]}\left(B_{i}(x)-B_{i+1}(x)\right)<\rho N^{1/3}\ \Big|\ \mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[T,U]}\left(B_{i}(x)-B_{i+1}(x)\right)<\rho N^{1/3}\ \Big|\ \mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\alpha^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(0\leq\inf_{xN^{-2/3}\in[T,U]}\left(B_{i}(x)-B_{i+1}(x)\right)<\rho N^{1/3}\right).

This can be made smaller than
\frac{1}{2}
for all large enough
N
by setting
\rho>0
small enough, depending on
\delta
,
T
,
U
, and
k
(note that
\alpha
depends on the same parameters); this can again be seen by the convergence of a rescaled version of
\bm{B}
to a collection of independent Brownian bridges with endpoint separation at least
\delta
, and noting that the corresponding Brownian bridge probability can be made arbitrarily small by setting
\rho
correspondingly. Thus (
9.19
) has been proved.

Recall from Definition
3.16
the decomposition
W=W_{\mathrm{int}}\cdot W_{\mathrm{low}}
with
W_{\mathrm{low}}=W_{\mathrm{low}}(B_{k-1},L^{N}_{k})
being the weight associated to the lower boundary condition
L^{N}_{k}
and
W_{\mathrm{int}}=W_{\mathrm{int}}(\bm{B})
being the weight associated to the interior interactions between the
B_{i}
. By Lemma
9.1
, on the event in (
9.19
), and conditionally on
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})
(and so with conditional probability at least
\frac{1}{2}
),

\displaystyle W_{\mathrm{int}}(\bm{B})\geq(1-q^{\rho N^{1/3}})^{(U-T)N^{2/3}\cdot(k-1)}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}

for large enough
N
(depending on
\rho
,
T-U
, and
k
, therefore on
T
,
U
,
\varepsilon
, and
k
). Further observe that, on
\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\cap\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})
, and with
\overline{B}_{k-1}(x)=B_{k-1}(x)-px
,

\displaystyle\min\left(\overline{B}_{k-1}(TN^{2/3}),\overline{B}_{k-1}(UN^{2/3})\right)-\sup_{xN^{-2/3}\in[T,U]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}

\displaystyle=(-R_{k-1}+R_{\mathrm{low}})N^{1/3}\geq UN^{1/3},

where recall
R_{k-1}
depends on
U
and
R_{\mathrm{low}}=R_{k-1}+U
was set earlier.

So it follows that
B_{k-1}
, with conditional probability (given
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})
) at least
\frac{3}{4}
, for all
U
large enough, stays
\frac{1}{2}UN^{1/3}
above
L^{N}_{k}
throughout the interval
[TN^{2/3},UN^{2/3}]
(e.g., again by using weak convergence to non-intersecting Brownian bridges and since
\frac{1}{2}U\gg U^{1/2}
for such
U
), and, on that event, by Lemma
9.1
,

W_{\mathrm{low}}(B_{k-1},L^{N}_{k})\geq(1-q^{\frac{1}{2}UN^{1/3}})^{(U-T)N^{2/3}}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}

for large enough
N
(depending on
U
).

Thus we have that both
W_{\mathrm{int}}(\bm{B})\geq\frac{1}{2}

and
W_{\mathrm{low}}(B_{k-1},L^{N}_{k})\geq\frac{1}{2}
on an event of conditional probability (given
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})
) at least
\frac{1}{4}
.
So, on
\mathsf{Sep}^{N}_{k-2}(\delta)\cap\mathsf{BdyCtrl}_{k-1}(R_{k-1})
and conditionally on
\smash{\mathsf{A}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{1})}
, we may lower bound
\mathbb E_{\mathcal{F}}[W(\bm{B},L^{N}_{k})\mid\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})]
by
\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{4}=\frac{1}{16}
.

Bounding the conditional random walk bridge probability.
Thus, and also using
W(\bm{B},L^{N}_{k})\leq 1
, we see that the first term in (
9.16
) is upper bounded by

(9.20)

\displaystyle 16\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\bigl(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,B_{1})\mid\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})\bigr)\ \Big|\ \mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right].

Recall from (
9.11
) that
B_{i}
is a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(TN^{2/3},L^{N}_{i}(TN^{2/3}))
to
(UN^{2/3},L^{N}_{i}(UN^{2/3}))
and that, on
\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})
,
\overline{L}^{N}_{1}(TN^{2/3})\leq(-\lambda T^{2}+R_{1})N^{1/3}
and
\overline{L}^{N}_{1}(UN^{2/3})\leq(-\lambda U^{2}+R_{1})N^{1/3}
. Let
\tilde{\bm{B}}=(\tilde{B}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{B}_{k-1})
be a collection of
k-1
Bernoulli random walk bridges with
\tilde{B}_{i}
from

\displaystyle\left(TN^{2/3},pTN^{2/3}+(-\lambda T^{2}+R_{1}+(k-i)U^{1/2})N^{1/3}\right)\quad\text{to}

\displaystyle\left(UN^{2/3},pUN^{2/3}+(-\lambda U^{2}+R_{1}+(k-i)U^{1/2})N^{1/3}\right).

Since these endpoints are higher than the corresponding endpoints of
\bm{B}
, it holds that the law of
\tilde{\bm{B}}
conditionally on
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\tilde{\bm{B}})
stochastically dominates that of
\bm{B}
conditional on
\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\bm{B})
by Lemma
8.2
. Thus it follows that (
9.20
) is upper bounded by (dropping the
\mathcal{F}
and the outer expectation since
\tilde{\bm{B}}
does not have any dependence on data from
\mathcal{F}
)

\displaystyle 16\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,\tilde{B}_{1})\ \Big|\ \mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\tilde{\bm{B}})\right).

Observe that there is an absolute constant
c>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NonInt}_{k-2}(\tilde{\bm{B}}))>c
for all large enough
U
, since
\tilde{B}_{i}
are separated by order
U^{1/2}
at their endpoints and the non-intersection is imposed on an interval of length
U-T
. Thus the previous display is upper bounded by

(9.21)

\displaystyle 16c^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,\tilde{B}_{1})\right).

Now we recall the definitions (
9.14
) of
C_{N}(T,U)
as the midpoint of the line joining the points
(TN^{2/3},pTN^{2/3}-\lambda T^{2}N^{1/3}+R_{1}N^{1/3})
and
(UN^{2/3},pUN^{2/3}-\lambda U^{2}N^{1/3}+R_{1}N^{1/3})
. It follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{B}_{1}(\tfrac{1}{2}(T+U)N^{2/3})\right]\leq C_{N}(T,U)+(k-1)U^{1/2}N^{1/3}.

Thus, recalling the definition (
9.15
) of
\mathsf{UpTail}
, the quantity (
9.21
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle 16c^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{B}_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(T+U)N^{2/3}\right)\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\tilde{B}_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}(T+U)N^{2/3}\right)\right]-kU^{1/2}N^{1/3}+\tfrac{1}{8}(U-T)^{2}N^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon

for large enough
U
, the inequality using upper bounds on Bernoulli random walk bridge one-point tails (Lemma
6.13
).

Returning to
\bm{L^{N}_{1}}
.

Overall, returning to (
9.16
) and (
9.18
), we have shown that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,L^{N}_{1})\ \Big|\ \mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right)\leq\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right)}.

We claim that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1}))<3\varepsilon
. Otherwise, we would have that
\smash{\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1}))}\geq 3\varepsilon
, which would imply that
\smash{\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,L^{N}_{1})\mid\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1}))\leq\varepsilon+\frac{1}{3}\leq\frac{1}{2}}
for large enough
U
. This would further imply (recalling the definition (
9.15
) of
\mathsf{UpTail}
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,L^{N}_{1})^{c}\mid\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right)\right)^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,L^{N}_{1})^{c}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{UpTail}(U,L^{N}_{1}\right)^{c})

\displaystyle=2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(L^{N}_{1}(\tfrac{1}{2}(T+U)N^{2/3})-\tfrac{1}{2}p(T+U)N^{2/3}<\left(-\tfrac{1}{4}\lambda(T+U)^{2}-\tfrac{1}{8}\lambda(U-T)^{2}+R_{1}\right)N^{1/3}\right).

By the one-point tightness of
L^{N}_{1}
(Assumption
2
), for large enough
U
(depending on
R_{1}
and
T
) and all
N>N_{0}
(for a
N_{0}
depending on
U
), this is upper bounded by
\varepsilon
, which is a contradiction; recall here the important observation made after (
9.10
) that
R_{1}
was chosen independently of
U
. This establishes that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1}))<3\varepsilon
.

With these choices of
R_{1}
,
R_{\mathrm{low}}
,
U
, and
N_{0}
, and recalling the definition (
9.9
) of
\mathsf{A}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{1})
and the bound (
9.10
) on the probability of
\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}^{c}
, we see that, for
N\geq N_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{Low}^{[T,U]}_{k}(R_{\mathrm{low}})\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{A}_{k}^{[T,U]}(R_{1})\right)+\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathsf{NotHigh}_{1}(T,R_{1})^{c}\cup\mathsf{NotHight}(U,R_{1})^{c}\Bigr)\leq 4\varepsilon.

Relabeling
\varepsilon
completes the proof.
∎

9.4.
The
k
th
curve cannot be too low at a given point

Here we prove Lemma
9.6
. We will need a version of the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property on a random interval. As we have seen, random intervals defined by information outside the interval satisfy a strong Gibbs property (Definition
6.1
and Proposition
6.2
), analogous to a strong Markov property, in which case the behavior inside the interval under the resampling is the same. Here, however, we will need to resample on a random interval defined in a way that is dependent on the behavior of the process inside, and the description of the resampling is recorded here.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
be integers, let
k\in\mathbb{N}
be a natural number, let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be an interval containing
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, and let
\bm{L}:\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
be a discrete line ensemble (Definition
3.6
) with the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property (Assumption
1
). Let
p\in(-1,0)
and recall the notation
\overline{L}_{k}(x)=L_{k}(x)-px
(note that we do not assume
\bm{L}
satisfies Assumption
2
, so
p
need not be as in that assumption). Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, and define

(9.22)

\begin{split}a^{\prime}&:=\max\bigl\{x\in\llbracket a,t\rrbracket:\overline{L}_{k}(x)\geq m\bigr\}\quad\text{and}\quad b^{\prime}:=\min\bigl\{x\in\llbracket t,b\rrbracket:\overline{L}_{k}(x)\geq m\bigr\}.\end{split}

Next define the
\sigma
-algebra

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\{k\},\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket,\bm{L}):=\Bigl\{A\cap\{a^{\prime}=x,b^{\prime}=y\}:A\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\{k\},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket,\bm{L}),x\in\llbracket a,t\rrbracket,y\in\llbracket t,b\rrbracket\Bigr\}.

For any
c,d
, let
\mathbb{E}^{x,y,\llbracket c,d\rrbracket}
be the expectation operator associated to a probability measure
\mathbb{P}^{x,y,\llbracket c,d\rrbracket}
under which
B
is distributed as a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
(c,x)
to
(d,y)
, and let
\overline{B}(x):=B(x)-px
.
Finally, let
\Omega=\{(\ell,c,d):c\in\llbracket a,t\rrbracket,b\in\llbracket t,d\rrbracket,\ell\in\Omega_{\llbracket c,d\rrbracket}\}
, where
\Omega_{\llbracket c,d\rrbracket}:=\{f:\llbracket c,d\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z},f\text{ a Bernoulli path}\}
is the space of Bernoulli paths (Definition
2.9
) on
\llbracket c,d\rrbracket
.
We will give the proof of the following lemma in Appendix
B
.

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\Lambda\subseteq\mathbb{Z}
be an interval, and let
\bm{L}:\mathbb{N}\times\Lambda\to\mathbb{Z}
be a discrete line ensemble satisfying Assumption
1
, and let
p\in(-1,0)
. Let
m\in\mathbb{R},\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\subseteq\mathbb{Z},
and
t\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, and let
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
be as in (
9.22
). Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\{k\},\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket,\bm{L})
. For any measurable function
F:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\left[F(L_{k}|_{\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\right]

\displaystyle=Z^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket}\left[F(B,a^{\prime},b^{\prime})W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1},\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket)\mid\overline{B}(s)<m\ \forall s\in\llbracket a^{\prime}+1,b^{\prime}-1\rrbracket\right],

where
z_{1}=L_{k}(a^{\prime})
,
z_{2}=L_{k}(b^{\prime})
, and
Z=\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket}[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1},\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket)\mid\overline{B}(s)<m

\forall s\in\llbracket a^{\prime}+1,b^{\prime}-1\rrbracket]
.

Proof of Lemma
9.6
.

For notational convenience, we prove the statement with
2K
in place of
K
. On the event

\mathsf{E}(t,a,b):=\left\{\parbox{289.07999pt}{\centering$\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(tN^{2/3})\leq-(R+2K)N^{1/3},$\\
$\Bigl(\sup_{xN^{-2/3}\in[a,t]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\Bigr)\wedge\Bigl(\sup_{xN^{-2/3}\in[t,b]}\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\Bigr)\geq-RN^{1/3}$\@add@centering}\right\},

define the random times
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle a^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=N^{-2/3}\cdot\max\left\{x\in\llbracket aN^{2/3},tN^{2/3}\rrbracket:\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\geq-(R+K)N^{1/3}\right\}\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle b^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=N^{-2/3}\cdot\min\left\{x\in\llbracket tN^{2/3},bN^{2/3}\rrbracket:\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(x)\geq-(R+K)N^{1/3}\right\}.

Since
L^{N}_{k}
is a Bernoulli path, it follows that
\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(a^{\prime}N^{2/3}),\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(b^{\prime}N^{2/3})=\lceil-(R+K)N^{1/3}\rceil
on
\mathsf{E}(t,a,b)
; in the remainder of the proof, we ignore the ceiling and assume that
\smash{\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(a^{\prime}N^{2/3})}=\overline{L}^{N}_{k}(b^{\prime}N^{2/3})=-(R+K)N^{1/3}
.

Let
B
be a Bernoulli random walk bridge from
