\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}}[F(\bm{\mathfrak{B}})\mid\mathsf{ScSep}_{k}(\delta,[a,b],\bm{\mathfrak{B}},\mathfrak{L}_{k+1})]\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}}[F(\bm{\mathfrak{B}})\mid\mathsf{NonInt}(\bm{\mathfrak{B}},\mathfrak{L}_{k+1})]
completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
The Yang-Baxter equation, S6V, and the
q
-Boson model

In this section we give a self-contained proof of the partition function expression in (
3.1
) and of Proposition
3.12
on the distributional relation of the top line of the colored Hall-Littlewood line ensemble and the colored height function of the colored S6V model. First, recall the description of the colored
q
-Boson model from Section
3.1
and the colored S6V model from Section
2.3
, with the domain restriction performed as in Section
3.2.1
. In particular, in both models we have a spectral parameter
z\in(0,1)
fixed and a boundary condition
\sigma:\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket\to\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
where the arrow horizontally entering
(1,k)
(for colored S6V) and the arrow horizontally entering
(-K,k)
for all large enough
K
(for colored
q
-Boson) are both of color
\sigma(k)
, for each
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
.
To explain the relation between the colored
q
-Boson model and the colored S6V model, we need the Yang-Baxter relation enjoyed by these models, which we introduce next. Then in Section
A.2
we establish the expression for the partition function of the colored
q
-Boson model and in Section
A.3
we give the proof of Proposition
3.12
.

A.1.
Yang-Baxter equation

The Yang-Baxter equation is an equality of partition functions of configurations consisting of three vertices. In our case, the first vertex has vertex weight given by the weights of the colored S6V model (Figure
8
) and the other two vertices have weights given by the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights (Figure
10
). Let us write the weights from Figure
8
with spectral parameter
u\in(0,1)
as
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{R}$}}_{u}(a,i;b,j)
with
a,i,b,j\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
, where recall
a,i,b,j
respectively represent the colors of the arrows vertically entering, horizontally entering, vertically exiting, and horizontally exiting from the vertex. Unlike the convention in Section
2.3
, here we will take
0
to represent the absence of an arrow, so as to match the setup of the colored
q
-Boson model. In this notation, the Yang-Baxter equation can be written as follows; it was originally due to
[
KRS81
,
Baz85
,
Jim86
]
, but the notation we have adopted is from
[
BW22
]
(it can also be verified directly from the formulas for the weights).

Lemma A.1

(Yang-Baxter equation,
[
BW22
, Proposition 2.3.1]
)
.

Fix
a_{1},i_{1},b_{2},j_{2}\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
and
\bm{I},\bm{J}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}
. Let
x,y\in(0,1)
and let
z=y/x
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b_{1},j_{1}\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket,\\
\bm{K}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}\end{subarray}}\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{R}$}}_{y/x}(a_{1},i_{1};b_{1},j_{1})\,\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{x}(\bm{I},j_{1};\bm{K},j_{2})\,\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{y}(\bm{K},b_{1};\bm{J},b_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b_{1},j_{1}\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket,\\
\bm{K}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}\end{subarray}}\,\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{y}(\bm{I},a_{1};\bm{K},b_{1})\,\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{x}(\bm{K},i_{1};\bm{J},j_{1})\,\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{R}$}}_{y/x}(b_{1},j_{1};b_{2},j_{2});

here
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
and
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{R}$}}_{u}
are zero if their arguments do not satisfy colored arrow conservation.

Diagrammatically, the Yang-Baxter equation can be written as follows (we adopt a style of depiction similar to
[
BW22
]
).

(A.1)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b_{1},j_{1}\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket,\\
\bm{K}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{N}\end{subarray}}\vbox{\hbox{

Here, the left and righthand sides mean the sum over the weights of the depicted configurations of the three vertices, and the arc between two edges indicates which of those two edges corresponds to exiting vertically (upper end of arc) and horizontally (lower end of arc).
The weights of the two vertices at the intersection of the two horizontal lines and the thick vertical line are the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{u}
weights (so they can hold an arbitrary number of arrows) with spectral parameter
u
as indicated by
x
or
y
at the beginning of the line (which should be followed straight through the cross), while the weight of the vertex at the center of the cross is the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{R}$}}_{z}
weights with spectral parameter
z=y/x
.

Figure 22
.
The model with the
M\times N
rectangle of
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{R}$}}_{z}
-weight vertices attached to the left. If any arrow tries to deviate from the configuration depicted except for finitely many columns at the right end (i.e., the portion to the right of the
\bm{\cdots}
), some arrow must travel infinitely far horizontally in the top
M

z\in(0,1)
; thus any such configuration has weight zero and the depicted configuration must occur in all but finitely many columns (that is, to the left of the
\bm{\cdots}

\bm{\cdots}
, where their trajectories are random.

A.2.
Partition function of the colored
q
-Boson model

With the Yang-Baxter equation in hand, we may proceed to derive the expression for the partition function of the colored
q
-Boson model given in (
3.1
). To do this we consider an augmented vertex model, as in Figure
22
, by the following informal description (see the next paragraph for a more formal one). We attach a copy of an
M\times N
rectangular grid (which we denote
\mathcal{R}_{1}
), each of whose vertices has vertex weights given by the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{R}$}}_{z}
-weights (i.e., with spectral parameter
z
as that of the colored
q
-Boson model), to the left of the original domain
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}\times\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
(which we denote
\mathcal{R}_{2}
). The augmented model has boundary conditions given by an arrow of color
\sigma(i)
horizontally entering at the
i
th
row of the just added rectangle, no arrows vertically entering, no arrows horizontally exiting, and all arrows vertically exiting at
(0,N+M)
in the original domain.

We are being somewhat informal in saying to attach the
M\times N
rectangle
\mathcal{R}_{1}
to the “left” of the semi-infinite domain
\mathcal{R}_{2}
; more formally, a configuration of the augmented model (with boundary condition
\sigma
) consists of an assignment of arrow configurations to the vertices of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
as well as
\mathcal{R}_{2}
such that (i) consistency and colored arrow conservation are satisfied within each, (ii) the entering boundary condition for
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is given by
\sigma
, (iii) the exiting boundary condition for
\mathcal{R}_{2}
is that
(\#\sigma^{-1}(\{j\}))_{j=1}^{N}
vertically exits from
(0,N+M)
, and (iv) the exiting arrow configurations of
\mathcal{R}_{1}
equals the boundary condition of
\mathcal{R}_{2}
as shown in Figure
22
, i.e., for all large enough
K
and all
y\in\llbracket 1,N+M\rrbracket
, the arrow configuration at
(-K,y)\in\mathcal{R}_{2}
equals
(0^{N},\tau_{y};0^{n},\tau_{y})
, where
\tau_{y}\in\llbracket 0,N\rrbracket
is the color of the arrow exiting
\mathcal{R}_{1}
from the
y
th
vertex on its right and top boundaries (counted counterclockwise).

We first observe that the horizontally entering arrows to
\mathcal{R}_{1}
are forced to go horizontally straight through: if any arrow does not pass straight through, at least one arrow will enter
\mathcal{R}_{2}
through one of the top
M
lines and then travel an infinite horizontal distance; any such configuration of arrows has weight zero since, under the
\scalebox{1.05}{\mbox{$\mathtt{L}$}}_{z}
weights, the individual vertex weight of passing horizontally through a given vertex in the top
M
rows is
z<1
. Thus the partition function
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{\sigma}
of the augmented model is the partition function
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{\sigma}
of the original colored
q
-Boson model (with boundary condition
\sigma
) multiplied by the weight of the configuration of arrows passing horizontally straight through
\mathcal{R}_{1}
; by Figure
8
, the latter is
\left((1-z)/(1-qz)\right)^{NM}
, so that

(A.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{aug}}_{\sigma}=\left(\frac{1-z}{1-qz}\right)^{NM}\cdot\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{\sigma}.

Next, observe that there are
MN
vertices present in the rectangle
\mathcal{R}_{1}
in the augmented model. We move each of these vertices to the right, one at a time (starting at the rightmost corner vertex of the rectangle and ending at the leftmost one), all the way across
\mathcal{R}_{2}
. By the Yang-Baxter equation Lemma
A.1
(see also (
A.1
)), this does not change the partition function
\mathcal{Z}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{aug}}
. Upon doing so, we obtain the situation depicted in Figure
23
.
Observe that again the only configuration of arrows with non-zero weight is the one depicted, in which the arrows pass horizontally straight through (this uses that an arrow is entering at every one of the top
N
rows). By Figure
10
, the configuration in
\mathcal{R}_{2}
has weight
1
, and from Figure
8
, the empty configuration in
\mathcal{R}_{1}
also has weight
1
. Thus, for any
\sigma
,

(A.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{aug}}=1.

) and (

) that
\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{\sigma}=\left((1-qz)/(1-z)\right)^{NM}
for any
\sigma
, completing the proof of the claim in (
3.1
).

Proof of Proposition

Fix

and a boundary condition

). We define a variant height function
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{S6V}}
as follows. Let

be sampled from the colored S6V measure on
\mathcal{R}_{1}
with boundary condition
\sigma
, where (as in Section

)

and

are the colors of the arrows exiting horizontally and vertically, respectively, from the vertex

. For

, define

(A.4)

\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{S6V}}(k;y):=\#\left\{i>y:j^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{(M,i)}\geq k\right\}=h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(k,0;y,M).

For
k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
and
y\in\llbracket N,N+M\rrbracket
, define

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{S6V}}(k;y):=\#\left\{i<N+M-y:b^{\mathrm{S6V}}_{(i,N)}\geq k\right\}.

In words,
\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{S6V}}(k;y)
counts the number of arrows of color at least
k
which exit from the right and top boundaries of
\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket\times\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket
from a site which is

Consider a sequence of Bernoulli paths

such that, (i)
L^{(k)}(0)=\#\sigma^{-1}(\llbracket k,N\rrbracket)
and
L^{(k)}(N+M)=0
, and (ii)
L^{(k)}(\bm{\cdot})-L^{(k+1)}(\bm{\cdot})
is also a Bernoulli path. We will show that

(A.5)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left((\tilde{h}^{\mathrm{S6V}}(k;y))_{k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}=(L^{(k)}(y))_{k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}\right)\\
&=\mathbb{P}\left((L^{\mathrm{cHL},(k)}_{1}(y))_{k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}=(L^{(k)}(y))_{k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket,y\in\llbracket 0,N+M\rrbracket}\right);\end{split}

this implies Proposition
3.12
by a projection of
y
onto
\llbracket 1,N-1\rrbracket
and (
A.4
).

To establish (

\displaystyle j^{\prime}_{(-1,y)}:=\min\left\{k\in\llbracket 1,N\rrbracket:\Delta(L^{(k+1)},y)=\Delta(L^{(k)},y)-1\right\},

where by convention
\Delta(L^{(N+1)},y)=0
for all

and
\min\emptyset=0
.

\smash{\mathcal{Z}^{\mathrm{cB}}_{\sigma}=((1-qz)/(1-z))^{NM}}

.

Appendix B
Proofs of miscellaneous lemmas

In this section we prove various miscellaneous statements in the main text whose proofs were deferred. Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
are proved in Section
B.1
; Lemma
6.13
in Section
B.2
; Lemma
8.6
in Section
B.4
; Lemma
9.7
in Section
B.5
; and Lemma
10.4
in Section
B.6
. An alternative proof of Theorem
2.8
when
\alpha=0
(as mentioned in Remark
6.7
) is given in Section
B.3
.

B.1.
Finite speed of propagation bounds for colored ASEP and colored S6V

Proof of Lemma
5.3
.

The lemma follows from the claim that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-ct)
, no particle in colored ASEP (with packed initial condition) of color greater than
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
is at any location to the right of
\lfloor 4t\rfloor
by time
t
. To see this, we merge all colors greater than or equal to
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
to be particles and all particles of color less than
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
to be holes, resulting in uncolored ASEP with step initial condition from location
\lfloor 2t\rfloor
, in which setting we must show the probability that the rightmost particle at time
t
is at location greater than
\lfloor 4t\rfloor
is at most
\exp(-ct)
. By ignoring left jump attempts, and letting
\smash{(X_{i})_{i=1}^{\lfloor 2t\rfloor-1}}
be a collection of independent and identically distributed exponential rate 1 random variables, this probability is upper bounded by
\smash{\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor 2t\rfloor-1}X_{i}<t)}
, which is indeed upper bounded by
\exp(-ct)
by standard concentration results (e.g.,
[
Jan18
, Theorem 5.1]
).
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

By monotonicity of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;\bm{\cdot},t)
and since
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,0;\bm{\cdot},t)\leq\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-x+1
for all
x
, it is sufficient to prove the case
z=\lfloor 4t\rfloor
. The event in the lemma with
z=\lfloor 4t\rfloor
contains the event that no arrow of color at least
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
exits horizontally from a vertex in
I_{t}:=\{\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor\}\times\llbracket-\lfloor 2t\rfloor,\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 4t\rfloor\rrbracket
. To lower bound the probability of this event, we first merge all arrows of color at least
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
to color 1, and all other arrows to color
0
, obtaining an uncolored S6V model with step initial condition at
-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
. Then, equivalently, we need to upper bound the probability that the lowest arrow, i.e., the one starting at
(1,-\lfloor 2t\rfloor)
, exits horizontally from
I_{t}
; we call this event
\mathsf{E}_{t}
. It can be described as the bottom-most arrow traveling at least
\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor
horizontally before traveling
\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
vertically. The trajectory of the bottom-most arrow can be broken up into alternating intervals of horizontal travel and intervals of vertical travel. Note that the
i
th
interval of horizontal travel begins with a vertex configuration as in the fourth panel of Figure
8
and is followed by a random number
X_{i}
of configurations as in the third panel of Figure
8
, where
X_{i}
is a geometric random variable, with distribution given by
\mathbb{P}(X_{i}=k)=\delta^{k}(1-\delta)
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
; thus the
i
th
interval of horizontal travel has length
1+X_{i}
. The
X_{i}
are independent for different
i
, and there are at most
\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 2t\rfloor
many intervals of horizontal travel, so
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{E}_{t})\leq\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{\smash{\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 2t\rfloor}}(1+X_{i})>\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor)=\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{\smash{\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 2t\rfloor}}X_{i}>\lfloor 2t\rfloor)
. Using that
\smash{\mathbb{E}[\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor\delta^{-1}t\rfloor-\lfloor 2t\rfloor}X_{i}]=t(1-2\delta)/(1-\delta)<2t}
, this is upper bounded by
\exp(-ct)
for a
c>0
independent of
\delta
, again by standard concentration inequalities (e.g.,
[
Jan18
, Theorem 2.1]
).
∎

B.2.
Two-point fluctuation bound for Bernoulli random walk bridge

Proof of Lemma
6.13
.

It is enough to prove the two-point bound without the supremum, i.e., to prove that there exist
C
,
c>0
such that for all
M>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|B(x)-B(y)-p(x-y)|>M|x-y|^{1/2}\right)\leq C\exp(-cM^{2}).

From here the claim is proved by a standard chaining argument, i.e., doing a union bound over dyadically decreasing scales and using the above two point bound at each scale; e.g., we may apply
[
Dau23
, Lemma 2.3]
or imitate the proofs of Proposition
9.3
or Lemma
10.5
.

Assume without loss of generality that
x>y
.
Observe that
\{B(w)-B(w-1)\}_{w=1}^{n}
is a collection of
pn
many
-1
s and
(1-p)n
many 0s in uniformly random order. Clearly,
B(x)-B(y)
is the number of
-1
s lying in positions
y+1,\ldots,x
. Observe that this has the same distribution as the number of
-1
s in the first
k:=x-y
positions, which is the number
X
of
-1
s drawn without replacement in the first
k
draws from a collection of
n
numbers of which
K:=pn
are
-1
s. In other words,
X
is a hypergeometric distribution with parameters
n
,
K
, and
k
. By a theorem of Hoeffding
[
Hoe63
, Theorems 1 and 4]
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|X-pk|\geq Mk^{1/2}\right)\leq 2\exp(-2M^{2}),

which completes the proof.
∎

B.3.
Alternative proof of ASEP sheet to Airy sheet convergence at
\bm{\alpha=0}

In this subsection we provide the alternate proof of Theorem
2.8
in the case of
\alpha=0
, that was mentioned in Remark
6.7
.

Alternative proof of Theorem
2.8
for
\alpha=0
.

As in the original proof in Section
7.1
, we again take
\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)=\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\theta,\varepsilon}(-x;-y)
as processes in
(x,y)
with
N=\varepsilon^{-1}
, and
\bm{L}^{(j),N}=\bm{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(j),\theta,\varepsilon}
. As in the proof of Theorem
2.8
in Section
7.1
, (
3.14
), (
7.3
), and (
7.4
) hold and
\bm{L}^{(1),N}
satisfies Assumptions
1
and
2
. So by Theorem
3.18
and Proposition
6.5
,
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}
is tight and all subsequential limits satisfy the Brownian Gibbs property; call such a subsequential limit
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1)}
. Combining Lemma
5.2
with
[
QS22
, Theorem 2.2 (2)]
yields that
\mathfrak{L}^{\smash{(1)}}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{P}_{1}
(this uses
\alpha=0
), and, since
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{\smash{(1)}}
has the Brownian Gibbs property,
[
DM21
, Theorem 1.1]
implies that
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\bm{\mathcal{P}}
. Since every subsequential limit is the same, we obtain that
\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}\xrightarrow{\smash{d}}\bm{\mathcal{P}}
. Invoking Theorem
7.1
and the fact that
\mathcal{S}(x;y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{S}(-x;-y)
, as processes on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, from
[
DV21a
, Proposition 1.23]
completes the proof.
∎

B.4.
Quantitative monotonicity for non-intersecting Bernoulli random walk bridge

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.

We construct a Markov chain whose value at any time
t
is a pair of Bernoulli paths
(B^{\shortuparrow}_{t},B^{\downarrow}_{t})
such that

(B.1)

\displaystyle B^{\downarrow}_{t}(x)\leq B^{\shortuparrow}_{t}(x)\leq B^{\downarrow}_{t}(x)+M

holds almost surely and the large
t
marginal distributions of the chain are respectively the marginal distributions of
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
. Any subsequential limiting coupling so produced will establish the lemma. We may assume
M\geq 1
as otherwise there is nothing to prove.

For
*\in\{\shortuparrow,\downarrow\}
, define
B^{*}_{0}
to be the minimal Bernoulli path from
(a,x^{*})
to
(b,y^{*})
which satisfies
g(x)\leq B^{*}_{0}(x)\leq f(x)
for all
x\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
(the assumptions guarantee at least one such path exists). We claim that (
B.1
) holds at
t=0
. That the first inequality holds is immediate by minimality of the paths.

For the second inequality, suppose to the contrary that there exists
x_{0}\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
such that
\smash{B^{\shortuparrow}_{0}(x_{0})=B^{\downarrow}_{0}(x_{0})+M+1}
, and we take
x_{0}
to be the smallest such point. Let
y_{0}>x_{0}
be the first such that
\smash{B^{\shortuparrow}_{0}(y_{0})=B^{\downarrow}_{0}(y_{0})+M}
; it exists since the final separation of the paths at
b
is at most
M
and the separation changes by an element of
\{-1,0,1\}
at each step. Now, by minimality of
x_{0}
and
y_{0}
,
B^{\shortuparrow}
has a flat-step starting at
x_{0}
and a down-step starting at
y_{0}-1
. Let
\smash{\tilde{B}^{\shortuparrow}_{0}}
be the path obtained from
B_{0}
by flipping the flat-step at
x_{0}
to a down-step and flipping the down-step at
y_{0}-1
to a flat-step; then
\smash{\tilde{B}^{\shortuparrow}_{0}}
is lower than
B^{\shortuparrow}_{0}
and does not intersect
g
(as
\smash{\tilde{B}^{\shortuparrow}_{0}(x)\geq B^{\downarrow}_{0}(x)\geq g(x)}
for all
x\in\llbracket x_{0},y_{0}\rrbracket
), contradicting the minimality of
\smash{B^{\downarrow}_{0}}
. Thus our claim is established, i.e., (
B.1
) holds at
t=0
.

Now we specify the Markov dynamics. At each time
t\geq 1
, an
X\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
and
\sigma\in\{0,-1\}
are chosen independently and uniformly at random. For each
*\in\{\shortuparrow,\downarrow\}
, if
B^{*}_{t-1}(X+1)=B^{*}_{t-1}(X-1)-1
(i.e., there is a down-step followed by a flat-step or vice versa from
X-1
to
X+1
in
B^{*}_{t-1}
), we set
B^{*}_{t}(X)=B^{*}_{t-1}(X-1)+\sigma
and
B^{*}_{t}(x)=B^{*}_{t-1}(x)
for all
x\neq X
, as long as the resulting
B^{*}_{t}
satisfies
g\leq B^{*}_{t}\leq f
; if not, no change is made.

It is easy to check that (
B.1
) is satisfied under these dynamics, and that the dynamics are aperiodic and irreducible. It is also straightforward that the marginal distributions of
B^{\shortuparrow}
and
B^{\downarrow}
are stationary for the marginal Markov chains; thus the limiting stationary distribution has the correct marginals. This completes the proof.
∎

B.5.
Gibbs property on a certain random interval

Proof of Lemma
9.7
.

For a stochastic process
X:\llbracket a,b\rrbracket\to\mathbb{Z}
taking values in
\Omega_{\llbracket a,b\rrbracket}
(the space of Bernoulli paths on
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, as defined before Lemma
9.7
),
t\in\llbracket a,b\rrbracket
, and
p\in(-1,0)
, let
\overline{X}(x)=X(x)-px
,
a^{\prime}(X):=\max\{x\in\llbracket a,t\rrbracket:\overline{X}(x)\geq m\}
, and
b^{\prime}(X):=\min\{x\in\llbracket t,b\rrbracket:\overline{X}(x)\geq m\}
.
With the notational convention for event names from Remark
6.16
in mind, for such a process
X
, define
\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2},X):=\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2},X)\cap\mathsf{IntVal}(x,y,X)
, where

\displaystyle\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2},X)

\displaystyle:=\{X(x)=z_{1},X(y)=z_{2}\},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{IntVal}(x,y,X)

\displaystyle:=\{a^{\prime}(X)=x,b^{\prime}(X)=y\}.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{x,y}:=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}(\{k\},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket,\bm{L})
,
a^{\prime}:=a^{\prime}(L_{k})
,
b^{\prime}:=b^{\prime}(L_{k})
, and
\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2}):=\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2},L_{k})
for short. In the case that
a^{\prime}=b^{\prime}=t
, there is nothing to prove, so we my assume that this does not hold. By the definition of
\mathcal{F}
from Lemma
9.7
, we must show that, for any
x\in\llbracket a,t\rrbracket
,
y\in\llbracket t,b\rrbracket
,
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{F}_{x,y}
(recalling the notation
\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}
introduced just before Lemma
9.7
),

(B.2)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[F(L_{k}|_{\llbracket a^{\prime},b^{\prime}\rrbracket},a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}\right]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[F(B,x,y)W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}(s)<m\ \forall s\in\llbracket x+1,y-1\rrbracket\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x+1,y-1\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}(s)<m\ \forall s\in\llbracket x+1,y-1\rrbracket\right]}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}\right],\end{split}

where the
W
factors are evaluated on the interval
\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
.
First, if
z_{1}-px<m
or
z_{2}-py<m
,
\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})=\emptyset
by definition of
a^{\prime}
and
b^{\prime}
, in which case the equality trivially holds. So we assume
z_{1}-px,z_{2}-py\geq m
. By conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{x,y}
, we see that the lefthand side of the previous display equals

(B.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{x,y}}\bigl[F(L_{k}|_{\llbracket x,y\rrbracket},x,y)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}\bigr]\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{A}}\Bigr].

Adopt the shorthand
\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2}):=\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2},L_{k})
and recall that
\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})=\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})\cap\mathsf{IntVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2},L_{k})
. Applying the Hall-Littlewood Gibbs property shows that the (inner) conditional expectation in (
B.3
) equals

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[F(B,x,y)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{IntVal}(x,y,B)}W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\right]}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}.

Note that
\mathsf{IntVal}(x,y,B)=\{\overline{B}(s)<m\ \forall s\in\llbracket x+1,y-1\rrbracket\}
when
\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})
holds, since
z_{1}-px,z_{2}-py\geq m
. Thus we can rewrite the previous display as (with the shorthand
\overline{B}<m
for
\overline{B}(s)<m\ \forall s\in\llbracket x+1,y-1\rrbracket
)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[F(B,x,y)W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\right]}\cdot\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\overline{B}<m\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[F(B,x,y)W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{B}<m}\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\right]}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[F(B,x,y)W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{x,y}}\left(\overline{L}_{k}(s)<m\ \forall s\in\llbracket x,y\rrbracket\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[F(B,x,y)W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}\cdot\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}_{x,y}}\left(\mathsf{IntVal}(x,y,L_{k})\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}.

Since all the factors outside the
\mathbb P_{\mathcal{F}}
factor are
\mathcal{F}_{x,y}
-measurable, and since by definition
\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})=\mathsf{IntVal}(x,y,L_{k})\cap\mathsf{BdyVal}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})
, the tower property of conditional expectation yields that the previous display equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}_{x,y}}\left[\frac{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[F(B,x,y)W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}{\mathbb{E}^{z_{1},z_{2},\llbracket x,y\rrbracket}\left[W(B,L_{k-1},L_{k+1})\mid\overline{B}<m\right]}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{E}(x,y,z_{1},z_{2})}\right].

Substituting this in (
B.3
), using the tower property again, and that
\mathsf{A}
is
\mathcal{F}_{x,y}
measurable, yields (
B.2
). This completes the proof.
∎

B.6.
Uniform separation for Brownian and random walk bridges

In this section we establish Lemma
10.4
which, recall, states that conditional on a Bernoulli random walk bridge staying above a lower boundary curve
f
, it will maintain some on-scale uniformly positive separation from
f
with high probability. The argument goes by proving a similar statement for Brownian bridges and then using the KMT coupling to obtain it for the Bernoulli case.

The Brownian bridge case is the following.

Lemma B.1

(Brownian bridge uniform separation)
.

Fix
M>0
,
\sigma>0
,
p>0
and
T\geq 2
. Let
U_{1}\in[-2T,-\frac{3}{2}T]
and
U_{2}\in[\frac{3}{2}T,2T]
. Let
B^{\mathrm{Br}}
be a Brownian bridge from
(U_{1}N^{2/3},z_{1})
to
(U_{2}N^{2/3},z_{2})
of variance
\sigma^{2}>0
, for some
z_{i}
such that
z_{i}-pU_{i}N^{2/3}\in[-MN^{1/3},MN^{1/3}]
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
. Also let
f:[U_{1}N^{2/3},U_{2}N^{2/3}]\to\mathbb{R}
be a function with
f(U_{i}N^{2/3})\leq z_{i}
. Let
\{B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f\}
denote
\{B^{\mathrm{Br}}(x)\geq f(x)\ \forall x\in[U_{1},U_{2}]\}
and suppose, for some
\rho>0
, that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f\right)\geq\rho.

Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,\rho,M,\sigma,T)
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(x)-f(x)\right)\leq\delta N^{1/3}\ \Big|\ B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Using the previous lemma and Lemma
6.14
, we give the straightforward proof of Lemma
10.4
.

Proof of Lemma
10.4
.

We will eventually set
\delta<\omega
and will use this fact in the coming arguments. By Lemma
6.14
, there exists
N_{0}=N_{0}(\varepsilon,\delta,T,M)
such that there is a coupling between
B^{\mathrm{RW}}
and
B^{\mathrm{Br}}
(of variance lower bounded by a positive constant depending on only
p
) such that, for
N\geq N_{0}
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon\rho
,
\sup_{[U_{1}N^{2/3},U_{2}N^{2/3}]}|B_{k}-B^{\mathrm{Br}}|\leq\delta N^{1/3}
. Thus

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{RW}}(x)-f(x)\right)\leq\delta N^{1/3}\ \Big|\ B^{\mathrm{RW}}\geq f\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{RW}}(x)-f(x)\right)\leq\delta N^{1/3},B^{\mathrm{RW}}\geq f\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(B^{\mathrm{RW}}\geq f\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(x)-f(x)\right)\leq 2\delta N^{1/3},B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f-\delta N^{1/3}\right)+\varepsilon\rho}{\mathbb{P}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f+\delta N^{1/3}\right)-\varepsilon\rho}.

Since
\delta<\omega
,
\mathbb{P}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f+\delta N^{1/3}\right)\geq\rho
by hypothesis. So (assuming
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
without loss of generality) the previous display is upper bounded by

\displaystyle 2\rho^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(x)-f(x)\right)\leq 2\delta N^{1/3},B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f-\delta N^{1/3}\right)+2\varepsilon.

By Lemma
B.1
we may pick
\delta<\omega
with
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,\rho,M,p)
such that the probability is less than
\rho\varepsilon
, which completes the proof after relabeling
\varepsilon
.
∎

Next we prove Lemma
B.1
on the uniform on-scale separation of a Brownian bridge from its lower boundary condition. The idea is to condition on the entire bridge except for its value at zero; more precisely, one conditions on the shape of the curve so that, given the value of
B^{\mathrm{Br}}(0)
, the whole process is determined by an affine shift. The conditional distribution of
B^{\mathrm{Br}}(0)
is explicit and the proof comes down to a calculation about the probability of this random variable being close to the lower edge of its support.

Similar ideas were introduced in
[
Ham22
]
and have been used in a number of subsequent works, including
[
CHH23
,
CHHM23
,
GHZ23
]
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

We assume that
\sigma^{2}=1
and
p=0
without loss of generality. Recall that
B^{\mathrm{Br}}
is a Brownian bridge defined on
[U_{1}N^{2/3},U_{2}N^{2/3}]
. For an interval
[a,b]\subseteq[U_{1}N^{2/3},U_{2}N^{2/3}]
, we adopt the notation
B^{\mathrm{Br},[a,b]};[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
to be
B^{\mathrm{Br}}
affinely shifted to equal zero at
a
and
b
, i.e., for
x\in[a,b]
,

(B.4)

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{Br},[a,b]}(x):=B^{\mathrm{Br}}(x)-\frac{x-a}{b-a}B^{\mathrm{Br}}(b)-\frac{b-x}{b-a}B^{\mathrm{Br}}(a).

We let
\tilde{\mathbb}{P}
be the probability measure
\mathbb{P}(\bm{\cdot}\mid B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f)
, so that under
\tilde{\mathbb}{P}
,
B^{\mathrm{Br}}
is distributed as a Brownian bridge conditioned to stay above
f
. We consider the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
generated by
B^{\mathrm{Br},[U_{1}N^{2/3},0]}(\bm{\cdot})
and
B^{\mathrm{Br},[0,U_{2}N^{2/3}]}(\bm{\cdot})
. Then, conditionally on
\tilde{\mathbb}{P}_{\mathcal{F}}
, the only remaining randomness is in the value
B^{\mathrm{Br}}(0)
. The distribution of this random variable is that of a normal random variable of mean
\mu=\frac{1}{2}(z_{1}+z_{2})
and variance
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}=|U_{1}|\cdot U_{2}N^{2/3}/(U_{2}+|U_{1}|)
conditioned to stay above an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable random variable
\mathrm{Cor}
(short for corner, a name given to a similar quantity in
[
Ham22
]
) defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cor}:=\inf\left\{w:B^{\mathrm{Br},w}(x)\geq f(x)\text{ for all }x\in[U_{1}N^{2/3},U_{2}N^{2/3}]\right\},

where
B^{\mathrm{Br},w}
is the reconstruction of
B^{\mathrm{Br}}
from
B^{\mathrm{Br},[U_{1}N^{2/3},0]}
and
B^{\mathrm{Br},[0,U_{2}N^{2/3}]}
when
B(0)=w
, i.e.,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{Br},w}(x):=\begin{cases}\frac{U_{1}-xN^{-2/3}}{U_{1}}w+\frac{xN^{-2/3}}{U_{1}}z_{1}+B^{\mathrm{Br},[U_{1}N^{2/3},0]}(x)&x\in[U_{1}N^{2/3},0]\\
\frac{U_{2}-xN^{-2/3}}{U_{2}}w+\frac{xN^{-2/3}}{U_{2}}z_{2}+B^{\mathrm{Br},[0,U_{2}N^{2/3}]}(x)&x\in[0,U_{2}N^{2/3}].\end{cases}

Notice that for any
R
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb}{P}\left(\mathrm{Cor}>RN^{1/3}\right)\leq\tilde{\mathbb}{P}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(0)>RN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(0)>RN^{1/3}\ \Big|\ B^{\mathrm{Br}}\geq f\right)

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(0)>RN^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle=\rho^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{N}(\mu,\widetilde{\sigma}^{2})>RN^{1/3}\right).

Since
\mu\geq-MN^{1/3}
and
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}\leq TN^{2/3}
, there is
R=R(\varepsilon,\rho,M,T)
(and since
T\geq 1
) such that the previous display is at most
\varepsilon
. We fix such a value of
R
for the rest of the proof.

Now observe that, if
w\geq\mathrm{Cor}+3\delta N^{1/3}
, then

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br},w}(x)-f(x)\right)\geq\delta N^{1/3}.

This is because the lefthand side is at least
0
at
w=\mathrm{Cor}
by definition of
\mathrm{Cor}
,
B^{\mathrm{Br},w}(x)
increases with
w
, and
\frac{U_{1}-xN^{-2/3}}{U_{1}}\cdot 3\delta N^{1/3}\geq\delta N^{1/3}
when
x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]
since
U_{1}\in[-2T,-\frac{3}{2}T]
, and similarly for
U_{2}
. Thus it follows that (recall
\mathcal{F}
from right after (
B.4
))

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb}{P}\left(\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(x)-f(x)\right)\leq\delta N^{1/3}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbb}{E}\left[\tilde{\mathbb}{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\inf_{x\in[-TN^{2/3},TN^{2/3}]}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(x)-f(x)\right)\leq\delta N^{1/3}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathbb}{E}\left[\tilde{\mathbb}{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(B^{\mathrm{Br}}(0)\leq\mathrm{Cor}+3\delta N^{1/3}\right)\right].

Letting
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, the previous display equals

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb}{E}\left[\tilde{\mathbb}{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\widetilde{\sigma}X+\mu\leq\mathrm{Cor}+3\delta N^{1/3}\mid\widetilde{\sigma}X+\mu\geq\mathrm{Cor}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathbb}{E}\left[\tilde{\mathbb}{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(X\leq\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathrm{Cor}-\mu)+3\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}\delta N^{1/3}\mid X\geq\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathrm{Cor}-\mu)\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{Cor}\leq RN^{1/3}}\right]+\varepsilon.

Now by Lemma
8.1
, the conditional probability in the previous display is upper bounded by the same with
\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathrm{Cor}-\mu)
replaced by an upper bound on it. Since
\widetilde{\sigma}^{2}\geq cTN^{2/3}
and
\mu\geq-MN^{1/3}
, on the event
\mathrm{Cor}\leq RN^{1/3}
, it holds that
\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathrm{Cor}-\mu)\leq c^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}(R+M)
. By the upper bound on
\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}
, we also see that
3\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}\delta N^{1/3}\leq 3c^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}\delta
. Thus, the previous display is upper bounded (using the bound on
3\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}\delta N^{1/3}
for the first line and Lemma
8.1
for the second) by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb}{E}\left[\tilde{\mathbb}{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(X\leq\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathrm{Cor}-\mu)+3c^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}\delta\ \Big|\ X\geq\widetilde{\sigma}^{-1}(\mathrm{Cor}-\mu)\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{Cor}\leq RN^{1/3}}\right]+\varepsilon

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{\mathbb}{E}\left[\tilde{\mathbb}{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(X\leq c^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}(R+M+3\delta)\ \Big|\ X\geq c^{-1/2}T^{-1/2}(R+M)\right)\right]+\varepsilon.

It is now clear using standard estimates on normal probabilities that, for
\delta
small enough depending on
R
,
M
, and
T
(therefore on
\varepsilon
,
\rho
,
M
, and
T
), the previous display is upper bounded by
2\varepsilon
. This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix C
LPP maximizer location asymptotics

In this section, we give the proof of Proposition
7.2
. Before doing so, we
set up some preliminary lemmas that will be needed. Recall the definition of
Z^{N}_{k}
as the maximizer of the maximum over
z
of (
7.7
).

C.1.
Monotonicity properties

We need a monotonicity property of
Z^{N}_{k}
and of LPP values. We start with the first; its proof is the same as that of
[
DOV22
, Lemma 3.6]
and is omitted.

Lemma C.1
.

For fixed
N
,
x
, and
y
, it almost surely holds that
Z^{N}_{k}(x,y)\geq Z^{N}_{k+1}(x,y)
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,N^{1/6}\rrbracket
.

Lemma C.2

(
[
DOV22
, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

Let
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})
be a sequence of continuous functions with
f_{i}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle z\mapsto f[(z,k)\to(x,1)]+f_{k}(z)

is non-increasing.

C.2.
Prelimiting quantities

Define
F^{N}_{k}
,
G^{N}_{k}
and
H^{N}
by

\displaystyle F^{N}_{k}(x,z)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{w\leq z}\left(\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{N^{1/6}}(x;w)+\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(w,N^{1/6}-1)\to(z,k)]\right)-\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}_{k}(z)

\displaystyle G^{N}_{k}(z,y)

\displaystyle:=\bm{\mathfrak{L}}^{(1),N}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]+\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}_{k}(z)

\displaystyle H^{N}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{S}^{N}(x;y)=\mathfrak{L}^{N}_{1}(x;y);

note that the definition of
G^{N}_{k}(z,y)
requires
z\leq y
. Also define

(C.1)

\begin{split}G_{k}(z,y)&:=\bm{\mathcal{P}}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]+\mathcal{P}_{k}(z)\\
H(x,y)&:=\mathcal{S}(x;y).\end{split}

Observe that
G^{N}_{k}\to G^{k}
(by Theorem
3.19
). The quantities in (
C.1
) are defined in this way to obtain the monotonicity stated in Lemma
C.2
, i.e.,
G_{k}(z,y)
is non-increasing in
z
by Lemma
C.2
, a property not possessed by
\bm{\mathcal{P}}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]
.

The proof of Proposition
7.2
will also need some asymptotic information about
G_{k}
, which we record in the following lemma. We defer its proof to Section
C.3
as it requires the introduction of the prelimiting model of Brownian LPP. Recall the meaning of the notation
\mathfrak{o}
from (
7.5
).

Lemma C.3
.

Fix
z<y
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle G_{k}\left(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k}\right)=-y^{2}k-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

Now we give the proof of Proposition
7.2
, which is an adaptation of the argument from
[
DOV22
, Lemma 7.1]
.

Proof of Proposition
7.2
.

Note that
H^{N}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}_{1}(y-x)
for fixed
x,y
by (
7.3
) and
\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{P}_{1}
as
N\to\infty
by Theorem
3.19
. We added
\mathfrak{L}^{(1),N}_{k}(z)
in the definition of
G^{N}_{k}
because we will need
G^{N}_{k}
to converge to
G^{k}
. We subtracted the same quantity from
F^{n}_{k}
so that the following holds, which follows from (
7.7
):

(C.2)

\displaystyle H^{N}(x,y)

\displaystyle\geq F^{N}_{k}(x,z)+G^{N}_{k}(z,y)+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11}).

Now the left hand side is tight in
N
and independent of
k
by the tightness of
\mathfrak{L}^{(1)}_{N}
(Theorem
3.19
). For any
\varepsilon>0
, we claim that if
|z+\sqrt{k/2x}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{k}
, then the right hand side is at most
-\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{kx}/2
. By (
C.2
) and the tightness of
H^{N}(x,y)
, thus would imply that
Z^{N}_{k}
, the maximizer over
z
, must lie within
\varepsilon\sqrt{k}
of
-\sqrt{k/2x}
, establishing the proposition.

For
z
and
y
in a fixed compact subset independent of
N
(but which may depend on
k
), we know from Theorem
3.19
that
\smash{G^{N}_{k}(z,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}G_{k}(z,y)}
as
N\to\infty
. Further, we know from Lemma
C.3
and by the convergence of
G^{N}_{k}
to
G_{k}
that for each fixed
z
and
y

(C.3)

\displaystyle G^{N}_{k}\left(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k}\right)=-y^{2}k-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

We can use this information to obtain an upper bound on
F^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})
. First, we know from (
7.7
) and the definitions of
F^{N}_{k}
,
G^{N}_{k}
, and
H^{N}
that, for all
x,y,z
,

\displaystyle F^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})\leq H^{N}(x,y\sqrt{k})-G^{N}_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11}).

Define

(C.4)

\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k}):=\inf_{y}\left(H^{N}(x,y\sqrt{k})-G^{N}_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\right),

so that
z\mapsto\tilde{F}(x,z\sqrt{k})
is non-decreasing, since, by Lemma
C.2
,
G^{N}_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})
is monotonically non-increasing in
z
. Note also that

(C.5)

F^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})\leq\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11})

for all
x
and
z
. Our next step is to upper bound
\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})
.

Since for fixed
x,y
we know
H^{N}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{P}_{1}(y-x)
as
N\to\infty
,
x\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)+x^{2}
is stationary, and exponentially decaying tail bounds for
\mathcal{P}_{1}(0)
(i.e., the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution) are well-known (e.g.,
[
AGZ10
, Exercise 3.8.3]
), it follows that
H^{N}(x,y\sqrt{k})=-(x-y\sqrt{k})^{2}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})
. Combining this with the estimate from (
C.3
) of
G^{N}_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})
yields

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})

\displaystyle=\inf_{y}\left(-(x-y\sqrt{k})^{2}-\left(-y^{2}k-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z}\right)\right)+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})

\displaystyle=\inf_{y}\left(2xy\sqrt{k}+\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z}\right)+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}),

where in the last equality we used that
x
is uniformly bounded, independently of
k
.
The optimizer of the infimum is
y=z+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2x}}
. Substituting this into the previous display yields that, for each fixed
x
and
z
,

(C.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})\leq 2\sqrt{2kx}+2xz\sqrt{k}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

With this information, we can show that the maximizer in (
7.7
) equals
-\sqrt{k/2x}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})
. Indeed,
(
7.7
) can be written as

\displaystyle H^{N}(x,y)=\sup_{z\leq y}\left(F^{N}_{k}(x,z)+G^{N}_{k}(z,y)\right)+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11}).

Now, since
x
and
y
are bounded independently of
k
, the lefthand side of the previous display is
\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})
(by Theorem
3.19
). So at
z=Z^{N}_{k}
the maximizer, we must have

\displaystyle F^{N}_{k}(x,Z^{N}_{k})+G^{N}_{k}(Z^{N}_{k},y)=\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

We will show that if
|z+\sqrt{k/2x}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{k}
, then
F^{N}_{k}(x,z)+G^{N}_{k}(z,y)
is smaller then
-c\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{k}
. More precisely, we will show that for some absolute constant
c>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z:z\leq y\\
|z+\sqrt{k/2x}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{k}\end{subarray}}\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z)+G^{N}_{k}(z,y)\leq-c\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{k}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k});

this suffices to establish our claim since, by (
C.5
),
F^{N}_{k}(x,z)\leq\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z)+\mathfrak{o}(N^{-1/11})
. Recall from just after (
C.4
) that
\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})
is monotonically non-decreasing in
z
.
We first reduce the previous display to a supremum over a finite set using the monotonicity in
z
of
\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}
and
G^{N}_{k}
. Letting

\displaystyle\lfloor x\rfloor_{N,k}

\displaystyle:=\max\left\{w<x:w\in\tfrac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{k}{x}}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{Z}\right\}

\displaystyle\lceil x\rceil_{N,k}

\displaystyle:=\min\left\{w>x:w\in\tfrac{1}{4}\sqrt{\frac{k}{x}}\varepsilon^{2}\mathbb{Z}\right\},

the monotonicity in
z
of
\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}
and
G^{N}_{k}
implies that it is sufficient to show that

(C.7)

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z:z\leq y\\
|z+\sqrt{k/2x}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{k}\end{subarray}}\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,\lceil z\rceil_{N,k})+G^{N}_{k}(\lfloor z\rfloor_{N,k},y)\leq-c\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{k}.

Combining the bounds on
G^{N}_{k}
and
\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}
from (
C.3
) and (
C.6
) respectively (taking
y
in the
G^{N}_{k}
bound to be
yk^{-1/2}
here, and using the fact that
y
is bounded independently of
k
), we obtain, for each fixed
z
,

(C.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{N}_{k}(x,\lceil z\rceil_{N,k})+G^{N}_{k}(\lfloor z\rfloor_{N,k},y)\leq 2\sqrt{2kx}+2x(z+\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon^{2}x^{-1/2}\sqrt{k})+\frac{k}{z-\tfrac{1}{4}\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{k/x}}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

If one substitutes
\varepsilon=0
, the righthand side is maximized at
z=-\sqrt{k/2x}
(under the condition
z\leq y
), at which value it equals
0
. Further, by the concavity of the righthand side as a function in
z
(and the negativity of the third derivative) and using Taylor’s theorem, if
|z+\sqrt{k/2x}|>\varepsilon\sqrt{k}
, the righthand side is at most
-c\varepsilon^{2}\sqrt{k}
. Since these bounds also apply simultaneously for all the finite number of
z
’s in (
C.7
), we obtain the asymptotic for
Z^{N}_{k}
.

The tightness of
\{Z^{N}_{k}(x,y)\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
for each
k
follows from the asymptotic and the monotonicity property
y\geq Z^{N}_{1}(x,y)\geq Z^{N}_{2}(x,y)\geq\ldots\geq Z^{N}_{N^{1/6}}
from Lemma
C.1
.
∎

Remark C.4
.

Let remark briefly on the difference of the above argument compared to
[
DOV22
]
. There, an analog of the
F^{N}_{k}
,
G^{N}_{k}
and
H^{N}_{k}
quantities were defined in a model known as Brownian last passage percolation (introduced here in Section
C.3
ahead). In their context, asymptotics for
F^{N}_{k}
were available (via estimates for a limiting analog
F_{k}
), and these were used to derive an upper bound on
G^{N}_{k}
to then upper bound the analog of (
C.8
). Here, we do not
a priori
have such estimates for
F^{N}_{k}
as we do not know it weakly converges to
F_{k}
; instead, we know the convergence of
G^{N}_{k}
to its analog in the parabolic Airy line ensemble, where the previously mentioned estimates for
F_{k}
apply. These will lead to the estimates on
G_{k}
recorded in Lemma
C.3
, yielding the estimates for
G^{N}_{k}
that were used. We essentially reversed the argument in
[
DOV22
]
to obtain estimates for
F^{N}_{k}
that allowed us to then bound (
C.8
).

Remark C.5
.

Recall from (
7.2
) and (
7.3
) that
L_{1}^{N,(k)}
is equal in law to a translation of
L_{1}^{N,(1)}
, which was important for our arguments in Section
7
. This statement is false for the lower-indexed curves, namely,
L_{j}^{N,(k)}
does not have the same law as that of the corresponding translation of
\smash{L_{j}^{N,(1)}}
for
j>1
. This is closely related to the fact that, as noted in Remark
3.3
, we cannot merge colors 1 and 0 in the colored
q
-Boson model. It can also be seen from the fact that, if such an equality in law were true, then it would imply precise asymptotics for
F_{k}^{N}
(since the asymptotics of
\smash{L_{j}^{(N)}}
, converging to the
j
th
curve of the Airy line ensemble, are well understood, e.g.,
[
DV21
, Corollary 5.3]
), which are actually in disagreement with those shown in (
C.5
) and (
C.6
).

C.3.
\bm{G_{k}}
asymptotics

Here we give the proof of Lemma
C.3
. In order to access a certain variational formula, we prove its analog in the model of Brownian LPP, and use the convergence of the latter to the Airy sheet to obtain the result for
G_{k}
itself. We briefly recall the model.

An up-right path from
(x,j)
to
(y,k)
for
x<y
and
j>k
is a function
\gamma:[x,y]\to\llbracket k,j\rrbracket
which is non-increasing and càdlàg (right continuous with left-limits) with
\gamma(x)=j
and
\gamma(y)=k
. The jump times
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{N-1})
of a path from line
N
to line
1
defined by
t_{i}
being the time
\gamma
jumps from line
i+1
to line
i
, i.e., the time
t
such that
\gamma(t^{-})=i+1
and
\gamma(t)=i
. A pair of up-right paths
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
from
(x,j)
to
(y,k)
and
(x^{\prime},j^{\prime})
to
(y^{\prime},k^{\prime})
, respectively, are said to be disjoint if either
\pi(t)>\pi^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in(x,y)\cap(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
(i.e., excluding endpoints), or
\pi(t)<\pi^{\prime}(t)
for all
t\in(x,y)\cap(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.

Let
\bm{B}=(B_{1},\ldots,B_{N})
be a collection of
N
independent and identically distributed Brownian motions on
[0,\infty)
. The weight of a path
\gamma
from
(x,N)
to
(y,1)
with jump times
(t_{1},\ldots,t_{N-1})
is given by (with the convention
t_{0}=y
and
t_{N}=x
)

\displaystyle\bm{B}[\gamma]=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(B_{i}(t_{i-1})-B_{i}(t_{i})\bigr).

Let
\bm{W}\!\bm{B}=(W\!B_{1},\ldots,W\!B_{N})
be the curves of the
watermelon
, defined by

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}(W\!B)_{i}(t)=\bm{B}[(0,N)^{k}\to(t,1)^{k}]

for each
k=1,\ldots,N
, where
\bm{B}[(0,N)^{k}\to(t,1)^{k}]
is the maximum weight over all collections of
k
disjoint paths
(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{k})
, with
\gamma_{i}
from
(0,N-i+1)
to
(t,1)
, with the weight of a collection of paths being the sum of the weights.

Recall the LPP notation from (
2.2
) (which agrees with the above convention with
k=1
). Let
x_{N}=2xN^{2/3}
, and similarly for
y_{N}
and
z_{N}
. Define

\displaystyle F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x,z)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/3}\left(\bm{W}\!\bm{B}[(x_{N},N)\to(N+z_{N},k)]-(W\!B)_{k}(N+z_{N})+2xN^{2/3}\right)

\displaystyle G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z,y)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/3}\left(\bm{W}\!\bm{B}[(N+z_{N},k)\to(N+y_{N},1)]+(W\!B)_{k}(N+z_{N})-2N-2yN^{2/3}\right)

\displaystyle H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}(x,y)

\displaystyle=N^{-1/3}\left(\bm{W}\!\bm{B}[(x_{N},N)\to(N+y_{N},1)]-2N-2(y-x)N^{2/3}\right).

It follows from
[
DOV22
, Proposition 2.2]
or
[
CH14
, Proposition 4.1]
that
G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}G_{k}(z,y)
for any fixed
z
and
y
(which may depend on
k
).

We will also need a certain stationarity property of
G_{k}
:

Lemma C.6
.

Fix
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}
and
z<y
. Then

\displaystyle G_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}G_{k}((z+\varepsilon)\sqrt{k},(y+\varepsilon)\sqrt{k}))+(\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon y)k.

Proof.

Recall that
G_{k}(z,y)=\bm{\mathcal{P}}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]+\mathcal{P}_{k}(z)
and that
\mathcal{P}_{i}(x)=\mathcal{A}_{i}(x)-x^{2}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\bm{\mathcal{A}}
is the Airy line ensemble and is stationary. Also note that for any up-right path
\gamma
from
(z,k)
to
(y,1)
,
\bm{\mathcal{P}}[\gamma]=\bm{\mathcal{A}}[\gamma]-y^{2}+z^{2}
. Thus it follows that

\displaystyle G_{k}(z,y)

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathcal{A}}[(z,k)\to(y,1)]-y^{2}+z^{2}+\mathcal{A}_{k}(z)-z^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\bm{\mathcal{A}}[(z+\varepsilon,k)\to(y+\varepsilon,1)]-y^{2}+\mathcal{A}_{k}(z+\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\bm{\mathcal{P}}[(z+\varepsilon,k)\to(y+\varepsilon,1)]-y^{2}+(y+\varepsilon)^{2}+\mathcal{P}_{k}(z+\varepsilon).

This equals
G(z+\varepsilon,y+\varepsilon)+\varepsilon^{2}+2\varepsilon y
. Replacing
z
by
z\sqrt{k}
,
y
by
y\sqrt{k}
, and
\varepsilon
by
\varepsilon\sqrt{k}
completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

In the proof we will prove the statement with
z_{0}
in place of
z
as we will need to use
z
as a generic variable. As mentioned, we will prove that

G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})=-y^{2}k-\sqrt{k}/(y-z_{0})+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k});

then since
G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}G_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})
as
N\to\infty
, we will be done.

We know from
[
DOV22
, Proposition 6.1]
that
F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})=(2\sqrt{2x}+2xz)\sqrt{k}+\mathfrak{o}(k^{1/2})
for any
0<x<z
, and from
[
DOV22
, Theorem 1.4, Proposition 4.1]
that
\smash{H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{S}(x;y)}=-(x-y)^{2}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})
.
By the variational definition of the LPP values, we also know that

(C.9)

\displaystyle H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}(x,y)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x,z)+G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z,y).

Let
x_{0}=1/(2(y-z_{0})^{2})
. The previous display implies that there is a random
z^{*,N}_{k}
such that

(C.10)

F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x_{0},z^{*,N}_{k}\sqrt{k})+G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z^{*,N}_{k}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})=H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}(x_{0},y\sqrt{k})=-(x_{0}-y\sqrt{k})^{2}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

We claim that

(C.11)

z^{*,N}_{k}=-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2x_{0}}}+y+\mathfrak{o}(1)=z_{0}+\mathfrak{o}(1)

and that
\{z^{*,N}_{k}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is tight. By (
C.9
), we know

G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\leq H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}(x,y\sqrt{k})-F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})

for all
x,y,z
. Taking
x=1/(2(y-z)^{2})
and the asymptotics
H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}(x,y\sqrt{k})=-(x-y\sqrt{k})^{2}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})
,
F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x,z\sqrt{k})=2\sqrt{2kx}+2\sqrt{k}xz+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})
yields that

(C.12)

G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\leq-y^{2}k-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k});

by taking
N\to\infty
and using
G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\to G_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})
in distribution we also obtain the upper bound half of the overall equality we are aiming to prove. Using this upper bound and the same asymptotics on
H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}
and
F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}
in (
C.10
) yields that the latter cannot hold if the claimed (
C.11
) does not hold. As with the tightness of
\smash{Z^{N}_{k}}
in Proposition
7.2
, the tightness of
\smash{z^{*,N}_{k}}
follows from the just established asymptotic and the monotonicity of the sequence in
k
from Lemma
C.1
.

It remains to prove a lower bound on
G_{k}(z\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})
matching the upper bound in (
C.12
). With (
C.11
) we see that

\displaystyle G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z^{*,N}_{k}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})

\displaystyle=H^{N,\mathrm{Br}}(x_{0},y\sqrt{k})-F^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(x_{0},z^{*,N}_{k}\sqrt{k})

\displaystyle=-(x_{0}-y\sqrt{k})^{2}-(2\sqrt{2x_{0}}+2x_{0}z^{*,N}_{k})\sqrt{k}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})

\displaystyle=-y^{2}k+2x_{0}y\sqrt{k}-(2\sqrt{2x_{0}}+2x_{0}(-(2x_{0})^{-1/2}+y))\sqrt{k}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

By simplifying, the previous line equals

(C.13)

\displaystyle G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}(z^{*,N}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})=-y^{2}k-\sqrt{2x_{0}k}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})

\displaystyle=-y^{2}k-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z_{0}}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

By using the tightness of
z^{*,N}_{k}
and the weak convergence of
G^{N,\mathrm{Br}}_{k}
to
G_{k}
, we obtain the existence of a
z^{*}_{k}
satisfying (
C.11
) and the previous display with
G_{k}
and
z^{*}_{k}
in place of their prelimiting analogs.

To go from
G_{k}(z^{*}_{k}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})
to
G_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})
, we use that
G_{k}(\bm{\cdot},y)
is monotonically decreasing by Lemma
C.2
, as well as the stationarity property from Lemma
C.6
. In the case that
z^{*}_{k}\geq z_{0}
, the monotonicity of
G_{k}(\bm{\cdot},y\sqrt{k})
along with (
C.13
) immediately yields that

(C.14)

\displaystyle G_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\geq G_{k}(z^{*}_{k}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\geq-y^{2}k-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z_{0}}+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k}).

In the case that
z^{*}_{k}<z_{0}
, we know from above that
z^{*}_{k}>z_{0}-\mathfrak{o}(1)
, i.e, for any
\varepsilon>0
it holds that
\sum_{k}\mathbb{P}\left(z^{*}_{k}<z_{0}-\varepsilon\right)<\infty
. We fix
\varepsilon>0
, and apply Lemma
C.6
along with monotonicity of
G_{k}(\bm{\cdot},y\sqrt{k})
again to obtain that, for any deterministic sequence
M_{k}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(G_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})<M_{k}-\varepsilon\sqrt{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(G_{k}((z_{0}-\varepsilon)\sqrt{k},(y-\varepsilon)\sqrt{k})<M_{k}-(\varepsilon^{2}-2\varepsilon y)k-\varepsilon\sqrt{k}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(G_{k}(z^{*}\sqrt{k},(y-\varepsilon)\sqrt{k})<M_{k}-(\varepsilon^{2}-2\varepsilon y)k-\varepsilon\sqrt{k}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\left(z^{*}_{k}<z_{0}-\varepsilon\right).

Now by (
C.13
), when
M_{k}=-(y-\varepsilon)^{2}k-\sqrt{k}/(y-\varepsilon-z_{0})+(\varepsilon^{2}-2\varepsilon y)k
, the first term in the previous display is summable, and we already noted that the second is also summable. We can write

\displaystyle M_{k}=-y^{2}k-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{y-z_{0}}-\varepsilon\sqrt{k}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{(y-z_{0})(y-z_{0}-\varepsilon)}\right),

and the parenthetical term is positive and
O(1)
. Thus we have established that
\mathbb{P}(G_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})<-y^{2}k-\sqrt{k}/(y-z_{0})-\varepsilon\sqrt{k})
is summable for every
\varepsilon>0
, i.e.,
G_{k}(z_{0}\sqrt{k},y\sqrt{k})\geq-y^{2}k-\sqrt{k}/(y-z_{0})+\mathfrak{o}(\sqrt{k})
.
This, along with (
C.14
) and (
C.12
), completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Directed landscape and KPZ fixed point convergence

In this section we prove Corollaries
2.12
(ASEP) and
2.22
(S6V), using an idea communicated to us by Shalin Parekh; it was also used by him in the work
[
Par23
]
establishing joint convergence of ASEP in the weakly asymmetric regime under multiple initial conditions with the basic coupling to the KPZ equation with multiple initial conditions driven by the same noise. We prove Corollary
2.12
in Section
D.1
and Corollary
2.22
in Section
D.2
. The latter relies on an approximate monotonicity result (Proposition
D.3
) which is proved in Section
D.3
, and a finite speed of discrepancy bound (Lemma
D.4
) proved in Section
D.4
.

D.1.
ASEP to directed landscape

We start by giving a lemma on height monotonicity for ASEP under the basic coupling. Recall the definition from below (
2.10
) of the ASEP height function
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},0;\bm{\cdot},t)
when started from an arbitrary height function
h_{0}
.

Lemma D.1

(Height monotonicity of ASEP)
.

Let
H\in\mathbb{Z}
be an integer and
h_{0},h_{0}^{\prime}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
be Bernoulli paths such that
h_{0}(x)+H\geq h_{0}^{\prime}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, under the basic coupling, almost surely,
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},0;x,t)+H\geq h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0}^{\prime},0;x,t)
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}
and
t>0
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the definitions of the basic coupling in Section
2.2.6
and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0},0;\bm{\cdot},t)
from below (
2.10
).
∎

We will also need to use the following basic observation, whose proof is trivial and omitted.

Lemma D.2
.

Let
X,Y
be random variables such that
\mathbb{E}[|X|],\mathbb{E}[|Y|]<\infty
. Suppose
X\geq Y
almost surely and
\mathbb{E}[X]=\mathbb{E}[Y]
. Then
X=Y
almost surely.

Proof of Corollary
2.12
(1) & (2).

Corollary
2.12
(2) follows immediately from (1), so we only need to establish the latter.
The convergence of
(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<})
to
(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<})
in the space
\smash{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})^{\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}}
is equivalent to the convergence

(D.1)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<}\right)\xrightarrow{\smash{d}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<}\right)

for every finite subset
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
. Fix such a
\mathcal{U}
and write
\mathcal{U}=\{s_{i}:i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\}
with
0\leq s_{1}<\ldots<s_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We first observe that
(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<})
is a tight sequence in
\varepsilon
. This follows from combining the fact that, by Definitions
2.7
and
2.10
,

(D.2)

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}(t-s)^{1/3}\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{ASEP},(t-s)^{-1}\varepsilon}((t-s)^{-2/3}x;(t-s)^{-2/3}y)

as processes in
(x,y)
, with Theorem
2.8
and the relation of
\mathcal{L}
with
\mathcal{S}
from Definition
2.4
(i), namely, for any
t>0
, and as processes in
x
and
y
,

(D.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}(t-s)^{1/3}\mathcal{S}((t-s)^{-2/3}x,(t-s)^{-2/3}y).

Let
(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<})
be any subsequential limit point of the lefthand side of (
D.1
). Then we further see from Theorem
2.8
, (
D.2
), and (
D.3
) that, for each
(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)
.
We next observe that
(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s_{i};\bm{\cdot},s_{i+1}):i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s_{i};\bm{\cdot},s_{i+1}):i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket)
, since each of the components of the lefthand vector are independent, and the same for the righthand vector.

With these facts in hand, (
D.1
) is reduced to showing that, for any
r,s,t\in\mathcal{U}
with
r<s<t
fixed, almost surely, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(D.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;y,t)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;z,s)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(z,s;y,t)\right);

this suffices since the righthand side is a deterministic function of the independent quantities
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},r;\bm{\cdot},s)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)
and the same relation holds with
\mathcal{L}
in place of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
.
We show (
D.4
) by returning to the prelimit. Fix
r,s,t
as above. We first claim that,
almost surely, for all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{Z}
(recall
\gamma=1-q
),

(D.5)

\begin{split}&h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,2\gamma^{-1}r\varepsilon^{-1};z,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1})+h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1};y,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1})\\
&\geq h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,2\gamma^{-1}r\varepsilon^{-1};y,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1}).\end{split}

This holds for
z\geq y
as both sides are equal at
z=y
, the first term on the lefthand side is non-increasing and decreases by at most 1 in going from
z
to
z+1
, and the second term on the lefthand side is

h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1};y,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1})=(z-y)\mathbbm{1}_{y\leq z}

and so increases by
1
on going from
z
to
z+1
. Similarly, for
z\leq y
, we have equality in (
D.5
) at
z=y
, the first term on the lefthand side weakly increases on going from
z
to
z-1
, and the second term on the lefthand side is
0
for all
z\leq y
.

We now view (
D.5
) as an ordering of initial conditions: for each
x,z
fixed, we regard the first term
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,2\gamma^{-1}r\varepsilon^{-1};z,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1})
as a (random) constant, and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1};\bm{\cdot},2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1})
and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,2\gamma^{-1}r\varepsilon^{-1};\bm{\cdot},2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1})
both as initial conditions. Evolving both for time
2\gamma^{-1}(t-s)\varepsilon^{-1}
under the basic coupling, Lemma
D.1
guarantees that, almost surely, for all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,2\gamma^{-1}r\varepsilon^{-1};z,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1})+h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,2\gamma^{-1}s\varepsilon^{-1};y,2\gamma^{-1}t\varepsilon^{-1})

\displaystyle\geq h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(x,2\gamma^{-1}r\varepsilon^{-1};y,2\gamma^{-1}t\varepsilon^{-1}).

Centering and rescaling according to Definition
2.10
, and taking
\varepsilon\to 0
, the previous display yields that, almost surely, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

(D.6)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;y,t)\geq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;z,s)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(z,s;y,t)\right).

However, we know that both sides of this inequality are marginally distributed as the corresponding expression with
\mathcal{L}
in place of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
and, further, we know that, almost surely, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

(D.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,r;y,t)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(x,r;z,s)+\mathcal{L}(z,s;y,t)\Bigr).

Thus, we know that the expectations of the lefthand and righthand sides of (
D.6
) are equal for every fixed
x,y
. Applying Lemma
D.2
with (
D.6
) yields that (
D.4
) holds for all
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
on a single probability one event; since both sides are continuous in
x
and
y
, we obtain that (
D.4
) holds on a probability one event for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.12
(3).

We start by recalling
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0};\bm{\cdot},t)
from Definition
2.11
, that
\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}(x):=2\varepsilon^{1/3}(x\varepsilon^{-2/3}-h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}(2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}))
, and that
\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}\to\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}
for each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
uniformly on compact sets. As in the proof of Corollary
2.12
(1) above, it is sufficient to prove that, for any finite
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0};\bm{\cdot},t):t\in\mathcal{U}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\left(\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0};\bm{\cdot},t):t\in\mathcal{U}\right),

where the righthand side is coupled via a common directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
as in (
2.4
). Fix such a
\mathcal{U}
. We will prove that

(D.8)

\begin{split}&\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0};\bm{\cdot},t),\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{U}\right)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\left(\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0};\cdot,t),\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{U}\right).\end{split}

First,
(\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0};\bm{\cdot},t):i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{U})
is a tight sequence in
\varepsilon
, and for each
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket,t\in\mathcal{U}
, we have the marginal convergence of
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(\smash{h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}};\bm{\cdot},t)
to
\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)};\bm{\cdot},t)
; the former follows from the latter, which in turn is given by
[
QS22
, Theorem 2.2 (2)]
. We also know from Corollary
2.12
(1) that

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{ASEP},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t):t\in\mathcal{U}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t):t\in\mathcal{U}\right).

Let
(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(0)}_{i};\bm{\cdot},t),\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},0;\bm{\cdot},t):t\in\mathcal{U})
be any subsequential limit point of the lefthand side of (
D.8
), so that the
\mathcal{L}
terms are coupled as the directed landscape and each
\smash{\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(0)}_{i};\bm{\cdot},t)}
is marginally distributed as the KPZ fixed point
\smash{\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0};\bm{\cdot},t)}
for each fixed
t\in\mathcal{U}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
.
Now, (
D.8
) is reduced to showing that, for any
t\in\mathcal{U}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
fixed, almost surely, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(D.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0};y,t)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}(z)+\mathcal{L}(z,0;y,t)\right).

As in the proof of Corollary
2.12
(1), we show (
D.9
) by returning to the prelimit. We first claim that, almost surely, for every
y,z\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,

(D.10)

\displaystyle h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}(z)+h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,0;y,0)\geq h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}(y).

As in the proof of Corollary
2.12
(1), this holds for
z\geq y
as both sides are equal at
z=y
, the first term on the lefthand side is non-increasing and decreases by at most 1 in going from
z
to
z+1
, and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,0;y,0)=(z-y)\mathbbm{1}_{y\leq z}
, so the second term on the lefthand side increases by
1
on going from
z
to
z+1
. Similarly, for
z\leq y
, we have equality at
z=y
, the first term on the lefthand side weakly increases on going from
z
to
z-1
, and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,0;y,0)=0
for all such
z
.

We now view (
D.10
) as an ordering of initial conditions: for each
z
fixed, we regard
h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}(z)
as a (random) constant and both
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,0;\bm{\cdot},0)
and
\smash{h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}(\bm{\cdot})}
as initial conditions. Evolving both for time
2\gamma^{-1}t\varepsilon^{-1}
(recall
\gamma=1-q
) under the basic coupling, Lemma
D.1
guarantees that, almost surely, for all
y,z\in\mathbb{Z}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle h^{(i),\varepsilon}_{0}(z)+h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(z,0;y,2\gamma^{-1}t\varepsilon^{-1})\geq h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0}^{(i),\varepsilon},0;y,2\gamma^{-1}t\varepsilon^{-1}).

Centering and rescaling according to Definition
2.11
, and taking
\varepsilon\to 0
, the previous display yields that, almost surely, for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

(D.11)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0};y,t)\geq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}(z)+\mathcal{L}(z,0;y,t)\right).

However, we know that both sides of this inequality are marginally distributed as
\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0};y,t)
(for the righthand side, using (
2.4
)).
Thus, the expectations of the lefthand and righthand sides of (
D.11
) are equal for every fixed
y
, so Lemma
D.2
implies that the two sides are equal on a probability 1 event for all
y\in\mathbb{Q}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
; since both sides are continuous in
y
, this yields equality for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
i\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket
, completing the proof.
∎

D.2.
S6V to directed landscape

The basic coupling for S6V does not have the height monotonicity properties it does in ASEP (Lemma
D.1
). Nevertheless, an approximate version holds, as recorded next. It will be proved in Section
D.3
.

Proposition D.3

(Approximate height monotonicity of S6V)
.

Fix
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
H\in\mathbb{Z}
and let
\smash{h_{0}^{(1)},h_{0}^{(2)}}:\llbracket-N-1,\infty\Rparen\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be two Bernoulli paths such that
\smash{h_{0}^{(1)}(x)}+H\geq\smash{h_{0}^{(2)}(x)}
for all
x\in\llbracket-N-1,\infty\Rparen
, and, for
i=1,2
,
\smash{h_{0}^{(i)}}(-N-1)\leq N
and
\smash{h_{0}^{(i)}}(x)=0
for all large
x
. There exist absolute constants
C,c>0
such that, for
t\in\mathbb{N}
and under the basic coupling, the following holds. For any
M\geq(\log N)^{2}
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cM)
,
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h_{0}^{(1)};x,t)+H\geq h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h_{0}^{(2)};x,t)-M
for all
x\in\llbracket-N-1,\infty\Rparen
.

We will also need a finite speed of discrepancy estimate for the S6V model under the basic coupling. Its straightforward proof will be given in Section
D.4
.

Lemma D.4

(Finite speed of discrepancy)
.

For any real numbers
0\leq b^{\shortuparrow}\leq b^{\shortrightarrow}<1
, there exist constants
c>0
and
C>1
such that the following holds. Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
be an integer, and for
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
s_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
,
N_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, and
h^{(i),k}_{0}:\llbracket-N_{i},\infty\Rparen\to\mathbb{Z}
be Bernoulli paths which equal
0
for all large
x
. Assume that
N_{1}\leq N_{2}
and, for
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
,
h^{(1),k}_{0}(x)=h^{(2),k}_{0}(x)
for all
x\in\llbracket-N_{1},N_{1}\rrbracket
. For
i\in\{1,2\}
and
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
, let
(j^{(i),k}_{(x,y)}:(x,y)\in\llbracket-N_{i},\infty\Rparen\times\llbracket s_{k},\infty\Rparen)
denote samples from stochastic six-vertex models with boundary conditions given by
\smash{h^{(i),k}_{0}}
at time
s_{k}
(recall from Section
2.3.3
), all coupled via the basic coupling. Then, for any
T\in\mathbb{N}
such that
T\leq\frac{1}{4}(1-b^{\shortrightarrow})N_{1}
, with probability at least
1-Cme^{-cN_{1}}
, we have that
j^{(1),k}_{(x,y)}=j^{(2),k}_{(x,y)}
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket
and
(x,y)\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket\times\llbracket-\lfloor\frac{1}{2}N_{1}\rfloor,\lfloor\frac{1}{2}N_{1}\rfloor\rrbracket
.

With these two statements in hand, we can give the proof of Corollary
2.22
along very similar lines as the proof of Corollary
2.12
(1).

Proof of Corollary
2.22
.

Item (2) is an immediate consequence of (1), so we only need to prove the latter. We first claim that we may assume that
N\leq\varepsilon^{-3}
, where recall
\varepsilon
is the parameter in Definition
2.21
of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
. More precisely, if
N>\varepsilon^{-3}
, we may couple an ASEP landscape
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
with that value of
N
to a copy
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
with
N=\varepsilon^{-3}
such that, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-c\varepsilon^{-3})
,
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x,s;y,t)=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x,s;y,t)
or all
|x|,|y|,|s|,|t|\leq\varepsilon^{-1/6}
. As in the proof of Theorem
2.20
in Section
7.2
, the existence of this coupling amounts to a finite speed of discrepancy bound; because we need it for the basic coupling and not the colored coupling as there, we use Lemma
D.4
. We record the parameters we will apply Lemma
D.4
with:
T=\lceil\varepsilon^{-7/6}\rceil
,
N_{1}=\varepsilon^{-3}
,
N_{2}
the value of
N
associated to
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
, and
m=T\cdot(2N_{1}+1)
. The height functions we consider are, at each time
s\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket
, the collection of step initial conditions starting from
x
for
x\in\llbracket-N_{1},N_{1}\rrbracket
, where the step initial condition from
x
means, for
i\in\{1,2\}
and in system
i
, particles at every location in
\llbracket x,N_{i}\rrbracket
(note that this indeed yields
m
many height functions).
Lemma
D.4
then yields the claimed coupling. In the remainder of the proof we assume
N=\varepsilon^{-3}
by working on the event of the above coupling holding, and we denote by
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
the associated S6V landscape. Since the probability of the coupling holding goes to
1
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, it suffices to show that
(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<})
.

Now, the convergence of
(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<})
to
(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<})
in the space
\smash{\mathcal{C}(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathbb{R})^{\mathcal{T}^{2}_{<}}}
is equivalent to the convergence

(D.12)

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<}\right)\xrightarrow{\smash{d}}\left(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<}\right)

for every finite subset
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{T}
. Fix such a
\mathcal{U}
and write
\mathcal{U}=\{s_{i}:i\in\llbracket 1,n\rrbracket\}
with
0\leq s_{1}<\ldots<s_{n}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

We observe that
(\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<})
is a tight sequence in
\varepsilon
. This follows from combining the fact that, by Definitions
2.19
and
2.21
,

(D.13)

\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}(t-s)^{1/3}\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{S6V},(t-s)^{-1}\varepsilon}((t-s)^{-2/3}x;(t-s)^{-2/3}y)

as processes in
(x,y)
, with Theorem
2.20
and the relation of
\mathcal{L}
with
\mathcal{S}
noted in (
D.3
).

Let
(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t):(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<})
be any subsequential limit point of the lefthand side of (
D.12
). Then we further see from Theorem
2.20
, (
D.3
), and (
D.13
) that, for each
(s,t)\in\mathcal{U}^{2}_{<}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)
.
We next observe that
(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s_{i};\bm{\cdot},s_{i+1}):i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}(\mathcal{L}(\bm{\cdot},s_{i};\bm{\cdot},s_{i+1}):i\in\llbracket 1,n-1\rrbracket)
, since each of the components of the lefthand vector are independent, and the same for the righthand vector.

With these facts in hand, (
D.12
) is reduced to showing that, for any
r,s,t\in\mathcal{U}
with
r<s<t
fixed, almost surely, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(D.14)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;y,t)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;z,s)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(z,s;y,t)\right);

this suffices since the righthand side is a deterministic function of the independent quantities
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},r;\bm{\cdot},s)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(\bm{\cdot},s;\bm{\cdot},t)
and the same relation holds with
\mathcal{L}
in place of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
.
We show (
D.14
) by returning to the prelimit. Fix
r,s,t
as above. We first claim that almost surely, for all
x,y,z\in\llbracket-N-1,\infty\Rparen
,

(D.15)

\begin{split}h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,r\varepsilon^{-1};z,s\varepsilon^{-1})+h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(z,s\varepsilon^{-1};y,s\varepsilon^{-1})\geq h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,r\varepsilon^{-1};y,s\varepsilon^{-1}).\end{split}

This holds for
z\geq y\geq-N-1
as both sides are equal at
z=y
, the first term on the lefthand side is non-increasing and decreases by at most 1 in going from
z
to
z+1
, and the second term on the lefthand side is

h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(z,s\varepsilon^{-1};y,s\varepsilon^{-1})=(z-y)\mathbbm{1}_{-N-1\leq y\leq z}

and so increases by
1
on going from
z
to
z+1
. Similarly, for
-N-1\leq z\leq y
, we have equality in (
D.15
) at
z=y
, the first term on the lefthand side weakly increases on going from
z
to
z-1
, and the second term on the lefthand side is
0
for all
-N-1\leq z\leq y
.

We now view (
D.15
) as an ordering of initial conditions: for each
x,z
fixed, we regard the first term
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,r\varepsilon^{-1};z,s\varepsilon^{-1})
as a (random) constant, and
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(z,s\varepsilon^{-1};\bm{\cdot},s\varepsilon^{-1})
and
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,r\varepsilon^{-1};\bm{\cdot},s\varepsilon^{-1})
both as initial conditions. Evolving both for time
(t-s)\varepsilon^{-1}
under the basic coupling, Proposition
D.3
and a union bound over
x,z\in\llbracket-N-1,N\rrbracket
guarantees that there exist
c,C>0
such that, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-c(\log N)^{2})
, for all
x,y,z\in\llbracket-N-1,N\rrbracket
,

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,r\varepsilon^{-1};z,s\varepsilon^{-1})+h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(z,s\varepsilon^{-1};y,t\varepsilon^{-1})\geq h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(x,r\varepsilon^{-1};y,t\varepsilon^{-1})-(\log N)^{2}.

Next we center and rescale according to Definition
2.21
to obtain
\mathcal{L}^{\mathrm{S6V},\varepsilon}
and note that since
N\geq 2\alpha\varepsilon^{-1}
, the arguments of
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}
still lie within the domain where the previous display holds. Recall also that the rescaling involves both sides being multiplied by
\varepsilon^{1/3}
, and that
N=\varepsilon^{-3}
; thus, the
(\log N)^{2}
term vanishes in the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit. So by taking
\varepsilon\to 0
, the previous display yields that, almost surely, for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;y,t)\geq\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(x,r;z,s)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}}(z,s;y,t)\right).

However, we know that both sides of this inequality are marginally distributed as the corresponding expression with
\mathcal{L}
in place of
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}
, so we further know from (
D.7
) that the expectations of the two sides are equal. Now, Lemma
D.2
yields that (
D.14
) holds for all
x,y\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
on a single probability one event; since both sides are continuous in
x
and
y
, we obtain that (
D.14
) holds on a probability one event for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. This completes the proof.
∎

D.3.
Approximate monotonicity for S6V

Here we give the proof of Proposition
D.3
.
