Figure 26
.
The left subfigure in each panel, with red arrows, represents a vertex in system 1, and the right subfigure, with blue arrows, represents a vertex in system 2; notice that each system is a uncolored, so here blue and red do not represent colors (in the sense of priority) associated to the arrows as in the main text of the paper. Black edges represent an absence of an arrow. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, in system
i
, the label of the horizontally incoming arrow is
p^{(i)}
, and that of the vertically incoming arrow is
r^{(i)}
. The outgoing labels are assigned as depicted.

Proof of Proposition
D.3
.

We break up the proof into a few parts. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, we will refer to the S6V model with boundary condition
\smash{h^{(i)}_{0}}
as “system
i
” (though note that the two systems are coupled via the basic coupling and so use the same Bernoulli random variables). Throughout this proof, we assume that
H=0
; otherwise we may place
H
arrows at
+\infty
in system 1 at time
0
if
H>0
, or
-H
arrows at
+\infty
in system 2 if
H<0
.

Specification of trajectories.
Given a sample from the S6V model started from
h^{(1)}_{0}
and
h^{(2)}_{0}
coupled via the basic coupling, we define arrow trajectories as follows. We define them in a local way: for every pair of input and output configurations of arrows at a vertex, we define how to pair the inputs with the outputs; this extends to define arrow trajectories globally. We assume that the incoming arrows have labels, and we specify the assignment of the same labels to the outgoing arrows. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, we use the notation
\smash{\ell^{(i),*}_{\mathrm{h}}}
for
*\in\{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{out}\}
for the label of the arrow horizontally incoming and horizontally outgoing, respectively, in the
i
th
system and
\smash{\ell^{(i),*}_{\mathrm{v}}}
for
*\in\{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{out}\}
for the label of the arrow vertically incoming and vertically outgoing, respectively, in the
i
th
system.

In the case that in each system a single arrow or no arrow is incident at a vertex, the pairing is obvious. If two arrows are incident at a vertex in both, then we assign
\ell^{(i),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{h}}=\ell^{(i),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{v}}
and
\smash{\ell^{(i),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{v}}=\ell^{(i),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{h}}}
for each
i\in\{1,2\}
; see the left panel of Figure
26
. If in one system no arrows are incident at a vertex
v
and in the other two arrows are incident at
v
, we do the pairing in the latter system (system
j
) as in the case where two arrows are present in both systems, i.e.,
\ell^{(j),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{h}}=\ell^{(j),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{v}}
and
\smash{\ell^{(j),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{v}}=\ell^{(j),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{h}}}
.

This leaves the case that one arrow is incident at a vertex
v
in one system and two arrows are incident at
v
in the other. We give the pairing in the case that the first system (with one arrow incident) is system 1 and the second system is system 2, and the analogous pairing is prescribed in the other case. If the arrow enters and exits horizontally in system
1
, then we set
\ell^{(i),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{h}}=\ell^{(i),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{h}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
(this is the only option in the
i=1
case) and
\smash{\ell^{(2),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{v}}=\ell^{(2),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{v}}}
; see middle panel of Figure
26
. If the arrow enters horizontally and exits vertically in system
1
, then we set
\smash{\ell^{(i),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{v}}=\ell^{(i),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{h}}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
\ell^{(2),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{h}}=\ell^{(2),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{h}}
; see right panel of Figure
26
. If the arrow in system 1 enters vertically and exits horizontally, we set
\smash{\ell^{(i),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{h}}=\ell^{(i),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{v}}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
\smash{\ell^{(2),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{v}}=\ell^{(2),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{h}}}
, and if it enters vertically and exits vertically, we set
\smash{\ell^{(i),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{v}}=\ell^{(i),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{v}}}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
\smash{\ell^{(2),\mathrm{out}}_{\mathrm{h}}=\ell^{(2),\mathrm{in}}_{\mathrm{h}}}
.

The definition is made such that if the trajectory of an arrow in system 1 coincides at some edge with the trajectory of an arrow in system 2, then the two trajectories agree from then onwards, as can be easily checked; we will refer to this situation as the two arrows “coupling.”

Reduction to claim about arrow position ordering.
For
t\in\mathbb{N}
fixed and
i\in\{1,2\}
, consider the set
\mathrm{Uncoupled}_{i}
of arrows in system
i
which have not coupled with any arrow of the other system by time
t
, and let
U_{i}=\#\mathrm{Uncoupled}_{i}
. Note that
U_{1}\geq U_{2}
. For
s<t
,
i\in\{1,2\}
, and
k\in\llbracket 1,U_{i}\rrbracket
, let us label by
X^{(i)}_{s}(k)
the location at time
s
of the arrow in
\mathrm{Uncoupled}_{i}
which, among arrows from the same set, is
k
th
from the bottom in the initial configuration
\eta^{(i)}_{0}
. Note that
X^{(1)}_{0}(k)\geq X^{(2)}_{0}(k)
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,U_{2}\rrbracket
.

We first claim that, if
X^{(2)}_{t}(k)\leq X^{(1)}_{t}(k+M)
for all
k\in\llbracket 1,\min(U_{1}-M,U_{2})\rrbracket
, then
h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h^{(2)}_{0};x,t)\leq h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h^{(1)}_{0};x,t)+M
for all
x\in\llbracket-N-1,\infty\Rparen
. Indeed, since arrows coupled by time
t
are counted in the height functions of both systems,

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h^{(2)}_{0};x,t)-h^{\mathrm{S6V}}(h^{(1)}_{0};x,t)

\displaystyle=\#\left\{k\in\llbracket 1,U_{2}\rrbracket:X^{(2)}_{t}(k)>x\right\}-\#\left\{k\in\llbracket 1,U_{1}\rrbracket:X^{(1)}_{t}(k)>x\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\#\left\{k\in\llbracket 1,\min(U_{1}-M,U_{2})\rrbracket:X^{(1)}_{t}(k+M)>x\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad-\#\left\{k\in\llbracket 1,U_{1}\rrbracket:X^{(1)}_{t}(k)>x\right\}+M\leq M.

So it suffices to show that there exist
c>0
such that, for any fixed
k
and
M
,

(D.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(X^{(2)}_{t}(k)>X^{(1)}_{t}(k+M)\right)\leq N\exp(-cM);

indeed, then for any
M\geq(\log N)^{2}
we may do a union bound over
k\in\llbracket 1,\min(U_{1}-M,U_{2})\rrbracket
, noting that the hypothesis that
\smash{h^{(i)}_{0}(-N-1)}\leq N
for
i\in\{1,2\}
implies that
U_{1},U_{2}\leq N
(and using that
N^{2}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}c(\log N)^{2})
is bounded by an absolute constant for all
N
).

Reduction to overtaking event for totally uncoupled arrow.
We introduce some terminology to show (
D.16
). We will say an arrow in system 2 “overtakes” an arrow in system 1 at a location
v
when the following situation occurs:
v
lies on the trajectory of the specified arrow in system 2 and

(D.17)

\displaystyle(a_{v}^{(1)},i_{v}^{(1)};b_{v}^{(1)},j_{v}^{(1)})=(0,1;0,1)\quad\text{and}\quad(a_{v}^{(2)},i_{v}^{(2)};b_{v}^{(2)},j_{v}^{(2)})=(1,0;1,0),

where for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
(a_{v}^{(i)},i_{v}^{(i)};b_{v}^{(i)},j_{v}^{(i)})
are the vertically entering, horizontally entering, vertically exiting, and horizontally exiting arrows, respectively, at vertex
v
in system
i
. Note that this implies that the arrows in both systems are uncoupled up to the point of exiting
v
.

We claim that, on the event in (
D.16
), it must be the case that at at least
M
vertices, the arrow in system 2 corresponding to
X^{\smash{(2)}}_{t}(k)
overtook an arrow in system
1
. Indeed, this follows from the observation that, for any
j
such that
\smash{X^{(2)}_{0}(k)\leq X^{(1)}_{0}(j)}
, if the arrow corresponding to
\smash{X^{\smash{(2)}}_{t}(k)}
does not overtake the arrow corresponding to
X_{t}^{(1)}(j)
at any vertex, then
X^{(2)}_{t}(k)\leq X^{(1)}_{t}(j)
; this is because uncoupled arrows cannot cross except by one overtaking the other as in (
D.17
).

Note that the terminology of “overtaking” does not include cases such as
(a_{v}^{(1)},i_{v}^{(1)};b_{v}^{(1)},j_{v}^{(1)})=(0,1;0,1)
and
\smash{(a_{v}^{(2)},i_{v}^{(2)};b_{v}^{(2)},j_{v}^{(2)})}=(1,1;1,1)
(see, e.g., arrows labeled
p^{(1)}
and
r^{(2)}
in middle panel of Figure
26
) or
(a_{v}^{(1)},i_{v}^{(1)};b_{v}^{(1)},j_{v}^{(1)})=(1,1;1,1)
and
(a_{v}^{(2)},i_{v}^{(2)};b_{v}^{(2)},j_{v}^{(2)})=(1,0;1,0)
which are perhaps also suggested by the terminology; this is due to the fact that such configurations correspond to the crossing of a coupled arrow with an uncoupled one, which is not relevant to our arguments (which only concern the relative orderings of the uncoupled paths in the systems).

Reduction to overtaking event for an initially uncoupled arrow.
Fix an
x\in\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen
such that
\eta_{0}^{(2)}(x)=1
and
\eta_{0}^{(1)}(x)=0
. Consider the arrow in system 2 starting from
x
at time
0
; in particular, the arrow is initially uncoupled (but may couple later). We will refer to it as “arrow
x
.” We will show the following: there exists
c>0
such that

(D.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\exists v_{1},\ldots,v_{M}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\llbracket-N,\infty\Rparen:\eqref{e.overtake definition}\text{ occurs at each }v_{i}\text{ for arrow }x\Bigr)\leq\exp(-cM).

Taking a union bound over the at most
N
values of
x
with the just imposed condition will then yield (
D.16
), since the arrow corresponding to
X^{\smash{(2)}}_{t}(k)
must be arrow
x
for some such
x
.

We call a vertex
v
“potentially coupling” for arrow
x
if
(a_{v}^{(1)},i_{v}^{(1)})=(0,1)
and
(a_{v}^{(2)},i_{v}^{(2)})=(1,0)
, i.e., in system 1 an arrow enters
v
horizontally and no arrow enters vertically, and in system 2 arrow
x
enters
v
vertically and no arrow enters horizontally.
We say that arrow
x
in system 2 passes through a potentially coupling vertex
v
without coupling if
v
is potentially coupling for arrow
x
and
(B_{v}^{\shortuparrow},B_{v}^{\shortrightarrow})\in\{(1,1),(0,0)\}
, i.e, the output arrow configurations at
v
differ in the two systems: the arrow in system
1
exits horizontally and arrow
x
in system
2
exits vertically, or vice versa. Recall that if arrow
x
in system 2 couples at a potentially coupling vertex
v
, i.e., the output direction is the same at
v
in both systems, then for the rest of arrow
x
’s trajectory it agrees with the arrow in system 1 incident at
v
; in particular, it cannot overtake any further arrow in system
1
(as defined in (
D.17
)).

We see that the event whose probability we are bounding is contained in the event that there exist at least
M
potentially coupling vertices for arrow
x
and it does not couple at at least the first
M
of them; here, “first” is under the usual component-wise partial order, i.e.,
w<v
for
v,w\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2}
(with
v=(v_{1},v_{2})
and
w=(w_{1},w_{2})
) if (i)
w_{1}<v_{1}
or (ii)
w_{1}=v_{1}
and
w_{2}<v_{2}
. Observe that the event of a given vertex
v
being potentially coupling for arrow
x
is determined by the Bernoulli random variables associated to vertices
w<v
. That is,
\{v\text{ is potentially coupling for arrow }x\}\in\sigma(\{B_{w}^{\shortrightarrow},B_{w}^{\shortuparrow}:w<v\})
. A consequence is that, conditional on
v
being potentially coupling for arrow
x
, the random variables
(B_{v}^{\shortrightarrow},B_{v}^{\shortuparrow})
are distributed as a pair of independent Bernoulli random variables of parameters
b^{\shortrightarrow}
and
b^{\shortuparrow}
, respectively (as they are independent of the random variables
(B_{w}^{\shortrightarrow},B_{w}^{\shortuparrow})_{w<v}
).

The probability bound.
Let
\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\ldots
be the (random) coordinates of arrow
x
’s potentially coupling vertices in order of appearance along its trajectory; if there is no
j
th
potentially coupling vertex, we define
\tau_{j}=(\infty,\infty)
. Define the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{i}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{i}:=\Bigl\{\mathsf{E}\cap\{\tau_{i}=v\}:v\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2},\mathsf{E}\in\sigma\bigl(\{B_{w}^{\shortuparrow},B_{w}^{\shortrightarrow}:w\leq v\}\bigr)\Bigr\}.

Let
\mathsf{NC}_{i}
(short for “no coupling”) be the event that
\tau_{i}\neq(\infty,\infty)
and arrow
x
passes through
\tau_{i}
without coupling, and let
\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,j\rrbracket}=\cap_{i=1}^{j}\mathsf{NC}_{i}
for each
j
. Note that
\mathsf{NC}_{i},\tau_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{i}
for each
i
. Next note that

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NC}_{1})\leq b^{\shortrightarrow}b^{\shortuparrow}+(1-b^{\shortrightarrow})(1-b^{\shortuparrow})=:\delta<1.

We claim that
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NC}_{i}\mid\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket})\leq\delta
for each
i
. By the tower property of conditional expectations, this is implied by the stronger inequality

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NC}_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1})\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}}\leq\delta\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}}.

The latter holds since, conditional on
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
,
\{\tau_{i}=v\}
is determined by the collection of random variables
\{B_{w}^{\shortrightarrow},B_{w}^{\shortuparrow}:\tau_{i-1}\leq w<v\}
; in particular, letting
\mathsf{E}_{v}
be the event that
(B_{v}^{\shortrightarrow},B_{v}^{\shortuparrow})=(1,1)
or
(0,0)
, we see that
\mathsf{E}_{v}
is conditionally independent of
\{\tau_{i}=v\}
given
\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
, so

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{NC}_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v>\tau_{i-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{E}_{v},\tau_{i}=v\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{v>\tau_{i-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{E}_{v}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\tau_{i}=v\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}}

\displaystyle=\delta\sum_{v>\tau_{i-1}}\mathbb{P}\left(\tau_{i}=v\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}}\leq\delta\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}}.

Thus it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket}\right)=\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{NC}_{1})\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{M}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathsf{NC}_{i}\mid\mathsf{NC}_{\llbracket 1,i-1\rrbracket}\right)\leq\delta^{M}.

This establishes (
D.18
) and completes the proof.
∎

D.4.
Finite speed of discrepancy for S6V under basic coupling

Here we give the proof of Lemma
D.4
; it bears similarities to that of
[
Agg20
, Proposition 2.17]
.

Proof of Lemma
D.4
.

We will prove the statement for the case of
m=1
and use a union bound to obtain the case of general
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Since we are in the
m=1
case, we may assume without loss of generality that
s_{1}=1
, and we drop the second superscript in the boundary conditions and write them, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, as
h_{0}^{(i)}
, and analogously we write
(j^{(i)}_{(x,y)})_{(x,y)\in\llbracket-N_{i},\infty\Rparen\times\llbracket s,\infty\Rparen}
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
, for the samples from the S6V models with respective boundary conditions
\smash{h_{0}^{(i)}}
(coupled via the basic coupling).
We adopt the convention for arrow trajectories in each system (i.e., the two S6V models with initial conditions
h_{0}^{(1)}
and
h_{0}^{(2)}
) from the beginning of the proof of Proposition
D.3
from Section
D.3
(recall Figure
26
): in particular, all arrows in the two systems which start from the same positions (which recall we refer to as being “coupled”) evolve first, and then the initially uncoupled arrows evolve subsequently, “bouncing” off of other uncoupled arrows and bouncing off or passing through coupled arrows. Recall that arrows which are uncoupled at least until a given time in their trajectories maintain their initial ordering until at least that time. Observe that all arrows which initially start in either system in
\llbracket-N_{1},N_{1}\rrbracket
are coupled by assumption.

Let
k
be the largest value in
\llbracket-N_{2},-N_{1}-1\rrbracket
such that there is an uncoupled arrow present at
(1,k)
in the configuration
\smash{\eta_{h_{0}^{(2)}}}
corresponding to
h_{0}^{(2)}
(as in (
2.21
)) (thus there is no arrow at
(1,k)
in
\eta_{h_{0}^{(1)}}
). Now, we see that the event that
j^{(1)}_{(x,y)}\neq j^{(2)}_{(x,y)}
for some
(x,y)\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket\times\llbracket-\frac{1}{2}N_{1},\frac{1}{2}N_{1}\rrbracket
is contained in the event that the uncoupled arrow starting initially at
(1,k)
in
\smash{\eta_{h_{0}^{(2)}}}
is incident on a vertex in
\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket\times\{-\lfloor\frac{1}{2}N_{1}\rfloor\}
.

For any
y,t\in\mathbb{N}
, consider the event
\mathsf{E}_{y,t}
that the uncoupled arrow exits horizontally from
(t,x)
and then exits horizontally from
(t+1,x+y^{\prime})
for some
y^{\prime}\geq y
, i.e., it travels vertical distance at least
y
in the time step
t
to
t+1
. Let
n
be the number of coupled arrows that exit horizontally between
(t,x+1)
and
(t,x+y-1)
(inclusive). Now, if
\mathsf{E}_{y,t}
occurs, it implies that all
n
of these coupled arrows passed straight through the vertex they horizontally entered on the vertical line
t+1
, and the uncoupled arrow passed vertically through at least
y-n-1
many vertices which had no coupled arrow incident (it must also have not passed horizontally through
(t+1,x)
, but we will not include this in the probability upper bound). Since all these sub-events are independent, the probability of
\mathsf{E}_{y,t}
occurring is at most
(b^{\shortrightarrow})^{n}(b^{\shortuparrow})^{y-n-1}\leq(b^{\shortrightarrow})^{y-1}
, since
b^{\shortuparrow}\leq b^{\shortrightarrow}
.

Thus the distance the arrow starting at
(1,k)
travels vertically in the horizontal interval
\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket
is bounded by a sum of
T
independent random variables
(X_{i})_{i\in\llbracket 1,T\rrbracket}
, each distributed as
\mathbb{P}\left(X_{i}\geq r\right)=(b^{\shortrightarrow})^{r-1}
for
r\in\mathbb{N}
. This sum has expectation
(1-b^{\shortrightarrow})^{-1}T
. So the probability that this sum exceeds
\frac{1}{2}N_{1}
, under the assumed condition that
T\leq\frac{1}{4}(1-b^{\shortrightarrow})N_{1}
, is bounded by
\exp(-cN_{1})
by standard concentration inequalities for sums of independent geometric random variables (e.g.,
[
Jan18
, Theorem 2.1]
), completing the proof.
∎

Appendix E
Decoupling along characteristics

In this section we give the proofs of Corollaries
2.15
and
2.16
. The former will be proved in Section
E.1
as a consequence of an analog of it for the limiting objects, KPZ fixed points (Proposition
E.1
), which will also be proven in the same section. Corollary
2.16
will be proven in Section
E.2
, using an asymptotic independence statement for the stationary horizon (Lemma
E.6
), which in turn will be proven in Section
E.3
.

E.1.
Asymptotic independence for the KPZ fixed point

Let
\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}(x,t)=\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)},0;x,t)
be the KPZ fixed point started from
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}
, coupled via the directed landscape, i.e., for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

(E.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}(x,t)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)\right).

The following is the analogous statement of Corollary
2.15
for the limiting objects, namely, KPZ fixed points coupled via the directed landscape. It will be proven later in this section.

Proposition E.1
.

Fix
R>0
and
T\geq 1
. For any
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
i\in\{1,2\}
, suppose
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{0},\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are independent (in particular, they may be deterministic), continuous, and satisfy (
2.12
) with
\beta
and
R
almost surely. There exist constants
C,\delta>0
depending on
R
and
T
such that the following holds. (1) There exist independent processes
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}:\mathbb{R}\times[T^{-1},T]\to\mathbb{R}
and a coupling with
(\mathfrak{h}^{(1)},\mathfrak{h}^{(2)})
such that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

(E.2)

\lim_{|\beta|\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in[T^{-1},T]}\sup_{|x|\leq\delta|\beta|^{1/2}}\left|\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}(x,s)-\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)}(x,s)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right)=0.

(2) There exist independent processes
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{char}},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{char}}:\mathbb{R}\times[T^{-1},T]\to\mathbb{R}
and a coupling with
(\mathfrak{h}^{(1)},\mathfrak{h}^{(2)})
such that

(E.3)

\begin{split}\lim_{|\beta|\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in[T^{-1},T]}\sup_{|x|\leq\delta|\beta|^{1/2}t}\left|\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}(x,s)-\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}_{\mathrm{char}}(x,s)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right)&=0\quad\text{and}\\
\lim_{|\beta|\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in[T^{-1},T]}\sup_{|x+\beta s|\leq\delta\beta^{1/2}t}\left|\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}(x,s)-\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{char}}(x,s)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right)&=0.\end{split}

Now we give the proof of Corollary
2.15
, which is essentially an immediate consequence of Proposition
E.1
.

Proof of Corollary
2.15
.

We prove (
2.13
); an analogous argument holds for (
2.14
). First recall from Corollary
2.12
that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon}
converges in distribution (in the product topology, across
\mathcal{T}
, of uniform convergence on compact sets), as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to
\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}
as given by (
E.1
), coupled across
i\in\{1,2\}
via the same directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
. Note that (
2.13
) is a statement about the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit, so we may define any processes
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i),\varepsilon}
and coupling of
(\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon})_{i\in\{1,2\}}
with
(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i),\varepsilon})_{i\in\{1,2\}}
for positive
\varepsilon
as long as the respective limiting processes
(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)})_{i\in\{1,2\}}
and
(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)})_{i\in\{1,2\}}
are coupled so that (
E.2
) is satisfied and
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}
are independent. By Proposition
E.1
, there exist such limiting processes
(\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)})_{i\in\{1,2\}}
with such a coupling with
(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)})_{i\in\{1,2\}}
.
We define
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i),\varepsilon}=\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)}
for all
\varepsilon>0
and
i\in\{1,2\}
. Since, as elements of
\mathcal{C}([-\delta\beta^{1/2},\delta\beta^{1/2}])^{\mathcal{T}}
,
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i),\varepsilon})=(\mathfrak{h}^{(i),\varepsilon},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(i)},\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)})}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, and since
\mathcal{T}
is finite, the Portmanteau theorem yields that (
2.13
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\lim_{|\beta|\to\infty}\,\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{s\in\mathcal{T}}\sup_{|x|\leq\delta|\beta|^{1/2}}\left|\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}(x,s)-\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(i)}(x,s)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right),

which equals zero by Proposition
E.1
.
∎

Next we turn to Proposition
E.1
. The following localization statement will be used in the proposition’s proof and its proof in turn will be given after. It restricts the time domain to
[t,t+1]
for arbitrary
t>0
; as we will see in the proof of Proposition
E.1
, this will suffice for our purposes by scale invariance properties of the limiting objects.

Lemma E.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
satisfy the second inequality in (
2.12
) for some
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\mathfrak{h}(x,t)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}(y))
. Let
R
be as in (
2.12
). The supremum has a minimum maximizer
y^{*}(x,t)
and, for each
t>0
fixed,
x\mapsto y^{*}(x,t)
is non-decreasing.
Additionally, for any
K\geq\smash{(1+|\beta|)^{-1/4}}
and any fixed
x,t
with
|x|\leq K(1+|\beta|)t
, there exist constants
C,c>0
depending only on
R
and
t
such that the following holds. With probability at least
1-C\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+|\beta|^{3/2}))
,

(E.4)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in[t,t+1]}|y^{*}(x,s)-x-\beta s|\leq K(1+|\beta|)t.

Proof of Proposition
E.1
.

We give the proof of (
E.2
) in detail, and at the end we will explain the modifications needed to establish (
E.3
).
We assume that
\beta\geq 0
, as the argument for
\beta<0
is analogous. By scale invariance properties of the directed landscape and KPZ fixed point (see Definition
2.4
and
[
MQR21
, Theorem 4.5]
, respectively), it suffices to prove the statement for the time interval
[t,t+1]
instead of
[T^{-1},T]
; one then takes
t=(T^{2}-1)^{-1}
and stretches the time dimension by a factor of
T-T^{-1}
. Finally, as the proposition only concerns the limit
\beta\to\infty
, we will assume
\beta
is large when required. We set the value
\delta=\frac{1}{16}
, so
|x|\leq\frac{1}{16}\beta^{1/2}t
in (
E.2
) and (
E.3
); however we will use
\delta
in the proof for notational convenience instead of its explicit value.

Recall
\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}(x,s)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s))
. By the monotonicity in the maximizer from Lemma
E.2
and since
|x|\leq\delta\beta^{1/2}t
, we know that the minimum maximizer
y^{*}(x,s)
of this supremum satisfies
y^{*}(-\delta\beta^{1/2}t,s)\leq y^{*}(x,s)\leq y^{*}(\delta\beta^{1/2}t,s)
for all
s\in[t,t+1]
. The probability bounds asserted in Lemma
E.2
can be used (with
K=\frac{1}{16}\frac{|\beta|}{1+|\beta|}
and
c=c(R,t)
with
R
as in (
2.12
)) to control the left and righthand sides of these inequalities to yield that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta^{3/2})
, for all
|x|\leq\delta\beta^{1/2}t
and
s\in[t,t+1]
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}(x,s)=\sup_{y\in[\frac{7}{8}\beta t,\frac{9}{8}\beta t]}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(y,0;x,s\bigr)\right).

Next, by Definition
2.4
of the directed landscape, with probability one, it holds for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
s\in[t,t+1]
that
\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s)=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2}))+\mathcal{L}(z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2});x,s))
. So we write, on an event with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta^{3/2})
, for all
s\in[t,t+1]
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}(x,s)=\sup_{y\in[\frac{7}{8}\beta t,\frac{9}{8}\beta t]}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2})\bigr)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2});x,s\bigr)\right).

We wish to put bounds on the set of
z
where the inner supremum can be attained. For each
y
and
s
, let
z_{s}^{*}(y,x)
be the smallest such maximizer; it exists by the fact that
\lim_{|y|\to\infty}(\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}(y))=-\infty
almost surely; to see the latter we use (
2.12
) and the fact that
\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s)=-s^{-1}(x-y)^{2}+\mathcal{A}(y,0;x,s)
with
\mathcal{A}(y,0;x,s)
being of unit order (where recall
\mathcal{A}(y,s;x,t):\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by
\mathcal{A}(y,s;x,t)=\mathcal{L}(y,s;x,t)+(x-y)^{2}/(t-s)
and satisfies certain stationarity properties
[
DOV22
, Lemma 10.2]
, e.g., its one-point distribution at any
(y,s;x,t)
is given by the GUE Tracy-Widom distribution).

We recognize
z_{s}^{*}(y,x)
as the location at height
(1-\beta^{-1/2})s
of a directed landscape geodesic from
(0,y)
to
(x,s)
. By geodesic ordering
[
BGH22
, Lemma 2.7]
, for each fixed
x
and
s
,
y\mapsto z_{s}^{*}(y,x)
is a non-decreasing function and for each fixed
y
and
s
,
x\mapsto z_{s}^{*}(y,x)
is also non-decreasing. Thus it is sufficient to lower bound
z_{s}^{*}(\frac{7}{8}\beta t,-\delta\beta^{1/2}t)
and upper bound
z_{s}^{*}(\frac{9}{8}\beta t,\delta\beta^{1/2}t)
, uniformly over
s\in[t,t+1]
. By transversal fluctuation estimates on geodesics in the directed landscape which are uniform in the endpoints (see
[
GZ22
, Lemma 3.11]
), we know that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in[t,t+1]}\left|z_{s}^{*}(\tfrac{7}{8}\beta t,-\delta\beta^{1/2}t)-(1-\beta^{-1/2})(-\delta\beta^{1/2}t)-\beta^{-1/2}\cdot\tfrac{7}{8}\beta t\right|>\tfrac{1}{16}\beta^{1/2}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp(-c\beta t^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{s\in[t,t+1]}\left|z_{s}^{*}(\tfrac{9}{8}\beta t,\delta\beta^{1/2}t)-(1-\beta^{-1/2})(\delta\beta^{1/2}t)-\beta^{-1/2}\cdot\tfrac{9}{8}\beta t\right|>\tfrac{1}{16}\beta^{1/2}t\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp(-c\beta t^{2})

(the righthand side bounds are not optimal).
So we see that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta t^{2})
, for all
s\in[t,t+1]
,
y\in[\frac{7}{8}\beta t,\frac{9}{8}\beta t]
, and
|x|\leq\delta\beta^{1/2}t

\tfrac{3}{4}\beta^{1/2}t\leq z_{s}^{*}(y,x)\leq\tfrac{5}{4}\beta^{1/2}t,

since
\delta=\frac{1}{16}
.
In other words, absorbing
t
into the constant
c
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta)
, for all
s\in[t,t+1]
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in[\frac{7}{8}\beta t,\frac{9}{8}\beta t]}\sup_{z\in[\frac{3}{4}\beta^{1/2}t,\frac{5}{4}\beta^{1/2}t]}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2})\bigr)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2});x,s\bigr)\right).

Now, applying Lemma
E.2
to
\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{0}
(recall that it satisfies (
2.12
)) with
K=\frac{1}{4}\frac{\beta}{1+\beta}t
to bound the maximizer of the supremum of
y
and a similar argument as above to bound the maximizer of the supremum over
z
yields that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta)
, for all
|x|\leq\delta\beta^{1/2}t
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{|y|\leq\frac{3}{8}\beta t}\sup_{|z|\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta^{1/2}t}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2})\bigr)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2});x,s\bigr)\right).

Now,
\mathcal{L}(z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2});x,s)=-s^{-1}\beta^{1/2}(x-z)^{2}+\mathcal{A}(z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2});x,s)
, and it follows from
[
DOV22
, Proposition 10.6]
that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta^{1/8})
,

(E.5)

\sup_{s\in[t,t+1]}\sup_{|x|\leq\delta\beta^{1/2}t}\sup_{|z|\leq 2\beta^{1/2}t}|\mathcal{A}(z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2});x,s)|\leq C\beta^{-1/12}\log\beta.

Thus we see that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta^{1/8})
, for all
s\in[t,t+1]
(and with the implicit constant in the
O
notation below depending only on
t
),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{|y|\leq\frac{3}{8}\beta t}\sup_{|z|\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta^{1/2}t}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2})\bigr)-\frac{\beta^{1/2}(x-z)^{2}}{s}\right)+O(\beta^{-1/12}\log\beta).

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in[\frac{7}{8}\beta t,\frac{9}{8}\beta t]}\sup_{z\in[\frac{3}{4}\beta^{1/2}t,\frac{5}{4}\beta^{1/2}t]}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(y)+\mathcal{L}\bigl(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2})\bigr)-\frac{\beta^{1/2}(x-z)^{2}}{s}\right)+O(\beta^{-1/12}\log\beta).

Observe that the ranges of
y
in the above two suprema are separated by a distance of order
\beta
, and those of
z
are separated by distance of order
\beta^{1/2}
. Now, it follows from
[
Dau22
, Proposition 2.6]
(after using scale invariance of
\mathcal{L}
) that there exist independent directed landscapes
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
such that, with probability which converges to
1
as
\beta\to\infty
,

(E.6)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{L}(u)=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(u)\text{ for all }u=(y,0;z,s)\text{ with }|y|\leq\tfrac{3}{8}\beta t,|z|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\beta^{1/2}t,s\in[0,t+1]\quad\text{and}\\
&\mathcal{L}(u)=\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{2}(u)\text{ for all }u=(y,0;z,s)\text{ with }y\in[\tfrac{7}{8}\beta t,\tfrac{9}{8}\beta t],z\in[\tfrac{3}{4}\beta^{1/2}t,\tfrac{5}{4}\beta^{1/2}t],s\in[0,t+1].\end{split}

This completes the proof of (
E.2
) by taking

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{|y|\leq\frac{3}{8}\beta t}\sup_{|z|\leq\frac{1}{2}\beta^{1/2}t}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{0}(y)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{1}}\bigl(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2})\bigr)-s^{-1}\beta^{1/2}(x-z)^{2}\right)\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}^{(2)}(x,s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\in[\frac{7}{8}\beta t,\frac{9}{8}\beta t]}\sup_{z\in[\frac{3}{4}\beta^{1/2}t,\frac{5}{4}\beta^{1/2}t]}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{0}(y)+\tilde{\mathcal{L}_{2}}\bigl(y,0;z,s(1-\beta^{-1/2})\bigr)-s^{-1}\beta^{1/2}(x-z)^{2}\right).

A very similar argument also yields (
E.3
) on the asymptotic independence of
\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}(0,s)
and
\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}(-\beta s,s)
, uniformly for
s\in[t,t+1]
, in the
\beta\to\infty
limit, i.e., evaluating each height function at a point which lies on the characteristic emanating from the origin. In more detail, by Lemma
E.2
, it follows that the maximizer for each height function in the variational formula
\sup_{y}(\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}}(y)+\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s))
is, for some large constant
K
, attained inside
\smash{[-K\delta\beta^{1/2}t,K\delta\beta^{1/2}t]}
with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta^{3/4})
for all
s\in[t,t+1]
and
|x|\leq\delta\beta^{1/2}t
. Then, analogous to what was done in the above proof of (
E.2
), one introduces a further supremum at height
\beta^{-1/2}s
and shows using geodesic fluctuation estimates
[
GZ22
, Lemma 3.11]
that the maximizers of that supremum of the two height functions are separated by at least
c\beta^{1/2}
for some
c>0
with probability at least
1-\exp(-c\beta)
(in the above proof the height chosen was
(1-\beta^{-1/2})s
instead as the endpoints were close at height
s
and not
0
). The contribution to the height functions from the temporal strip
[0,\beta^{-1/2}s]
is again
O(\beta^{-1/12}\log\beta)
with probability at least
1-C\exp(-c\beta^{1/8})
as
\beta\to\infty
and one can then again invoke the asymptotic independence from
[
Dau22
, Proposition 2.6]
to complete the proof.
∎

Remark E.3

(Upgrading Proposition
E.1
to a quantitative statement)
.

An inspection of the proof of
[
Dau22
, Proposition 2.6]
shows that it can be upgraded to obtain that (
E.6
) holds with at least probability
1-C\exp(-c\beta^{3/2})
, i.e., a quantitative probability lower bound can be derived instead of just that the limit as
\beta\to\infty
is
1
. With this estimate, it would follow that the limit (
E.2
) in the statement of Proposition
E.1
(and thus also the limit in
\beta
in (
2.13
) in Corollary
2.15
) can be replaced by a lower bound of
1-C\exp(-c\beta^{1/8})
(coming from (
E.5
), which then becomes the bottleneck in the overall probability estimate). To obtain the upgraded estimate on the probability of (
E.6
) holding, one needs an upper bound of
C\exp(-c\beta^{3/2})
on the probability that a geodesic in the prelimiting LPP model of Poissonian LPP has transversal fluctuations at least order
\beta^{1/2}
, the minimum separation between the areas of interest; the latter estimate is recorded in
[
HS18
, Theorem 2.6]
.

Remark E.4

(Weakening the independence assumption)
.

One can also obtain the coupling in Proposition
E.1
(and thus also a form of Corollary
2.15
) under the weaker assumption that, with
R>0
fixed, for every
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, the initial conditions
\smash{(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)})}
satisfying (
2.12
) with
\beta
and
R
can be coupled with independent initial conditions
(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1),\mathrm{ind}},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2),\mathrm{ind}})
such that, for
i\in\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x|\leq\beta^{2}}\left|\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}(x)-\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i),\mathrm{ind}}(x)\right|\geq C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|\right)=0;

such a condition is satisfied, for example, for the stationary horizon (see Lemma
E.6
ahead).
Indeed, on the intersection of the complement of the event in the previous display and the event that for all
(x,t)\in[-\delta\beta^{1/2},\delta\beta^{1/2}]\times[T^{-1},T]
the maximizers in
\mathfrak{h}(\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}},0;x,t)=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{(i)}_{0}(y)})
and the analogous variational formula for
\mathfrak{h}(\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i),\mathrm{ind}}},0;x,t)
both lie in
[-\beta^{2},\beta^{2}]
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{h}(\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}},0;x,t)
and
\mathfrak{h}(\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i),\mathrm{ind}}},0;x,t)
differ by at most
C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|
for all such
(x,t)
. One can then apply Proposition
E.1
to obtain a process
\tilde{\mathfrak{h}}
which is
C|\beta|^{-1/12}\log|\beta|
close to
\smash{\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i),\mathrm{ind}},0;\bm{\cdot},\bm{\cdot})}
and thus also to
\mathfrak{h}(\smash{\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(i)}},0;\bm{\cdot},\bm{\cdot})
(changing
C
to
2C
) on an event whose probability tends to 1. The probability of the condition that the maximizers lie in
[-\beta^{2},\beta^{2}]
(that the above holds on) can be bounded using Lemma
E.2
.

Proof of Lemma
E.2
.

In the proof we assume
\beta\geq 0
; an analogous argument applies to the case of
\beta<0
. We also note that it is sufficient to prove the result under the assumption that
K(1+\beta)\geq K_{0}
for some
K_{0}=K_{0}(R,t)
(in addition to the already assumed
K\geq(1+\beta)^{-1/4})
; all lower values of
K
are handled by adjusting the constant
C
(i.e., the bound is made to be trivial in this range).

That the maximizer
y^{*}(x,s)
exists again follows (as in the existence of
z^{*}_{s}(y,x)
in the proof of Proposition
E.1
) from the fact that
\lim_{|y|\to\infty}(\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}(y))=-\infty
almost surely.

[
RV24
, Lemma 3.8]
yields that
x\mapsto y^{*}(x,s)
is non-decreasing for every fixed
s
.
Next, let

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}(y)=\mathfrak{h}_{0}(y)-2\beta y;

then (
2.12
) implies that
|\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}(y)|\leq|y|+R\beta
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Now, it is easy to calculate that
\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}(-(x-y)^{2}/s+2\beta y)
(i.e., ignoring
\bar{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
and
\mathcal{A}
in
\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}(y))
) is attained at
y=\beta s+x
, where the supremum equals
\beta^{2}s+2\beta x
. By
[
DOV22
, Proposition 10.6]
,
\sup_{s\in[t,t+1]}\mathcal{A}(\beta s+x,0;x,s)
is lower bounded by
-K(1+\beta)
with probability at least
1-\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2}))
. So we see that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2}))
, for all
s\in[t,t+1]
,

(E.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}(\beta s+x,s)\geq\beta^{2}s+2\beta x-\beta(t+1)-|x|-R\beta-K(1+\beta).

We recall the estimate
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{A}(y,0;x,t)-|y-x|)>\frac{1}{2}K(1+\beta))\leq C\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2}))
from
[
GH22
, Proposition 9.5]
and
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{s\in[t,t+1]}|\mathcal{A}(y,0;x,s)-\mathcal{A}(y,0;x,t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}K(1+\beta))\leq C\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2}))
from
[
DOV22
, Proposition 10.5]
. Together these imply that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R},s\in[t,t+1]}\left(\mathcal{A}(y,0;x,s)-|y-x|\right)>K(1+\beta)\right)\leq C\exp\left(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2})\right).

Recall also that
\mathfrak{h}_{0}(y)\leq 2\beta y+|y|+R\beta
. Then we see that, with probability at least
1-\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2}))
, for all
s\in[t,t+1]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{y:|y-\beta s-x|\geq K(1+\beta)t}\bigl(\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,s)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}(y)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{y:|y-\beta s-x|\geq K(1+\beta)t}\left(-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{s}+2\beta y+2|y|\right)+\sup_{y:|y-\beta s-x|\geq K(1+\beta)t}\Bigl(\mathcal{A}(y,0;x,s)-|y|+R\beta\Bigr)

(E.8)

\displaystyle\leq\beta^{2}s+2\beta x-K^{2}(1+\beta)^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{t+1}+3|x|+2t(K(1+\beta)+\beta)+K(1+\beta)+R\beta.

Thus (
E.7
) is strictly larger than (
E.8
) with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2}))
as long as

(E.9)

\displaystyle K^{2}(1+\beta)^{2}\frac{t^{2}}{t+1}>4|x|+2R\beta+2(1+t)K(1+\beta)+3\beta t+\beta.

Now, for all
K
,
\beta
such that
K\geq(1+\beta)^{-1/4}
and
K(1+\beta)\geq K_{0}
for some fixed large constant
K_{0}=K_{0}(R,t)
it holds that
\beta(3t+R+1)\leq\frac{1}{2}K^{2}(1+\beta)^{2}t^{2}/(t+1)
(by letting
K_{0}\geq(4+R)^{2}((t+1)\cdot\max(t,1)/t^{2})^{2}
so that the inequality is implied by
(4+R)K^{3/2}(1+\beta)^{3/2}\max(t,1)\geq\beta(3t+R+1)
, which is satisfied under the assumptions). Since also
|x|\leq K(1+\beta)t
, it follows that (
E.9
) holds for large enough
K_{0}(R,t)
. Thus
(
E.7
)
is larger than (
E.8
) with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cK^{3/2}(1+\beta^{3/2}))
. This completes the proof.
∎

E.2.
Proofs of space-time covariance statements

We begin by proving half of Corollary
2.16
, namely the limits of the on-diagonal terms in (
2.15
), which is essentially a consequence of results of
[
LS23
]
.

Proof of Corollary
2.16
, on-diagonal limits in (
2.15
).

We are studying the limits of
S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{kk}
. The
k=2
case was established in
[
LS23
, Corollary 2.6]
.
We next deal with the
k=1
case. By color merging (Remark
2.6
), this is equivalent to the case of uncolored ASEP with stationary initial condition of density
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\varepsilon^{1/3}
, i.e., the particle configuration is independent Bernoulli(
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\varepsilon^{1/3}
).
Now consider ASEP run from that stationary initial condition, and introduce a second-class particle at the origin. In terms of colored particles, we have a single particle of color 1 at the origin, and the color 2 particles are placed according to an independent Bernoulli(
\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\varepsilon^{1/3}
) process everywhere else. Let
X^{\beta,\varepsilon}(t)
be the position of the color
1
particle at time
t
and let
\eta_{t}
be the particle configuration of the color 2 particles at time
t
, i.e.,
\eta_{t}(x)=1
if
x
is occupied by a color
2
particle and
\eta_{t}(x)=0
otherwise. Then by
[
BS09
, eq. (2.1)]
, for all
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(X^{\beta,\varepsilon}(t)=x\right)=4(1-\beta^{2}\varepsilon^{2/3})^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}\left(\eta_{t}(x),\eta_{0}(0)\right)=4(1-\beta^{2}\varepsilon^{1/3})S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{11}(t,x);

note that this implies
S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{11}(t,x)\geq 0
. For
g:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
, let
\Delta_{i}g(i)=g(i+1)-g(i)
. We now perform the integration against a smooth compactly supported test function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. We define
f_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
by
f_{\varepsilon}(x)=f(\varepsilon^{2/3}x)
and let
M
be such that
f(x)=0
on
[-M,M]^{c}
. It follows from the previous display that, for all
\varepsilon>0
small enough,

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{11}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+i)f_{\varepsilon}(i)\right|

\displaystyle=4\left(1-\beta^{2}\varepsilon^{2/3}\right)^{-1}\left|\sum_{i=-\lfloor M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor}^{\lfloor M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\left(X^{\beta,\varepsilon}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})=\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+i\right)f_{\varepsilon}(i)\right|

\displaystyle\leq 8\cdot\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|f(x)|\sum_{i=-\lfloor M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor}^{\lfloor M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor}\mathbb{P}\left(X^{\beta,\varepsilon}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})=\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+i\right).

The sum of probabilities is upper bounded by
\mathbb{P}(X^{\beta,\varepsilon}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})\geq\lfloor(2x-M)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor)
, which in turn can be upper bounded, by adding and subtracting the location
2\beta\varepsilon^{-2/3}
on the characteristic and using
[
LS23
, Theorem 2.5]
, by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|X^{\beta,\varepsilon}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1})+2\beta\varepsilon^{-2/3}|\geq\lfloor(2x-M)\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+2\beta\varepsilon^{-2/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\exp(-c\beta^{3}),

which tends to 0 as
\beta\to\infty
. This completes the proof.
∎

In the remainder of this section we focus on proving the second half of Corollary
2.16
, namely, (
2.16
).
The following is a consequence of summation by parts and will be useful in its proof. Here
h_{0}^{(\ell)}
and
h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h^{(k)}_{0},0;x,t)
are the ASEP height functions associated to
\eta^{(\ell)}_{0}
and
\eta^{(k)}_{t}
, respectively, as defined in Section
2.2.6
.

Lemma E.5
.

Let
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
be finitely supported and
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then for any
t>0
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\{k,\ell\}=\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\eta^{(k)}_{t}(x+i),\eta^{(\ell)}_{0}(y)\right)f(i)

\displaystyle=-(6M+1)^{-1}\sum_{|j|\leq 3M}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0}^{(k)},0;x+i,t),h_{0}^{(\ell)}(y+j)\right)(\Delta^{2}f)(i-j),

where
(\Delta^{2}f)(x)=f(x+1)+f(x-1)-2f(x)
is the discrete Laplacian.

Proof.

We first note that, by the stationarity of the particle configuration,
\mathrm{Cov}(\eta^{(k)}_{t}(x+i),\eta^{(\ell)}_{0}(y))=\mathrm{Cov}(\eta^{(k)}_{t}(x+i+j),\eta^{(\ell)}_{0}(y+j))
for any
x,y,i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Thus for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\eta^{(k)}_{t}(x+i),\eta^{(\ell)}_{0}(y)\right)f(i)

\displaystyle=(6M+1)^{-1}\sum_{|j|\leq 3M}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\eta^{(k)}_{t}(x+i+j+1),\eta^{(\ell)}_{0}(y+j+1)\right)f(i).

Next, for any function
g:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}g(z)=g(z+1)-g(z)
be the discrete forward derivative. Then it holds by definition that
\eta_{0}^{(\ell)}(z+1)=-\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}h^{(\ell)}_{0}(z)
and
\eta_{t}^{(k)}(z+1)=-\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h^{(k)}_{0},0;z,t)
.
We now do the change of variable
i\mapsto i-j
and apply summation by parts on
i
to obtain that the previous display equals (the boundary terms disappear since
f
is finitely supported, and
\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{i}
indicates the discrete forward derivative with respect to
i
)

\displaystyle-(6M+1)^{-1}\sum_{|j|\leq 3M}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h^{(k)}_{0},0;x+i,t),\eta^{(\ell)}_{0}(y+j+1)\right)\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{i}(f(i-j-1)).

Changing the order of summation (they are both finite sums) and applying summation by parts with respect to
j
yields

\displaystyle(6M+1)^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{|j|\leq 3M}\mathrm{Cov}\left(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h^{(k)}_{0},0;x+i,t),h^{(\ell)}_{0}(y+j)\right)\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{j}\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{i}(f(i-j))

\displaystyle-(6M+1)^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h^{(k)}_{0},0;x+i,t),h^{(\ell)}_{0}(y+3M+1)\right)\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{i}(f(i-3M-1))

\displaystyle+(6M+1)^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h^{(k)}_{0},0;x+i,t),h^{(\ell)}_{0}(y-3M)\right)\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{i}(f(i+3M)).

The final two sums are equal and so cancel each other, as can be seen by performing the change of variable
i\mapsto i+3M+1
and
i\mapsto i-3M
in the first and second, respectively, and then invoking the stationarity of the covariance. This completes the proof after noting that
\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{i}\Delta^{\hskip-0.81949pt\mathrm{f}}_{j}(f(i-j))=-(\Delta^{2}f)(i-j)
.
∎

We will also need to know an asymptotic independence statement for the stationary horizon, the limit of the height functions associated to the stationary colored particle configuration. The following will be proven in Section
E.3
.

Lemma E.6
.

For every
\beta>0
, there exists a pair of independent standard two-sided Brownian motions
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
, a random process
M_{\beta}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, and a coupling of
(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{0},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\beta})
of the stationary horizon with parameter
(0,\beta)
with
B_{1},B_{2},M_{\beta}
such that the following holds:
(i)
(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{0}(x),\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\beta}(x))=(2^{1/2}B_{1}(x),2^{1/2}B_{2}(x)+2\beta x+M_{\beta}(x))
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and (ii)
M_{\beta}(x)
satisfies, for some absolute constants
C,c>0
and all
K,R,\beta>0
,

(E.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x|\leq R\beta^{2}}|M_{\beta}(x)|>K\beta^{-1}\right)\leq CR\beta^{4}\exp(-cK).

In particular, by taking
K=\beta^{1/2}
,
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{0}(x)
and
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\beta}(x)-2\beta x
are asymptotically independent as
\beta\to\infty
.

Proof of Corollary
2.16
, (
2.16
) and off-diagonal terms of (
2.15
).

The limits of the off-diagonal terms of (
2.15
) being zero is implied by (
2.16
), so it is sufficient to prove the latter. We start under the assumed condition that
\{k,\ell\}=\{1,2\}
; we will later treat the two cases
(k,\ell)=(1,2)
and
(k,\ell)=(2,1)
separately.
Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a smooth compactly supported function, and let
M>0
be such that
f
is supported on
[-M,M]
. Define
f_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
by
f_{\varepsilon}(x)=f(\varepsilon^{2/3}x)
for all
\varepsilon>0
; so
f_{\varepsilon}
is supported on
\llbracket-\lceil M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rceil,\lceil M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rceil\rrbracket
.
For shorthand, let
h^{(k)}_{2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t}(x)=h^{\mathrm{ASEP}}(h_{0}^{(k)},0;x,2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t)
. We note that
(h_{0}^{(1)},h_{0}^{(2)})
are the height functions at time 0 for the multi-color stationary ASEP with parameters
\bm{\rho}=(\frac{1}{2}\beta\varepsilon^{1/2},\frac{1}{2})
as defined in Section
2.2.8
.

By Lemma
E.5
, taking
M
there to be
\lfloor M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}S^{\beta,\varepsilon}_{kl}(2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1},\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+i)f_{\varepsilon}(i)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon^{2/3}}{6\lceil M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rceil+1}\sum_{|j|\leq 3\lceil M\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rceil}\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\varepsilon^{1/3}h^{(k)}_{2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t}(\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+i),\varepsilon^{1/3}h_{0}^{(\ell)}(\lfloor 2y\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+j)\right)

(E.11)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times(\varepsilon^{-2/3})^{2}(\Delta^{2}f_{\varepsilon})(i-j).

In our case, since
h_{0}^{(k)}
and
h_{0}^{(\ell)}
are (marginally) stationary for (uncolored) ASEP, we know by Cauchy-Schwarz along with the tail probability bounds of
[
LS23
, Theorem 2.4]
that, for any deterministic sequences
(i_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
and
(j_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
such that
i_{\varepsilon},j_{\varepsilon}\in[-CM\varepsilon^{-2/3},CM\varepsilon^{-2/3}]
for each
\varepsilon>0
and any fixed
C
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{1/3}\left(h^{(k)}_{2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t}(\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+i_{\varepsilon})+\lfloor x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+\tfrac{1}{2}i_{\varepsilon}-\tfrac{1}{2}t\varepsilon^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\times\varepsilon^{1/3}\left(h_{0}^{(\ell)}(\lfloor 2y\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+j_{\varepsilon})+\lfloor y\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+\tfrac{1}{2}j_{\varepsilon}\right)

is uniformly integrable as a sequence in
\varepsilon
. Thus the convergence in distribution asserted by Corollaries
2.12
and
2.14
implies convergence of the covariance, i.e., we have, letting
z_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{2/3}i_{\varepsilon}
and
z_{j}=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{2/3}j_{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\varepsilon^{1/3}h^{(k)}_{2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t}(\lfloor 2x\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+i_{\varepsilon}),\varepsilon^{1/3}h_{0}^{(\ell)}(\lfloor 2y\varepsilon^{-2/3}\rfloor+j_{\varepsilon})\right)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{4}\cdot\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(k)},0;x+z_{i},t),\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(\ell)}(y+z_{j})\right),

where
(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)})
are the stationary horizon with parameters
(\beta,0)
, and the
\smash{\frac{1}{4}}
comes from extra scaling factors of
\frac{1}{2}
needed for each of
h^{(k)}_{2\gamma^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1}t}
and
h_{0}^{(\ell)}
to converge to
\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(k)}_{0},0;\cdot,t)
and
\mathfrak{h}^{(\ell)}_{0}
, respectively; see Definition
2.11
.

Since
(\Delta^{2}f_{\varepsilon})(i_{\varepsilon}-j_{\varepsilon})=f(\varepsilon^{2/3}(i_{\varepsilon}-j_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2/3})+f(\varepsilon^{2/3}(i_{\varepsilon}-j_{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon^{2/3})-2f(\varepsilon^{2/3}(i_{\varepsilon}-j_{\varepsilon}))
, it is also immediate that
(\varepsilon^{-2/3})^{2}(\Delta^{2}f_{\varepsilon})(i_{\varepsilon}-j_{\varepsilon})\to f^{\prime\prime}(2(z_{i}-z_{j}))
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. It thus follows that the righthand side of (
E.11
) converges, as
\varepsilon\to 0
, to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{24M}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[-3M,3M]}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(k)},0;x+z_{i},t),\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(\ell)}(y+z_{j})\right)f^{\prime\prime}(2(z_{i}-z_{j}))\,\mathrm{d}z_{i}\,\mathrm{d}z_{j}.

Since
f
is smooth and supported on
[-M,M]
by assumption,
|f^{\prime\prime}(2(z_{i}-z_{j}))|
is bounded and supported on
|z_{i}-z_{j}|\leq\frac{1}{2}M
. Also, the covariance function in the integrand is stationary, so we may shift both its arguments by
-z_{i}
. So the proof is complete if we show that

(E.12)

\sup_{z\in[-M,M]}\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(k)},0;x,t),\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(\ell)}(y+z)\right)\to 0

as
\beta\to\infty
; recall also from our assumptions that
|x|,|y|
are allowed to be as large as
\beta^{2}
. The previous display in turn is a consequence of Lemma
E.6
, as we explain next.

We first address the case
(k,\ell)=(2,1)
; the case
(k,\ell)=(1,2)
is analogous and will be handled after.
For notational convenience, let
\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{t}(z)=\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0},0;z,t)
for all
z
. Since
(\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)})
is distributed as the stationary horizon with parameter
(\beta,0)
, by the coupling from Lemma
E.6
, we can write
(\mathfrak{h}^{(1),\beta}_{0}(z),\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(z))=(2^{1/2}B_{1}(z)+2\beta z+M_{\beta}(z),2^{1/2}B_{2}(z))
for all
z
, where
(B_{1}
,
B_{2}
) are two independent standard two-sided Brownian motions and
M_{\beta}
is a process on
\mathbb{R}
. Then we can write, for every
\beta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{t}(x),\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)}(y+z)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cov}\left(\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+B_{2}(w)\Bigr),B_{1}(y+z)+2\beta(y+z)+M_{\beta}(y+z)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cov}\left(\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+B_{2}(w)\Bigr),M_{\beta}(y+z)\right),

the second equality by the independence of
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
, and
\mathcal{L}
. Now, by Cauchy-Schwarz, it is enough to show that
\mathbb{E}[M_{\beta}(y+z)^{2}]\to 0
as
\beta\to\infty
since all moments of
\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+B_{2}(y))
are uniformly bounded in
\beta
(as follows, for instance, from
[
FV21
, Theorem 1.1]
). That
\mathbb{E}[M_{\beta}(y+z)^{2}]\to 0
as
\beta\to\infty
follows from Lemma
E.6
, using that
|y+z|\leq 2\beta^{2}
for all large
\beta
. This bound is uniform over
z
so (
E.12
) is established in the
(k,\ell)=(2,1)
case.

Finally we turn to the case of
(k,\ell)=(1,2)
. For notational convenience we let
\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{t}(z)=\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{h}^{(1),\beta}_{0},0;z,t)
for all
z
. We also have by Lemma
E.6
that
(\mathfrak{h}^{(1),\beta}_{0},\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0})
can be coupled with
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
,
M_{\beta}
such that
(\mathfrak{h}^{(1),\beta}_{0}(z),\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(z))=(2^{1/2}B_{1}(z)+2\beta z+M_{\beta}(z),2^{1/2}B_{2}(z))
for all
z
. Then we may write, for all
\beta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{(1)}_{t}(x),\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}(y+z)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cov}\left(\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)}(w)\Bigr),\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}(y+z)\right).

Now, it follows from Lemma
E.2
(since
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1)}
satisfies the second inequality in (
2.12
), i.e., the one written there for
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}
) that the event
\mathsf{LocalizedMax}
that the supremum in the first argument of the covariance term is attained within
w\in[-\beta^{2},\beta^{2}]
has probability at least
1-C\exp(-c\beta^{3})
. Recall
\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}(y+z)]=0
since it is a two-sided Brownian motion. So we may write

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}\left(\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1),\beta}(w)\Bigr),\mathfrak{h}^{(2)}_{0}(y+z)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1),\beta}(w)\Bigr)\right)\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}(y+z)\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{LocalizedMax}}+\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{LocalizedMax}^{c}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\Bigl|\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1),\beta}(w)\Bigr)\Bigr|^{3}\right]^{1/3}\mathbb{E}\left[|\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}(y+z)|^{3}\right]^{1/3}\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{LocalizedMax}^{c})^{1/3}

(E.13)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sup_{|w|\leq\beta^{2}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1),\beta}(w)\Bigr)\right)\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(2)}(y+z)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{LocalizedMax}}\right],

using the general form of the Hölder inequality. Now, the first expectation in the first term of (
E.13
) grows at most polynomially in
\beta
: this follows by the triangle inequality since we can write
\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{(1),\beta}(w))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+2^{1/2}\tilde{B}(w)+2\beta w)
for
\tilde{B}
a standard two-sided Brownian motion; it holds as a process in
w
that
\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+2\beta w\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\smash{d}}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(w-\beta t,0;x,t)+2\beta x+\beta^{2}t

[
DOV22
, Lemma 10.2]
; it holds as a process in
w
that
\tilde{B}(w)=B(w-\beta t)+(\beta t)^{1/2}X
for
B
another standard two-sided Brownian motion and
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
an independent standard normal random variable; and since we have exponentially decaying tails for
\sup_{w\in\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+2^{1/2}B(w))

[
FV21
]
.
Next, the second expectation in the first factor of (
E.13
) does not depend on
\beta
, and
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{LocalizedMax}^{c})\to 0
superexponentially quickly in
\beta
as
\beta\to\infty
, so the first term in (
E.13
) vanishes in the limit. Using Lemma
E.6
, we write the second term as

(E.14)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sup_{|w|\leq\beta^{2}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+B_{1}(w)+2\beta w+M_{\beta}(w)\Bigr)\right)B_{2}(y+z)\right]\\
&-\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\sup_{|w|\leq\beta^{2}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+B_{1}(w)+2\beta w+M_{\beta}(w)\Bigr)\right)B_{2}(y+z)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{LocalizedMax}}^{c}\right].\end{split}

The second term in the previous display tends to
0
as
\beta\to\infty
by a similar argument as just above, using the Hölder inequality (as for the first term of (
E.13
)) and using that the third moment of the first factor in the expectation grows at most polynomially while
\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{LocalizedMax}^{c})
decays at least exponentially, both in
\beta
. Next we note that the quantity inside the first expectation of the previous display is upper and lower bounded, respectively, by

\displaystyle\sup_{|w|\leq\beta^{2}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+B_{1}(w)+2\beta w\Bigr)B_{2}(y+z)+\sup_{|w|\leq\beta^{2}}|M_{\beta}(w)|\cdot|B_{2}(y+z)|\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\sup_{|w|\leq\beta^{2}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(w,0;x,t)+B_{1}(w)+2\beta w\Bigr)B_{2}(y+z)-\sup_{|w|\leq\beta^{2}}|M_{\beta}(w)|\cdot|B_{2}(y+z)|.

By independence of
\mathcal{L}
,
B_{1}
, and
B_{2}
, the expectation of the first terms in both these quantities is zero, and, by Cauchy-Schwarz and (
E.10
), the expectation of the second term in both quantities tends to zero as
\beta\to\infty
. Putting this together yields that (
E.14
) tends to zero as
\beta\to\infty
, which implies the same for the lefthand side of (
E.13
). This establishes (
E.12
) in the case of
(k,\ell)=(1,2)
and hence completes the proof.
∎

E.3.
Asymptotic independence of the stationary horizon

Here we give the proof of Lemma
E.6
.

Proof of Lemma
E.6
.

It is well-known (see, e.g.,
[
MP10
, Exercise 2.16]
or
[
KS14
, (5.13)]
) that if
B:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is a standard Brownian motion and
\sigma>0
, then

(E.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x\geq 0}(\sigma B(x)-2\beta x)>m\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{4m\beta}{\sigma^{2}}\right).

Let
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
be independent standard two-sided Brownian motions. By
[
BSS24
, Definition D.1 and eq. (D.1)]
,
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{0}(x)
and
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\beta}(x)
jointly (as processes) have the same distribution as
2^{1/2}B_{1}(x)
and

\displaystyle 2^{1/2}B_{1}(x)+\sup_{-\infty<y\leq x}\left(2^{1/2}B_{2}(y)+2\beta y-2^{1/2}B_{1}(y)\right)-\sup_{-\infty<y\leq 0}\left(2^{1/2}B_{2}(y)+2\beta y-2^{1/2}B_{1}(y)\right).

We will assume we work on a probability space which supports
B_{1},B_{2},\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{0}
, and
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\beta}
such that the above identity in law holds as an almost sure equality on the space. Then we can rewrite
\mathfrak{h}_{0}^{\beta}(x)-2\beta x
as
2^{1/2}B_{2}(x)+M_{\beta}(x)
, where

\begin{split}&M_{\beta}(x):=\sup_{-\infty<y\leq x}\left(2^{1/2}(B_{2}(y)-B_{2}(x))+2\beta(y-x)-2^{1/2}(B_{1}(y)-B_{1}(x))\right)\\
&-\sup_{-\infty<y\leq 0}\left(2^{1/2}B_{2}(y)+2\beta y-2^{1/2}B_{1}(y)\right).\end{split}

Now, observe that by the Markov property, invariance under time reversal, and scale invariance of
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
, each of the suprema are for fixed
x
(i.e., marginally) equal in distribution to

(E.16)

\displaystyle\sup_{y\geq 0}\left(2B(y)-2\beta y\right),

where
B
is a standard Brownian motion. Thus by (
E.15
) and a union bound we see that
M_{\beta}(x)
satisfies, for an absolute constant
c>0
and all
K>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(E.17)

\mathbb{P}(|M_{\beta}(x)|>K\beta^{-1})\leq 2\exp(-cK).

We have to obtain a similar bound for
\sup_{x\in[-R\beta^{2},R\beta^{2}]}|M_{\beta}(x)|
. We first claim that, there exist
c>0
and
C
such that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

(E.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{z\in[-\beta^{-2},\beta^{-2}]}|M_{\beta}(x)-M_{\beta}(x+z)|\geq K\beta^{-1}\right)\leq C\exp(-cK).

By symmetry, it suffices to bound
\sup_{z\in[-\beta^{-2},\beta^{-2}]}M_{\beta}(x)-M_{\beta}(x+z)
. Let
B(w)=2^{-1/2}(B_{2}(x-w)-B_{2}(x)-(B_{1}(x-w)-B_{1}(x)))
; note that it is distributed as a standard two-sided Brownian motion. Then, for
z\in[-\beta^{-2},\beta^{-2}]
,

\displaystyle M_{\beta}(x)-M_{\beta}(x+z)

\displaystyle=\sup_{0\leq w<\infty}\bigl(2B(w)-2\beta w\bigr)-\sup_{-z\leq w<\infty}\bigl(2B(w)-2\beta(w+z)-2B(-z)\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0\leq w<\infty}\bigl(2B(w)-2\beta w\bigr)-\sup_{\beta^{-2}\leq w<\infty}\bigl(2B(w)-2\beta w+2\beta^{-1}\bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+2\sup_{w\in[-\beta^{-2},\beta^{-2}]}|B(-w)|.

Now,
\sup_{z\in[-\beta^{-2},\beta^{-2}]}|B(-z)|
is at most
K\beta^{-1}
with probability at least
1-\exp(-cK^{2})
.
Next, by (
E.15
), the first supremum is at most
K\beta^{-1}
with probability at least
1-\exp(-cK)
. We can write the second supremum as

\displaystyle\sup_{\beta^{-2}\leq w<\infty}\Bigl(2\bigl(B(w)-B(\beta^{-2})\bigr)-2\beta(w-\beta^{-2})\Bigr)+2B(\beta^{-2});

by the Markov property, the supremum is equal in distribution to (
E.16
). Thus, by (
E.15
), Gaussian tail bounds, and a union bound, the last display is smaller than
K\beta^{-1}
with probability at least
1-2\exp(-cK)
. By relabeling
K
, this establishes (
E.18
), and, on combining with (
E.17
), we obtain
\mathbb{P}(\sup_{y\in[-\beta^{-2},\beta^{-2}]}|M_{\beta}(x+y)|>K\beta^{-1})\leq C\exp(-cK)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Now doing a union bound over a selection of order
R\beta^{4}
many intervals of size
\beta^{-2}
which cover
[-R\beta^{2},R\beta^{2}]
yields that, for all
\beta>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x\in[-R\beta^{2},R\beta^{2}]}|M_{\beta}(x)|>K\beta^{-1}\right)\leq CR\beta^{4}\exp(-cK).\qed
